Preface

Setup and aim

Our aim is to provide asymptotic formulas for the probabilities

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
, where
X_{n}
is a Markov chain,
x
is some initial state,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}:={\mathbb{P}}[\ \cdot\ |X_{1}=x]
,

\displaystyle S_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}),

and
z_{N}
are real numbers not too far from
\mathbb{E}(S_{N})
.

Such results are called
local limit theorems (LLT)
,
1
1

1

By contrast,
central
limit theorems describe
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a\sqrt{{\rm Var}(S_{N})},b\sqrt{{\rm Var}(S_{N})})]
.
and they have a long history, see the end of this chapter.
The novelty of this work is that we allow the Markov chain to be
inhomogeneous
. This means that we allow the set of states, the transition probabilities, and the summands
f_{n}

to depend on
n
.

We will always assume that
f_{n}
are uniformly bounded real-valued functions, and that
\{X_{n}\}
is uniformly elliptic, a technical condition which will be stated in chapter
1
, and which implies uniform exponential mixing.

These assumptions place us in the Gaussian domain of attraction. The analogy with classical results for sums of independent identically distributed (iid) random variables suggests that in the best of all situations, we should expect the following (in what follows
V_{N}={\rm Var}(S_{N})\text{ and }A_{N}\sim B_{N}\Leftrightarrow A_{N}/B_{N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1
):

(1)

Local deviations:
If
\displaystyle\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, then
\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\sim\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}|a-b|.

(2)

Moderate deviations:
If
\displaystyle\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\to 0
, then

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\sim\frac{e^{-\frac{1+o(1)}{2}\bigl(\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\bigr)^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}|a-b|.

(3)

Large deviations:
If
\displaystyle\left|\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\right|<c
with
c>0
sufficiently small, then for every
x
in the state space of
X_{1}
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\sim\frac{e^{-V_{N}\mathfs I_{N}(\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}})}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\cdot\int_{a}^{b}e^{-t\xi_{N}\bigl(\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\bigr)}dt\cdot\rho_{N}\left(x,\tfrac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\right),\text{ where }

\circ

\mathfs I_{N}(\cdot)
are the Legendre transforms of
\mathfs F_{N}(\xi):=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi S_{N}})
.

\circ

\xi_{N}:(-c,c)\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy
\xi_{N}(0)=0
,
\mathrm{sgn}(\xi_{N}(\eta))=\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)
, and
\exists C>0
independent of
N
s.t.
C^{-1}|\eta|\leq|\xi_{N}(\eta)|\leq C|\eta|
for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
|\eta|<c
.

\circ

\rho_{N}(x,t)\xrightarrow[t\to 0]{}1
uniformly in
N,x
, and
\rho_{n}(\cdot,\cdot)
are uniformly bounded away from
0,\infty
on
\mathfrak{S}_{1}\times[-c,c]
where
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
is the state space of
X_{1}
.

\circ

c,\xi_{N},\rho_{N}
depend on the Markov chain, but not on
z_{N}
or on
(a,b)
.

(The asymptotic results in the large deviation regime are more precise than
in the moderate deviation case, but less universal. See Chapter
6
for more details.)

Although the asymptotic formulas (1)–(3) above are true in many cases, they do sometime fail — even when
S_{N}
is a sum of iid’s.
The aim of this work is to give general sufficient conditions for (1)–(3), and to provide the necessary asymptotic corrections when some of these conditions fail. To do this we first identify all the obstructions to (1)–(3), and then we analyze
S_{N}
when these obstructions happen.

The obstructions to the local limit theorems

The
algebraic range
is the smallest closed additive subgroup
G\leq\mathbb{R}
for which there are
c_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
so that
f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})-c_{n}\in G
almost surely for all
n
. We show that the following list is a complete set of obstructions to (1)–(3):

(I)

Lattice behavior
: The algebraic range is
t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

(II)

Center-tightness
:
{\rm Var}(S_{N})
does not tend to infinity. In chapter
2
we will see that in this case
{\rm Var}(S_{N})
must be bounded.

(III)

Reducibility
:

f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})=g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})

where
\{c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is center-tight, and the algebraic range of
\{g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is strictly smaller than the algebraic range of
\{f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
.

One of our main results is that (1)–(3) hold whenever (I), (II), (III) fail.

How to show that the obstructions do not occur

While it is usually easy to rule out the lattice obstruction (I), it is often not clear how to rule out (II) and (III). What is needed is a tool that determines from the data of
f_{n}
and
X_{n}
whether
\{f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is center-tight or reducible.

In chapter
1
, we introduce numerical constants
d_{n}(\xi)

(n\geq 3,\xi\in\mathbb{R})
which are defined purely in terms of the transition probabilities
\pi_{n,n+1}(x,E):={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}\in E|X_{n}=x)
and the functions
f_{n}(x,y)
, and which can be used to determine which obstructions occur and which vanish:

\circ

If
\sum d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\infty
for all
\xi\neq 0
, then the obstructions (I),(II),(III) do not occur, and the asymptotic expansions (1)–(3) hold.

\circ

If
\sum d_{n}^{2}(\xi)<\infty
for all
\xi\neq 0
, then
{\rm Var}(S_{N})
is bounded (obstruction II).

\circ

If
\sum d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\infty
for some but not all
\xi\neq 0
, then
{\rm Var}(S_{N})\to\infty
but we are either lattice or reducible: (II) fails, but at least one of (I),(III) occurs.

We call
d_{n}(\xi)
the
structure constants
of
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}
and
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}
.

What happens when the obstructions do occur

(I) The lattice case

The lattice obstruction (I) already happens for sums of iid’s, and the classical approach how to adjust (1)–(3) to this setup extends without much difficulty to the inhomogeneous Markov case.

Suppose the algebraic range is
t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
, i.e. there are constants
c_{n}
such that
f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})-c_{n}\in t\mathbb{Z}
almost surely for all
n
. Assume further that
t\mathbb{Z}
is the smallest group with this property.
In this case

S_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}\text{ a.s. for all $N$},

where
\gamma_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{i}\mod t\mathbb{Z}
. Instead of analyzing
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
, which might be equal to zero, we study

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}=kt],\text{ with }k\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ fixed and }\ z_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}.

We show that in case (I), if the algebraic range is
t\mathbb{Z}
, and obstructions (II) and (III) do not occur, then (as in the case of iid’s):

(1’)

If
\displaystyle\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
,
z_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}=kt]\sim\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}|t|.

(2’)

If
\displaystyle\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\to 0
,
z_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}=kt]\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}e^{-\frac{1+o(1)}{2}\bigl(\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\bigr)^{2}}|t|.

(3’)

If
\displaystyle\left|\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\right|<c
with
c>0
sufficiently small,
z_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}
and
(a,b)\cap t\mathbb{Z}\neq\varnothing
, then for every
x
in the state space of
X_{1}
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\sim\frac{e^{-V_{N}\mathfs I_{N}(\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}})}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\cdot\sum_{\tau\in(a,b)\cap t\mathbb{Z}}|t|e^{-\tau\xi_{N}(\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}})}\cdot\rho_{N}\left(x,\tfrac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\right)

where
\mathfs I_{N}(\cdot)
,
\rho_{N}
and
\xi_{N}
have the properties listed in the non-lattice case (3).

The previous results hold for lattice valued, irreducible, non-center tight additive functionals,
that is, when (I) holds and (II),(III) fail. Here is an equivalent condition in terms of the data of
X_{n}
and
f_{n}
:

\exists t\neq 0\text{ s.t. }\sum d_{n}^{2}(\xi)<\infty\text{ exactly when }\xi\in\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}.

Under this condition, (1’)–(3’) hold with parameter
|t|
.

(II) The center-tight case

We show that obstruction (II) happens iff
f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
can be put in the form

f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})=a_{n+1}(X_{n+1})-a_{n}(X_{n})+h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+c_{n}

(
\ast
)

where
a_{n}(X_{n})
are uniformly bounded,
c_{n}
are constants,
h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
have mean zero, and
\sum{\rm Var}[h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})]<\infty
.

The freedom in choosing
a_{n}(X_{n})
is too great to allow general statements on the asymptotic behavior of
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
, see Example
2.25
.
2
2

2

Throughout this work, Example X.Y is example number Y in chapter X. Similarly for Theorems, Propositions etc.
But as we shall we see in chapter
2
, (

∗

) does provide us with some almost sure control:

S_{N}=a_{N+1}(X_{N+1})-a_{1}(X_{1})+\sum_{n=1}^{N}h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+\gamma_{N},

where
\gamma_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}c_{i}
, and
\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
converges almost surely. This means that in the center-tight scenario,
S_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})
can be decomposed into the sum of two terms: A bounded oscillatory term which only depends on
X_{1},X_{N+1}
, and a term which depends on the entire past
X_{1},\ldots,X_{N+1}
and which converges almost surely.

(III) The reducible case

In the reducible case, we can decompose

f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})=g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})

(
\ast\ast
)

where
\{c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is center-tight, and the algebraic range of
\{g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is strictly smaller than the algebraic range of
\{f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
.

In principle, it is possible that
\{g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is reducible too, but in chapter
5
we show that one can find an “optimal” decomposition (

∗
⁣
∗

) where
\{g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is not reducible, and cannot be decomposed further.
The algebraic range of the “optimal”
\{g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is the “infimum” of all possible reduced ranges:

G_{ess}:=\bigcap\left\{G:\begin{array}[]{l}\text{$G$ is the algebraic range of some $\{g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}$}\\
\text{which satisfies \eqref{red-decomp} with $\{c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}$ center-tight}\end{array}\right\}.

We call
G_{ess}
the
essential range
of
\{f_{n}\}
. It can be calculated explicitly from the data of
f_{n}
and
X_{n}
in terms of the structure constants, see Theorem
9.2
.

It follows from the definitions that
G_{ess}
is a proper closed subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
, so
G_{ess}=\{0\}
or
t\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}
. In the reducible case,
G_{ess}=\{0\}
or
t\mathbb{Z}
, because if
G_{ess}=\mathbb{R}
, then the algebraic range (which contains
G_{ess}
) is also equal to
\mathbb{R}
.

If
G_{ess}=\{0\}
, then the optimal
\{g_{n}\}
has algebraic range
\{0\}
, and
g_{n}
are constant functions. In this case
f_{n}
is center-tight, and we are
back in case (II).

If
G_{ess}=t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
, then
\{g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
is lattice, non-center-tight, and irreducible. Therefore

S_{N}=\underset{S_{N}({g})}{\underbrace{\sum_{n=1}^{N}g_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})}}+\underset{S_{n}({c})}{\underbrace{\sum_{n=1}^{N}c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})}}

(
\dagger
)

where
S_{n}({g})
satisfies the lattice local limit theorems (1’)–(3’) with parameter
t
, and
{\rm Var}[S_{N}({c})]=O(1)
. Trading constants between
g
and
c
, we can also arrange
\mathbb{E}(S_{N}(c))=O(1)
.

Unfortunately even though
{\rm Var}[S_{n}({f})]\to\infty
and
{\rm Var}[S_{N}({c})]=O(1)
, examples show that
S_{N}({c})
is still powerful enough to disrupt the local limit theorem for
S_{N}
, lattice or non-lattice (example
5.64
).
Heuristically, what happens is that the mass of
S_{N}({g})
concentrates on cosets of
t\mathbb{Z}
according to (1’)–(3’), but
S_{N}({c})
smudges this mass to a neighborhood of the lattice in a non-universal manner.

This suggests that (1)–(3) should be approximately true for intervals
(a,b)
of length
|a-b|\gg|t|
, but false for intervals of length
|a-b|\ll|t|
.
In chapter
5
we prove results in this direction.

For intervals with size
|a-b|>2|t|
, we show that for all
z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, for all
N
large enough

\frac{1}{3}\left(\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|a-b|}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\right)\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\leq 3\left(\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|a-b|}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\right).

If
|a-b|>L>|t|
, we can replace

3
by a constant
C(L)
such that
C(L)\xrightarrow[L/|t|\to\infty]{}1
.

For general intervals, possibly with length less than
|t|
, we show the following: There are uniformly bounded
functions
b_{N}(x_{1},x_{N+1})
and a random variable
\mathfrak{H}=\mathfrak{H}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},\ldots)
so that for every
z_{N}\in t\mathbb{Z}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, for every
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
continuous with compact support,

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}_{x}[\phi(S_{N}-z_{N}-b_{N}(X_{1},X_{N+1}))]=\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|t|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{x}[\phi(mt+\mathfrak{H})].

(
\ddagger
)

For
\phi\approx 1_{[a,b]}
with
|a-b|\gg|t|
, the right-hand-side of (

‡

) is approximately equal to
\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|a-b|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
, in accordance with (1), see Lemma
5.70
.
But for
|a-b|\ll|t|
, the right-hand-side depends on the essential range
t\mathbb{Z}
and on the detailed structure of
\{c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\}
through
t
,
b_{N}(X_{1},X_{N+1})
and
\mathfrak{H}
.

What are
b_{N}(X_{N},X_{N+1})
and
\mathfrak{H}
? Recall that
the term
c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
on the right-hand-side of (

†

) is center-tight. As such, it can be put in the form

c_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})=a_{n+1}(X_{n+1})-a_{n}(X_{n})+h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+c_{n}^{\ast},

where
\sup_{n}(\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|a_{n}|)<\infty
,
c_{n}^{\ast}
are constants,
\mathbb{E}(h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}))=0
, and
\sum h_{n}
converges almost surely. Let
\gamma_{N}:=\sum_{n=1}^{N}c_{n}^{\ast}=\mathbb{E}(S_{N}(c))+O(1)=O(1)
. The proof of (

‡

) shows that

\circ

b_{N}=a_{N+1}(X_{N+1})-a_{1}(X_{1})+\{\gamma_{N}\}_{t\mathbb{Z}},\text{ where }\{x\}_{t\mathbb{Z}}=|t|\{x/|t|\}=x\mod t\mathbb{Z}
;

\circ

\mathfrak{H}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
.
3
3

3

It is possible to replace
\mathfrak{H}
by a different random variable
\mathfrak{F}
which is bounded, see chapter
5
.

This works as follows.
Let
z_{N}^{\ast}:=z_{N}-[\gamma_{N}]_{t\mathbb{Z}}
, where
[x]_{t\mathbb{Z}}:=x-\{x\}_{t\mathbb{Z}}\in t\mathbb{Z}
. Then
z_{N}^{\ast}\in t\mathbb{Z}
,
\frac{z_{N}^{\ast}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}=\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})+O(1)}{V_{N}}\to z
, and

S_{N}-b_{N}-z_{N}=[S_{N}(g)-z_{N}^{\ast}]+S_{N}(h).

By subtracting
b_{N}
from
S_{N}
, we are shifting the distribution of
S_{N}
to the distribution of the sum of two terms: The first,
S_{N}(g)
, is an
irreducible

t\mathbb{Z}
-valued additive functional; and the second,
S_{N}(h)
, converges almost surely to
\mathfrak{H}
.

Suppose for the sake of discussion that
S_{N}(g),S_{N}(h)
were independent, then the lattice LLT for
S_{N}(g)
and the definition of
\mathfrak{H}

would imply that

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}_{x}[\phi(S_{N}-b_{N}-z_{N})]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(x)m(dx),

where
m:=\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}m_{t\mathbb{Z}}\ast m_{\mathfrak{H}}
, and
m_{\mathfrak{H}}(E):={\mathbb{P}}[\mathfrak{H}\in E]
,
m_{t\mathbb{Z}}:=|t|\cdot
counting measure of
t\mathbb{Z}
. Calculating, we find that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi dm=
right-hand-side of (

‡

).

In general,
S_{N}(g)
and
S_{N}(h)
are not independent, and the problem of proving (

‡

) reduces to the problem of proving that
S_{N}(g)
and
S_{N}(h)
are
asymptotically
independent. This is done in chapter
5
.

For further consequences of (

‡

), including an interpretation in terms of the asymptotic distributional behavior of
S_{N}
modulo
t\mathbb{Z}
, see chapter
5
.

Final words on the setup of this work

Before we end the preface, we would like to comment on a choice we made when we wrote this work, specifically, our focus on additive functionals of the form

f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}).

This choice is somewhat unorthodox: The theory of Markov processes is mostly concerned with the case
f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n})
(see e.g.
Do
;
N
;
SV
), and the theory of
stochastic processes is mostly concerned with the case
f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1},\ldots)
, under assumptions of weak dependence of
X_{k},X_{n}
when
|k-n|\gg 1
(see e.g.
Ibragimov-Linnik
;
Ruelle-TF
).
We decided to study
f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
for the following reasons:

\circ

The case
f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
is richer than the case
f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n})
because it contains gradients
a_{n+1}(X_{n+1})-a_{n}(X_{n})
. Two additive functionals which differ by a gradient with uniformly bounded
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|a_{n}|
will have the same CLT behavior, but they may have different LLT behavior, because their algebraic ranges can be different. This leads to an interesting reduction theory which we would have missed had we only considered the case
f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n})
.
4
4

4

We cannot reduce the case
f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
to the case
f_{n}(Y_{n})
by working with the Markov chain
Y_{n}=(X_{n},X_{n+1})
because
\{Y_{n}\}
may no longer satisfy some of our standing assumptions, specifically the uniformly ellipticity condition (see chapter 1).

\circ

The case
f_{n}(X_{n},\ldots,X_{n+m})
with
m>1

can be deduced from the case
f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
, and does not require new ideas, see Example
1.3
and the discussion in §
3.3
. We decided to keep
m=1
and leave the extension to
m>1
to the reader.

\circ

The case
f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1},\ldots)
is of great interest, and we hope to address it in the future, but at the moment our results do not cover it.

We hope to stimulate research into the local limit theorem of additive functionals of general non-stationary stochastic processes with mixing conditions. Such work will have applications outside the theory of stochastic processes, such as the theory of dynamical systems. Our work here is a step in this direction.

Notes and references

Local limit theorems for sums of iid’s.
The first LLT is of course the celebrated de Moivre–Laplace Theorem. De Moivre, in his 1738 book
de-Moivre
, gave approximations for
{\mathbb{P}}[a\leq S_{n}\leq b]
when
S_{n}=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n}
, and
X_{i}
are iid, equal to zero or one with equal probabilities. Laplace extended de Moivre’s results to the case when
X_{i}
are equal to zero or one with non-equal probabilities
Laplace1
;
Laplace2
.
Pólya, in 1921, extended these results to the vector valued iid which generate the simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and deduced his famous criterion for the recurrence of simple random walks
Polya
.

The next historical landmark is Gnedenko’s 1948 work
Gnedenko48
;
Gnedenko49
which initiated the study of the LLT for sums of iid with
general
lattice distributions. He asked for the weakest possible assumptions on the distribution of iid’s
X_{i}
which lead to a LLT with Gaussian or stable limit. Khinchin popularized the problem by emphasizing its importance to the foundations of quantum statistical physics
Khinchin
, and it was studied intensively by the Russian school, with important contributions by Linnik, Ibragimov, Prohorov, Richter, Saulis, Petrov and others. We will comment on some of these contributions in later chapters. For the moment, we refer the reader to the excellent books by Gnendenko & Kolmogorov
GK
, Ibragimov & Linnik
Ibragimov-Linnik
, and Petrov
Petrov-Book

and the many references they contain.

The early works on the local limit theorem all focused on the lattice case. The Gnedenko–Kolmogorov book
GK
contains the first result we are aware of which could be considered to be a non-lattice local limit theorem. The authors assume that each of the iid’s
X_{i}
have a probability density function
p(x)\in L^{r}
with finite variance
\sigma^{2}
, and show that the density function
p_{n}(x)
of
X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n}
satisfies

\sigma\sqrt{n}p_{n}(\sigma\sqrt{n}x)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-x^{2}/2}.

There could be non-lattice iid’s without density functions, for example the iid’s

X_{i}
equal to
(-1),
0, or
\sqrt{2}
with equal probabilities (the algebraic range is
\mathbb{R}
, because the group generated by
(-1)
and
\sqrt{2}
is dense). Shepp
Shepp
was the first to consider non-lattice LLT in such cases. His approach was to provide
asymptotic formulas for
{\mathbb{P}}[a\leq S_{n}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})\leq b]
for arbitrary intervals
[a,b]
, or for

\sqrt{2\pi{\rm Var}(S_{N})}\mathbb{E}[\phi(S_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N}))]

for all test functions
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
which are continuous with compact support.
In this monograph, we use a slight modification of Shepp’s formulation of the LLT. Instead of working with
S_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})
, we work with
S_{N}-z_{N}
subject to the assumptions that
z_{N}
is “not too far” from
\mathbb{E}(S_{N})
, and that
S_{N}-z_{N}\in
algebraic range.

Stone proved non-lattice LLT in Shepp’s sense for sums of vector valued iid in
S
, extending earlier work of Rvačeva
Rvaceva
who treated the lattice case. These works are important not only because of the intrinsic interest in the vector valued case, but also because of technical innovations which became tools of the trade, see e.g.
Br
.

Local limit theorems for stationary stochastic processes.

The earliest local limit theorem for non-iid sequences
\{X_{i}\}
is due to Kolmogorov
Kolmogorov-MC-LLT
. He considered stationary homogeneous Markov chains
\{X_{i}\}
with a finite set of states
\mathfrak{S}=\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
, and proved a local limit theorem for the occupation times

S_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\overset{\to}{f}(X_{i}),\text{ where }\overset{\to}{f}(x)=(1_{a_{1}}(x),\ldots,1_{a_{n}}(x)).

Following further developments for finite state Markov chains by Sirazhdinov
Sirazhdinov
, Nagaev
N
was able to obtain a very general local limit theorems for
S_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(X_{i})
for a large class of stationary homogeneous countable Markov chains
\{X_{i}\}
and for a variety of unbounded functions
f
, both in the gaussian and stable cases.

Nagaev’s paper introduced the method of characteristic function operators, which is also applicable outside the context of Markov chains. This opened the way for proving LLT for other weakly dependent stationary stochastic processes, and in particular to time series of probability preserving dynamical systems. Guivarc’h & Hardy
GH
proved gaussian local limit theorems for Birkhoff sums
S_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(T^{i}x)
for Anosov diffeomorphisms
T:X\to X
with an invariant Gibbs measure, and Hölder continuous functions
f
.
Rosseau-Egele
RE
and Broise
Broise
proved such theorems for piecewise expanding interval map possessing an absolutely continuous invariant measure,
X=[0,1]
, and
f\in BV
. Aaronson & Denker
Aaronson-Denker-LLT
gave general LLT for stationary processes generated by Gibbs-Markov maps both in the gaussian and in the non-gaussian domain of attraction.
These results have found many applications in infinite ergodic theory, dynamical systems and hyperbolic geometry, see for example
Aaronson-Denker-Geodesic
,
Aaronson-Denker-C-minus-Z
,
Aaronson-Denker-Exactness
. The influence of Nagaev’s method can also be recognized in other works on other asymptotic problems in dynamics and geometry, see for example
Avila-Dolgopyat-Duryev-Sarig
,
Babillot-Ledrappier
,

Hafouta-Kifer-Book
,
Katsuda-Sunada
,

Lalley-Chebotarev
,
Lalley-Renewal
,
Ledrappier-Sarig-Cpt
,
Ledrappier-Sarig-Non-cpt
,
Sharp-Homology
,
Pollicott-Sharp-Chebotarev
,

Sharp-Free-Groups
. For the connection between the LLT and the behavior of local times for stationary stochastic processes, see
Denker-Zheng
;
DSV08
.

Local limit theorems for non-stationary stochastic processes.

The interest in limit theorems for sums of
non
-identically distributed, independent, random variables goes back to the works of Chebyshev
Chebyshev-Acta
, Lyapunov
Lyapunov
, and Lindeberg
Lindeberg
who considered the central limit theorem for such sums.

The study of LLT for sums of non-identically distributed random variables started later, in the works of Prohorov
Prohorov
and Rozanov
Rozanov
.
A common theme in these works and those that followed them is to assume an asymptotic for
{\mathbb{P}}[a\leq\frac{S_{N}-A_{N}}{B_{N}}\leq b]
for suitable normalizing constants
A_{N},B_{N}
, and then ask what extra conditions imply an asymptotic for
{\mathbb{P}}[a\leq{S_{N}-A_{N}}\leq b]
.

An important counterexample by Gamerklidze
Gamkrelidze
pointed the way towards the phenomenon that the distribution of
S_{N}
may lie close to a proper sub-group of its algebraic range without actually charging it, and a variety of sufficient conditions which rule this out were developed over the years. We mention especially Rozanov’s condition in the lattice case
Rozanov
(see the end of chapter
3
), the Mineka-Silverman condition in the non-lattice case
Mineka-Silverman
, and Statulevicius’s condition
Statulevicius-Sums-of-Independent
, and conditions motivated by additive number theory such as those appearing in
Moskvin
and
Moskvin-Freiman-Judin
. For a discussion of these conditions, see
Mukhin-1991
.

Dolgopyat proved a LLT for sums of non-identically distributed, independent random variables which also applies to the reducible case
D-Ind
.

Dobrushin proved a general central limit theorem for inhomogeneous Markov chains in
Do
(see chapter
2
).
Local limit theorems for inhomogeneous Markov chains are considered in
Statulevicius-LLT-MC
. Merlevède, M. Peligrad and C. Peligrad proved local limit theorems for sums

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}(X_{i})
where
\{X_{i}\}
is a
\psi
-mixing inhomogeneous Markov chain, under the irreducibility condition of Mineka & Silverman
Peligrad
. Hafouta obtained local limit theorems for a class of inhomogeneous Markov chains in
Hafouta-Sequential
. In a different direction, central limit theorems for time-series of inhomogeneous sequences of Anosov diffeomorphisms are proved in
Ba
and
Conze-Le-Borgne
.

An important source of examples of inhomogeneous Markov chains is a Markov chain in random environment, when considered for a specific (“quenched”) realizations of the environment (see chapter
8
). Hafouta & Kifer proved local limit theorems for non-conventional ergodic sums in
Hafouta-Kifer-Nonconventional
, and local limit theorems for random dynamical systems including Markov chains in random environment in
Hafouta-Kifer-Book
.
Demers, Péne & Zhang
DPZ
prove a LLT for an integer valued
observable for a random dynamical system.

Comparing the theory of inhomogeneous Markov chains to theory of Markov chains in random environment studied in
Hafouta-Kifer-Book
, we note the following differences:

(a)

The theory of inhomogeneous Markov chains applies to fixed realizations of noise and not just to almost every realization of noise;

(b)

In the random environment setup, a center–tight additive functional must be a coboundary, while in the general case it can also have a component with summable variances;

(c)

In the non center-tight random environment setup, the
variance grows linearly for a.e. realization of noise. But for a general inhomogeneous Markov chain it can grow arbitrarily slowly.

The contribution of this work.
The novelty of this work is in providing optimal sufficient conditions for the classical asymptotic formulas for
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
, and in the analysis of

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
when these conditions fail.

In particular, we derive a new asymptotic formula for
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
in the reducible case, subject to assumption that
V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N})\to\infty
, and we prove a structure theorem for
S_{N}
in case
V_{N}\not\to\infty
.

Unlike previous works, our analysis does not require any assumptions on the rate of growth of
V_{N}
, beyond convergence to infinity.

The work on this monograph was partially supported by the BSF grant 201610.
The authors thank the staff of Weizmann Institute for excellent working conditions.
O.S. was also partially supported by ISF grant 1149/18.
D.D. was partially supported by NSF grants DMS 1665046 and DMS 1956049.
The authors are indebted to Manfred Denker, Yuri Kifer, and Ofer Zeitouni for useful discussions and suggestions. The authors are in particularly indebted to Yeor Hafouta for many useful and penetrating comments on the first draft of this work.

Chapter 1
Additive functionals on Markov arrays

This chapter discusses the setup and standing assumptions used in this work.

1
The basic setup

1.1
Inhomogeneous Markov chains

A
Markov chain
is given by the following data:

\circ

State spaces:
Borel spaces
(\mathfrak{S}_{n},\mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}_{n}))

(n\geq 1)
, where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is a complete separable metric space, and
\mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}_{n})
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is the set of “the possible states of the Markov chain at time
n
.”

\circ

Transition probabilities:
Borel probability measures
\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)
on
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}

(x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n},n\geq 1)
, so that for every Borel
E\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
, the function
x\mapsto\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,E)
is measurable. The measure

\pi_{n}(x,E)
is “the probability of event
E
at time
n+1
, given that the state at time
n
was
x
.”

\circ

Initial distribution:

\pi(dx)
, a Borel probability measure on
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
.

\pi(E)
is “the probability that the state
x
at time
1
satisfies
x\in E
.”

The
Markov chain
associated with this data is the Markov process
\mathsf{X}:=\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}

such that
X_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
for all
n
, and so that for all Borel
E_{i}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{i}
,

{\mathbb{P}}(X_{1}\in E_{1})=\pi(E_{1})\ ,\ {\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}\in E_{n+1}|X_{n}=x_{n})=\pi_{n,n+1}(x_{n},E_{n+1}).

\mathsf{X}
is uniquely defined, with joint distribution

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in E_{n}):=

(1.1)

\displaystyle\int_{E_{n-1}}\int_{E_{n-2}}\cdots\int_{E_{1}}\pi_{n-1,n}(x_{n-1},E_{n})\pi(dx_{1})\pi_{1,2}(x_{1},dx_{2})\cdots\pi_{n-2,n-1}(x_{n-2},dx_{n-1}).

\mathsf{X}
satisfies the following important
Markov property
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}\in E|X_{n},X_{n-1},\ldots,X_{1})={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}\in E|X_{n})=\pi_{n,n+1}(X_{n},E).

(1.2)

See, for instance,
(
Br
, Ch. 7)
.

In what follows

{\mathbb{P}},\mathbb{E}\text{ and }{\rm Var}

denote the probability, expectation, and variance calculated using this joint distribution. In the special case when
\pi
is the point mass at
x
, we write

{\mathbb{P}}_{x},\mathbb{E}_{x}\text{ and }{\rm Var}_{x}
.

If the state spaces and the transition probabilities do not depend on
n
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{S}_{n}=\mathfrak{S}_{1}

and

\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy)=\pi_{1,2}(x,dy)
for all
n
, then we call
\mathsf{X}
a
homogeneous
Markov chain
. Otherwise,
\mathsf{X}
is called an
inhomogeneous
Markov chain
. In this work, we are mainly interested in the inhomogeneous case.

Example 1.1

(Markov chain with finite state spaces)
. These are Markov chains
\mathsf{X}
with state spaces

\mathfrak{S}_{n}=\{1,\ldots,d_{n}\}\ ,\ \mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}_{n})=\{\text{ subsets of $\mathfrak{S}_{n}$}\}.

In this case the transition probabilities are completely characterized by the rectangular stochastic matrices with entries

\pi^{n}_{xy}:=\pi_{n,n+1}(x,\{y\})\ \ (x=1,\ldots,d_{n}\ ;\ y=1,\ldots,d_{n+1}),

and the initial distribution is completely characterized by the probability vector

\pi_{x}:=\pi(\{x\})\ \ (x=1,\ldots,d_{n}).

The joint distribution of
\{X_{n}\}
is given by

{\mathbb{P}}(X_{1}=x_{1},\cdots,X_{n}=x_{n})=\pi_{x_{1}}\pi^{1}_{x_{1}x_{2}}\pi^{2}_{x_{2}x_{3}}\cdots\pi^{n-1}_{x_{n-1}x_{n}},

and this leads to the following discrete version of (
1.1
):

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(X_{1}\in E_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\in E_{n})=\sum_{x_{n-1}\in E_{n-1}}\sum_{x_{n-2}\in E_{n-2}}\cdots\sum_{x_{1}\in E_{1}}\pi_{x_{1}}\pi^{1}_{x_{1}x_{2}}\pi^{2}_{x_{2}x_{3}}\cdots\pi^{n-1}_{x_{n-1}x_{n}}.

Example 1.2

(Markov chains in random environment).

Let
\mathsf{X}
denote a homogeneous Markov chain with state space
\mathfrak{S}
, transition probability
\pi(x,dy)
, and initial distribution concentrated at a point
x_{1}
.
It is possible to view
\mathsf{X}
as a model for the motion of a particle on
\mathfrak{S}
as follows. At time
1
, the particle is located at
x_{1}
, and a particle at position
x
will jump after one time step to a random location
y
, distributed like
\pi(x,dy)
:

{\mathbb{P}}(y\in E)=\pi(x,E)
.
With this interpretation,

X_{n}=\text{ the position of the particle at time $n$}.

The homogeneity of
\mathsf{X}
is reflected in the fact that the law of motion which governs the jumps does not change in time.

Let us now refine the model by adding a dependence of the transition probabilities on an external parameter
\omega
, which we think of as “the environment.” For example,
\omega
can represent a external force field which affects the likelihood of various movements, and which can be modified by
God or some other experimentalist. The transition probabilities become

\pi(x,\omega,dy).

Suppose the environment
\omega
changes in time according to some deterministic rule. This is modeled by a map
T:\Omega\to\Omega
, where
\Omega
is the collection of all possible states of the environment, and
T
is a deterministic law of motion which says that an environment at state
\omega
will evolve after one unit of time to the state
T(\omega)
.
Iterating we see that if the initial state of the environment at time zero was
\omega
, then its state at time
n
will be
\omega_{n}=T^{n-1}(\omega)=(T\circ\cdots\circ T)(\omega).

Returning to our particle, we see that if the initial condition of the environment at time one is
\omega
, then the transition probabilities at time
n
are

\pi^{\omega}_{n,n+1}(x,dy)=\pi(x,T^{n-1}(\omega),dy).

Thus each
\omega\in\Omega
gives rise to an inhomogeneous Markov chain
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
, which describes the Markovian dynamics of a particle, coupled to a changing environment, and corresponding to the initial condition that at time one, the particle is at position
x_{1}
and the environment is at state
\omega
.

If
T(\omega)=\omega
, the environment stays fixed, and the Markov chain is homogeneous, otherwise the Markov chain is inhomogeneous. We will return to Markov chains in random environment in chapter
8
.

Example 1.3

(Markov chains with finite memory).

We can weaken the Markov property (
1.2
) by specifying that for some fixed
k_{0}\geq 1
, for all
E\in\mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}_{n+1})
,

{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}\in E|X_{n},\ldots,X_{1})=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}\in E|X_{n},\ldots,X_{n-k_{0}+1})&n>k_{0};\\
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}\in E|X_{n},\ldots,X_{1})&n\leq k_{0}.\end{cases}

Stochastic processes like that are called “Markov chains with finite memory” (of length
k_{0}
). Markov chains with memory of length
1
are ordinary Markov chains. Markov chains with memory of length
k_{0}>1
can be recast as ordinary Markov chains by considering the stochastic process
\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}=\{(X_{n},\ldots,X_{n+k_{0}-1})\}_{n\geq 1}
with its natural state spaces, initial distribution, and transition kernels.

Example 1.4

(A non-example).
Every inhomogeneous Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
can be presented as a homogeneous Markov chain
\mathsf{Y}
, but this is not very useful.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{i}
denote the state spaces of
\mathsf{X}
. These are complete separable metric spaces, and therefore they are Borel isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
, or to
\mathbb{Z}
, or to a finite set (see e.g.
Sri
, §3). So we can construct Borel bi-measurable injections
\varphi_{i}:\mathfrak{S}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Let

Y_{n}=(\varphi_{n}(X_{n}),n).

We claim that
\mathsf{Y}=\{Y_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is a homogeneous Markov chain.
Let
\delta_{\xi}
denote the Dirac measure
at
\xi
, defined by
\delta_{\xi}(E):=1
when
E\owns\xi
and
\delta_{\xi}(E):=0
otherwise.
Let
\mathfrak{S}_{n},\pi_{n,n+1}
and
\pi
denote the states spaces, transition probabilities, and initial distribution of
\mathsf{X}
. Define a
homogeneous
Markov chain

\mathsf{Z}
with

\circ

state space
\mathfrak{S}:=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}

\circ

initial distribution
\widehat{\pi}:=(\pi\circ\varphi_{1}^{-1})\times\delta_{1}
, a measure on
\mathfrak{S}_{1}\times\{1\}

\circ

transition probabilities

\widehat{\pi}\bigl((x,n),A\times B\bigr):=\begin{cases}\pi_{n,n+1}\bigl(\varphi_{n}^{-1}(x),\varphi_{n+1}^{-1}(A)\bigr)\delta_{n+1}(B)&x\in\varphi_{n}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})\\
\delta_{0}(A)\delta_{1}(B)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

A direct calculation shows that the joint distribution
\mathsf{Z}
is equal to the joint distribution of
\mathsf{Y}=\{(\varphi_{n}(X_{n}),n)\}_{n\geq 1}
. So
\mathsf{Y}
is a homogeneous Markov chain.

Such presentations will not be useful to us, because they destroy useful structures
which are essential for our work on the local limit theorem. For example, they destroy the uniform ellipticity property in section

2
below.

1.2
Inhomogeneous Markov arrays

For technical reasons that we will explain later, it is useful to consider a generalization of a Markov chain, called a
Markov array
.

To define a Markov array, we need the following data:

\circ

Row lengths:

k_{N}+1
where
k_{N}\geq 1
and
(k_{N})_{N\geq 1}
is strictly increasing.

\circ

State spaces:

(\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)},\mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}))
,
(1\leq n\leq k_{N}+1)
, where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}
is a complete separable metric space with more than one point, and
\mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)})
is its Borel
\sigma
-algebra.

\circ

Transition probabilities:

\{\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)\}_{x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}}

(1\leq n\leq k_{N})

where
\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)
are Borel probability measures on

\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N)}
, so that for every Borel
E\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N)}
, the function
x\mapsto\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,E)
is measurable, and for all
x
,
and
\pi_{n,n+1}(x,\cdot)
is not carried by a single atom.

\circ

Initial distributions:
Borel probability measures
\pi^{(N)}(dx)
on
\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}
.

For each
N\geq 1
, this data determines a finite Markov chain of length
k_{N}+1

\mathsf{X}^{(N)}=(X^{(N)}_{1},X^{(N)}_{2},\ldots,X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})
, called the
N
-th row
of the array. We will continue to denote the joint probability distribution, expectation, and variance of
\mathsf{X}^{(N)}
by
{\mathbb{P}},\mathbb{E}
, and
{\rm Var}
. These objects depend on
N
, but the index
N
will always be obvious from the context, and can be suppressed. As always, in cases when we wish to condition on the initial state
X_{1}^{(N)}=x
, we will write
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}
and
\mathbb{E}_{x}
.

The rows
\mathsf{X}^{(N)}=(X^{(N)}_{1},X^{(N)}_{2},\ldots,X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})
can be arranged in an array of random variables

\mathsf{X}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}X^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(1)}_{k_{1}+1}\\
X^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(2)}_{k_{1}+1},\ldots,X^{(2)}_{k_{2}+1}\\
X^{(3)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(3)}_{k_{1}+1},\ldots,X^{(3)}_{k_{2}+1},\ldots,X^{(3)}_{k_{3}+1}\\
\ \ \ \ \ \ \cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\end{array}\right.

Each horizontal row
\mathsf{X}^{(N)}=(X^{(N)}_{1},X^{(N)}_{2},\ldots,X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})
comes equipped with a joint distribution, which depends on
N
. But
no joint distribution on elements of different rows is specified.

Example 1.5

(Markov chains as Markov arrays).

Every Markov chain
\{X_{n}\}
gives rise to a Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}=N+1
and rows
\mathsf{X}^{(N)}=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{N+1})
. In this case
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}=\pi_{n,n+1}
, and
\pi^{(N)}=\pi
.

Conversely, any Markov array so that
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}=\pi_{n,n+1}
, and
\pi^{(N)}=\pi
determines a Markov chain with state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, transition probabilities
\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}=\pi_{n,n+1}
, and initial distributions
\pi^{(N)}=\pi
.

Example 1.6

(Change of measure).

Suppose
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
is a Markov chain with data
\mathfrak{S}_{n},\pi_{n,n+1},\pi
, and let
\varphi_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)
be a family of positive measurable functions on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
.
Define new transition probabilities by

\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}(x,dy):=\frac{\varphi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}(x,y)}{\int\varphi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}(x,y)\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy)}\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy).

Then the data
k_{N}=N+1
,
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}:=\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
\pi^{(N)}:=\pi
and
\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}
determines a Markov array called the
change of measure
of
\{X_{n}\}
with
weights

\varphi_{n}^{(N)}
.

Why study Markov arrays?
There are several reasons, and the one most relevant to this work is the following: The theory of large deviations for Markov
chains
, relies on a change of measure which results in Markov
arrays
.
Thus, readers who are only interested in local limit theorems for Markov chains in the
local regime

\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(S_{N})}}\to z
, may ignore the theory of arrays and limit their attention to Markov chains. But those who are also interested in the
large deviations regime
,
where

|\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{{{\rm Var}(S_{N})}}|
is of order 1,
will need the theory for Markov arrays.

1.3
Additive functionals

An
additive functional
of a Markov chain is a sequence
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
of measurable functions
f_{n}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
, where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
are the states spaces of the Markov chain. The pair
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\},\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}
determines a stochastic process

S_{N}=f_{1}(X_{1},X_{2})+f_{2}(X_{2},X_{3})+\cdots+f_{N}(X_{n},X_{N+1})\ \ \ (N\geq 1).

We will often abuse terminology and call
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
and
\{S_{N}\}_{N\geq 1}
“additive functionals.”

An
additive functional
of a Markov array
\mathsf{X}
with row lengths
k_{N}+1
and state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}
is an array of measurable functions
f^{(N)}_{n}:\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
with row lengths
k_{N}
:

\mathsf{f}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}f^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(1)}_{k_{1}}\\
f^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(2)}_{k_{1}},\ldots,f^{(2)}_{k_{2}}\\
f^{(3)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(3)}_{k_{1}},\ldots,f^{(3)}_{k_{2}},\ldots,f^{(3)}_{k_{3}}\\
\ \ \ \ \ \ \cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\cdots\end{array}\right.

Again, this determines a sequence of random variables

S_{N}=f_{1}^{(N)}(X_{1}^{(N)},X_{2}^{(N)})+f_{2}^{(N)}(X_{2}^{(N)},X_{3}^{(N)})+\cdots+f_{k_{N}}^{(N)}(X_{k_{N}}^{(N)},X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)})\ \ \ (N\geq 1),

which we also refer to as “additive functional.” But be careful!
This is not a stochastic process
, because no joint distribution of
S_{1},S_{2},\ldots
is specified.

Suppose
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}
are two additive functionals on
\mathsf{X}
. If
\mathsf{X}
is a Markov chain,

\mathsf{f}+\mathsf{g}:=\{f_{n}+g_{n}\},\hskip 11.74988ptc\mathsf{f}:=\{cf_{n}\},\hskip 11.74988pt|\mathsf{f}|:=\sup_{n}\left(\sup_{x,y}|f_{n}(x,y)|\right)

and
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|:=\sup\limits_{n}\left(\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})|\right)
.

Similarly, if
\mathsf{X}
is a Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, then

\mathsf{f}+\mathsf{g}:=\{f^{(N)}_{n}+g^{(N)}_{n}\},\hskip 11.74988ptc\mathsf{f}:=\{cf^{(N)}_{n}\},\hskip 11.74988pt|\mathsf{f}|:=\sup_{N}\sup_{1\leq n\leq k_{N}}\left(\sup_{x,y}|f_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)|\right),

and

\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|:=\sup_{N}\sup_{1\leq n\leq k_{N}}\left(\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n}^{(N)},X_{n+1}^{(N)})|\right).

The notation
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K\text{ a.s.}
will mean that
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|\leq K
(
“a.s.” stands for “almost surely”).
An additive functional is called
uniformly bounded
if there is a constant
K
such that
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
, and
uniformly bounded a.s.

if
\exists K
such that
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
a.s.

2
Uniform ellipticity

2.1
The definition

A Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
with state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and transition probabilities
\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy)
is called
uniformly elliptic
, if there exists a Borel probability measure
\mu_{n}
on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, Borel measurable functions
p_{n}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}\to[0,\infty)
, and a constant
0<\epsilon_{0}<1
called the
ellipticity constant
such that for all
n\geq 1
,

(a)

\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy)=p_{n}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}(dy)
;

(b)

0\leq p_{n}\leq 1/\epsilon_{0}
;

(c)

\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}p_{n}(x,y)p_{n+1}(y,z)\mu_{n+1}(dy)>\epsilon_{0}
.

We will see in Proposition
1.12
below that one can always assume without loss of generality that
\mu_{n}
are the measures
\mu_{n}(E)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}\in E)
.

The integral in (c) is the two-step transition probability
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+2}=z|X_{n}=x)
, and we will sometime call (c) a
two-step ellipticity condition
. For more general
\gamma
-step ellipticity conditions
, see §
3.3
.

Example 1.7

(Doeblin chains)

Suppose
\mathsf{X}
has finite state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
s.t
|\mathfrak{S}_{n}|\leq M<\infty
for all
n
, and
\pi^{n}_{xy}:=\pi_{n,n+1}(x,\{y\})
satisfy

(1)

\exists\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}>0
s.t. for all
n\geq 1
and

(x,y)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
, either
\pi_{xy}^{n}=0
or

\pi_{xy}^{n}>\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}
;

(2)

for all
n
, for all
(x,z)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+2}
, there exists
y\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
such that
\pi_{xy}^{n}\pi_{yz}^{n+1}>0
.

Doeblin chains are uniformly elliptic:
Take
\mu_{n}
to be the uniform measure on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
p_{n}(x,y):=\pi^{n}_{xy}/|\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}|
. Then (a) is clear, (b) holds with any
\epsilon_{0}<1/M
, and (c) holds with
\epsilon_{0}:=(\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}/M)^{2}
.
Doeblin chains are named after W. Doeblin, who studied homogeneous countable Markov chains satisfying similar conditions.

Here is the formulation of the uniform ellipticity conditions for Markov arrays.
A Markov array
\mathsf{X}
with state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}
, transition probabilities
\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)
, and row lengths
k_{N}+1
is called
uniformly elliptic
, if there exist Borel probability measures
\mu_{n}^{(N)}
on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}
, Borel measurable functions
p_{n}^{(N)}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N)}\to[0,\infty)
, and a constant
0<\epsilon_{0}<1
as follows: For all
N\geq 1
and
1\leq n\leq k_{N}
,

(a)

\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)=p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(dy)
;

(b)

0\leq p_{n}^{(N)}\leq 1/\epsilon_{0}
;

(c)

\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)p_{n+1}^{(N)}(y,z)\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(dy)>\epsilon_{0}
.

Example 1.8

Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is a uniformly elliptic Markov chain and suppose
\mathsf{Y}
is a Markov array obtained from
\mathsf{X}
by the change of measure construction
described in Example
1.6
.
If the weights
\varphi^{(N)}_{n}(x,y)
are uniformly bounded away from zero and infinity, then
\mathsf{Y}
is uniformly elliptic.

2.2
Contraction estimates and exponential mixing

Suppose
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}
are complete and separable metric spaces. A
transition kernel
from
\mathfrak{X}
to
\mathfrak{Y}
is a family
\{\pi(x,dy)\}_{x\in\mathfrak{X}}
of Borel probability measures on
\mathfrak{Y}
so that
x\mapsto\pi(x,E)
is measurable for all
E\subset\mathfrak{X}
Borel. A transition kernel
\{\pi(x,dy)\}_{x\in\mathfrak{X}}
determines two
Markov operators
, one acting on measures and the other acting on functions.
The action on measures takes a probability measure
\mu
on
\mathfrak{X}
and maps it to a probability measure on
\mathfrak{Y}
via

\pi(\mu)(E):=\int_{\mathfrak{X}}\pi(x,E)\mu(dx).

The action on functions takes a bounded Borel function
u:\mathfrak{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
and maps it to a bounded Borel function on
\mathfrak{X}
via

\pi(u)(x)=\int_{\mathfrak{Y}}u(y)\pi(x,dy).

The two operators are dual:

\int u(y)\,\pi(\mu)(dy)=\int\pi(u)(x)\,\mu(dx).

These operators are contractions in the following sense. Define the
oscillation
of a function
u:\mathfrak{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
to be

\mathop{\rm Osc}(u):=\sup_{y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathfrak{Y}}|u(y_{1})-u(y_{2})|.

The
contraction coefficient
of
\{\pi(x,dy)\}_{x\in\mathfrak{X}}
is

\delta(\pi):=\sup\{|\pi(x_{1},E)-\pi(x_{2},E)|:x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathfrak{X},\;\;E\in\mathfs B(\mathfrak{Y})\}.

The
total variation distance

between two probability measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
on
\mathfrak{X}
is

\displaystyle\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|_{{\rm Var}}

\displaystyle:=\sup\{|\mu_{1}(A)-\mu_{2}(A)|:A\subset\mathfrak{X}\text{ is measurable}\}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\sup\biggl\{\int w(x)(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})(dx)\big|w:\mathfrak{X}\to[-1,1]\text{ is measurable}\biggr\}.

Caution!
\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|_{{\rm Var}}
is actually
one half
of the total variation of
\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}
, because it is equal to
(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{+}(\mathfrak{X})
and to
(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{-}(\mathfrak{X})
, but not to

|\mu|(\mathfrak{X})=(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{+}(\mathfrak{X})+(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})^{-}(\mathfrak{X}).

Lemma 1.9
(
SV
)

Suppose
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}
are complete and separable metric spaces, and
\{\pi(x,dy)\}_{x\in\mathfrak{X}}
is a transition kernel from
\mathfrak{X}
to
\mathfrak{Y}
. Then:

(a)

0\leq\delta(\pi)\leq 1
.

(b)

\delta(\pi)=\sup\{\mathop{\rm Osc}[\pi(u)]\ |\ \ u:\mathfrak{Y}\to\mathbb{R}\text{ measurable, and }\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)\leq 1\}
.

(c)

If
\mathfrak{Z}
is a complete separable metric space,

\pi_{1}
is a transition kernel from
\mathfrak{X}
to
\mathfrak{Y}
, and

\pi_{2}
is a transition kernel from
\mathfrak{Y}
to
\mathfrak{Z}
, then

\delta(\pi_{1}\circ\pi_{2})\leq\delta(\pi_{1})\delta(\pi_{2})
.

(d)

\mathop{\rm Osc}[\pi(u)]\leq\delta(\pi)\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)
for every
u:\mathfrak{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
bounded and measurable.

(e)

\|\pi(\mu_{1})-\pi(\mu_{2})\|_{{\rm Var}}\leq\delta(\pi)\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|_{{\rm Var}}
for all Borel probability measures

\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
on

\mathfrak{X}
.

(f)

Suppose
\lambda
is a probability measure on
\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}

with marginals
\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}
,
\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}}
, and transition kernel
\{\pi(x,dy)\}
, i.e.
\lambda(E\times\mathfrak{Y})=\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}(E)
,
\lambda(\mathfrak{X}\times E)=\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}}(E)
, and

\lambda(dx,dy)=\int_{\mathfrak{X}}\pi(x,dy)\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}(dx).

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}),g\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}})
be two elements with zero integral. Then

\left|\int_{\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}}f(x)g(y)\lambda(dx,dy)\right|\leq\sqrt{\delta(\pi)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}})}.

Proof

(a) is trivial.

The inequality
\leq
in (b) is because for every
E\in\mathfs B(\mathfrak{Y})
,
u:=1_{E}
satisfies
\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)\leq 1
.
To see
\geq
,  fix some
u:\mathfrak{Y}\to\mathbb{R}
measurable such that
\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)\leq 1
. Suppose first that
u
is a simple function (a measurable function with finitely many values), then we can write

\displaystyle u=c+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}1_{A_{i}}

where
c\in\mathbb{R}
,
|\alpha_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)
, and
A_{i}
measurable and pairwise disjoint. For every pair of points
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathfrak{X}
,

\displaystyle|\pi(u)(x_{1})-\pi(u)(x_{2})|=\left|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}[\pi(x_{1},A_{i})-\pi(x_{2},A_{i})]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{\pi(x_{1},A_{i})>\pi(x_{2},A_{i})}\!\!\!\!\!\alpha_{i}[\pi(x_{1},A_{i})-\pi(x_{2},A_{i})]\right|+\left|\sum_{\pi(x_{1},A_{i})<\pi(x_{2},A_{i})}\!\!\!\!\!\alpha_{i}[\pi(x_{1},A_{i})-\pi(x_{2},A_{i})]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)\delta(\pi)+\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)\delta(\pi)=\delta(\pi)\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)=\delta(\pi).

So
\mathop{\rm Osc}[\pi(u)]\leq\delta(\pi)
for all simple functions
u
with
\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)\leq 1
. A standard approximation argument now shows that
\mathop{\rm Osc}[\pi(u)]\leq\delta(\pi)
for all measurable
u
s.t.
\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)\leq 1
.
This proves (b).
Part (c) and (d) immediately follow.

To see (e), we restrict to the non-trivial case
\mu_{1}\neq\mu_{2}
. Let
\mu:=\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}
, and decompose
\mu=\mu^{+}-\mu^{-}
where
\mu^{\pm}
are singular positive measures (this is the Jordan decomposition). Since
\mu(\mathfrak{X})=0
,
\mu^{+},\mu^{-}
has equal total mass, and

\mu^{\pm}(\mathfrak{X})=\frac{1}{2}(\mu^{+}(\mathfrak{X})+\mu^{-}(\mathfrak{X}))=\frac{1}{2}|\mu|(\mathfrak{X})\equiv\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|_{\mathrm{Var}}.

Let

{\widehat{\mu}}_{1}:=\mu^{+}/\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|_{\mathrm{Var}}\ ,\ {\widehat{\mu}}_{2}:=\mu^{-}/\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|_{\mathrm{Var}}\ ,\ {\widehat{\mu}}:={\widehat{\mu}}_{1}-{\widehat{\mu}}_{2}=\frac{\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}}{\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|_{\mathrm{Var}}}.

Note that
{\widehat{\mu}}_{1}
and
{\widehat{\mu}}_{2}
are probability measures.

For every non-constant measurable function
w:\mathfrak{Y}\to[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathfrak{Y}}w(y)\pi(\mu)(dy)}{\|\mu_{1}-\mu_{2}\|_{\mathrm{Var}}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathfrak{Y}}w(y_{1})\pi({\widehat{\mu}}_{1})(dy_{1})-\int_{\mathfrak{Y}}w(y_{2})\pi({\widehat{\mu}}_{2})(dy_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathfrak{X}}\pi(w)(x_{1}){\widehat{\mu}}_{1}(dx_{1})-\int_{\mathfrak{X}}\pi(w)(x_{2}){\widehat{\mu}}_{2}(dx_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathfrak{X}}\int_{\mathfrak{X}}[\pi(w)(x_{1})-\pi(w)(x_{2})]{\widehat{\mu}}_{1}(dx_{1}){\widehat{\mu}}_{2}(dx_{2})\text{, because ${\widehat{\mu}}_{i}(\mathfrak{X})=1$,}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\delta(\pi)\mathop{\rm Osc}(w)\leq\delta(\pi),\text{by (b) and because $\mathrm{Osc}(w)\leq 2\|w\|_{\infty}\leq 2$.}

Passing to the supremum over all
w(y)
gives part (e).

Part (f) is the content of Lemma 4.1 in
(
SV
, Lemma 4.1)
, and we reproduce the proof given there.
Consider the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfs G:=\{\mathfrak{X}\times E:E\subset\mathfrak{Y}\text{ is measurable}\}
, which represents the information on the
\mathfrak{Y}
–coordinate of
(x,y)\in\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}
.

Let
\widetilde{\pi}_{y}
be a measurable family of conditional probabilities given
\mathfs G
, i.e.
\widetilde{\pi}_{y}
is a probability measure on
\mathfrak{X}\times\{y\}
,
y\mapsto\int fd\widetilde{\pi}_{y}
is Borel for every Borel function
f:\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}\to[0,1]
,

\lambda=\int_{\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}}\widetilde{\pi}_{y}d\lambda
, and for every
\lambda
–absolutely integrable
f(x,y)
,

\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}(f(x,y)|\mathfs G)(y)=\int_{\mathfrak{X}}fd\widetilde{\pi}_{y}\ \text{$\lambda$-a.e.}

We may identify
\widetilde{\pi}_{y}
with a probability measure
\widehat{\pi}(y,dx)
on
\mathfrak{X}
defined by

\widehat{\pi}(y,E)=\widetilde{\pi}_{y}(E\times\{y\})\ \ \ (E\subset\mathfrak{X}\text{ Borel}).

It is useful to think of
\widehat{\pi}(y,dx)
as the transition kernel “which goes the opposite way” to
\pi(x,dy)
. Indeed, if
\pi(x,dy)
is the transition probability of a Markov chain
\{X_{n}\}
from
n
to
n+1
, and
\lambda
is the joint distribution of
(X_{n},X_{n+1})
, then
\widehat{\pi}(y,dx)
is the transition probability from
n+1
to
n
, i.e.
\widehat{\pi}(y,E)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}\in E|X_{n+1}=y)
.

The operators
\pi:L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}})\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})
and
\widehat{\pi}:L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}})
are dual to one another, because
\int_{\mathfrak{X}}f(x)\pi(g)(x)d\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}(x)
and
\int_{\mathfrak{Y}}\widehat{\pi}(f)(y)g(y)d\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}}(y)
are both equal to
\int f(x)g(y)\lambda(dx,dy)
.

Claim:

Q:=\pi\circ\widehat{\pi}:L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})
is self-adjoint,
Q
preserves the linear subspace
L^{2}_{0}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}):=\{f\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}):\int fd\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}=0\}
, and the spectral radius of
Q:L^{2}_{0}\to L^{2}_{0}
is at most
\delta(Q)
.

Proof of the claim:

Q
is self adjoint, because
Q^{\ast}=(\pi\widehat{\pi})^{\ast}=\widehat{\pi}^{\ast}\pi^{\ast}=\pi\widehat{\pi}
.

It is useful to notice that
Q
is given by

(Qf)(x)=\int_{\mathfrak{X}}f(x^{\prime})Q(x,dx^{\prime})

where
Q(x,E)
is the probability measure on
\mathfrak{X}
given by

Q(x,E)=\int\widehat{\pi}(y,E)\pi(x,dy)
.

Q(x,dx^{\prime})
is a transition probability from
\mathfrak{X}
to
\mathfrak{X}
. Notice that

Q(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})=\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}
:

\displaystyle(Q\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})(E)=\int_{\mathfrak{X}}Q(x,E)\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}(dx)=\int_{\mathfrak{X}}\int_{\mathfrak{Y}}\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}(dx)\pi(x,dy)\widetilde{\pi}_{y}(E\times\{y\})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}}\widetilde{\pi}_{y}(E\times\{y\})\lambda(dx,dy)=\int_{\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}}\widetilde{\pi}_{y}(E\times\mathfrak{Y})d\lambda=\lambda(E\times\mathfrak{Y})=\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}(E).

Thus, for all
f\in L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})
,
\int Qfd\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}=\int fd(Q\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})=\int fd\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}
.
It follows that

Q:L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})\to L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})
preserves the linear space

L^{2}_{0}.

For every
\varphi\in L^{2}_{0}\cap L^{\infty}
,
\|\varphi\|_{\infty}\leq\mathop{\rm Osc}(\varphi)
. Since
Q
preserves
L^{2}_{0}\cap L^{\infty}
, for every
f
in this space, we have by parts (c) and (d) that

\|Q^{n}f\|_{2}\leq\|Q^{n}f\|_{\infty}\leq\mathop{\rm Osc}(Q^{n}f)\leq\delta(Q)^{n}\mathop{\rm Osc}(f).

(2.1)

This implies that the spectral radius of
Q:L^{2}_{0}\to L^{2}_{0}
is less than or equal to
\delta(Q)
. Otherwise there is an
L^{2}_{0}
-function, part of whose spectral decomposition corresponds to the part of the spectrum outside
\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}:|\lambda|\leq\delta(Q)+\epsilon\}
(self-adjoint operators have real spectrum). Any sufficiently close
L^{2}_{0}\cap L^{\infty}
–function would have components with similar properties; but the existence of such components is inconsistent with (
2.1
). The proof of the claim is complete.

We are ready for the proof of (f). Since
Q:L_{0}^{2}\to L_{0}^{2}
is a self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space with spectral radius
\leq\delta(Q)
,
\langle Q(f),f\rangle_{L^{2}_{0}}\leq\delta(Q)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{0}}
for all
f\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})
. It follows that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\pi}(f)\|_{L^{2}_{0}(\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}})}^{2}=\langle\widehat{\pi}(f),\widehat{\pi}(f)\rangle_{L^{2}_{0}(\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}})}=\langle Q(f),f\rangle_{L^{2}_{0}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})}\leq\delta(Q)\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{0}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}})}.

So every
f\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu_{\mathfrak{X}}),g\in L^{2}_{0}(\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}})

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathfrak{X}\times\mathfrak{Y}}f(x)g(y)\lambda(dx,dy)\right|=\left|\int_{\mathfrak{Y}}\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}}(dy)\int_{\mathfrak{X}}\widehat{\pi}(y,dx)f(x)g(y)\right|=\langle\widehat{\pi}(f),g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mu_{\mathfrak{Y}})}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\pi}(f)\|_{2}\|g\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\delta(Q)}\|f\|_{2}\|g\|_{2},\text{ as required.\hskip 123.76965pt$\Box$}

We now return to the setup of Markov arrays
\mathsf{X}=\{X^{(N)}_{n}:1\leq n\leq k_{N}+1,N\geq 1\}
and consider the following

two-step transition probabilities

\pi_{n,n+2}^{(N)}(x,E):=\int\pi_{n+1,n+2}^{(N)}(y,E)\,\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)

defined for
1\leq n<N<\infty
,
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}
, and
E\in\mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}_{n+2}^{(N)})
.
The uniform ellipticity condition gives the following uniform bound for
\delta(\pi_{n,n+2}^{(N)})
:

Lemma 1.10

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a uniformly elliptic Markov array with ellipticity coefficient
\epsilon_{0}
. Then
\sup\limits_{N}\sup\limits_{1\leq n<k_{N}}\delta(\pi_{n,n+2}^{(N)})\leq 1-\epsilon_{0}
. Similarly for Markov chains.

Proof

We fix
N
and drop the superscripts
(N)
.

Uniform ellipticity implies that

\pi_{n,n+2}(x,E)\ll\mu_{n+2}
and that the Radon-Nikodym density is bounded from
below by
{\varepsilon}_{0}.
This allows us to write

\pi_{n,n+2}(x,dy)={\varepsilon}_{0}\mu_{n+2}(dy)+(1-{\varepsilon}_{0})\widehat{\pi}_{n,n+2}(x,dy).

(2.2)

Note that the first term does not depend on
x.

Let
u:\mathfrak{S}_{n+2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function with
\mathop{\rm Osc}(u)\leq 1
, then we can write
u(\cdot)=c+w(\cdot)
where
c
is a constant and
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}.
A direct calculation shows that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}u(z)\pi_{n,n+2}(x_{1},dz)-\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}u(z)\pi_{n,n+2}(x_{2},dz)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}w(z)\pi_{n,n+2}(x_{1},dz)-\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}w(z)\pi_{n,n+2}(x_{2},dz)\right|

\displaystyle=(1-{\varepsilon}_{0})\left|\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}w(z)\widehat{\pi}_{n,n+2}(x_{1},dz)-\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n}}w(z)\widehat{\pi}_{n,n+2}(x_{2},dz)\right|

\displaystyle\leq(1-{\varepsilon}_{0})\|w\|_{\infty}\left[\pi_{n,n+2}(x_{1},\mathfrak{S}_{n+2})+\pi_{n,n+2}(x_{2},\mathfrak{S}_{n+2})\right]\leq 1-{\varepsilon}_{0},

where the last inequality holds since
\|w\|_{\infty}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

\square

Proposition 1.11

If
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic, then there exist
\theta\in(0,1)
and
C_{mix}>0
, which only depend on the ellipticity constant
\epsilon_{0}
as follows. Suppose
h_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)
are measurable functions on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N)}
, and let
h_{n}^{(N)}:=h_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n}^{(N)},X_{n+1}^{(N)})
, then

(1)

If
h_{n}^{(N)}
is bounded and
\mathbb{E}(h_{n}^{(N)})=0
, then for all
1\leq m<n\leq k_{N}

\|\mathbb{E}\bigl(h_{n}^{(N)}|X_{m}^{(N)}\bigr)\|_{\infty}\leq C_{mix}\theta^{n-m}\|h_{n}^{(N)}\|_{\infty}.

(2.3)

(2)

If
{\rm Var}(h_{n}^{(N)}),{\rm Var}(h_{m}^{(N)})<\infty
and
\mathbb{E}(h_{n}^{(N)}),\mathbb{E}(h_{m}^{(N)})=0
, then for all
1\leq m<n\leq k_{N}

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}(h_{n}^{(N)}|X_{m}^{(N)})\|_{2}\leq C_{mix}\theta^{n-m}\|h_{n}^{(N)}\|_{2}.

(2.4)

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}(h_{m}^{(N)}h_{n}^{(N)})|\leq C_{mix}\theta^{n-m}\|h_{m}^{(N)}\|_{2}\|h_{n}^{(N)}\|_{2}.

(2.5)

The analogous statements hold for Markov chains.

Proof

We fix
N
and let
\pi_{n,n+1}:=\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}
,
X_{n}=X_{n}^{(N)}
,
h_{n}:=h^{(N)}_{n}
.
Define for
k\leq n

w_{n,k}(X_{k}):=\mathbb{E}(h_{n}|X_{k}),

then

w_{n,n}(X_{n}):=\mathbb{E}(h_{n}|X_{n})=\int h_{n}(X_{n},y)\pi_{n,n+1}(X_{n},dy)=\pi_{n,n+1}[h_{n}(X_{n},\cdot)].

By the Markov property,
w_{n,n}(X_{n})=\mathbb{E}(h_{n}|X_{n},X_{n-1},\ldots,X_{1})
, and this allows us to write
\pi_{n-1,n}(w_{n,n})(X_{n-1})\equiv\mathbb{E}(w_{n,n}(X_{n})|X_{n-1})=\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(h_{n}|X_{n},\ldots,X_{1})|X_{n-1}))=\mathbb{E}(h_{n}|X_{n-1})
. So

\pi_{n-1,n}(w_{n,n})(X_{n-1})=w_{n,n-1}(X_{n-1}).

Applying the Markov operator
\pi_{n-2,n-1}
on both sides gives in a similar way

(\pi_{n-2,n-1}\circ\pi_{n-1,n})(w_{n,n})(X_{n-2})=w_{n,n-2}(X_{n-2}).

Continuing in this way we arrive eventually to the identity

{w_{n,m}(X_{m})}:=\mathbb{E}(h_{n}|X_{m})=(\pi_{m,m+1}\circ\cdots\circ\pi_{n-1,n})(w_{n,n})(X_{m}).

By the previous lemmas

\mathop{\rm Osc}[{w_{n,m}}]\leq(1-\epsilon_{0})^{\lfloor\frac{n-m}{2}\rfloor}\mathop{\rm Osc}[w_{n,n}].

Notice that for every bounded measurable function
v
,

\|v\|_{\infty}\leq|\mathbb{E}(v)|+\mathop{\rm Osc}(v).

Since by assumption
\mathbb{E}({w_{n,m}(X_{m})})=\mathbb{E}(h_{n})=0
,

\|{w_{n,m}(X_{m})}\|_{\infty}\leq(1-\epsilon_{0})^{\lfloor\frac{n-m}{2}\rfloor}\mathop{\rm Osc}[w_{n,n}].

\mathop{\rm Osc}[w_{n,n}]\leq 2\|w_{n,n}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|h_{n}\|_{\infty}
, and part 1 follows.

Part 2 is proved in a similar way, using Lemma
1.9
(f).
\square

2.3
Hitting probabilities and bridge probabilities

Throughout this section, let
\mathsf{X}
be an inhomogeneous Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}
, and data
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}
,
\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}
,
\pi^{(N)}
.
Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic:

\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}(x,dy)=p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}(dy)

where
0\leq p_{n}^{(N)}\leq 1/\epsilon_{0}
and

\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)p_{n+1}^{(N)}(y,z)\mu_{n+1}(dy)>\epsilon_{0}
.

The following proposition estimates
{\mathbb{P}}(X^{(N)}_{n}\in E)
in terms of
\mu_{n}^{(N)}
:

Proposition 1.12

Under the above assumptions, for every
3\leq n\leq k_{N}+1<\infty
and every Borel set
E\subset\mathfrak{S}_{N}^{(N)}
,

\epsilon_{0}\leq\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}^{(N)}\in E)}{\mu_{n}^{(N)}(E)}\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}.

Similarly for Markov chains.

Proof

We fix a row
N
, and drop the superscripts
(N)
. Define a probability measure on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
by
P_{n}(E)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}\in E)
, then for every
1\leq n<k_{N}
, for every bounded measurable
\varphi:\mathfrak{S}_{n+2}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\int\varphi dP_{n+2}=\mathbb{E}(\varphi(X_{n+2}))=\mathbb{E}\biggl(\mathbb{E}\biggl(\mathbb{E}\bigl(\varphi(X_{n+2})\big|X_{n+1},X_{n}\bigr)\bigg|X_{n}\biggr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl(\varphi(X_{n+2})\big|X_{n+1}\bigr)\big|X_{n}\bigr)\bigr)\ \ (\because\text{Markov property})

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\int\!\!\!\int\varphi(z)\,\pi_{n+1,n+2}(y,dz)\,\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy)P_{n}(dx)

\displaystyle=\int\!\!\!\int\!\!\!\int\varphi(z)\,p_{n+1}(y,z)p_{n}(x,y)\mu_{n+2}(dz)\mu_{n+1}(dy)P_{n}(dx)

\displaystyle=\int\varphi(z)\left[\int\!\!\!\left(\int p_{n+1}(y,z)p_{n}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}(dy)\right)P_{n}(dx)\right]\mu_{n+2}(dz)

The quantity in the square brackets is bounded below by
\epsilon_{0}
and bounded above by
\epsilon_{0}^{-1}
.
So the measures
P_{n+2},\mu_{n+2}
are equivalent, and
\epsilon_{0}\leq\frac{dP_{n+2}}{d\mu_{n+2}}\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}
.
\square

Notice that in checking the uniform ellipticity condition, we are free to modify
\mu_{n}^{(N)}
by a density bounded away form zero and infinity. Thus, proposition
1.12
allows us to assume without loss of generality that
\mu_{n}^{(N)}(E)={\mathbb{P}}(X^{(N)}_{n}\in E)
for
3\leq n\leq k_{N}
.

The ellipticity property implies that for all
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)},z\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+2}^{(N)}
,

Z_{n}^{(N)}(x,z):=\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)p_{n+1}^{(N)}(y,z)\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(dy)\neq 0.

This allows us to make the following definition: The
bridge distribution
of
X^{(N)}_{n+1}
given that
X_{n}^{(N)}=x
and
X_{n+2}^{(N)}=z
is the measure on
\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N)}
which assigns to a Borel set
E\subset\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n+1}
the probability

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\ E\ \ \bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n}^{(N)}=x\\
X_{n+2}^{(N)}=z\end{array}\right):=\frac{1}{Z_{n}^{(N)}(x,z)}\int_{E}p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)p_{n+1}^{(N)}(y,z)\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(dy).

(2.6)

The definition makes sense because
Z_{n}^{(N)}(x,z)\neq 0
.
The following lemma explains why the formula (
2.6
) is reasonable:

Lemma 1.13

Let
\psi_{E}(x,z):=
right hand side of (
2.6
), then

\psi_{E}(X_{n}^{(N)},X_{n+2}^{(N)})={\mathbb{P}}\left(X_{n+1}^{(N)}\in E\bigg|X_{n}^{(N)},X_{n+2}^{(N)}\right)\ \ \text{${\mathbb{P}}$-almost everywhere.}

We omit the proof, which is routine.
The lemma does not “prove” (
2.6
): Conditional probabilities are only defined almost everywhere, and are by their very nature non–canonical. But (
2.6
) makes sense everywhere. It is a definition, not a theorem.

3
Structure constants

Throughout this section we assume that
\mathsf{f}
is an additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}
, and transition probabilities as in the ellipticity condition:
\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)=p_{n}(x,y)\mu_{n}(dy)
, where
\mu_{n}^{(N)}(E)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}^{(N)}\in E).

See §
2.3

why we may assume this on
\mu_{n}^{(N)}
.

3.1
Hexagons, balance, and structure constants

A
Level
N
hexagon at position
3\leq n\leq k_{N}
is a configuration

P_{n}^{(N)}:=\left(x_{n-2};\begin{array}[]{c}x_{n-1}\\
y_{n-1}\end{array};\begin{array}[]{c}x_{n}\\
y_{n}\end{array};y_{n+1}\right)

where
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{(N)}
.
A hexagon is called
admissible
if

\displaystyle p_{n-2}^{(N)}(x_{n-2},x_{n-1})p_{n-1}^{(N)}(x_{n-1},x_{n})p_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n},y_{n+1})\neq 0

\displaystyle p_{n-2}^{(N)}(x_{n-2},y_{n-1})p_{n-1}^{(N)}(y_{n-1},y_{n})p_{n}^{(N)}(y_{n},y_{n+1})\neq 0

Admissible hexagons exist because of uniform ellipticity.

The space of level
N
admissible hexagons at position
n
will be denoted by

\mathrm{Hex}(N,n).

One can put a natural probability measure on
\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)

by taking
\{Y^{(N)}_{n}\}
to be an independent copy of
\{X^{(N)}_{n}\}
, and looking at the distribution of

\left(X_{n-2}^{(N)};\begin{array}[]{c}X_{n-1}^{(N)}\\
Y_{n-1}^{(N)}\end{array};\begin{array}[]{c}X_{n}^{(N)}\\
Y_{n}^{(N)}\end{array};Y_{n+1}^{(N)}\right)\text{ conditioned on }\begin{array}[]{l}X^{(N)}_{n-2}=Y^{(N)}_{n-2}\\
X^{(N)}_{n+1}=Y^{(N)}_{n+1}.\end{array}

Writing the measure explicitly is possible, but cumbersome.
It is better to think of it as the result of the following sampling procedure for
\left({x_{n-2};\begin{array}[]{c}x_{n-1}\\
y_{n-1}\end{array};\begin{array}[]{c}x_{n}\\
y_{n}\end{array};y_{n+1}}\right)
:

\circ

(x_{n-2},x_{n-1})
is sampled from the distribution of
(X^{(N)}_{n-2},X^{(N)}_{n-1})
;

\circ

(y_{n},y_{n+1})
is sampled from the distribution of
(Y^{(N)}_{n},Y^{(N)}_{n+1})
(so it is independent of
(x_{n},x_{n+1})
);

\circ

x_{n}
and
y_{n-1}
are conditionally independent given the previous choices, and are sampled using the
bridge distributions

{\mathbb{P}}(x_{n}\in E|x_{n-1},y_{n+1})={\mathbb{P}}\left(X^{(N)}_{n}\in E\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X^{(N)}_{n-1}=x_{n-1}\\
X^{(N)}_{n+1}=y_{n+1}\end{array}\right)

{\mathbb{P}}(y_{n-1}\in E|x_{n-2},y_{n})={\mathbb{P}}\left(Y^{(N)}_{n-1}\in E\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}Y^{(N)}_{n-2}=x_{n-2}\\
Y^{(N)}_{n}=y_{n}\end{array}\right).

We call the resulting measure the
hexagon measure
on
\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)
.

The
balance
of a hexagon

P_{n}^{(N)}:=\left(x_{n-2};\begin{array}[]{c}x_{n-1}\\
y_{n-1}\end{array};\begin{array}[]{c}x_{n}\\
y_{n}\end{array};y_{n+1}\right)

is

\displaystyle\Gamma(P_{n}^{(N)}):=f_{n-2}^{(N)}(x_{n-2},x_{n-1})+f_{n-1}^{(N)}(x_{n-1},x_{n})+f_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n},y_{n+1})

(3.1)

\displaystyle-f_{n-2}^{(N)}(x_{n-2},y_{n-1})-f_{n-1}^{(N)}(y_{n-1},y_{n})-f_{n}^{(N)}(y_{n},y_{n+1}).

Definition 1.14

The structure constants of
\mathsf{f}=\{f^{(N)}_{n}\}
are

\displaystyle u_{n}^{(N)}:=u_{n}^{(N)}(\mathsf{f}):=\mathbb{E}\bigl((\Gamma(P_{n}^{(N)})^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}\text{(expectation on $\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)$)}

(3.2)

\displaystyle d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi):=d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{f}):=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P_{n}^{(N)})}-1|^{2})^{1/2}\text{(expectation on $\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)$)}

\displaystyle U_{N}:=U_{N}(\mathsf{f}):=\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}(u_{n}^{(N)})^{2}\ ,\ D_{N}(\xi):=\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}.

If
\mathsf{X}
is a Markov chain, we write
u_{n}=u_{n}^{(N)}
,
d_{n}(\xi)=d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)
.

The significance of the structure constants will become clear in later chapters. At this point we can only hint and say that the behavior of
U_{N}
determines if
{\rm Var}(S_{N})\to\infty
, and the behavior of
D_{N}(\xi)
determines “how close”
f
is to an additive functional whose values all belong to the lattice
(2\pi/\xi)\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 1.15

Suppose
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}
are two additive functionals of on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
, then

(a)

d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi+\eta,\mathsf{f})^{2}\leq 8(d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{f})^{2}+d_{n}^{(N)}(\eta,\mathsf{f})^{2})
;

(b)

d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{f}+\mathsf{g})^{2}\leq 8(d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{f})^{2}+d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{g})^{2})
;

(c)

d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{f})\leq|\xi|u_{n}^{(N)}(\mathsf{f})
;

(d)

u_{n}^{(N)}(\mathsf{f}+\mathsf{g})^{2}\leq 2[u_{n}^{(N)}(\mathsf{f})^{2}+u_{n}^{(N)}(\mathsf{g})^{2}]
.

Proof

For any
z,w\in\mathbb{C}
such that
|z|,|w|\leq 2
, we have

5
5

5

(zw+z+w)^{2}=z^{2}w^{2}+z^{2}+w^{2}+2(z^{2}w+zw^{2}+zw)
, and
|z^{2}w^{2}|\leq 4|zw|\leq 2|z|^{2}+2|w|^{2}
,
|z^{2}w|\leq 2|z|^{2}
,
2|zw|\leq|z|^{2}+|w|^{2}
,
|zw^{2}|\leq 2|w|^{2}
.

|zw+z+w|^{2}\leq 8(|z|^{2}+|w|^{2}).

So if
P
is a level
N
hexagon
P
at position
n
, and
\xi_{P}:=\xi\Gamma(P)
,
\eta_{P}:=\eta\Gamma(P)
, then

\displaystyle|e^{i(\xi_{P}+\eta_{P})}-1|^{2}=|(e^{i\xi_{P}}-1)(e^{i\eta_{P}}-1)+(e^{i\xi_{P}}-1)+(e^{i\eta_{P}}-1)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 8\bigl(|e^{i\xi_{P}}-1|^{2}+|e^{i\eta_{P}}-1|^{2}\bigr).

(3.3)

Part (a) follows by integrating over all
P\in\mathrm{Hex}(n,N)
. Part (b) has a similar proof which we omit.
Part (c) is follows from the inequality
|e^{i\theta}-1|^{2}=4\sin^{2}\frac{\theta}{2}\leq|\theta|^{2}
.
Part (d) follows from Minkowski’s inequality and
|ab|\leq\frac{1}{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})
.
\square

Example 1.16
(Gradients)

Suppose
f_{n}(x,y)=a_{n+1}(y)-a_{n}(x)+c_{n}
for all
n
, then the balance of each hexagon is zero and
u_{n},d_{n}(\xi)
are all zero. For a converse statement, see §
6.1
.

Suppose
f_{n}(x,y)=a_{n+1}(y)-a_{n}(x)+c_{n}\mod\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
for all
n
. Then
e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}=1
for all hexagons
P
, and
d_{n}(\xi)
are all zero. For a converse statement, see §
10.1
.

Example 1.17
(Sums of independent random variables)

Let
S_{N}=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{N}.

where
X_{i}
are independent real valued random variables with non-zero variance. Let us see what
u_{n}
and
d_{n}(\xi)
measure in this case.

Proposition 1.18

\displaystyle u_{n}^{2}=2\bigl({\rm Var}(X_{n-1})+{\rm Var}(X_{n})\bigr)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{N}u_{n}^{2}\asymp{\rm Var}(S_{N})
(i.e
\exists N_{0}
such that the ratio of the two sides is uniformly bounded for
N\geq N_{0}
).

Proof

Let
\{Y_{n}\}
be an independent copy of
\{X_{n}\}
, and let
X_{i}^{\ast}:=X_{i}-Y_{i}
(the symmetrization of
X_{i}
). A simple calculation shows that the balance of a position
n
hexagon is equal in distribution to

X_{n-1}^{\ast}+X_{n}^{\ast}

.
Clearly
\mathbb{E}[X_{i}^{\ast}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[(X_{i}^{\ast})^{2}]=2{\rm Var}(X_{i})
. Consequently,

\displaystyle u_{n}^{2}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(X^{\ast}_{n-1})^{2}+(X^{\ast}_{n})^{2}]=2{\rm Var}(X_{n-1})+2{\rm Var}(X_{n}).

Summing over
n
we obtain
\sum_{n=3}^{N}u_{n}^{2}\asymp{\rm Var}(S_{N})
.
\square

We remark that the proposition also holds for Markov arrays satisfying the
one-step
ellipticity condition (see §
3.3
).

Next we relate
d_{n}^{2}(\xi)
to the distance of
X_{i}
from a coset of
\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
.
The distance of a random variable
X
from a coset
\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
is measured by the following quantity:

\mathfrak{D}(X,\xi):=\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm dist}^{2}\left(X,\theta+\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}\right)\right]^{1/2}.

The minimum exists because the quantity we are minimizing is a periodic and continuous function of
\theta
.

Proposition 1.19

For every
\xi\neq 0

d_{n}(\xi)=0
iff
X_{i}\in
coset of
\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
a.s.
(i=n-1,n)
. In addition,
there exists
C(\xi)>1
such that if
d_{n}(\xi)\neq 0
then

C(\xi)^{-1}\leq\frac{d_{n}^{2}(\xi)}{\mathfrak{D}(X_{n-1},\xi)^{2}+\mathfrak{D}(X_{n},\xi)^{2}}\leq C(\xi).

Proof

Choose
\theta_{i}\in[0,\frac{2\pi}{\xi}]
s.t.
\mathfrak{D}(X_{i},\xi)=\mathbb{E}[{\rm dist}^{2}(X_{i},\theta_{i}+\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z})]
. There is no loss of generality in assuming that
\theta_{i}=0
, because the structure constants of
f_{i}(x)=x
and
g_{i}(x)=x-\theta_{i}
are the same. Henceforth we assume that

\mathfrak{D}(X_{i},\xi)=\mathbb{E}[{\rm dist}^{2}(X_{i},\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z})].

(3.4)

As in the proof of the previous proposition,
the balance of a position
n
hexagon is equal in distribution to

X_{n-1}^{\ast}+X_{n}^{\ast},

where
X_{i}^{\ast}:=X_{i}-Y_{i}
and
\{Y_{i}\}
is an independent copy of
\{X_{i}\}
.
So
d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i(X_{n-1}^{\ast}+X_{n}^{\ast})}-1|^{2})
.

We need the following elementary facts:

\displaystyle|e^{i(x+y)}-1|^{2}=4\sin^{2}\tfrac{x+y}{2}=4(\sin\tfrac{x}{2}\cos\tfrac{y}{2}+\sin\tfrac{y}{2}\cos\tfrac{x}{2})^{2}\ \ \ (x,y\in\mathbb{R})

(3.5)

\displaystyle\tfrac{4}{\pi^{2}}{\rm dist}^{2}(t,\pi\mathbb{Z})\leq\sin^{2}t\leq{\rm dist}^{2}(t,\pi\mathbb{Z})\ \ \ (t\in\mathbb{R})

(3.6)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[X_{i}^{\ast}\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2\xi}]+\tfrac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}]\geq\frac{1}{4}\ \ \ (i\geq 1)

(3.7)

(
3.5
) is trivial; (
3.6
) is because of the inequality
2t/\pi\leq\sin t\leq t
on
[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
, which the reader may verify by drawing the graphs.
To see (
3.7
) note that

\mathbb{R}=\left([0,\tfrac{\pi}{2\xi}]+\tfrac{\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}\right)\uplus\left([0,\tfrac{\pi}{2\xi}]+\tfrac{\pi}{2\xi}+\tfrac{\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}\right),

and therefore there exists
k=0,1
such that
{\mathbb{P}}[X_{i}\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2\xi}]+\tfrac{k\pi}{2\xi}+\tfrac{\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}]\geq\frac{1}{2}
. Since
Y_{i}
is an independent copy of
X_{i}
,
{\mathbb{P}}[X_{i},Y_{i}\in[0,\tfrac{\pi}{2\xi}]+\tfrac{k\pi}{2\xi}+\tfrac{\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}]\geq\frac{1}{4}
. This event is a subset of

\bigl[X_{i}^{\ast}\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2\xi}]+\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}\bigr]
.

Returning to the identity
d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i(X_{n-1}^{\ast}+X_{n}^{\ast})}-1|^{2})
, we see that by (
3.5
)

\displaystyle d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi(X^{\ast}_{n-1}+X^{\ast}_{n})}-1|^{2})

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}\left(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n-1}}{2}\cos^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n}}{2}+\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n}}{2}\cos^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n-1}}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\sin(\xi X^{\ast}_{n-1})\sin(\xi X^{\ast}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=4\mathbb{E}\left(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n-1}}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left(\cos^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n}}{2}\right)+4\mathbb{E}\left(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n}}{2}\right)\mathbb{E}\left(\cos^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n-1}}{2}\right)

(3.8)

where we used the symmetry of the distribution of
X_{i}^{\ast}
to see that
\mathbb{E}[\sin(\xi X_{i}^{\ast})]=0
.
By (
3.7
),
\mathbb{E}\left(\cos^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{i}}{2}\right)\geq\cos^{2}(\frac{\pi}{4}){\mathbb{P}}[X_{i}^{\ast}\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2\xi}]+\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}]\geq\frac{1}{8}
, and therefore there exists
C_{n}\in[\frac{1}{8},4]
such that

d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=C_{n}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n-1}}{2}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n}}{2}\right)\right].

(3.9)

It remains to bound
\mathbb{E}\left(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{n-1}}{2}\right)
in terms of
\mathfrak{D}(X_{i},\xi)
.

Recall that
X_{i}^{\ast}=X_{i}-Y_{i}
where
Y_{i}
is an independent copy of
X_{i}
, and use (
3.5
) and independence to find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{i}}{2}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sin\frac{\xi X_{i}}{2}\cos\frac{\xi Y_{i}}{2}-\sin\frac{\xi Y_{i}}{2}\cos\frac{\xi X_{i}}{2}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X_{i}}{2})\mathbb{E}(\cos^{2}\tfrac{\xi X_{i}}{2})-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}(\sin(\xi X_{i}))^{2}\leq 2\mathbb{E}(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X_{i}}{2})

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}({\rm dist}^{2}(\tfrac{\xi X_{i}}{2},\pi\mathbb{Z}))\equiv\tfrac{\xi^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}({\rm dist}^{2}(X_{i},\tfrac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}))=\frac{\xi^{2}}{2}\mathfrak{D}(X_{i},\xi),\text{ by \eqref{theta=zero},\eqref{ineq1}.}

Next by (
3.6
) and the definition of
\mathfrak{D}(X_{i},\xi)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sin^{2}\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{i}}{2}\right)\geq\tfrac{4}{\pi^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left({\rm dist}^{2}(\tfrac{\xi X^{\ast}_{i}}{2},\pi\mathbb{Z})\right)=\frac{\xi^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left({\rm dist}^{2}(X_{i}-Y_{i},\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y_{i}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{X_{i}}\left({\rm dist}^{2}(X_{i},Y_{i}+\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z})\right)\right]\geq\frac{\xi^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{Y_{i}}\left[\mathfrak{D}(X_{i},\xi)\right]=\frac{\xi^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\mathfrak{D}(X_{i},\xi).

The proposition follows from (
3.9
).

\square

3.2
The ladder process

The material of this section is needed
for the proofs of the gradient lemma and the reduction lemma in chapters
2
and
3
, but will not be used elsewhere.

Suppose
\mathsf{X}=\{X^{(N)}_{i}\}
is a Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, state spaces
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}
, and transition probabilities
\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)
. Let
\mu^{(N)}_{n}(E):={\mathbb{P}}(X^{(N)}_{n}\in E)
. Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic. In particular,

\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)=p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(dy),

with
p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)
as in the uniform ellipticity condition.

We would like to define a new Markov array
\mathsf{L}
, called the
Ladder process
, with the following structure (figure
1
):

(a)

Each row has entries

\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)}=(Z_{n-2}^{(N)},Y_{n-1}^{(N)},X_{n}^{(N)})\ \ \ \ (3\leq n\leq k_{N}+1),

(b)

\{Z^{(N)}_{i}\}
is an independent copy of
\mathsf{X}
,

(c)

Y_{n-1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}^{(N)}
are independent given
\{X_{i}^{(N)}\},\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}
, and

(d)
