\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(Y^{(N)}_{n-1}\in E\bigg|\{X_{i}^{(N)}\}=\{x_{i}\},\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}=\{z_{i}\}\right)={\mathbb{P}}\left(X^{(N)}_{n-1}\in E\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X^{(N)}_{n-2}=z_{n-2}\\
X^{(N)}_{n}=x_{n}\end{array}\right),

see the discussion of bridge probabilities above.

Figure 1:
The ladder process.

\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}
,
\{X_{i}^{(N)}\}
are independent copies.
Y_{n}^{(N)}

are conditionally independent given
\{X_{i}^{(N)}\},\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}
.

Let
\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)}=(z_{n-2},y_{n-1},x_{n})
. Define the probability measures

\displaystyle m_{n}^{(N)}(d\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)}):=\frac{p^{(N)}_{n-2}(z_{n-2},y_{n-1})p_{n-1}^{(N)}(y_{n-1},x_{n})}{\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}^{(N)}}p_{n-2}^{(N)}(z_{n-2},\eta)p_{n-1}^{(N)}(\eta,x_{n})\mu_{n-1}^{(N)}(d\eta)}\mu_{n-2}^{(N)}(dz_{n-2})\mu_{n-1}^{(N)}(dy_{n-1})\mu_{n}^{(N)}(dx_{n}).

Lemma 1.20

\mathsf{L}
exists, is Markov, and is uniformly elliptic
with ellipticity constant

\epsilon_{0}^{2}
(with respect to the background measure
m_{n}
),
where
\epsilon_{0}
is the ellipticity constant of
\mathsf{X}
.
For every
N
,

(1)

\{X_{n}^{(N)}\}_{n=3}^{k_{N}+1}
,
\{Z_{n}^{(N)}\}_{n=1}^{k_{N}-1}
are independent, and distributed like the corresponding pieces of the
N
-th rows of
\mathsf{X}
.

(2)

Y_{n}^{(N)}
are conditionally independent given
\{X_{i}^{(N)}\}
,
\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}
.

(3)

P_{n}^{(N)}:=\left(Z_{n-2}^{(N)},{\begin{array}[]{l}Z_{n-1}^{(N)}\\
Y_{n-1}^{(N)}\end{array}}{\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{n}^{(N)}\\
X_{n}^{(N)}\end{array}},X_{n+1}^{(N)}\right)
is distributed like the level
N
, position
n
, random hexagon.

Proof

Let

{\mathbb{P}}\left(dy_{n}\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X^{(N)}_{n-1}=z_{n-1}\\
X^{(N)}_{n+1}=x_{n+1}\end{array}\right)
denote the bridge measure on
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}
with boundary conditions
X^{(N)}_{n-1}=z_{n-1},X^{(N)}_{n+1}=x_{n+1}
. Define the Markov array
\mathsf{L}
with

\circ

Rows
\underline{L}^{(N)}_{n}=(z_{n-2},y_{n-1},x_{n})
(
3\leq n\leq k_{N}+1
,
N\geq 1
)

\circ

State spaces:

\underline{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}^{(N)}:=\mathfrak{S}_{n-2}^{(N)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}^{(N)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}

(3\leq n\leq k_{N}+1)
.

\circ

Initial distribution:
\pi^{(N)}(dz_{1},dy_{2},dx_{3})=\!\!\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{3}^{(N)}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\mu_{1}^{(N)}(dz)\mu_{3}^{(N)}(dx){\mathbb{P}}\left(dy\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X^{(N)}_{1}=z\\
X^{(N)}_{3}=x\end{array}\right)

\circ

Transition probabilities
\pi_{n}^{(N)}((z_{n-2},y_{n-1},x_{n}),E_{n-1}\times E_{n}\times E_{n+1})=

\displaystyle=\int_{E_{n-1}\times E_{n}\times E_{n+1}}p_{n-2}^{(N)}(z_{n-2},z_{n-1})p_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n},x_{n+1}){\mathbb{P}}\left(dy_{n}\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X^{(N)}_{n-1}=z_{n-1}\\
X^{(N)}_{n+1}=x_{n+1}\end{array}\right).

(We evolve
z_{n-2}\to z_{n-1}
and
x_{n}\to x_{n+1}
independently according to
\pi_{n-2}^{(N)}(z_{n-2},dz)
,
\pi_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n},dx)
, and then sample
y_{n}
using the relevant bridge distribution.)

It is routine to check that
\mathsf{L}
has the structure described at the beginning of the section, and that it satisfies the properties listed in the lemma.

Here for example is the proof of uniform ellipticity. In what follows we fix
N
, suppose
x_{i},y_{i},z_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}_{i}
, and write
p_{n}^{(N)}=p
whenever the subscript is clear from the variables.

Then
\pi_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n},d\underline{L}_{n+1})=P(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})m_{n+1}(d\underline{L}_{n+1})
, where

\displaystyle P(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1}):=p(z_{n-2},z_{n-1})p(x_{n},x_{n+1}).

If
\mathsf{X}
has ellipticity constant
{\varepsilon}_{0}
, then

P(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}
, and

\displaystyle\int P(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})P(\underline{L}_{n+1},\underline{L}_{n+2})m_{n+1}(d\underline{L}_{n+1})

\displaystyle\geq\iiint p(z_{n-2},z_{n-1})p(x_{n},x_{n+1})p(z_{n-1},z_{n})p(x_{n+1},x_{n+2})\times

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\frac{p(z_{n-1},y_{n})p(y_{n},x_{n+1})}{\int p(z_{n-1},\eta)p(\eta,x_{n+1})\mu_{n}(d\eta)}\mu_{n-1}(dz_{n-1})\mu_{n}(dy_{n})\mu_{n+1}(dx_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\iint p(z_{n-2},z_{n-1})p(x_{n},x_{n+1})p(z_{n-1},z_{n})p(x_{n+1},x_{n+2})\mu_{n-1}(dz_{n-1})\mu_{n+1}(dx_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\int p(z_{n-2},z_{n-1})p(z_{n-1},z_{n})\mu_{n-1}(dz_{n-1})\int p(x_{n},x_{n+1})p(x_{n+1},x_{n+2})\mu_{n+1}(dx_{n+1})

\displaystyle\geq{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}.

So the ladder process is uniformly elliptic with ellipticity constant
{\varepsilon}_{0}^{2}
.

\square

3.3
\gamma
-step ellipticity conditions

We mention a few possible variants of the uniform ellipticity condition discussed in this chapter.
Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is a Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}+1
and transition probabilities taking the form
\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)=p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(dy)
.

The
one-step ellipticity condition
is that for some
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
, for all
N\geq 1
,
1\leq n\leq k_{N}
, and for every
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)},y\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N+1)}
,

{\varepsilon}_{0}<p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1}.

Notice that this implies that all transitions
x\to y
have positive probability.

The
\gamma
-step ellipticity condition
(
\gamma=2,3,\ldots
)

is that for some
{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
, for all
N\geq 1,n\leq k_{N}
,

0\leq p_{n}^{(N)}\leq 1/{\varepsilon}_{0}

and for all
n\leq k_{N}-\gamma+1
, and every
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)},z\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+\gamma}^{(N)}
, the iterated integral

\int\limits_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N)}}\!\!\!\!\cdots\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+\gamma}^{(N)}}p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{\gamma-2}p_{n+i}^{(N)}(y_{i},y_{i+1})p_{n+\gamma-1}^{(N)}(y_{\gamma-1},z)\,\mu_{n+1}(dy_{1})\cdots\mu_{n\gamma}(dy_{\gamma-1})

is bigger than
\epsilon_{0}
(with the convention that
\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{0}:=1
).

The ellipticity condition we use in this work corresponds to
\gamma=2
. This is weaker than the one-step condition, but stronger than the
\gamma
-step condition for
\gamma\geq 3
.

The results of this work could in principle be reproduced assuming only a
\gamma
-step condition with
\gamma\geq 2
. To do this, one needs to replace the space of hexagons by the space of
2(\gamma+1)
-gons

\left(x_{n-\gamma};\begin{array}[]{c}x_{n-\gamma+1}\\
y_{n-\gamma+1}\end{array}\cdots\begin{array}[]{c}x_{n}\\
y_{n}\end{array};y_{n+1}\right)

with its associated structure constants, and its associated
\gamma
-ladder process

\underline{L}^{(N)}_{n}=(Z_{n-\gamma-1}^{(N)},Y_{n-\gamma}^{(N)},\ldots,Y_{n-1}^{(N)},X_{n}^{(N)})
.
Since no new ideas are needed, and since our notation is already heavy enough as it is, we will only treat the case
\gamma=2
in this work.

3.4
Uniform ellipticity and strong mixing conditions

The contents of this section are not used elsewhere in this work.

Suppose
(\Omega,\mathfs F,{\mathbb{P}})
is a probability space, and let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be two sub
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathcal{F}
. There
are several standard measures for the dependence between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
:

\displaystyle\alpha(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):=\sup\{|{\mathbb{P}}(A\cap B)-{\mathbb{P}}(A){\mathbb{P}}(B)|:A\in\mathcal{A},B\in\mathcal{B}\};

\displaystyle\rho(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):=\sup\left\{|\mathbb{E}(fg)-\mathbb{E}(f)\mathbb{E}(g)|:\begin{array}[]{l}f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A}),g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{B});\\
\|f-\mathbb{E}(f)\|_{2}=1,\|g-\mathbb{E}(g)\|_{2}=1\end{array}\right\};

\displaystyle\phi(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):=\sup\left\{\bigl|{\mathbb{P}}(B|A)-{\mathbb{P}}(B)\bigr|:A\in\mathcal{A},B\in\mathcal{B},{\mathbb{P}}(A)\neq 0\right\};

\displaystyle\psi(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}):=\sup\left\{\left|\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(A\cap B)}{{\mathbb{P}}(A){\mathbb{P}}(B)}-1\right|:A\in\mathcal{A},B\in\mathcal{B}\text{ with non-zero probabilities}\right\}.

If one of these quantities vanishes then they all vanish, and this happens iff
{\mathbb{P}}(A\cap B)={\mathbb{P}}(A){\mathbb{P}}(B)
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
,
B\in\mathcal{B}
. In this case we say that
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
are
independent
.
In the dependent case,
\alpha,\rho,\phi,\psi
can be used to bound the covariance between (certain)
\mathcal{A}
-measurable and
\mathcal{B}
-measurable random variables:

Theorem 3.1

Suppose
X
is
\mathcal{A}
-measurable,
Y
is
\mathcal{B}
-measurable, then

(1)

|{\rm Cov}(X,Y)|\leq 8\alpha(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})^{1-\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{q}}\|X\|_{p}\|Y\|_{q}
whenever
p\in(1,\infty]
,
q\in(1,\infty]
,

\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}<1
,
X\in L^{p}
,
Y\in L^{q}
.

(2)

|{\rm Cov}(X,Y)|\leq\rho(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\|X-\mathbb{E}Y\|_{2}\|Y-\mathbb{E}Y\|_{2}
whenever
X,Y\in L^{2}
.

(3)

|{\rm Cov}(X,Y)|\leq 2\phi(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\|X\|_{1}\|Y\|_{\infty}
whenever
X\in L^{1},Y\in L^{\infty}
.

(4)

|{\rm Cov}(X,Y)|\leq\psi(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\|X\|_{1}\|Y\|_{1}
whenever
X\in L^{1},Y\in L^{\infty}
.

For proof and references, see
(
Bradley
, vol 1, ch. 3)
.

Definition 1.21

Let
\mathsf{X}:=\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a general stochastic process, not necessarily stationary or Markov. Let
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{n}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, and let
\mathcal{F}_{m}^{\infty}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X_{k}
for
k\geq m
.

(1)

\mathsf{X}
is called
\alpha
-mixing
, if

\alpha(n):=\sup_{k\geq 1}\alpha(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{F}_{k+n}^{\infty})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

(2)

\mathsf{X}
is called
\rho
-mixing
, if

\rho(n):=\sup_{k\geq 1}\rho(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{F}_{k+n}^{\infty})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

(3)

\mathsf{X}
is called
\phi
-mixing
, if

\phi(n):=\sup_{k\geq 1}\phi(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{F}_{k+n}^{\infty})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

(4)

\mathsf{X}
is called
\psi
-mixing
, if

\psi(n):=\sup_{k\geq 1}\psi(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{k},\mathcal{F}_{k+n}^{\infty})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0.

Theorem 3.2

If
(\Omega,\mathfs F,{\mathbb{P}})
is a probability space, and
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
are sub-
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathcal{F}
, then
\alpha:=\alpha(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
,

\rho:=\rho(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
,

\phi:=\phi(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
,

\psi:=\psi(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
satisfy the inequalities

2\alpha\leq\phi\leq\frac{1}{2}\psi\ ,\ \rho\leq 2\sqrt{\phi}.

(3.10)

For the proof, see
(
Bradley
, vol 1, Prop. 3.11)
). It follows that

\text{$\psi$-mixing}\Rightarrow\text{$\phi$-mixing}\Rightarrow\text{$\rho$-mixing}\Rightarrow\text{$\alpha$-mixing}.

These implications are strict, see
(
Bradley
, vol 1 §5.23)
.

Let us see what is the connection of
\phi
-mixing to uniform ellipticity.
First we’ll show that uniform ellipticity implies exponential
\psi
-mixing, and then we’ll give a weak converse of this statement for finite state Markov chains.

Proposition 1.22

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a uniformly elliptic Markov chain, then for every
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
,
\mathsf{X}
conditioned on
X_{1}=x
is
\psi
-mixing. Moreover,
\alpha(n),\rho(n),\phi(n),\psi(n)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0
exponentially fast, uniformly in
x
.

Proof

We will need the following fact:

Claim.

There exists a constant
K
which only depends on the ellipticity constant of
\mathsf{X}
as follows.
For every
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
,
k\geq 2
, and for every bounded measurable function
h_{k}:\mathfrak{S}_{k}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have the inequality
\|\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k}(X_{k})|X_{k-2})\|_{\infty}\leq K\mathbb{E}_{x}(|h_{k}(X_{k})|)
.

Proof of the claim.

By the uniform ellipticity of
\mathsf{X}
, the transition kernels of
\mathsf{X}
can be put in the form
\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy)=p_{n}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}(dy)
, where
0\leq p_{n}\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1}
and
\int p_{n}(x,y)p_{n+1}(y,z)\mu_{n+1}(dy)>{\varepsilon}_{0}
. In addition, Prop.
1.12
tells us that the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\mu_{n+1}
with respect to the measure
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(X_{n+1}\in E)
is almost everywhere in
[{\varepsilon}_{0},{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-1}]
. It follows that for all
\xi
,

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k+2}(X_{k+2})|X_{k}=\xi)|\leq\iint p_{k}(\xi,y)p_{k+1}(y,z)|h_{k+2}(z)|\mu_{k+1}(dy)\mu_{k+2}(dz)

\displaystyle\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-2}\int|h_{k+2}(z)|\mu_{k+2}(dz)\leq{\varepsilon}_{0}^{-3}\mathbb{E}_{x}(|h_{k+2}(X_{k+2})|).

We now prove the proposition.
Fix
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
, and let
\psi_{x}
denote the
\psi
measure of dependence for
\mathsf{X}
conditioned on
X_{1}=x
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{k}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X_{k}
.
Using the Markov property, it is not difficult to see that

\psi_{x}(n)=\sup_{k\geq 1}\psi_{x}(\mathcal{F}_{k},\mathcal{F}_{k+n}),

see
(
Bradley
, vol 1, pp. 206–7)
.

Suppose now that
n>2
, and fix some
A\in\mathcal{F}_{k},B\in\mathcal{F}_{k+n}
with positive
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}
-measure. Let

h_{k}:=1_{A}
and
h_{k+n}:=1_{B}-{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(B)
. Then

\displaystyle|{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(A\cap B)-{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(A){\mathbb{P}}_{x}(B)|=|\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k}h_{k+n})|=|\mathbb{E}_{x}(\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k}h_{k+n}|\mathcal{F}_{k}))|

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k}\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k+n}|X_{k}))|\leq\mathbb{E}_{x}(|h_{k}|)\|\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k+n}|X_{k})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}_{x}(A)\|\mathbb{E}_{x}(\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k+n}|X_{k+n-2})|X_{k})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(A)\cdot C_{mix}\theta^{n-2}\|\mathbb{E}_{x}(h_{k+n}|X_{k+n-2})\|_{\infty},\text{ by uniform ellipticity and \eqref{Exp-Mixing-L-infinity}}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(A)\cdot C_{mix}\theta^{n-2}\cdot K\mathbb{E}_{x}(|h_{k+n}|),\text{ by the claim}

\displaystyle\leq 2KC_{mix}\theta^{n-2}{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(A){\mathbb{P}}_{x}(B).

Dividing by
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(A){\mathbb{P}}_{x}(B)
and passing to the supremum over
A\in\mathcal{F}_{k},B\in\mathcal{F}_{k+n}
, gives

\psi_{x}(n)\leq 2KC_{mix}\theta^{n-2}.

Recall from Proposition
1.11
that
C_{mix},\theta
depend on the ellipticity constant of
\mathsf{X}
, but not on
x
. So
\psi_{x}(n)\to 0
exponentially fast, uniformly in
x
.
By (
3.10
),
\alpha_{x}(n),\rho_{x}(n),\phi_{x}(n)\to 0
exponentially fast, uniformly in
n
.

\square

Proposition 1.23

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a Markov chain such that

(1)

\exists\kappa>0
s.t.
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}=x)>\kappa
for every
n\geq 1
,
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
(in particular,
|\mathfrak{S}_{n}|<1/\kappa
).

(2)

\phi(n)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0
.

Then
\mathsf{X}
satisfies the
\gamma
-step ellipticity condition for all
\gamma
large enough.

Proof

By (1), all state spaces are finite sets.
Define a measure on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
by
\mu_{n}(E)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}\in E)
, and let

\displaystyle p_{n}(x,y):=\frac{{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}=y|X_{n}=x)}{{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}=y)}.

This is well-defined by (1), and:

(a)

By construction,
\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy)=p_{n}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}(dy)
.

(b)

By (1),

p_{n}(x,y)\leq 1/{{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}=y)}\leq\kappa^{-1}
.

(c)

By (2), for all
\gamma
large enough,
\phi(\gamma)<\frac{1}{2}\kappa
. For such
\gamma
,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}\!\!\!\!\cdots\!\!\!\!\int\limits_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+\gamma}}p_{n}(x,y_{1})\prod_{i=1}^{\gamma-2}p_{n+i}(y_{i},y_{i+1})p_{n+\gamma-1}(y_{\gamma-1},z)\,\mu_{n+1}(dy_{1})\cdots\mu_{n+\gamma}(dy_{\gamma-1})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+\gamma}=z|X_{n}=x)\geq{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+\gamma}=z)-\phi(\mathcal{F}_{n},\mathcal{F}_{n+\gamma})\geq\kappa-\phi(\gamma)>\frac{1}{2}\kappa.

We obtain the
\gamma
-ellipticity condition with ellipticity constant
\frac{1}{2}\kappa
.
\square

4
Notes and references

For a comprehensive treatment of inhomogeneous Markov chains on general state spaces, see Doob’s book
Doob
.
The uniform ellipticity condition is one of a plethora of contraction conditions for Markov operators, which were developed over the years as sufficient conditions for results such as Propositions
1.11
and
1.12
. We mention in particular the works of Markov
Markov
, Doeblin
Doeblin-Czech
;
Doeblin-Roum
, Hajnal
Hajnal
, Doob
Doob
, and Dobrushin
Do
(see also Seneta
Seneta-History-Doeblin
and Sethuraman & Varadhan
SV
).

The contraction coefficient mentioned in section
2.2
is also called an “ergodicity coefficient,” and it plays a major role in Dobrushin’s proof of the CLT for inhomogeneous Markov chains
Do
. Our treatment of contraction coefficients follows closely
SV
. In particular, Lemma
1.9
and the proof of part (f) of that lemma is taken from there.

Proposition

1.12
is similar in spirit to Doeblin’s estimates for the stationary probability vector of a Markov chain satisfying Doeblin’s condition in terms of the stochastic matrix of the chain
Doeblin-Czech
;
Doeblin-Roum
.

For a discussion of the “change of measure” construction see chapter
6
.
The quantities
\mathfrak{D}(X,\xi)
were introduced by Mukhin for the purpose of studying local limit theorem for sums of independent random variables. See
Mukhin-1991
and references therein.

For a comprehensive account of measures of dependence and mixing conditions, see
Bradley
.

Chapter 2
Variance growth, center-tightness, and the central limit theorem

In this chapter we analyze the variance of
S_{N}=f_{1}(X_{1},X_{2})+\cdots+f_{N}(X_{N},X_{N+1})
as
N\to\infty
, characterize the additive functionals for which
{\rm Var}(S_{N})\not\to\infty
, and prove Dobrushin’s Theorem: If
V_{N}\to\infty
then the central limit theorem holds.

5
Main results

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, let
\mathsf{f}
an additive functional on
\mathsf{X}
, and define
\displaystyle S_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}f_{i}^{(N)}(X^{(N)}_{i},X^{(N)}_{i+1})
.

Definition 2.24

\mathsf{f}
is called
center-tight
if there are constants
m_{N}
s.t. for every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
M
s.t.
{\mathbb{P}}[|S_{N}-m_{N}|>M]<\epsilon\text{ for all $N$.}

Center-tightness is an obstruction
to the local limit theorem. We shall see below (Theorem
5.2
) that
\mathsf{f}
is center-tight iff
{\rm Var}(S_{N})\not\to\infty
. Obviously, in such a situation the right hand side in

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\overset{?}{\sim}\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|a-b|}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}

can be made bigger than one by choosing
|a-b|
sufficiently big, and the asymptotic relation fails.
One could hope for a different universal asymptotic behavior, but as the following class of examples shows, this is hopeless:

Example 2.25

(Non-universality in the LLT for center-tight functionals):

Let
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be a sequence of identically distributed independent random variables with uniform distribution on
[0,1]
. Choose an
arbitrary
sequence of random variables
\{Z_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
taking values in
[0,1]
. By the isomorphism theorem for Lebesgue spaces, there are measurable functions
g_{n}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
such that

g_{0}\equiv 0\ ,\ g_{n}(X_{n})=Z_{n}\text{ in distribution}.

Let
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
with

f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}):=g_{n+1}(X_{n+1})-g_{n}(X_{n}).

Then

S_{N}=Z_{N+1}
in distribution, whence
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\in(a,b))={\mathbb{P}}(Z_{N+1}\in(a,b))
is completely arbitrary.

Every Markov array admits center-tight additive functionals.
Here are three constructions which lead to such examples (in the uniformly bounded, uniformly elliptic case,
all
center-tight additive functional arise this way, see Theorem
5.2
below):

Example 2.26

(Gradients):
Gradients on Markov chains are additive functionals of the form

f_{n}(x,y):=(\nabla{\mathsf{a}})_{n}(x,y):=a_{n+1}(y)-a_{n}(x).

where
a_{n}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable, and
\mathsf{a}=\{a_{n}\}
is a.s. uniformly bounded.

Gradients on Markov arrays are defined similarly by the formula

f_{n}^{(N)}(x,y):=a_{n+1}^{(N)}(y)-a_{n}^{(N)}(x).
where
a^{(N)}_{n}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable, and
\mathsf{a}=\{a_{n}^{(N)}\}
is a.s. uniformly bounded. We write
\mathsf{f}=\nabla\mathsf{a}
, and say that
\mathsf{f}
is the
gradient
of
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{a}
is the
potential
of
\mathsf{f}
.
6
6

6

In the ergodic theoretic literature,
\mathsf{f}
is called a
coboundary
and
\mathsf{a}
is called a
transfer function
.

The gradient of an a.s. uniformly bounded potential is center-tight because if
|\mathsf{a}|\leq K
, then

|S_{N}|=|a_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}(X_{N+1})-a_{1}^{(N)}(X_{1})|\leq 2K
.

Example 2.27

(Summable variance):
We say that an additive functional
\mathsf{f}
on a Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
has
summable variance
if it is a.s. uniformly bounded, and

V_{\infty}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\rm Var}[f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})]<\infty.

The definition of summable variance for additive functionals on arrays is similar, except that now
V_{\infty}
is defined by

\displaystyle V_{\infty}:=\sup_{N}\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}{\rm Var}[f_{n}^{(N)}(X^{(N)}_{n},X^{(N)}_{n+1})]<\infty.

If
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic and
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
a.s., then summable variance implies center-tightness.
This follows from Chebyshev’s inequality and the following lemma:

Lemma 2.28

Let
\mathsf{f}
be a uniformly bounded functional of the uniformly elliptic Markov array.
Then

V_{N}\leq{\overline{V}}_{N}\left(1+\frac{2C_{mix}}{1-\theta}\right)
where

\displaystyle{\overline{V}}_{N}:=\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}{\rm Var}(f_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n}^{(N)},X_{n+1}^{(N)}))
, and
C_{mix}
and
0<\theta<1
are as in Prop.
1.11
.

Proof

We give the proof for Markov chains (the proof for arrays is identical):

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\left(S_{N}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\rm Var}(f_{n})+2\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sum_{m=n+1}^{N}{\rm Cov}(f_{n},f_{m})

\displaystyle\leq{\overline{V}}_{N}+2C_{mix}\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sum_{m=n+1}^{N}\theta^{m-n}\sqrt{{\rm Var}(f_{n}){\rm Var}(f_{m})},\text{ with $C_{mix},\theta$ as in \eqref{Exp-Mixing-L-three}}

\displaystyle\leq{\overline{V}}_{N}+2C_{mix}\sum_{j=1}^{N-1}\theta^{j}\sum_{n=1}^{N-j}\sqrt{{\rm Var}(f_{n}){\rm Var}(f_{n+j})}<{\overline{V}}_{N}+\frac{2C_{mix}{\overline{V}}_{N}}{1-\theta}

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
\square

Example 2.29

Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic. Then every additive functional of the form
\mathsf{f}=\mathsf{g}+\mathsf{h}
where
\mathsf{g}
is a gradient and
\mathsf{h}
has summable variance is center-tight.

We will now state the main results of this chapter. We assume throughout that

(E)

\mathsf{X}=\{X^{(N)}_{n}\}
is a
uniformly elliptic
inhomogeneous Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, state spaces
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}
, transition probabilities
\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}
, initial distributions
\pi^{(N)}
, and ellipticity constant
\epsilon_{0}
.

(B)

\mathsf{f}=\{f^{(N)}_{n}\}
is an
a.s. uniformly bounded
additive functional on
\mathsf{X}
, satisfying the bound
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
almost surely.

Let
V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N})
, and
\displaystyle U_{N}:=\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}(u^{(N)}_{n})^{2}

where
u^{(N)}_{n}
are as in (
3.2
).

Theorem 5.1

There are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
which only depend on
\epsilon_{0},K
s.t. for every uniformly elliptic array with ellipticity constant
\epsilon_{0}
and every additive functional

\mathsf{f}
on
\mathsf{X}
s.t.
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
a.s.,

C_{1}^{-1}U_{N}-C_{2}\leq{\rm Var}(S_{N})\leq C_{1}U_{N}+C_{2}\ \ \text{ for all }N.

Corollary 2.30

Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is a
Markov chain
. Either
{\rm Var}(S_{N})\to\infty
or
{\rm Var}(S_{N})=O(1)
. Moreover,
{\rm Var}(S_{N})\asymp\sum\limits_{n=3}^{N}u_{n}^{2}
where
u_{n}
are the structure constants from (
3.2
).

(The corollary is clearly false for arrays.) Returning to arrays, we’ll show:

Theorem 5.2

{\rm Var}(S_{N})
is bounded iff

\mathsf{f}
is center-tight iff

\mathsf{f}=\nabla a+\mathsf{h}

where
\mathsf{a}
is a uniformly bounded potential, and
\mathsf{h}
has summable variance.

Corollary 2.31

\mathsf{f}
is center-tight iff
\sup\limits_{N}U_{N}<\infty
.

Theorem
5.1
is a statement on the localization of cancellations.
In general, if the variance of an additive functional of a stochastic process does not tend to infinity, then there must be some strong cancellations in
S_{N}
. A priori, these cancellations may involve summands located far apart from one another. Theorem
5.1
says that strong cancellations must already occur among three consecutive terms
f_{n-2}^{(N)}+f_{n-1}^{(N)}+f_{n}^{(N)}
: This is what
U_{N}
measures.

If
f
depends only on one variable
f_{n}(x,y)=f_{n}(x)
, and we have the one-step
ellipticity condition
p_{N}(x,y)\geq{\varepsilon}_{0}
one can define the ladder process using
quadrilaterals

Q_{n}^{N}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}X_{n-1}^{N}&\begin{array}[]{c}X_{n}^{N}\\
Y_{n}^{N}\end{array}&Y_{n+1}^{N}\end{array}\right)

instead of hexagons. As a result
u_{n}
is replaced by

({\overline{u}}_{n}^{(N)})^{2}\asymp\iint|f_{n}^{(N)}(y_{1})-f_{n}^{(N)}(y_{2})|^{2}d\mu_{n}(y_{1})d\mu_{n}(y_{2})=2{\rm Var}{(f_{n})}.

(5.1)

Repeating the arguments from the proof of Theorem
5.1
we obtain that there are constants

{\widehat{C}}_{1},{\widehat{C}}_{2}
such that

{{\widehat{C}}_{1}}^{-1}{\sum_{n}{\rm Var}(f_{n}(X_{n}))}-{\widehat{C}}_{2}\leq V_{N}\leq{\widehat{C}}_{1}\left(\sum_{n}{\rm Var}(f_{n}(X_{n}))\right)+{\widehat{C}}_{2}.

This estimate has been previously obtained in
Do
;
SV
under weaker ellipticity assumptions.
A similar estimate does
not
hold in case
f_{n}^{(N)}
depends on two variables. Indeed if

f_{n}^{(N)}
is a gradient, then
V_{N}
is bounded while

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\rm Var}(f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}))
can be arbitrarily large.

We end the chapter with the reproduction of the proofs of the following two known well-known results.

Theorem 5.3
(Dobrushin)

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
.
If
{\rm Var}(S_{N})\to\infty
, then for every interval,

{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{S_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(S_{N})}}\in(a,b)\right]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{a}^{b}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt.

The proof we give, which is due to Sethuraman & Varadhan, is based on McLeish’s martingale central limit theorem. For the convenience of the reader we prove the martingale CLT in section
6.4
.

The next result reduces in the case of identically distributed independent random variables to Khintchin-Kolmogorov’s Two-Series Theorem. The result is stated for Markov chains, and not Markov arrays, because it relates to the properties of
S_{N}
as a stochastic process.

Theorem 5.4

Let
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}
be an a.e. uniformly bounded additive functional of a uniformly elliptic inhomogeneous Markov chain
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}
. If
\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}{\rm Var}[f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})]
is finite, then

\sum\limits_{n=1}^{\infty}\left[f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}))\right]\text{ converges almost surely.}

6
Proofs

6.1
The Gradient Lemma

Lemma 2.32
(Gradient Lemma)

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
, and assume
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
almost surely. Then we can write

\mathsf{f}=\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}+\nabla\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{c},

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c}
are additive functionals on
\mathsf{X}
with the following properties:

(a)

|\mathsf{a}|\leq 2K
and
a^{(N)}_{n}(x)
are measurable functions on
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}
.

(b)

|\mathsf{c}|\leq K
and
c^{(N)}_{n}
are constant functions.

(c)

|\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}|\leq 6K
and
\widetilde{f}^{(N)}_{n}(x,y)
satisfy
\|\widetilde{f}^{(N)}_{n}\|_{2}\leq u_{n}^{(N)}
for all
3\leq n\leq k_{N}+1
.

If
\mathsf{X}
is a Markov chain, we can choose
f^{(N)}_{n}=f_{n}
,
a^{(N)}_{n}=a_{n}
,
c^{(N)}_{n}=c_{n}
.

Proof for Doeblin chains:
Before proving the lemma in full generality, we consider the important special case of Doeblin chains (Example
1.7
), for which the proof is particularly simple.

Recall that a Doeblin chain is a Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
with
finite
state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
of
uniformly bounded cardinality
, and whose associated transition matrices
\pi^{n}_{xy}:=\pi_{n,n+1}(x,\{y\})

satisfy the following properties:

(E1)

\exists\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}>0
s.t. for all
n\geq 1
and

(x,y)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
, either
\pi_{xy}^{n}=0
or

\pi_{xy}^{n}>\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}
;

(E2)

for all
n
, for all
(x,z)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+2}
,
\exists y\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
such that
\pi_{xy}^{n}\pi_{yz}^{n+1}>0
.

We saw in example
1.7
that
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic.

We re-label the states in
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
so that
\mathfrak{S}_{n}=\{1,\ldots,d_{n}\}
where
d_{n}\leq d
, and in such a way that

\pi_{11}^{n}>0\text{ for all }n
.
Assumption (E2) guarantees that for every
n\geq 3
and every
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
there exists a state
\xi_{n-1}(x)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
s.t.

\pi^{n-2}_{1,\xi_{n-1}(x)}\pi^{n-1}_{\xi_{n-1}(x),x}>0.

Let

\displaystyle a_{0}\equiv 0,\ \ a_{1}\equiv 0,\ \ \text{ and }a_{n}(x):=f_{n-2}(1,\xi_{n-1}(x))+f_{n-1}(\xi_{n-1}(x),x)\text{ for }n\geq 3

\displaystyle c_{0}:=0,\ \ c_{1}:=0,\ \ \text{ and }c_{n}:=f_{n-2}(1,1)\text{ for }n\geq 3

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}:=\mathsf{f}-\nabla\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{c}.

We claim that
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c}
satisfy our requirements.
To explain why and to motivate the construction, consider the special case
u_{n}=0
. In this
\|\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}\|_{2}=0
and the lemma reduces to constructing functions
b_{n}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\to\mathbb{R}
s.t.
\mathsf{f}=\nabla\mathsf{b}+\mathsf{c}
. We first try to solve
\mathsf{f}=\nabla\mathsf{b}
with
\mathsf{c}=0
. Any solution must satisfy

f_{n}(x,y)=b_{n+1}(y)-b_{n}(x).

(6.1)

Necessarily,

b_{n}(y)=b_{2}(x_{2})+f_{2}(x_{2},x_{3})+\cdots+f_{n-2}(x_{n-2},x_{n-1})+f_{n-1}(x_{n-1},y)

for all paths
(x_{2},\ldots,x_{n-1},y)
with positive probability. The path
x_{2}=\cdots=x_{n-2}=1
,
x_{n-1}=\xi_{n-1}(y)
suggests to define

\displaystyle b_{2}\equiv 0\ ,\ b_{n}(y)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=2}^{n-3}f_{k}(1,1)+f_{n-2}(1,\xi_{n-1}(y))+f_{n-1}(\xi_{n-1}(y),y)

This works: for every
n\geq 3
, if
\pi_{xy}^{n}>0
then

\displaystyle b_{n+1}(y)-b_{n}(x)=[f_{n-2}(1,1)+f_{n-1}(1,\xi_{n}(y))+f_{n}(\xi_{n}(y),y)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt-f_{n-2}(1,\xi_{n-1}(x))-f_{n-1}(\xi_{n-1}(x),x)-f_{n}(x,y)]+f_{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle\therefore b_{n+1}(y)-b_{n}(x)=\Gamma_{n}\left(1\ \begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\xi_{n-1}(x)\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\xi_{n}(y)}\\
x\end{array}\ y\right)+f_{n}(x,y)\overset{!}{=}f_{n}(x,y).

Here is the justification of
\overset{!}{=}
. In the setup we consider, the natural measure on the level n hexagons is atomic, and every admissible hexagon has positive mass. So
u_{n}=0
implies that
\Gamma_{n}(P)=0
for every admissible hexagon, and
\overset{!}{=}
follows.

We proved (
6.1
), but we are not yet done because
\mathsf{b}
is not necessarily uniformly bounded. To fix this decompose
b_{n}(y)=a_{n}(y)+\sum_{k=2}^{n-3}f_{k}(1,1)
. Then
|\mathsf{a}|\leq 2K
, and a direct calculation shows that

f_{n}(x,y)=a_{n+1}(y)-a_{n}(x)+f_{n-2}(1,1),

whence
\mathsf{f}=\nabla\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{c}
as we claimed.

This proves the lemma in case
u_{n}=0
.
The general case
u_{n}\geq 0
is done in exactly the same way, except that now
the identity (
6.1
) gives for
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}:=\mathsf{f}-\nabla a-\mathsf{c}

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}(x,y)

\displaystyle=f_{n}(x,y)-(a_{n+1}(y)-a_{n}(x))-c_{n}=-\Gamma_{n}\left(1\ \begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\xi_{n-1}(x)\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\xi_{n}(y)}\\
x\end{array}\ y\right).

If
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
, then
|\Gamma_{n}|\leq 6K
, whence
|\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}|\leq 6K
. Next,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{f}_{n}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Gamma_{n}\left(1\ \begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\xi_{n-1}(X_{n})\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\xi_{n}(X_{n+1})}\\
X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)^{2}\right].

In the scenario we consider the space of admissible hexagons has a finite number of elements, and each has probability uniformly bounded below. So there is a global constant
C
which only depends on
\sup|\mathfrak{S}_{n}|
and on
\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}
in (E2) such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\Gamma_{n}\left(1\ \begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\xi_{n-1}(X_{n})\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\xi_{n}(X_{n+1})}\\
X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)^{2}\right]\leq C\mathbb{E}[\Gamma(P)^{2}],

where the last expectation is over all position
n
hexagons.
So
\|\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{C}\cdot u_{n}^{2}
.

(The gradient lemma says that we can choose
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{c}
so that
C=1
. The argument we gave does not quite give this, but the value of the constant is not important for the applications we have in mind.)

The proof of the gradient lemma in the general case:

Recall the ladder process
\mathsf{L}=\{\underline{L}^{(N)}_{n}\}
,
\underline{L}^{(N)}_{n}=(Z_{n-2}^{(N)},Y_{n-1}^{(N)},X_{n}^{(N)})
from
§
3.2
.
In what follows we omit the superscripts
(N)
on the right hand side of identities. Define

\displaystyle F_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)}):=F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})=f_{n-2}(Z_{n-2},Y_{n-1})+f_{n-1}(Y_{n-1},X_{n})

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{(N)}):=\Gamma_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})=\Gamma\left(Z_{n-2}\begin{array}[]{l}Z_{n-1}\\
Y_{n-1}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{n}\\
X_{n}\end{array}X_{n+1}\right),\ \ \text{see \eqref{balance}}.

Then we have the following identity:

f_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{n+1})=F_{n+1}(\underline{L}_{n+1})-F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})+f_{n-2}(Z_{n-2},Z_{n-1})-\Gamma_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1}).

(6.6)

Next define
a_{n}^{(N)}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}\to\mathbb{R}
and
c_{n}^{(N)}\in\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle a_{n}^{(N)}(\xi):=\mathbb{E}\biggl(\mathbb{E}(F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})|X_{n}=\xi\bigr)\biggr)\ \ (3\leq n\leq k_{N})

(6.7)

\displaystyle c_{n}^{(N)}:=\mathbb{E}[f_{n-2}(Z_{n-2},Z_{n-1})].

(6.8)

We will show that the lemma holds with
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}:=\mathsf{f}-\nabla\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{c}
.

Since
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
by assumption, it is clear that
|\mathsf{a}|\leq 2K
and
|\mathsf{c}|\leq K
. It remains to bound
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
in
L^{\infty}
and
L^{2}
.

Claim:
For every
(\xi,\eta)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
,

\displaystyle c_{n}^{(N)}=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\biggl(f_{n-2}(Z_{n-2},Z_{n-1})\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n+1}=\eta\\
X_{n}=\xi\\
\end{array}\biggr)\right],

\displaystyle a_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}\biggl(F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n+1}=\eta\\
X_{n}=\xi\\
\end{array}\biggr)

\displaystyle a_{n+1}^{(N)}(\eta)=\mathbb{E}\biggl(F_{n+1}(\underline{L}_{n+1})\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n+1}=\eta\\
X_{n}=\xi\end{array}\biggr)

Proof of the claim.
The proof is based on Lemma
1.20
.
The first identity is because
\{Z_{n}\}
is independent from
\{X_{n}\}
.
The second identity is because conditioned on
X_{n}
,
\underline{L}_{n}
is independent of
X_{n+1}
. The third identity is because conditioned on
X_{n+1}
,
\underline{L}_{n+1}
is independent of
X_{n}
.

With the claim proved, we can proceed to bound
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
.
Taking the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}(\ \cdot\ |X_{n+1}^{(N)}=\eta\ ,\ X_{n}^{(N)}=\xi)
on both sides of
(
6.6
), we find that

f_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\eta)=a_{n+1}(\eta)-a_{n}(\xi)+c_{n}-\mathbb{E}\left(\Gamma_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n+1}=\eta\\
X_{n}=\xi\end{array}\right),

whence

{\widetilde{f}}_{n}(\xi,\eta):=-\mathbb{E}\left(\Gamma_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n+1}=\eta\\
X_{n}=\xi\end{array}\right)
.

Clearly
|\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}|\leq 6K
. To bound the
L^{2}
norm we recall that the marginal distribution of
\{X_{n}\}
with respect to the distribution of the ladder process is precisely the distribution of our original array. Therefore

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(N)}\|_{2}^{2}\equiv\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{f}_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{n+1})^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\Gamma_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n+1}X_{n}\end{array}\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\Gamma_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})^{2}\right)\right]

because conditional expectations contract
L^{2}
-norms.

Next we use Lemma
1.20
(3) to see that
\Gamma_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})
is equal in distribution to the balance of a random level
N
hexagon at position
n
, whence
\mathbb{E}(\Gamma_{n}^{2})=(u^{(N)}_{n})^{2}
.
\square

The gradient lemma splits an additive functional into a gradient term, and a term
with controlled variance. The next lemma estimates the covariances between the two terms.

Lemma 2.33

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is a uniformly bounded functional of a uniformly elliptic Markov array. There is a constant
C
s.t. if
h_{\ell_{N}}^{(N)}
are
uniformly bounded measurable functions on
\mathfrak{S}_{\ell_{N}}^{(N)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{\ell_{N+1}}^{(N)}
,
and

\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f_{n}^{(N)}|\leq K
,
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|h_{\ell_{N}}^{(N)}|\leq L
,
then

\mathrm{Cov}\left(S_{N},h_{\ell_{N}}^{(N)}(X_{\ell_{N}}^{(N)},X_{\ell_{N+1}}^{(N)})\right)\leq CKL.

Proof

This follows from the decomposition
\displaystyle{\rm Cov}(S_{N},h_{\ell_{N}}^{(N)})=\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}{\rm Cov}(f_{n}^{(N)},h_{\ell_{N}}^{(N)})
and the exponential mixing of
\mathsf{X}
(Proposition
1.11
).
\square

6.2
The estimate for
{\rm Var}(S_{N})

We prove Theorem
5.1
.
Let
\mathsf{f}=\{f^{(N)}_{n}\}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}=\{X^{(N)}_{n}\}
with row lengths
k_{N}+1
. Our aim is to bound
{\rm Var}(S_{N})
above and below by affine functions of the structure constants
U_{N}=\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}(u_{n}^{(N)})^{2}
. Assume
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
almost surely.

Throughout the proof, we fix
N
and drop the superscripts
(N)
. So
X^{(N)}_{n}=X_{n}
,
f^{(N)}_{n}=f_{n}
,
u^{(N)}_{n}=u_{n}
etc.

Lower bound for the variance.

Let’s split
U_{N}=\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}u_{n}^{2}
into three sums:

U_{N}=\sum_{\gamma=0,1,2}U_{N}(\gamma),\text{ where }U_{N}(\gamma):=\left(\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}u_{n}^{2}1_{[n=\gamma\mod 3]}(n)\right).

For every
N
there is at least one
\gamma_{N}\in\{0,1,2\}
such that

U_{N}(\gamma_{N})\geq\frac{1}{3}U_{N}.

Let

\alpha_{N}:=\gamma_{N}+1.

and define
\beta_{N}
by

k_{N}-\beta_{N}+1=\max\{n\leq k_{N}:n=\alpha_{N}\mod 3\}.

With these choices,
\alpha_{N},\beta_{N}\in\{1,2,3\}
, and
k_{N}-\beta_{N}+1=\alpha_{N}\mod 3
.

We begin by bounding from below the variance of

\displaystyle S_{N}^{\prime}:=\sum_{k=\alpha_{N}}^{k_{N}-\beta_{N}}f_{j}(X_{j},X_{j+1}).

Write
k_{N}-\beta_{N}+1=3M_{N}+\alpha_{N}
, with
M_{N}\in\mathbb{N}
, then

S_{N}^{\prime}=F_{0}+\cdots+F_{M_{N}-1},\text{ where }{F_{k}:=f_{3k+\alpha_{N}}+f_{3k+\alpha_{N}+1}+f_{3k+\alpha_{N}+2}}.

Observe that
S_{N}^{\prime}
is a function of the following variables:

\framebox{$X_{\alpha_{N}}$},X_{\alpha_{N}+1},X_{\alpha_{N}+2},\framebox{$X_{\alpha_{N}+3}$},X_{\alpha_{N}+4},X_{\alpha_{N}+5},\cdots,\framebox{$X_{k_{N}-\beta_{N}+1}$},

where we have boxed the terms with indices congruent to
\alpha_{N}
mod
3
.
Let
\mathfs F_{N}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the boxed random variables. Conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{N}
,
F_{k}
are independent.
Therefore,

\displaystyle{\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\prime}|\mathcal{F}_{N})=\sum_{k=0}^{M_{N}-1}{\rm Var}(F_{k}|\mathcal{F}_{N})=\sum_{k=0}^{M_{N}-1}{\rm Var}(F_{k}|{X_{3k+\alpha_{N}},X_{3(k+1)+\alpha_{N}}})

Taking the expectation on both sides, and using the general inequality
{\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\prime})\geq\mathbb{E}({\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\prime}|\mathcal{F}_{N}))
, we obtain

{\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\prime})\geq\sum_{k=0}^{M_{N}-1}\mathbb{E}\biggl({\rm Var}(F_{k}|{X_{3k+\alpha_{N}},X_{3(k+1)+\alpha_{N}}})\biggr).

To estimate the summands, we recall that for every random variable
W
,
{\rm Var}(W)=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[(W^{\prime}-W^{\prime\prime})^{2}]
where
W^{\prime},W^{\prime\prime}
are two independent copies of
W
.
Thus

\displaystyle{\rm Var}(F_{k}|X_{3k+\alpha_{N}}=a,X_{3(k+1)+\alpha_{N}}=b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\Gamma\left({X_{3k+\alpha_{N}}\ \begin{array}[]{l}X_{3k+\alpha_{N}+1}\\
Y_{3k+\alpha_{N}+1}\end{array}\ \begin{array}[]{l}X_{3k+\alpha_{N}+2}\\
Y_{3k+\alpha_{N}+2}\end{array}\ Y_{3(k+1)+\alpha_{N}}}\right)^{2}\bigg|{\begin{array}[]{l}X_{3k+\alpha_{N}}=Y_{3k+\alpha_{N}}=a\\
X_{3(k+1)+\alpha_{N}}=Y_{3(k+1)+\alpha_{N}}=b\end{array}}\biggr],

whence
\mathbb{E}\bigl({\rm Var}(F_{k}|X_{3k+\alpha_{N}},X_{3(k+1)+\alpha_{N}})\bigr)\equiv\mathbb{E}(\Gamma(P)^{2})\equiv(u^{(N)}_{{3k+\alpha_{N}+2}})^{2}
where
\Gamma(P)
is the balance of a random hexagon
P\in\mathrm{Hex}(N,{3k+\alpha_{N}+2})
. So

\displaystyle{\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\prime})\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{M_{N}-1}(u^{(N)}_{{3k+\alpha_{N}+2}})^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{M_{N}-1}(u^{(N)}_{3(k+1)+\gamma_{N}})^{2}\ \ \ \ (\because\alpha_{N}=\gamma_{N}+1)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}u_{n}^{2}1_{[n=\gamma_{n}\mod 3]}(n)-2\sup\{u_{j}^{2}\}\geq\frac{1}{2}U_{N}(\gamma_{N})-2\cdot(6K)^{2}

\displaystyle>\frac{1}{6}U_{N}-100K^{2},\text{ by choice of $\gamma_{N}$.}

Now we claim that
|{\rm Var}(S_{N})-{\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\prime})|
is uniformly bounded from below. To see this,
let
f_{j}^{\ast}:=f_{j}-\mathbb{E}(f_{j})
, and let
A_{N}:=\{j\in\mathbb{N}:1\leq j\leq\alpha\text{ or }k_{N}-\beta\leq j\leq k_{N}\}
. Then
S_{N}=S_{N}^{\prime}+\sum_{j\in A_{N}}f_{j}
, whence

{\rm Var}(S_{N})={\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\prime})+{\rm Var}(\sum_{j\in A_{N}}f_{j})+2\sum_{j\in A_{N}}{\rm Cov}(S_{N}^{\prime},f_{j}).

Since
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
and
|A_{N}|\leq 6
, the second term bounded by
4K^{2}|A_{N}|\leq 24K^{2}
. Next by uniform ellipticity and (
2.5
), there are mixing constants
\theta\in(0,1)
and
C_{mix}>0
which only depend on
{\varepsilon}_{0}
, the ellipticity constant of
\mathsf{X}
, so that

\displaystyle{\rm Cov}(S_{N}^{\prime},f_{j})\leq C_{mix}\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\|f_{n}^{\ast}\|_{2}\|f_{j}^{\ast}\|_{2}\theta^{|n-j|}\leq\frac{2C_{mix}K^{2}}{1-\theta}.

It follows that
{\rm Var}(S_{N})\geq{\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\prime})-const\geq constU_{N}-const
, where the constants depends only on
K
and the ellipticity constant
{\varepsilon}_{0}
.

Upper bound for the variance.
Write
\mathsf{f}=\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}+\nabla\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{c}
as in the gradient lemma. In particular,

{\rm Var}({\widetilde{f}}_{n}(X_{n-1},X_{n}))\leq u_{n}^{2}
.
Then

{\rm Var}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}f_{n}\right)={\rm Var}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}{\widetilde{f}}_{n}\right)+{\rm Var}\left(a_{N+1}-a_{1}\right)+2{\rm Cov}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}{\widetilde{f}}_{n},a_{N+1}-a_{1}\right).

The first term is smaller than
C_{1}U_{N}+C_{2}^{\prime}
due to the gradient Lemma and Lemma
2.28

the second term is smaller than
C_{2}^{\prime\prime}
due to Lemma
2.33
.

\Box

6.3
Characterization of center-tight additive functionals

We prove Theorem
5.2
. Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded functional on a uniformly elliptic array
\mathsf{X}
. We will show that the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)

{\rm Var}(S_{N})=O(1)
;

(b)

\mathsf{f}
is the sum of a gradient and an additive functional with summable variance;

(c)

\mathsf{f}
is center tight.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b):
By the gradient lemma

\mathsf{f}=\nabla\mathsf{a}+(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}+\mathsf{c}),

where
a_{n}^{(N)}(x)
are measurable functions on
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}
with uniformly bounded
L^{\infty}
norm,
c_{n}^{(N)}
are uniformly bounded constants, and
\|\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}_{n}\|_{2}\leq u_{n}^{(N)}
. By Theorem
5.1
,
\displaystyle\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}(u_{n}^{(N)})^{2}<\infty
, so
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}+\mathsf{c}
has summable variance, proving (b).

(b)
\Rightarrow
(c):
We already saw that gradients and functionals with summable variance are center-tight. Since the sum of center-tight functionals is center-tight, (c) is proved.

(c)
\Rightarrow
(a):
Assume by way of contradiction that
\exists N_{i}\uparrow\infty
such that
V_{N_{i}}={\rm Var}(S_{N_{i}})\to\infty
. By Dobrushin’s CLT (see
Do
,
SV
and
§
6.5
),
\displaystyle\frac{S_{N_{i}}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N_{i}})}{\sqrt{V_{N_{i}}}}
converges in distribution to a standard Gaussian distribution. But
center-tightness implies that there are constants
\mu_{N}^{\prime}
s.t.
\frac{S_{N}-\mu_{N}^{\prime}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
converges in distribution to the deterministic random variable
W\equiv 0
, and both statements cannot be true simultaneously.
\Box

6.4
McLeish’s martingale central limit theorem

A
martingale difference array
with row lengths
k_{N}
is a (possibly non-Markov) array
\Delta
of random variables

\Delta=\{\Delta_{j}^{(N)}:N\geq 1,1\leq j\leq k_{N}\}

together with an array of
\sigma
-algebras
\{\mathfs F^{(N)}_{j}:N\geq 1,1\leq j\leq k_{N}\}
, so that:

(1)

For each
N
,
\Delta^{(N)}_{1},\ldots,\Delta^{(N)}_{k_{N}}
are
random variables on the same probability space
(\mathfrak{S}_{N},\mathfs F_{N},\mu_{N})
.

(2)

\mathfs F_{1}^{(N)}\subset\mathfs F_{2}^{(N)}\subset\mathfs F_{3}^{(N)}\subset\cdots\subset\mathfs F_{k_{N}}^{(N)}
are sub
\sigma
-algebras of
\mathfs F_{N}
.

(3)

\Delta^{(N)}_{j}
is
\mathfs F^{(N)}_{j}
–measurable,
\mathbb{E}(|\Delta^{(N)}_{j}|)<\infty
, and

\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{(N)}_{j+1}|\mathfs F^{(N)}_{j})=0.

We say that
\Delta
has
finite variance
, if every
\Delta^{(N)}_{j}
has finite variance. Notice that
\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{(N)}_{j})=0
for all
j=2,\ldots,k_{N+1}
. If in addition
\mathbb{E}(\Delta^{(N)}_{1})=0
for all
N
, then we say that
\Delta
has
zero mean
.

Example 2.34

Suppose
\{S_{n}\}
is a martingale relative to
\{\mathfs F_{n}\}
, then

\Delta_{1}^{(N)}:=S_{1}\ ,\ \Delta_{j}^{(N)}:=S_{j}-S_{j-1}\ ,\ \mathfs F_{j}^{(N)}:=\mathfs F_{j}\ ,\ j=1,\ldots,N

is a martingale difference array.

The following basic observation on martingale difference arrays is a key to many of their properties:

Lemma 2.35

Suppose
\Delta
is a martingale difference array with finite variance, then for each
N

\Delta_{1}^{(N)},\ldots,\Delta_{k_{N}}^{(N)}
are uncorrelated, and if
\Delta
has zero mean, then

{\rm Var}(\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\Delta_{n}^{(N)})=\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{n}^{(N)})^{2}].

Proof

Fix
N
and write
\Delta_{j}^{(N)}=\Delta_{j}
,
\mathfs F_{j}^{(N)}=\mathfs F_{j}
.

If
i<j
, then

\mathbb{E}(\Delta_{j}\Delta_{i})=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}(\Delta_{j}\Delta_{i}|\mathfs F_{j-1})]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}(\Delta_{i}\mathbb{E}(\Delta_{j}|\mathfs F_{j-1}))]=\mathbb{E}(\Delta_{i}\cdot 0)=0
. The identity for the variance immediately follows.
\square

Theorem 6.1
(McLeish’s Martingale Central Limit Theorem)

Let
\Delta=\{\Delta^{(N)}_{j}\}
be a martingale difference array with row lengths
k_{N}
, zero mean, and finite variance, and let
V_{N}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{j}^{(N)})^{2}]
. Suppose:

(1)

\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}\frac{|\Delta_{j}^{(N)}|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
has uniformly bounded
L^{2}
norm;

(2)

\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}\frac{|\Delta_{j}^{(N)}|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0

in probability; and

(3)

\frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}(\Delta^{(N)}_{n})^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1
in probability.

Then for all intervals
(a,b)
,
{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}\Delta_{j}^{(N)}\in(a,b)\right]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{a}^{b}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt.

We prepare the ground for the proof.

A sequence of random variables
\{Y_{n}\}
on
(\Omega,\mathfs F,\mu)
is called
uniformly integrable
if for every
\epsilon
,
\exists K
s.t.
\mathbb{E}(|Y_{n}|1_{[|Y_{n}|>K]})<\epsilon
for all
n
. This is strictly stronger than tightness (there are tight non-integrable random variables).

Example 2.36

If
M_{p}:=\sup\|Y_{n}\|_{p}<\infty
for some
p>1
, then
\{Y_{n}\}
is uniformly integrable.

Indeed, by Chebyshev’s inequality,
\mu[|Y_{n}|>K]\leq\frac{1}{K^{p}}M_{p}^{p}
, and by Hölder’s inequality

\mathbb{E}(|Y_{n}|1_{[|Y_{n}|>K]})\leq M_{p}\mu[|Y_{n}|>K]^{1/q}=O(K^{-p/q})
for the
q
s.t.
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.

Lemma 2.37

Suppose
Y_{n},Y\in L^{1}(\Omega,\mathfs F,\mu)
, then
Y_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{L^{1}}Y
iff
\{Y_{n}\}
are uniformly integrable and
Y_{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}Y
in probability. In this case
\mathbb{E}(Y_{n})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathbb{E}(Y)
.

Proof

We include the well-known, standard proof for completeness.

Proof of
(\Rightarrow)
:
Since
Y\in L^{1}
, it follows (for example from the Dominated Convergence
Theorem) that
\displaystyle\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(|Y|1_{|Y|\geq K})=0.

Given
{\varepsilon}
take
K
so that
\mathbb{E}(|Y|1_{|Y|\geq K})<{\varepsilon}.

Let
\delta={\mathbb{P}}(|Y||\leq K)
then it is easy to see that

\mathbb{E}(|Y|1_{F})<{\varepsilon}\text{ for all measurable sets $F$ s.t. $\mu(F)<\delta$}.

(6.9)

Fix
{\varepsilon}>0
, and choose
\delta
as in (
6.9
).

Suppose
\|Y_{n}-Y\|_{1}\to 0
. By
Markov’s inequality
{\mathbb{P}}[|Y_{n}-Y|>{\varepsilon}]\leq\|Y_{n}-Y\|_{1}/{\varepsilon}\to 0
, and
Y_{n}\to Y
in probability.

Markov’s inequality also implies that
{\mathbb{P}}[|Y_{n}|>K]\leq K^{-1}\sup\|Y_{n}\|_{1}=O(K^{-1})
, so
there exists
K
s.t.
{\mathbb{P}}[|Y_{n}|>K]<\delta
for all
n
. By the choice of
\delta
,

\displaystyle\int_{[|Y_{n}|>K]}|Y_{n}|d\mu\leq\int_{[|Y_{n}|>K]}|Y|d\mu+\int_{[|Y_{n}|>K]}|Y_{n}-Y|d\mu\leq{\varepsilon}+\|Y_{n}-Y\|_{1}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}{\varepsilon}.

Uniform integrability follows.

Proof of
(\Leftarrow)
: Given a random variable
Z
, let
Z^{K}:=Z1_{[|Z|\leq K]}
. Since
\{Y_{n}\}
is uniformly integrable, for every
\epsilon
there is a
K>1
s.t.
\|Y_{n}^{K}-Y_{n}\|_{1}<\epsilon
for all
n
.
Similarly,
\|Y^{K}-Y\|_{1}<\epsilon
for all
K
large enough. Thus for all
n
,

\displaystyle\|Y_{n}-Y\|_{1}\leq\|Y_{n}^{K}-Y^{K}\|_{1}+2\epsilon\leq\epsilon\mu[|Y_{n}^{K}-Y^{K}|\leq\epsilon]+2K\mu[|Y_{n}^{K}-Y^{K}|>\epsilon]+2\epsilon

\displaystyle\leq 3\epsilon+2K\biggl(\mu[|Y_{n}-Y|>\epsilon]+\mu[|Y_{n}|>K]+\mu[|Y|>K]\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq 3\epsilon+2K\mu[|Y_{n}-Y|>\epsilon]+2\mathbb{E}(|Y_{n}|1_{[|Y_{n}|>K]})+2\mathbb{E}(|Y|1_{|Y|>K})

\displaystyle\therefore\limsup_{n\to\infty}\|Y_{n}-Y\|_{1}\leq 3\epsilon+2\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}(|Y_{n}|1_{[|Y_{n}|>K]})+2\mathbb{E}(|Y|1_{|Y|>K}),

where we have used the assumption that
Y_{n}\to Y
in probability.
The last expression can be made arbitrarily small, by choosing
\epsilon
sufficiently small,
K
sufficiently large, and appealing to the uniform integrability of
Y_{n}
.
\square

Lemma 2.38
(McLeish)

Let
\{W_{j}^{(N)}:1\leq j\leq k_{N}\}
be a triangular array of random variables
7
7

7

Not necessarily a martingale difference array or a Markov array.
, where
W_{1}^{(N)},\ldots,W_{k_{N}}^{(N)}
are defined on the same probability space. Fix
t\in\mathbb{R}
and let
\displaystyle T_{N}(t):=\prod_{j=1}^{k_{N}}(1+itW_{j}^{(N)})
. Suppose

(1)

\{T_{N}(t)\}
is uniformly integrable and
\mathbb{E}(T_{N})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1
,

(2)

\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}(W_{j}^{(N)})^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1
in probability,

(3)

\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}{|W_{j}^{(N)}|}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0
in probability.

Then
\mathbb{E}(e^{it(W_{1}^{(N)}+\cdots+W_{k_{N}}^{(N)})})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}
.

Proof

Define a function
r(x)
on
[-1,1]
by the identity

e^{ix}=(1+ix)e^{-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}+r(x)}
, then

r(x)=-\log(1+ix)+ix+\frac{1}{2}x^{2}=O(|x|^{3})
. Fix
C
s.t.
|r(x)|\leq C|x|^{3}
for
|x|<1
.

Substituting
S_{N}:=W_{1}^{(N)}+\cdots+W_{k_{N}}^{(N)}
in
e^{ix}=(1+ix)e^{-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}+r(x)}
gives (in what follows we drop the superscripts
(N)
and abbreviate
T_{n}:=T_{n}(t)
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(e^{itS_{N}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(\prod_{j=1}^{k_{N}}e^{itW_{j}})=\mathbb{E}(T_{N}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}t^{2}W_{j}^{2}+r(tW_{j})})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(T_{N}U_{N}),\text{ where }U_{N}:=\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}t^{2}(W_{j}^{(N)})^{2}+r(tW_{j}^{(N)})\right].

T_{N}
and
U_{N}
have the following properties:

(a)

\mathbb{E}(T_{N})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1
, by assumption.

(b)

\{T_{N}\}
is uniformly integrable by assumption, and
|T_{N}U_{N}|=|e^{itS_{N}}|=1
.

(c)

\displaystyle U_{N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{\text{prob}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}\right)
, because

\circ

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}\left(W_{j}^{(N)}\right)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{\text{prob}}1
, by assumption,

\circ

\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}\left|W_{j}^{(N)}\right|\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{\text{prob}}0
by assumption, so with asymptotic probability one,

\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}r(tW^{(N)}_{j})\right|\leq C|t|^{3}\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}\left|W^{(N)}_{j}\right|\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}\left(W_{j}^{(N)}\right)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{\text{prob}}0.

We claim that this implies that
\mathbb{E}(e^{itS_{N}})=\mathbb{E}(T_{N}U_{N})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}
. Let
L:=e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}
. Since
|\mathbb{E}(T_{N}U_{N})-L|\leq|\mathbb{E}(T_{N}(U_{N}-L))|+L|\mathbb{E}(T_{N})-1|
, (a) tells us that

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}(T_{N}U_{N})-L|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbb{E}(T_{N}(U_{N}-L))|+o(1).

(6.10)

Next, for every
K,\epsilon
,
\mu[|T_{N}(U_{N}-L)|>\epsilon]\leq\mu[|T_{N}|>K]+\mu[|U_{N}-L|>\epsilon/K]
. Therefore by (b) and (c),

\displaystyle T_{N}(U_{N}-L)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0\text{ in probability}.

(6.11)

Finally,
|T_{N}(U_{N}-L)|\leq 1+L|T_{N}|
, so
T_{N}(U_{N}-L)
is uniformly integrable by (b).
By Lemma
2.37
,
\mathbb{E}(T_{N}(U_{N}-L))\to 0
, and by (
6.10
),
\mathbb{E}(e^{itS_{N}})=\mathbb{E}(T_{N}U_{N})\to e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}
.
\square

Proof of the Martingale CLT
McLeish
:

Let
\Delta=\{\Delta_{j}^{(N)}\}
be a martingale difference array with row lengths
k_{N}
, which satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
6.1
, and let

S_{N}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}\Delta_{j}^{(N)}\text{ and }V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N})\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}[(\Delta_{j}^{(N)})^{2}]\ \text{(see Lemma \ref{Lemma-Not-Correlated})}.

It is tempting to apply McLeish’s Lemma to the normalized array

\Delta_{j}^{(N)}/\sqrt{V_{N}}
, but to do this we need to check the uniform integrability of
\prod_{j=1}^{n}(1+it\Delta^{(N)}_{j}/\sqrt{V_{N}})
and this is difficult.
It is easier to work with the following array of truncations:

W_{1}^{(N)}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\Delta_{1}^{(N)}\ ,\ W_{n}^{(N)}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\Delta_{n}^{(N)}1_{[\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(\Delta^{(N)}_{k})^{2}\leq 2V_{N}]}.

It is easy to check that
\{W_{n}^{(N)}\}
is a martingale difference array relative to
\mathfs F_{n}^{(N)}
, and that
\{W^{(N)}_{n}\}
has zero mean, and finite variance.

In addition,

S_{N}^{\ast}:=\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}W_{n}^{(N)}
are close to
S_{N}/\sqrt{V_{N}}
in probability:

\mu[S_{N}^{\ast}\neq\tfrac{S_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}]\leq\mu\biggl[\exists 1\leq j\leq k_{N}\text{ s.t. }\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}(\Delta^{(N)}_{k})^{2}>2V_{N}\biggr]\leq\mu\biggl[\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}(\Delta^{(N)}_{k})^{2}>2V_{N}\biggr]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0

because
\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}\left(\Delta^{(N)}_{j}\right)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{\text{prob}}1
by assumption.

Thus to prove the theorem, it is enough to show that
S_{N}^{\ast}
converges in distribution to the standard Gaussian distribution. To do this, we check that
\{W^{(N)}_{n}\}
satisfies the conditions of McLeish’s Lemma.

Fix
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
T_{N}=T_{N}(t):=\prod_{j=1}^{k_{N}}(1+itW_{j}^{(N)}).

Let
J_{N}:=\max\{2\leq j\leq k_{N}:\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}(\Delta^{(N)}_{n})^{2}\leq 2V_{N}\}
(or
J_{N}=1
if the maximum is over the empty set). Writing
W_{j}=W_{j}^{(N)}
and
\Delta_{j}=\Delta_{j}^{(N)}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|T_{N}|=\prod_{j=1}^{k_{N}}(1+t^{2}W_{j}^{2})^{1/2}=\prod_{j=1}^{J_{N}}\biggl(1+\frac{t^{2}\Delta_{j}^{2}}{V_{N}}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{j=1}^{J_{N}-1}\biggl(1+\frac{t^{2}\Delta_{j}^{2}}{V_{N}}\biggr)\right)^{1/2}\cdot\biggl(1+\frac{t^{2}\Delta_{J_{N}}^{2}}{V_{N}}\biggr)^{1/2},\text{ where }\prod_{j=1}^{0}(\cdots):=1

\displaystyle\leq\exp\biggl(\frac{t^{2}}{2V_{N}}\sum_{j=0}^{J_{N}-1}\Delta_{j}^{2}\biggr)\biggl(1+\frac{t^{2}}{V_{N}}\Delta_{J_{N}}^{2}\biggr)^{1/2}\leq e^{t^{2}}\biggl(1+|t|\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}\biggl|\frac{\Delta_{j}^{(N)}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\biggr|\biggr).

Thus

\|T_{N}(t)\|_{2}^{2}\leq e^{2t^{2}}\left(1+|t|\mathbb{E}\left(\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}\left|\frac{\Delta^{(N)}_{j}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right|\right)\right)^{2}.

By the first assumption of the theorem, the last quantity is uniformly bounded for each
t
. It follows that

\{T_{N}(t)\}_{N\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable for each
t
. Next, successive conditioning shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(T_{N})=1+it\mathbb{E}\left(\Delta_{1}^{(N)}\right)=1.

The first condition of McLeish’s Lemma is verified.

The second condition of McLeish’s Lemma follows from the assumption

\displaystyle\frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\left(\Delta_{n}^{(N)}\right)^{2}\to 0

in probability, and the estimate

\displaystyle\mu\left[\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}(W^{(N)}_{n})^{2}\neq\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}(\tfrac{\Delta_{n}^{(N)}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})^{2}\right]\leq\mu\left[\exists 1\leq n\leq k_{N}\text{ s.t. }\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\Delta_{j}^{(N)})^{2}>2V_{N}\right]\leq

\displaystyle\leq\mu\left[\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}(\Delta_{n}^{(N)})^{2}>2V_{N}\right]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0,\text{ because $\frac{1}{V_{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}}(\Delta_{n}^{(N)})^{2}\to 1$ in probability.}

The third condition of McLeish’s Lemma follows from the assumption that

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}|W^{(N)}_{j}|\to 0
in probability, for similar reasons.

So McLeish’s lemma applies to
\{W^{(N)}_{n}\}
, and
\mathbb{E}(e^{itS_{N}^{\ast}})\to e^{-\frac{1}{2}t^{2}}
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. By Lévy’s continuity theorem, this implies that
S_{N}^{\ast}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{\text{dist}}N(0,1)
.

As explained above, this implies that

\frac{S_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{\text{dist}}N(0,1)
.
\Box

6.5
Proof of Dobrushin’s central limit theorem

Let
\mathsf{X}=\{X^{(N)}_{n}\}
be a uniformly elliptic Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, and let
\mathsf{f}=\{f^{(N)}_{n}\}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on
\mathsf{X}
. Define as before

\displaystyle S_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}f_{n}^{(N)}(X^{(N)}_{n},X^{(N)}_{n+1})\ ,\ V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N}).

Without loss of generality,

\mathbb{E}[f_{n}^{(N)}(X^{(N)}_{n},X^{(N)}_{n+1})]=0\text{ and }|f_{n}^{(N)}|\leq K\text{ for all }n,N.

Define
\mathfs F^{(N)}_{n}:=\sigma(X^{(N)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(N)}_{n+1})
for
n\geq 1
, and

\mathfs F^{(N)}_{0}:=
trivial
\sigma
-algebra. Fix
N
and write
f_{k}=f_{k}^{(N)}(X^{(N)}_{k},X^{(N)}_{k+1})
and
\mathfs F_{k}=\mathfs F_{k}^{(N)}
, then
\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{k})=f_{k}
,
\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{0})=\mathbb{E}(f_{k})=0
, and therefore

\displaystyle S_{N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{N}}f_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{N}}\bigl(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{k})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{0})\bigr)=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{N}}\sum_{n=1}^{k}\bigl(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{n})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{n-1})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\sum_{k=n}^{k_{N}}\bigl(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{n})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{n-1})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\Delta^{(N)}_{n},\text{ where }\Delta^{(N)}_{n}:=\sum_{k=n}^{k_{N}}\bigl(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{(N)}|\mathfs F_{n}^{(N)})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{(N)}|\mathfs F_{n-1}^{(N)})\bigr).

The array
\{\Delta^{(N)}_{n}:1\leq n\leq k_{N};N\geq 1\}
is a martingale difference array relative to the filtrations
\mathfs F^{(N)}_{n}
, with zero mean and finite variances. To prove the theorem, it suffices to check that
\{\Delta^{(N)}_{n}\}
satisfies the conditions of the martingale CLT.

Step 1:

\displaystyle\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}\frac{|\Delta_{j}^{(N)}|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
has uniformly bounded
L^{2}
norm, and
\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq k_{N}}\frac{|\Delta_{j}^{(N)}|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{prob}0
.

Proof.
The proof is based on the exponential mixing of uniformly elliptic Markov arrays (Proposition
1.11
): Let
K:=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
, then there are constants
C_{mix}>1
and
0<\theta<1
such that for all
k\geq n
,

\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{(N)}|\mathfs F^{(N)}_{n})\|_{\infty}\leq C_{mix}K\theta^{k-n-1}.

It follows that
|\Delta_{j}^{(N)}|<2C_{mix}K\sum_{\ell=-1}^{\infty}\theta^{\ell}=\frac{2C_{mix}K\theta^{-2}}{1-\theta}
. The step follows from the assumption that
V_{N}\to\infty
.

Step 2:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}(\Delta_{n}^{(N)})^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1
in probability.

Proof.
We follow
SV
closely.

Let
Y^{(N)}_{i}:=(\Delta^{(N)}_{i})^{2}/V_{N}
. We will show that

\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}-1\right\|_{2}^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0
,
and use the general fact that
L^{2}
-convergence implies convergence in probability (by Chebyshev’s inequality).

Notice that
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y_{i}^{(N)}\Big)=1
, because by
Lemma
2.35
, this expectation equals

\frac{1}{V_{N}}\times{\rm Var}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\Delta_{n}^{(N)}\right)=\frac{1}{V_{N}}{\rm Var}(S_{N})=1.

So

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}-1\bigr\|_{2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}\bigr)^{2}\biggr]-2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}\biggr]+1

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}\bigl(Y^{(N)}_{i}\bigr)^{2}\biggr]+2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i<j}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}Y^{(N)}_{j}\biggr]-2+1

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=O(\max\limits_{1\leq\ell\leq{k_{N}}}\|Y^{(N)}_{\ell}\|_{\infty})\cdot\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{\ell=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}\biggr]+2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i<j}Y^{(N)}_{i}Y^{(N)}_{j}\biggr]-1.

We saw in the proof of step 1 that
\|\Delta_{j}^{(N)}\|_{\infty}
are uniformly bounded. Thus
\max\limits_{1\leq\ell\leq{k_{N}}}\|Y^{(N)}_{\ell}\|_{\infty}=O(1/V_{N})
, so

\bigl\|\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}-1\bigr\|_{2}^{2}=2\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sum_{i<j}Y^{(N)}_{i}Y^{(N)}_{j}\bigr]-1+o(1).

It remains to show that

2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i<j}Y^{(N)}_{i}Y^{(N)}_{j}\biggr]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1.

(6.12)

The proof of (
6.12
) is based on the following fact:

\mathrm{Osc}(N):=\max_{1\leq i\leq k_{N}}\mathrm{Osc}\left(\mathbb{E}\biggl(\sum_{j=i+1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{j}\bigg|\mathfs F^{(N)}_{i}\biggr)\right)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0.

(6.13)

Here
\mathrm{Osc}
is the oscillation, which was defined in §
2.1
.
Before proving this, we explain why (
6.13
) implies (
6.12
). Write
x=y\pm\epsilon
whenever
y-\epsilon\leq x\leq y+\epsilon
.
Every bounded
function
\varphi
satisfies
\varphi=\mathbb{E}(\varphi)\pm\mathrm{Osc}(\varphi)
. So

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i<j}Y^{(N)}_{i}Y^{(N)}_{j}\biggr]=2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}\sum_{j=i+1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{j}\biggr]=2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\sum_{j=i+1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{j}\big|\mathfs F^{(N)}_{i}\bigr)\biggr]

\displaystyle=2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\sum_{j=i+1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{j}\bigr)\biggr]\pm 2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}Y^{(N)}_{i}\biggr]\mathrm{Osc}(N)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}(Y^{(N)}_{i})\sum_{j=i+1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}(Y^{(N)}_{j})\pm 2\mathrm{Osc}(N)\ \ (\because\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}(Y^{(N)}_{i})=1)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}(Y^{(N)}_{i})\right)^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}(Y^{(N)}_{i})^{2}\pm 2\mathrm{Osc}(N)

\displaystyle=1+O\biggl(\max_{1\leq i\leq k_{N}}\|Y_{i}^{(N)}\|_{\infty}\biggr)\pm 2\mathrm{Osc}(N),\because\sum\mathbb{E}(Y^{(N)}_{i})^{2}\leq\underset{=1}{\underbrace{\sum\mathbb{E}(Y^{(N)}_{i})}}\max\|Y_{i}^{(N)}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=1+O(V_{N}^{-1})+O(\mathop{\rm Osc}(N)).

So (
6.13
) implies (
6.12
), and with it the step.

We turn to the proof of (
6.13
). Henceforth we fix
N
and drop all the
(N)
superscripts. First we note that a routine modification of the proof of Lemma
2.35
shows that for all
j,k>i
,
\mathbb{E}(\Delta_{j}\Delta_{k}|\mathfs F_{i})=0.

It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{j=i+1}^{k_{N}}Y_{j}\bigg|\mathfs F_{i}\biggr)\equiv\frac{1}{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\sum_{j=i+1}^{k_{N}}\Delta^{2}_{j}\bigg|\mathfs F_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\bigl(\sum_{n=i+1}^{k_{N}}\Delta_{n}\bigr)^{2}\bigg|\mathfs F_{i}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\biggl(\;\sum_{n=i+1}^{k_{N}}\sum_{k=n}^{k_{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{n})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{n-1})\right]\biggr)^{2}\bigg|\mathfs F_{i}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\biggl(\sum_{k=i+1}^{k_{N}}\sum_{n=i+1}^{k}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{n})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{n-1})\biggr)^{2}\bigg|\mathfs F_{i}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}\bigg(\biggl(\sum_{k=i+1}^{k_{N}}\left[f_{k}-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{i})\right]\biggr)^{2}\bigg|\mathfs F_{i}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{k,\ell=i+1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bigl(\left[f_{k}-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{i})\right]\bigr)\bigl(f_{\ell}-\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i})\bigr)\bigg|\mathfs F_{i}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{k,\ell=i+1}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[f_{k}f_{\ell}+\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{i})\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i})-f_{k}\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i})-f_{\ell}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{i})\bigg|\mathfs F_{i}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{k,\ell=i+1}^{k_{N}}\left[\mathbb{E}\bigl[f_{k}f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i}\bigr]-\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i})\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{i})\right]

(6.14)

The oscillation of the summands can be estimated as follows.
By Lemma
1.9
(d)

\mathrm{Osc}\biggl(\mathbb{E}\bigl(u(X_{k}^{(N)},X_{k+1}^{(N)})\big|X_{j}^{(N)}\bigr)\biggr)\leq\delta\left(\pi^{(N)}_{j,k}\right)\mathrm{Osc}(u),

where
\delta\left(\pi^{(N)}_{j,k}\right)
is the contraction coefficient of the

(k-j)
-step Markov operator
\pi^{(N)}_{j,k}
.
In the uniformly elliptic case, by Lemma
1.10
,

\delta(\pi^{(N)}_{j,j+2})\leq 1-\epsilon_{0}
, where
\epsilon_{0}>0
is the ellipticity constant of
\mathsf{X}
.
Iterating Lemma
1.9
(c) we conclude that
there exists
C_{0}>0
and
0<\theta<1
such that for all
k>i+1
, and for every bounded function
u:\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{(N)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k+1}^{(N)}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathrm{Osc}\biggl(\mathbb{E}\bigl(u(X_{k}^{(N)},X_{k+1}^{(N)})\big|\mathfs F_{i}^{(N)}\bigr)\biggr)\leq C_{0}\theta^{k-i}\mathrm{Osc}(u).

This, (
2.3
), and the inequalities
|f_{j}|\leq K
,
\mathrm{Osc}(u)\leq 2\|u\|_{\infty}
and
\mathrm{Osc}(uv)\leq\|u\|_{\infty}\mathrm{Osc}(v)+\|v\|_{\infty}\mathrm{Osc}(u)
imply the existence of constants
C_{1}>0
and
0<\theta<1
such that for every
N\geq 1
and
i+2\leq k\leq\ell\leq k_{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Osc}\bigl(\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i})\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{i})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Osc}(\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i}))\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{i})\|_{\infty}+\|\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i})\|_{\infty}\mathrm{Osc}(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathfs F_{i}))\leq C_{1}\theta^{k-i}\theta^{\ell-i}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Osc}\biggl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[f_{k}f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{i}\bigr]\biggr)=\mathrm{Osc}\biggl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[f_{k}\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{k})|\mathfs F_{i}\bigr]\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\theta^{k-i}\mathrm{Osc}(f_{k}\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{k}))\leq C_{0}\theta^{k-i}[K\cdot\mathrm{Osc}(\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{k}))+\mathrm{Osc}(f_{k})\|\mathbb{E}(f_{\ell}|\mathfs F_{k})\|_{\infty}]

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\theta^{k-i}\theta^{\ell-k}.

We have stated these bounds for
k,\ell\geq i+2
, but in fact they remain valid for
k=i+2
or
\ell=i+2
, if we increase
C_{1}
to guarantee that
C_{1}\theta^{2}>2K^{2}
.

Substituting these bounds in (
6.14
), we find that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Osc}(N)\leq\frac{2C_{1}}{V_{N}}\sum_{k,\ell=i+1}^{\infty}\theta^{k-i}\theta^{\ell-k}\leq\frac{2C_{1}}{V_{N}}\left(\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}\right)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0.

This proves (
6.13
), and completes the proof of step 2.

Steps 1 and 2 verify the conditions of the martingale CLT. So

\frac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\Delta^{(N)}_{n}
converges in distribution to the standard Gaussian distribution. By construction,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}S_{N}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\sum_{n=1}^{k_{N}}\Delta^{(N)}_{n}
, and the theorem is proved.
\Box

6.6
Almost sure convergence for sums of functionals with summable variance

We prove Proposition
5.4
.
Let
f_{0}^{\ast}:=0
,

f_{n}^{\ast}:=f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
, let

\mathcal{F}_{0}
denote the trivial
\sigma
-algebra, and let

\mathcal{F}_{n}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
.
Then
f_{k}^{\ast}
is
\mathcal{F}_{k+1}
-measurable, so

f_{k}^{\ast}=\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{k+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{0})=\sum_{n=0}^{k}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n}).

Therefore (numbered equalities are justified below):

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}f_{k}^{\ast}=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\left[\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right]=\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{k=n}^{N}\left[\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\overset{(1)}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)-\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(2)}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)-\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{N}\left(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(3)}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)-\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1}).

To justify the numbered inequalities almost surely, we need to establish the convergence of the series which they involve.

By (
2.4
),
\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})\|_{2}+\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\|_{2}\leq 2C_{mix}\sqrt{{\rm Var}(f_{k})}\theta^{k-n+1}
, so by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the assumption
\sum{\rm Var}(f_{n})<\infty
,

\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\left\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right\|_{2}<\infty.

This justifies
\overset{(1)}{=}
and
\overset{(2)}{=}
.

Next by assumption,
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
a.s. for some constant
K
. By (
2.3
),
\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{0})\|_{\infty}+\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\|_{\infty}\leq 4KC_{mix}\theta^{n-k}
so

\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1})|<\infty.

This justifies
\overset{(3)}{=}
.

In summary,
{\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}f_{k}^{\ast}=\sum_{n=0}^{N}\Delta_{n}-Z_{N}}
, where

\Delta_{n}:=\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right)\ ,\ Z_{N}:=\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1}).

To finish the proof, we show that
\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Delta_{n}
and

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}Z_{N}
exist a.s.

Claim 1.

M_{N}:=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Delta_{n}
is a martingale relative to
\{\mathcal{F}_{N}\}
, and
\sup\|M_{N}\|_{2}<\infty
. Consequently,
\lim M_{N}
exists almost surely.

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(M_{N+1}-M_{N}|\mathcal{F}_{N})=\mathbb{E}(\Delta_{N}|\mathcal{F}_{N})\overset{!}{=}\sum_{k=N}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1})|\mathcal{F}_{N})-\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N})|\mathcal{F}_{N})=0.

To justify
\overset{!}{=}
we note that the series

\displaystyle\Delta_{N}=\sum_{k=N}^{\infty}\left[\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\right]

converges in
L^{2}
, because
\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\|_{\infty}=O(\theta^{k-n}),
so its conditional expectation can be calculated term-by-term.

Next we show that
\|M_{N}\|_{2}
is uniformly bounded:

\displaystyle\|M_{N+1}\|_{2}\leq\biggl\|\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\biggr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\biggl\|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k\wedge N}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n+1})-\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{n})\biggr\|_{2}=\biggl\|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{(k\wedge N)+1})\biggr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\|\sum_{k=0}^{N}f_{k}^{\ast}\bigr\|_{2}+\bigl\|\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1})\bigr\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{N}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}+2\sum_{0\leq k<\ell\leq N}{\rm Cov}(f_{k}^{\ast},f_{\ell}^{\ast})}+\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1})\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}+2C_{mix}\sum_{0\leq k<\ell\leq\infty}\theta^{\ell-k}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{2}\|f_{\ell}^{\ast}\|_{2}}+C_{mix}\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{\infty}\theta^{k-N}.

The last expression is uniformly bounded, because
\sum{\rm Var}(f_{k})<\infty
and

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq k<\ell<\infty}\theta^{\ell-k}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{2}\|f_{\ell}^{\ast}\|_{2}\leq\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\theta^{r}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{2}\|f_{k+r}^{\ast}\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{1-\theta}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=N+1}^{\infty}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{\infty}\theta^{k-N}=\frac{1}{1-\theta}\sup_{k}\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{\infty}.

Claim 2.

Z_{N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0
almost surely.

Proof.
It is enough to prove that
\sum\|Z_{N}\|_{2}^{2}<\infty
, because this implies using Chebyshev’s inequality that

\sum{\mathbb{P}}[|Z_{N}|>\epsilon]\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\sum\|Z_{N}\|_{2}^{2}<\infty\text{ for all }\epsilon>0,

whence, by the Borel-Cantelli Lemma,
\limsup|Z_{N}|\leq\epsilon
a.s. for all
\epsilon
. Equivalently,
\lim Z_{N}=0
a.s.

Here is the proof that
\sum\|Z_{N}\|_{2}^{2}<\infty
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\|Z_{N}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}\geq k_{1}>N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\mathbb{E}(f_{k_{1}}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1})\mathbb{E}(f_{k_{2}}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1})\biggr]

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}\geq k_{1}>N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[f_{k_{2}}^{\ast}\mathbb{E}(f_{k_{1}}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1})\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq C_{mix}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}\geq k_{1}>N}\theta^{k_{2}-N+1}\|f_{k_{2}}^{\ast}\|_{2}\|\mathbb{E}(f_{k_{1}}^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}_{N+1})\|_{2}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{by \eqref{Exp-Mixing-L-three}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{mix}^{2}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}\geq k_{1}>N}\theta^{k_{2}-N+1}\|f_{k_{2}}^{\ast}\|_{2}\cdot\theta^{k_{1}-N+1}\|f_{k_{1}}^{\ast}\|_{2}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{by \eqref{Exp-Mixing-L-two}}

\displaystyle=C_{mix}^{2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\theta^{j}\sum_{k>0}\theta^{2k}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\|f_{k+N+j}^{\ast}\|_{2}\|f_{k+N}^{\ast}\|_{2}

(after changing indices
j=k_{2}-k_{1}
,
k=k_{1}-N+1
)

\displaystyle\leq C_{mix}^{2}\sum_{j\geq 0}\theta^{j}\sum_{k>0}\theta^{2k}\sqrt{\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\|f_{k+N+j}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\|f_{k+N}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{mix}^{2}}{1-\theta}\sum_{k>0}\theta^{2k}\sum_{N=k}^{\infty}\|f_{N}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{C_{mix}^{2}}{1-\theta}\sum_{N=1}^{\infty}\|f_{N}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\theta^{2k}<\infty,

because
0<\theta<1
and
\sum\|f_{k}^{\ast}\|_{2}^{2}<\infty.

\square

6.7
Convergence of moments.

Dobrushin’s CLT (Theorem
5.3
) shows that if
V_{N}\to\infty
then for any
bounded continuous
function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi\left(\frac{S_{N}-{\mathbb{E}}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi(z)e^{-z^{2}/2}dz.

(6.15)

In applications, one often need to have convergence of expectations for unbounded functions,
such as polynomials. This problem is addressed in the present section.

Lemma 2.39

Let
\mathsf{f}
be a centered bounded additive functional of a uniformly elliptic Markov chain such that

V_{N}\to\infty.
Then for each
r\in\mathbb{N}
there is a constant
C_{r}
such that for all
N
,

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[S_{N}^{r}\right]\right|\leq C_{r}V_{N}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}.

Corollary 2.40

Under the assumptions of Lemma
2.39

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{{\mathbb{E}}[S_{N}^{r}]}{V_{N}^{r/2}}=\begin{cases}0&r\text{ is odd,}\\
(r-1)!!=\prod_{k=0}^{(r/2)-1}(r-2k-1)&r\text{ is even}.\end{cases}

The corollary follows from Dobrushin’s CLT (Theorem
5.3
), using the fact that by Lemma
2.39
and the de la Vallée-Poussin Lemma,
(S_{N}/\sqrt{V_{N}})^{r}
is uniformly integrable for all
r>1
even, and therefore
\lim\mathbb{E}[(S_{N}/\sqrt{V_{N}})^{r}]=\mathbb{E}[N^{r}]
, where
N
is a Gaussian random variable with mean zero and variance one.

The proof of Lemma
2.39
proceeds by expanding
S_{N}^{r}
into a sum of
r
-tuples
f_{n_{1}}\cdots f_{n_{r}}

(n_{1}\leq\cdots\leq n_{r})
, and by estimating the expectation of each tuple. (Here and throughout,
f_{n}=f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
.)
In view of the gradient lemma it is sufficient to prove Lemma
2.39

under the assumption that there is some constant
C>0
such that

{\widetilde{u}}_{n}:=\|f_{n}\|_{L^{2}}
satisfy
\displaystyle\sum_{n}{\widetilde{u}}_{n}^{2}\leq CV_{N}.

Consider an
r
tuple
f_{n_{1}}\cdots f_{n_{r}}
where

n_{1}\leq n_{2}\leq\dots\leq n_{r}.

Segments of the form
[n_{j},n_{j+1}]
will be called
edges
. The vertices belonging to an edge are called
bound
, the other vertices are
called
free
.

A
marking
is a non-empty collection of edges satisfying the following two conditions.
Firstly, each vertex
n_{j}
belongs to at most
one edge. Secondly, for every free vertex
n_{l}
, either

(i)

there exists a minimal
f(l)>l
such that
n_{f(l)}
is bound, and for all

l\leq i<f(l)
,
n_{i+1}-n_{i}\leq n_{f(l)+1}-n_{f(l)}
; or

(ii)

there exists a maximal
p(l)<l
such that
n_{p(l)}
is bound, and for all
p(l)<i\leq l
,
n_{i}-n_{i-1}\leq n_{p(l)}-n_{p(l)-1}
.

If (i) holds we will say that
n_{l}
is associated to the edge

[n_{f(l)},n_{f(l)+1}]
otherwise it is associated to

[n_{p(l)-1},n_{p(l)}].

Lemma 2.41

There are constants
L=L(r)>0
and
0<\theta<1
such that

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{n_{i}}\right]\right|\leq L\sum_{markings}\;\prod_{[n_{j},n_{j+1}]\text{ is an edge}}\left(\theta^{(n_{j+1}-n_{j})}\;\;{\widetilde{u}}_{n_{j}}{\widetilde{u}}_{n_{j+1}}\right).

Proof

If
r=1
then the result holds since
{\mathbb{E}}[f_{n}]=0
(in this case there are no
markings,
and we let
the empty
sum be equal to zero).

If
r=2
then the lemma says that

\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[f_{n_{1}}f_{n_{2}}\right]\right|\leq K\theta^{n_{2}-n_{1}}\|f_{n_{1}}\|_{L^{2}}\|f_{n_{2}}\|_{L^{2}}

which is true due to Proposition
1.11
(2).

For
r\geq 3
we use induction. Take
j
such that
n_{j+1}-n_{j}
is the largest. Then

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{n_{i}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{j}f_{n_{i}}\right]{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=j+1}^{r}f_{n_{i}}\right]+O\left(\theta^{(n_{j+1}-n_{j})}\left\|\prod_{i=1}^{j}f_{n_{i}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\left\|\prod_{i=j+1}^{r}f_{n_{i}}\right\|_{L^{2}}\right).

Let
K:=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
, then the second term is smaller than

\displaystyle\theta^{(n_{j+1}-n_{j})}{\widetilde{u}}_{n_{j}}{\widetilde{u}}_{n_{j+1}}K^{r-2}
. Thus this term is controlled by the marking with only one marked edge

[n_{j},n_{j+1}].

Applying the inductive assumption to each factor in the first term we obtain the result.

\square

Lemma 2.42

There exists
{\overline{C}}_{r}>0
s.t. for every
set
\mathcal{C}
of
r
tuples
1\leq n_{1}\leq\dots\leq n_{r}\leq N
,

\Gamma_{\mathcal{C}}:=\sum_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{r})\in\mathcal{C}}\left|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{r}f_{n_{i}}\right]\right|\leq{\overline{C}}_{r}V_{N}^{\lfloor r/2\rfloor}.

Lemma
2.42
implies Lemma
2.39
since

{\mathbb{E}}\left[S_{N}^{r}\right]=\sum_{s=1}^{r}\;\;\sum_{k_{1}+\dots+k_{s}=r}\frac{r!}{k_{1}!\cdots k_{s}!}\sum_{1\leq n_{1}<\dots<n_{s}\leq N}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{s}f_{n_{j}}^{k_{j}}\right].

Therefore it suffices to prove Lemma
2.42
.

Proof

By Lemma
2.41

\Gamma_{\mathcal{C}}\leq L\sum_{(n_{1},\dots,n_{r})\in\mathcal{C}}\;\;\underset{\text{ of $(n_{1},\ldots,n_{r})$}}{\sum_{\text{ markings
$(e_{1},\ldots,e_{s})$}}}\;\;\prod_{j=1}^{s}\left({\widetilde{u}}_{e_{j}^{-}}{\widetilde{u}}_{e_{j}^{+}}\theta^{(e_{j}^{+}-e_{j}^{-})}\right)

where the marked edges are
e_{j}=[e_{j}^{-},e_{j}^{+}]
,
j=1,\dots,s.
