Collecting all terms with a fixed set of marked edges

(e_{1},\dots,e_{s})
we obtain

\Gamma_{\mathcal{C}}\leq C(r)\sum_{s}\sum_{(e_{1},\dots,e_{s})}\prod_{j=1}^{s}\left({\widetilde{u}}_{e_{j}^{-}}{\widetilde{u}}_{e_{j}^{+}}\theta^{(e_{j}^{+}-e_{j}^{-})}(e_{j}^{+}-e_{j}^{-})^{r-2}\right)

(6.16)

where
\displaystyle C(r)\prod_{j}(e_{j}^{+}-e_{j}^{-})^{r}
accounts for all tuples which admit a marking
(e_{1},\dots e_{s})
.
Indeed, for every edge
e=[e^{-},e^{+}]
there are at most
0\leq j\leq r-2
vertices which may be associated
to
e
and the positions of those vertices are located inside

\left[e^{-}-(r-2)(e^{+}-e^{-}),e^{-}\right)\cup\left(e^{+},e^{+}+(r-2)(e^{+}-e^{-})\right].

It follows that there are at most
2(r-2)(e^{+}-e^{-})
choices to place each vertex associated to a given
edge.
This gives

\prod_{e}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{r-2}\left[2(r-2)(e^{+}-e^{-})\right]^{j}\right)\leq C(r)\prod_{e}(e^{+}-e^{-})^{r-2}

possibilities for tuples with marking
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{s})

proving (
6.16
).

The sum over
(e_{1},\dots e_{s})
in (
6.16
)
can be estimated by

\left(\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sum_{m=1}^{N-n}{\widetilde{u}}_{n}{\widetilde{u}}_{n+m}\theta^{m}m^{r-2}\right)^{s}.

For each
m
,

\displaystyle\sum_{n}{\widetilde{u}}_{n}{\widetilde{u}}_{n+m}=O(V_{N})
due to the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and because
\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\widetilde{u}}_{n}^{2}\leq CV_{N}
by assumption. Summing over
m

gives

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathcal{C}}\leq const\sum_{2s\leq r}V_{N}^{s}

where the condition
2s\leq r
appears because each edge involves two distinct
vertices, and no vertex belongs to more than one edge. The result follows.
∎
\square

7
Notes and references

The connection between the non-growth of variance and representation in terms of gradients is well-known for stationary stochastic processes. The first result in this direction we are aware of is Leonov’s Theorem
Leonov
.
He showed that the asymptotic variance of a homogeneous additive functional of a stationary homogeneous Markov chain is zero iff the additive functional is the sum of a gradient and a constant. Rousseau-Egele
RE
and Guivarc’h & Hardy
GH
extended this to the context of dynamical systems preserving an invariant Gibbs measure. Kifer
Kifer-CLT
, Conze & Raugi
Conze-Raugi-Sequential
, Dragičević,Froyland & González-Tokman
Dragicevic-Froyland-Gonzalez-Tokman
have proved versions of Leonov’s theorem for random and/or sequential dynamical systems.

The connection between center-tightness and gradients is a central feature of the theory of cocycles over ergodic transformations.
Suppose
T:X\to X
is an ergodic probability preserving transformation on a non-atomic probability space. For every measurable
f:X\to\mathbb{R}
,
\{f\circ T^{n}\}
is a stationary stochastic process, and

S_{N}=f+f\circ T+\cdots+f\circ T^{N-1}

are called the “ergodic sums of the cocycle
f
.” A “coboundary” is a function of the form
f=g-g\circ T
with
g
measurable.
Schmidt characterized cocycles with center-tight
S_{N}
as those arising from coboundaries
(
Schmidt-Cocycles
, page 181)
. These results extend to cocycles taking values in locally compact groups, see Moore & Schmidt
Moore-Schmidt
and Aaronson & Weiss
Aaroson-Weiss-tightness
. For more on this, see Aaronson
(
Aaronson-Book
, chapter 8)
, and
Bradley
(
Bradley
, chapters 8,19)
.
We also refer to
GH55
for an analogous result in the continuous setting.

Notice that inhomogeneous theory is different from the stationary theory in that there is another cause for center-tightness: Having summable variance. This cannot happen in the stationary homogeneous world (unless all
f_{i}
are constant).

Theorem
5.3
is a special case of a more general result due to Dobrushin, which can be found in
Do
. The conditions for Dobrushin’s full result are more general than uniform boundedness or uniform ellipticity. Our proof follows the paper of Sethuraman & Varadhan
SV
, except for some changes we needed to make to deal with additive functionals of the form
f_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1})
, and not just
f_{k}(X_{k})
as in
SV
. McLeish’s Lemma, the martingale CLT, and their proofs are due to McLeish
McLeish
. We refer the reader to Hall & Heyde
Hall-Heyde
for the history of this result, further extensions, and references.

Theorem
5.4
is extends
the Kolmogorov-Khintchin “Two-Series Theorem”
Kolmogorov-Two-Series
. There are other extensions to sums of dependent random variables. We mention for example a version for martingales (Hall & Heyde
(
Hall-Heyde
, chapter 2)
), for sums of negatively dependent random variables (Matuła,
Matula
) and
for expanding maps (
Conze-Raugi-Sequential
).

The proofs of theorems
5.3
and
5.4
use
Gordin’s “martingale-coboundary decomposition”
Gordin
, see also
Hall-Heyde
,
Korepanov-Kosloff-Melbourne
.

Chapter 3
The essential range and irreducibility

In this chapter we discuss the following question: How small can we make the range of an additive functional, by subtracting from it a center-tight functional?

8
Definitions and motivation

Let
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}
be an additive functional of a Markov chain
\mathsf{X}:=\{X_{n}\}
. The
algebraic range
of
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is the intersection
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
of all closed groups
G
s.t. ,

\exists c_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ s.t. }{\mathbb{P}}[f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})-c_{n}\in G]=1\text{ for all }n\geq 1.

(8.1)

We will see later (Lemma
3.51
) that
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
itself satisfies (
8.1
), therefore
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is the smallest closed group satisfying (
8.1
).

Example 3.43

(The simple random walk).
Suppose
\{X_{n}\}
are independent random variables such that
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}=\pm 1)=\frac{1}{2}
, and let
f_{n}(x,y)=x
. Then

S_{n}=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{n}

is the simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
. The algebraic range in this case is
2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof
:
G_{alg}\subset 2\mathbb{Z}
, because we can take
c_{n}:=-1
. Assume by contradiction that
G_{alg}\subsetneq 2\mathbb{Z}
, then
G_{alg}=t\mathbb{Z}
for
t\geq 4
, and the supports of
S_{n}
are cosets of
t\mathbb{Z}
.

But this is false, because
\exists a_{1},a_{2}
s.t.
|a_{1}-a_{2}|<t
and
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{n}=a_{i})\neq 0
: For
n
even take
a_{i}=(-1)^{i}
, and for
n
odd take
a_{i}=1+(-1)^{i}
.

\square

The
lattice case
is the case when
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z}
for some
t\geq 0
. The
non-lattice case
is the case when
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
.
The distinction is important for the following reason. If
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z}
and
\gamma_{N}:=c_{1}+\cdots+c_{N}
, then

{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z})=1\text{ for all }N.

In this case it is not true that
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))\sim\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|a-b|}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}
whenever
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, because
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))=0
whenever
|a-b|<t
and
z_{N}+(a,b)
falls inside the gaps of
\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}
. This is the
lattice obstruction
to the local limit theorem.

There is a related, but more subtle, obstruction.
An additive functional
\mathsf{f}
is called
reducible
on
\mathsf{X}
, if there is
another additive functional
\mathsf{g}
on
\mathsf{X}
such that
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}
is center-tight, and

G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})\subsetneq G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}).

In this case we say that
\mathsf{g}
is a
reduction
of
\mathsf{f}
, and call the algebraic range of
\mathsf{g}
a
reduced range
of
\mathsf{f}
.

Example 3.44

(Simple random walk with continuous first step):
Suppose
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
are independent real valued random variables such that
X_{1}
has continuous non-uniform distribution
\mathfrak{F}
with compact support, and
X_{2},X_{3},\ldots
are equal to
\pm 1
with equal probabilities. Let
f_{n}(x,y)=x
, then

S_{n}=X_{1}+X_{2}+\cdots+X_{n}.

Because of the continuously distributed first step,
G_{alg}(\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
. But if we subtract from
\mathsf{f}
the center-tight functional
\mathsf{c}
with components

c_{n}(x,y)=x\text{ when $n=1$ and }c_{n}(x,y)\equiv 0\text{ when $n>1$},

then the result
\mathsf{g}:=\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{c}
has algebraic range
2\mathbb{Z}
. So
\mathsf{f}
is reducible.

The reduction
\mathsf{g}
satisfies the lattice local limit theorem (see the preface), because it generates the (delayed) simple random walk. But by the assumptions on
\mathfrak{F}
, the original functional
\mathsf{f}=\mathsf{g}+\mathsf{c}
does not satisfy the LLT, lattice or non-lattice. This can be seen by direct calculation from the observation that the distribution of
S_{n}
is the convolution of
\mathfrak{F}
and the centered binomial distribution. See chapter
5
for details.

Here we see an instance of the
reducibility obstruction
to the local limit theorem: A situation when the LLT fails because the additive functional is a sum of a lattice term which satisfies the lattice LLT and a non-lattice center-tight term which spoils it. The reducibility obstruction to the LLT raises the following questions:

1.

Given an additive functional
\mathsf{f}
, how small can we make its algebraic range by subtracting from it a center-tight term?

2.

Is there an “optimal” center-tight functional
\mathsf{c}
such that the algebraic range of
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{c}
cannot be reduced further?

Motivated by these questions, we introduce the following definitions.
The
essential range
of
\mathsf{f}
is

G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}):=\bigcap\left\{G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g}):\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}\text{ is center tight}\right\}.

This is a closed sub-group of
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

An additive functional without reductions is called
irreducible
. Equivalently,
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible iff

G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}).

In this terminology questions 1 and 2 call for the calculation of
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
and ask for an irreducible reduction of
\mathsf{f}
.

9
Main results

9.1
Results for Markov chains

The questions raised at the end of the last section can be answered using the structure constants
d_{n}(\xi)
introduced in (
3.2
).
Define the
co-range
of
\mathsf{f}
to be the set

H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}):=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}d_{n}(\xi)^{2}<\infty\}.

Theorem 9.1

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
. If
\mathsf{f}
is center-tight then
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
, and
if not then either
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{0\}
, or
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z}
for some

t\geq\pi/(6\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|)
.

Theorem 9.2

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
, then

(a)

If
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=0
, then
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
.

(b)

If
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
, then
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}
.

(c)

If
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
, then
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{0\}
.

Theorem 9.3

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
. Then there exists an irreducible uniformly bounded additive functional
\mathsf{g}
such that
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}
is center-tight, and

G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}).

Corollary 3.45

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
.
If
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
, then
|t|\leq 12\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
.

The corollary follows directly from Theorems
9.1
and
9.2
(b).

9.2
Results for Markov arrays

The previous discussion applies to Markov arrays.
Let
\mathsf{f}
be an additive functional on a Markov array
\mathsf{X}
with row lengths
k_{N}+1
:

(1)

The
algebraic range

G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is the intersection of all closed subgroups
G
of
\mathbb{R}
such that for all
1\leq k\leq k_{N},N\geq 1

\exists c^{(N)}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ s.t. }{\mathbb{P}}[f^{(N)}_{k}(X^{(N)}_{k},X^{(N)}_{k+1})-c^{(N)}_{k}\in G]=1.

(2)

The
essential range

G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is the intersection of the algebraic ranges of all additive functionals of the form
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{h}
where
\mathsf{h}
is center-tight.

(3)

The
co-range
is

H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}):=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:\sup\limits_{N}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{k_{N}}d_{k}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty\}.

(4)

An additive functional
\mathsf{f}
is called
irreducible
if
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

This is consistent with the definitions for Markov chains, see Corollary
3.46
below.

Theorem 9.4

The results of Theorems
9.1
,
9.2

9.3
and of Corollary
3.45

hold for all a.s. uniformly bounded additive functionals on uniformly elliptic Markov arrays.

Corollary 3.46

Suppose
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}=\{f^{(N)}_{n}\}
be an additive functional on a Markov array
\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}=\{X^{(N)}_{n}\}
s.t.
f^{(N)}_{n}=f_{n}
and
X^{(N)}_{n}=X_{n}
.
Then

G_{alg}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=G_{alg}({\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{f}})\ ,\ G_{ess}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=G_{ess}({\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{f}})\ ,\ H(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=H({\mathsf{X}},{\mathsf{f}}).

Proof

The equality of the algebraic ranges and co-ranges is trivial, but the equality of the essential ranges requires justification, because some center-tight functionals of
\{X^{(N)}_{n}\}
are not of the form
h^{(N)}_{n}=h_{n}
.

However, since the co-ranges agree, the essential ranges must also agree, by the version of
Theorem
9.2
for arrays.
\square

9.3
Hereditary arrays

Some results for Markov chains do not extend to general Markov arrays. Of particular importance is the following fact, which we need for the proof of the LLT (see the proof of Theorem
12.1
, claim 2). Recall the definition of
D_{N}(\xi)
from (
3.2
).

Theorem 9.5

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
, then

D_{N}(\xi)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty\text{ uniformly on compact subsets of }\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}).

(9.1)

Proof

Suppose
\xi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, then
\displaystyle\sup_{N}D_{N}(\xi)=\infty
, whence

D_{N}(\xi)=\sum\limits_{k=3}^{N}d_{k}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}d_{k}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}\equiv\sup_{N}D_{N}(\xi)=\infty.

Since
D_{N}(\xi)
is non-decreasing and
\xi\mapsto D_{N}(\xi)
are continuous, the convergence is uniform on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.
\square

The following two examples show that Theorem
9.5
fails for some arrays:

Example 3.47

Let
X_{n}
be a sequence of independent uniform
random variables with zero mean and variance equal to one. Form an array by setting

X^{(N)}_{k}=\begin{cases}X_{k}&1\leq k\leq N+1,N\text{ odd }\\
0&1\leq k\leq N+1,N\text{ even }\end{cases}\ \ \ \ \ (k=1,\ldots,N)

and let
f_{k}^{(N)}(x,y):=x.

Then for every
0\neq\xi\in\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
,
D_{N}(\xi)\not\to\infty
.

Proof

We claim that
\sup\limits_{N}D_{2N+1}(\xi)=\infty
for every
\xi\neq 0
.

To see this,
suppose
P={\left(X_{n-2}\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-1}\\
Y_{n-1}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n}\\
Y_{n}\end{array},Y_{n+1}\right)}

is a random level
2N+1
hexagon at position
n
, then
\Gamma(P)=X_{n-1}+X_{n}-Y_{n-1}-Y_{n}
where
X_{i},Y_{j}
are independent random variables each having uniform distribution with mean zero and unit variance. So
\Gamma(P)
is a
non lattice random variable

and
for every
\xi\neq 0
,
d_{n}^{(2N+1)}(\xi)^{2}=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}-1|^{2})=c(\xi)
, where
c(\xi)
is a positive constant independent of
n
. So

D_{2N+1}(\xi)=(2N-1)c(\xi)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty.

Thus
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{0\}
. But
D_{N}(\xi)\not\to\infty
for
\xi\neq 0
, because
D_{2N}(\xi)=0
.
\square

Example 3.48

Suppose
X_{n}
are a sequence of independent identically distributed random variables, equal to
\pm 1
with probability
\frac{1}{2}
. Form an array with row lengths
N+1
by setting
X^{(N)}_{n}=X_{n}
, and let

f^{(N)}_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}):=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{N}}\right)X_{n}\ \ (1\leq n\leq N+1).

Then
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
for all
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, but the convergence is not uniform on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(f)
.

Proof

\Gamma\left(+1\begin{array}[]{l}+1\\
+1\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}+1\\
-1\end{array}+1\right)=1+N^{-1/3}
. Since

\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)
consists of
2^{6}
hexagons, the hexagon
\left(+1\begin{array}[]{l}+1\\
+1\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}+1\\
-1\end{array}+1\right)
has probability
2^{-6}
. It follows that

\displaystyle d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-6}|e^{i\xi(1+N^{-1/3})}-1|^{2}=\frac{1}{16}\sin^{2}\frac{\xi(1+N^{-1/3})}{2}

\displaystyle D_{N}(\xi)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{N-2}{16}\sin^{2}\frac{\xi(1+N^{-1/3})}{2}\sim\begin{cases}16^{-1}N\sin^{2}\frac{\xi}{2}&\xi\not\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}\\
16^{-1}\sqrt[3]{N}&\xi\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}.\end{cases}

We see that
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
for all
\xi\neq 0
, whence
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{0\}
, and
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
for all
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},f)
. But the convergence is not uniform on any compact neighborhood of
2\pi k
,
k\neq 0
, because

D_{N}(\xi_{N})\equiv 0
for
\xi_{N}=2\pi k(1+N^{-1/3})^{-1}\to 2\pi k
.
\square

Because of the importance of property (
9.1
) to the proof of the LLT, we would like to characterize the additive functionals on Markov arrays which satisfy it. Examples 1 and 2 point the way.

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}
.
A
sub-array
of
\mathsf{X}
is an array
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
of the form

\{X^{(N_{\ell})}_{k}:1\leq k\leq k_{N_{\ell}}+1,\ell\geq 1\}
where
N_{\ell}\uparrow\infty
.
The
restriction
of
\mathsf{f}
to
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
is

\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}}=\{f^{(N_{\ell})}_{k}:1\leq k\leq k_{N_{\ell}},\ell\geq 1\}.

(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is called
hereditary
, if
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
for all sub-arrays

\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, and
stably hereditary
if
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})
is hereditary whenever
\mathsf{g}=\{(1+\epsilon_{N})f^{(N)}_{k}\}
with
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
.

Theorem 9.6

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
, then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

\mathsf{f}
is hereditary;

(2)

for all
\xi
,
\liminf\limits_{N\to\infty}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{k_{N}}d_{k}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty\Rightarrow\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{k_{N}}d_{k}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty
;

(3)

for all
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
,
D_{N}(\xi)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty
;

(4)

H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},f|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})=H(\mathsf{X},f)
for every sub-array
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{X}
.

In addition,
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary iff the convergence in (3) is uniform on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

Example 3.49

(Markov chains):
Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
. If
f^{(N)}_{n}=f_{n}
and
X^{(N)}_{n}=X_{n}
, then
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary.

Proof.
This follows from Theorems
9.5
and

9.6
.
\square

Example 3.50

(“Change of measure”):

Let
\mathsf{Y}
be an array obtained from a Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
using the change of measure construction
(example
1.6
). Let
\varphi^{(N)}_{n}
denote the weights of the change of measure.
If
\exists C>0
s.t.

C^{-1}<\varphi_{n}^{(N)}<C\text{ for all $n,N$},

then for every a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional
\mathsf{f}
on
\mathsf{X}
, the additive functional
f^{(N)}_{n}:=f_{n}
is stably hereditary on
\mathsf{Y}

.

Proof

If
d_{n}(\xi,\mathsf{X})
are the structure constants of
\mathsf{f}
on
\mathsf{X}
, and
d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{Y})
are the structure constants of
\mathsf{f}
on
\mathsf{Y}
, then

C^{-6}d_{n}(\xi,\mathsf{X})\leq d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{Y})\leq C^{6}d_{n}(\xi,\mathsf{X}).
So
H(\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{f})=H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

Theorem
9.5
says that

D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{X})\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
. Since

D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{Y})\geq C^{-6}D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{X})
,
D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{X})\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{f})
.
\square

Sometimes (but not always, Example
3.48
), every hereditary functional is stably hereditary:

Theorem 9.7

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
.

(a)

Suppose
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z}
or
\{0\}
. If
\mathsf{f}
is hereditary then

\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary.

(b)

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is integer valued and not center-tight, and
|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
, then
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\frac{k}{2\pi}\mathbb{Z}
for some
0<k\leq 12K
, and if
\mathsf{f}
is hereditary then
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary.

10
Proofs

10.1
Reduction lemmas

Lemma 3.51

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an additive functional on a Markov array
\mathsf{X}
with row lengths
k_{N}+1
. For every
N\geq 1
and
1\leq n\leq k_{N}
,
there exists
c_{k}^{(N)}\text{ s.t. }f^{(N)}_{n}(X_{n}^{(N)},X_{n+1}^{(N)})-c^{(N)}_{n}\in G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})\text{ almost surely}.

Proof

G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is the intersection of all closed subgroups
G
such that

\exists c_{k}^{(N)}\text{ s.t. }f^{(N)}_{n}(X_{n}^{(N)},X_{n+1}^{(N)})-c^{(N)}_{n}\in G\text{ almost surely}.

(10.1)

This is a closed subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
.
The lemma is trivial when
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
(take
c^{(N)}_{n}\equiv 0
), so we focus on the case
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})\neq\mathbb{R}
.

In this case (
10.1
) holds with some
G=t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\geq 0
, and
f^{(N)}_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
must be a discrete random variable. Let
A^{(N)}_{n}
denote the set of values attained by
f^{(N)}_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
with positive probability. Since
G=t\mathbb{Z}
satisfies (
10.1
),
A^{(N)}_{n}\subset
coset of
t\mathbb{Z}
, and

D^{(N)}_{n}:=A^{(N)}_{n}-A^{(N)}_{n}\subset t\mathbb{Z}.

Let
G_{0}
denote the group generated by
\bigcup_{N\geq 1}\bigcup_{1\leq n\leq k_{N}}D^{(N)}_{n}
. Then
G_{0}
is a subgroup of
t\mathbb{Z}
. In particular,
G_{0}
is closed.

By the previous paragraph,
G_{0}\subset t\mathbb{Z}
for any group
t\mathbb{Z}
which satisfies (
10.1
). So
G_{0}\subseteq G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.
Next, we fix
n,N
and observe that all the values of
f^{(N)}_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
belong to the same translate of
A_{n}^{(N)}-A_{n}^{(N)}
, and therefore to the same coset of
G_{0}
. So
G_{0}
satisfies (
10.1
), and
G_{0}\supset G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.
So
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{0}
.
Since
G_{0}
satisfies (
10.1
),
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
satisfies (
10.1
).

\square

Lemma 3.52
(Reduction Lemma)

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
. If
\xi\neq 0
and
\displaystyle\sup_{N}\sum_{k=3}^{k_{N}}d_{k}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty
, then there exists a uniformly bounded additive functional
\mathsf{g}
on
\mathsf{X}
s.t.

\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}\text{ is center-tight, and }G_{alg}(\mathsf{g})\subset\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}.

If
X^{(N)}_{n}=X_{n}
and
f_{n}^{(N)}=f_{n}
(as in the case additive functionals of Markov chains), then we can take
\mathsf{g}
such that
g^{(N)}_{n}=g_{n}
.

Proof for Doeblin chains:
As in the case of the gradient lemma, the reduction lemma has a particularly simple proof in the important special case of Doeblin Markov chains (Example
1.7
).
Recall that Doeblin chains have finite state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Let
\pi_{xy}^{n}:=\pi_{n,n+1}(x,\{y\})
, and relabel the states
\mathfrak{S}_{n}=\{1,\ldots,d_{n}\}
in such a way that
\pi_{11}^{n}=\pi_{n,n+1}(1,\{1\})\neq 0
for all
n
. The Doeblin condition guarantees that for every
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, there exists a state
\xi_{n}(x)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}
such that
\pi_{1,\xi_{n}(x)}^{n-1}\pi_{\xi_{n}(x),1}^{n}>0
.

Define as in the proof of the gradient lemma,

\displaystyle a_{0}\equiv 0,\ \ a_{1}\equiv 0,\ \ \text{ and }a_{n}(x):=f_{n-2}(1,\xi_{n-1}(x))+f_{n-1}(\xi_{n-1}(x),x)\text{ for }n\geq 3

\displaystyle c_{0}:=0,\ \ c_{1}:=0,\ \ \text{ and }c_{n}:=f_{n-2}(1,1)\text{ for }n\geq 3

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}:=\mathsf{f}-\nabla\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{c}.

Then
\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{n}(x,y)=f_{n}(x,y)-(a_{n+1}(y)-a_{n}(x))-c_{n}=-\Gamma_{n}\left(1\ \begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\xi_{n-1}(x)\end{array}\begin{array}[]{c}{\xi_{n}(y)}\\
x\end{array}\ y\right)
, where
\Gamma_{n}
denotes the balance of a hexagon, see (
3.1
).

For Doeblin chains, there are finitely many admissible hexagons at position
n
, and the hexagon measure assigns each of them a mass which is uniformly bounded from below. Let
C^{-1}
be a uniform lower bound for this mass, then

\displaystyle|e^{i\xi\widetilde{f}_{n}(x,y)}-1|^{2}\leq C\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma_{n}}-1|^{2})=Cd_{n}^{2}(\xi).

Decompose
\widetilde{f}_{n}(x,y)=g_{n}(x,y)+h_{n}(x,y)
where

g_{n}(x,y)\in\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}\text{ and }h_{n}(x,y)\in[-\frac{\pi}{\xi},\frac{\pi}{\xi}).

Clearly
|\mathsf{g}|\leq|\mathsf{f}|+|\nabla a|+|\mathsf{c}|+|\mathsf{h}|\leq 6|\mathsf{f}|+\pi/\xi
, and
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{h})\subset\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
.

We show that
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}
is center tight.
We need the following inequality:
8
8

8

Proof of (
10.2
): Since
y=\sin x
is concave on
[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
, its graph lies above the chord
y=2x/\pi
and below the tangent
y=x
. So
2x/\pi\leq\sin x\leq x
on
[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
. Now use the identity
|e^{ix}-1|^{2}=2(1-\cos x)=4\sin^{2}\frac{x}{2}.

\frac{4x^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\leq|e^{ix}-1|^{2}\leq x^{2}\text{ for all $|x|\leq\pi$}.

(10.2)

By (
10.2
),
|h_{n}(x,y)|^{2}\leq\frac{\pi^{2}}{4\xi^{2}}|e^{i\xi h_{n}(x,y)}-1|^{2}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{4\xi^{2}}|e^{i\xi\widetilde{f}_{n}(x,y)}-1|^{2}\leq C\frac{\pi^{2}}{4\xi^{2}}d_{n}^{2}(\xi)
, whence

\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}{\rm Var}(h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+c_{n})=\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}{\rm Var}(h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}))\leq\frac{C\pi^{2}}{4\xi^{2}}\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}d_{n}^{2}(\xi)<\infty.

So
\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}
has summable variance. Therefore
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}=\nabla a+(\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c})
is center tight.
\square

Preparations for the proof in the general case.

Lemma 3.53

Suppose
E_{1},\ldots,E_{N}
are measurable events, and let
W
denote the random variable which counts how many of
E_{i}
occur simultaneously, then

{\mathbb{P}}(W\geq t)\leq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{k}).

Proof

Apply Markov’s inequality to
W=\sum 1_{E_{k}}
.
\square

Suppose
W
is a real-valued random variable. A
circular mean
of
W
is a real number
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)
which minimizes the quantity
\mathbb{E}(|e^{i(W-\theta)}-1|^{2})
. Such numbers always exist, because
\theta\mapsto\mathbb{E}(|e^{i(W-\theta)}-1|^{2})
is continuous and
2\pi
-periodic. But circular means are not unique: If, for example,
W
is uniformly distributed on
[-\pi,\pi]
, then every
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)
is a circular mean.

The
circular variance
of a real random variable
W
is defined to be

\mathrm{CVar\,}(W):=\min_{\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)}\mathbb{E}(|e^{i(W-\theta)}-1|^{2})\equiv\min_{\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)}4\mathbb{E}\bigl(\sin^{2}\tfrac{W-\theta}{2}\bigr).

For every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, let

\langle x\rangle:=\text{unique element of $[-\pi,\pi)$ s.t. $x-\langle x\rangle\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}$.}

(10.3)

It is not difficult to see, using (
10.2
), that for every circular mean
\theta

\frac{4}{\pi^{2}}{\rm Var}\langle W-\theta\rangle\leq\mathrm{CVar\,}(W)\leq{\rm Var}(W).

(10.4)

Lemma 3.54

For every real-valued random variable
W
, we can write
W=W_{1}+W_{2}
where

W_{1}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
almost surely, and
{\rm Var}(W_{2})\leq\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\mathrm{CVar\,}(W)
.

Proof

W_{1}:=(W-\theta)-\langle W-\theta\rangle
,
W_{2}:=\langle W-\theta\rangle+\theta
,

\theta:=
a circular mean.
\square

Proof of the Reduction Lemma in the general case:
Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
, with row lengths
k_{N}
, and fix
\xi\neq 0
such that

\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty.

Let
\mathsf{L}
denote the ladder process associated to
\mathsf{X}
(see
section
3.2
). We remind the reader that this is a Markov array with entries
\underline{L}^{(N)}_{n}=(Z^{(N)}_{n-2},Y^{(N)}_{n-1},X^{(N)}_{n})

(3\leq n\leq k_{N})
, and for every
N
: (a)
\{X^{(N)}_{n}\}
,
\{Z^{(N)}_{n}\}
are two independent copies of
\mathsf{X}^{(N)}
; (b)
Y^{(N)}_{n}
are conditionally independent given
\{X^{(N)}_{i}\}
and
\{Z^{(N)}_{i}\}
; and (c) the conditional distribution of
Y^{(N)}_{n}
given
\{Z^{(N)}_{i}\}
and
\{X^{(N)}_{i}\}
is given by

{\mathbb{P}}\left(Y^{N}_{n-1}\in E\bigg|\begin{array}[]{l}\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}=\{\zeta^{(N)}_{i}\}\\
\{X_{i}^{(N)}\}=\{\xi^{(N)}_{i}\}\end{array}\right)=\begin{array}[]{l}\text{bridge probability for $\mathsf{X}$ that $X^{(N)}_{n-1}\in E$}\\
\text{given that $X_{n-2}^{(N)}=\zeta^{(N)}_{n-2}$ and $X^{(N)}_{n}=\xi^{(N)}_{n}$.}\\
\text{(see \lx@sectionsign\ref{Section-Bridge}).}\end{array}

Let
\mathsf{F},\mathsf{H}
be the additive functionals on
\mathsf{L}
with entries

\begin{aligned} &F^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n}):=f_{n-2}^{(N)}(Z_{n-2}^{(N)},Y_{n-1}^{(N)})+f_{n-1}^{(N)}(Y_{n-1}^{(N)},X_{n}^{(N)})\\
&H_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{(N)}):=\left\langle\xi\Gamma\left(Z_{n-2}^{(N)}\ \ {\begin{array}[]{l}Z_{n-1}^{(N)}\\
Y_{n-1}^{(N)}\end{array}}\ \ {\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{n}^{(N)}\\
X_{n}^{(N)}\end{array}}\ \ X_{n+1}^{(N)}\right)\right\rangle\end{aligned}\ \ \ \ (3\leq n\leq k_{N},\ N\geq 1)

(see (
3.1
) and (
10.3
)). Clearly
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{F}|\leq 2\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
and
|\mathsf{H}|\leq\pi
.

Step 1:

|\mathbb{E}(H_{n}^{(N)})|\leq\frac{\pi}{4}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}
,

\mathbb{E}[(H_{n}^{(N)})^{2}]\leq\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}
,
and

\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}[(H_{3}^{(N)}+\cdots+H_{k_{N}}^{(N)})^{2}]<\infty.

Proof of step 1.

We fix
N
and drop the superscripts
{(N)}
.

The map
\imath:\left(Z_{n-2}\ {\begin{array}[]{l}Z_{n-1}\\
Y_{n-1}\end{array}}\ {\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{n}\\
X_{n}\end{array}},X_{n+1}\right)\mapsto\left(Z_{n-2}\ {\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{n-1}\\
Z_{n-1}\end{array}}\ {\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n}\\
Y_{n}\end{array}},X_{n+1}\right)

preserves the natural measure on the space of hexagons, and is an involution:
\imath^{2}=id
. Clearly

\Gamma\circ\imath=-\Gamma.

Using the partial symmetry
\langle-x\rangle=-\langle x\rangle\text{ for all }x\not\in-\pi+2\pi\mathbb{Z},

we find that
H_{n}\circ\imath=-H_{n}
on
[H_{n}\neq-\pi]
.
So
\mathbb{E}(H_{n}1_{[H_{n}\neq-\pi]})=0
, and therefore

|\mathbb{E}(H_{n})|=\pi{\mathbb{P}}(H_{n}=-\pi)\leq\frac{\pi}{4}\mathbb{E}(|e^{iH_{n}}-1|^{2})=\frac{\pi}{4}\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma}-1|^{2})=\frac{\pi}{4}d_{n}(\xi)^{2},

which is the first statement we needed to show.

Next we observe from (
10.2
) that

\mathbb{E}(H_{n}^{2})\leq\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma}-1|^{2})=\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}
, which is the second statement we had to prove.

The two statements already proven and the boundedness of
d_{n}

show that there is a constant
C
s. t.
\displaystyle{\rm Var}(H_{n})\leq Cd_{n}^{2}(\xi)^{2}.

Now the third statement follows from Lemma
2.28
.
The proof of step 1 is complete.

From now on, fix a constant
D
such that

\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}+\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}^{(N)}\right)^{2}\right]<D.

Step 2:

For every
N\geq 1
there exists

\displaystyle\underline{\zeta}^{(N)}=(\zeta^{(N)}_{1},\ldots,\zeta^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})\in\prod_{i=1}^{k_{N}+1}\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{(N)}
s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl(H_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{(N)})^{2}\bigg|\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}=\underline{\zeta}^{(N)}\biggr)<\pi^{2}D,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{(N)})\right)^{2}\bigg|\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}=\underline{\zeta}^{(N)}\right]<\pi^{2}D,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\left[\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathrm{CVar\,}\left(\xi F_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)})\bigg|\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}=\underline{\zeta}^{(N)},X_{n}^{(N)}\right)\right]<\pi^{2}D\text{ and }

\displaystyle|f^{(N)}_{n}(\zeta^{(N)}_{n},\zeta^{(N)}_{n+1})|\leq\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|\text{ for all }3\leq n\leq k_{N}.

Here and throughout
\underline{L}_{n}=(Z_{n-2}^{(N)},Y_{n-1}^{(N)},X_{n}^{(N)})
, and
\mathbb{E}_{X}
indicates averaging on
\{X_{i}^{(N)}\}
.

Proof of Step 2.
We fix
N
and drop the
(N)
superscripts.

Let

\Omega_{1}:=\left\{\underline{\zeta}:\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}(H_{n}^{2}|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta})\leq\pi^{2}D\right\}.

By step 1,

\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}^{2}\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta}\right)\right]=\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}(H_{n}^{2})\leq\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}\leq\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}D,

where
\mathbb{E}_{Z}=
integration over
\underline{\zeta}
with respect to the distribution of
\{Z^{(N)}_{i}\}
(recall that
\{Z^{(N)}_{i}\}\overset{dist}{=}\{X^{(N)}_{i}\}
).
By Markov’s inequality,
{\mathbb{P}}[\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}\in\Omega_{1}]>\frac{3}{4}
.

Let
\Omega_{2}:=\{\underline{\zeta}:\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\bigr)^{2}\big|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta}\bigr]<\pi^{2}D\}
. As before, by Markov’s inequality,
{\mathbb{P}}[\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}\in\Omega_{2}]\geq 1-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}
.

Let

\Omega_{3}:=\left\{\underline{\zeta}:\mathbb{E}_{X}\biggl[\sum\limits_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathrm{CVar\,}\bigl(\xi F(\underline{L}_{n+1})\big|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n+1}\bigr)\biggr]<\pi^{2}D\right\}
,

\theta^{\ast}(\underline{L}_{n},X_{n+1},Z_{n-1}):=-\xi f_{n-2}(Z_{n-2},Z_{n-1})+\xi F(\underline{L}_{n})+\xi f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}).

Then

\exp[{iH_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})}]=\exp[{i\xi F(\underline{L}_{n+1})-i\theta^{\ast}(\underline{L}_{n+1},X_{n+1},Z_{n-2})}].

Given
X_{n+1}
and
\{Z_{i}\}
,

\underline{L}_{n+1}
is conditionally independent from
\underline{L}_{n}
,
\{X_{i}\}_{i\neq n+1}
. So

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Z,X}\biggl(\mathrm{CVar\,}\biggl(\xi F(\underline{L}_{n+1})\big|\{Z_{i}\},X_{n+1}\biggr)\biggr)=\mathbb{E}\biggl(\mathrm{CVar\,}\biggl(\xi F(\underline{L}_{n+1})\big|\underline{L}_{n},\{Z_{i}\},\{X_{i}\}\biggr)\biggr)

\displaystyle\overset{!}{\leq}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\mathbb{E}\bigl(|e^{i\xi F(\underline{L}_{n+1})-i\theta^{\ast}(\underline{L}_{n},X_{n+1},Z_{n-1})}-1|^{2}\big|\underline{L}_{n},\{X_{i}\},\{Z_{i}\}\bigr)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i(\xi F(\underline{L}_{n+1})-\theta^{\ast})}-1|^{2})\equiv\mathbb{E}(|e^{iH_{n}}-1|^{2})=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma}-1|^{2})=d_{n}(\xi)^{2},

where
\overset{!}{\leq}
is because
\theta^{\ast}
is conditionally constant.
So

\mathbb{E}_{Z}\left[\mathbb{E}_{X}\left(\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathrm{CVar\,}\bigl(\xi F(\underline{L}_{n+1})\big|\{Z_{i}\},X_{n+1})\right)\right]<D.

By Markov’s inequality,

{\mathbb{P}}(\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}\in\Omega_{3})\geq 1-\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}
.

Finally, let
\Omega_{4}:=\{\underline{\zeta}:|f_{n}(\zeta_{n},\zeta_{n+1})|\leq\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|\}
, then
{\mathbb{P}}(\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}\in\Omega_{4})=1
.

In summary

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[\bigcup_{1\leq i\leq 4}\Omega_{i}^{c}\right]\leq\frac{2}{\pi^{2}}+\frac{1}{4}<1.

Necessarily
\Omega_{1}\cap\Omega_{2}\cap\Omega_{3}\cap\Omega_{4}\neq\varnothing
. Any
\underline{\zeta}=\underline{\zeta}^{(N)}
in the intersection satisfies the requirements of step 2.

Step 3:

There exist measurable functions
\theta_{n}^{(N)}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}\to[-\pi,\pi)
s.t.

\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl(|e^{i\xi F_{n}^{(N)}(\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)})-i\theta_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n})}-1|^{2}\bigg|\{Z_{i}^{(N)}\}=\underline{\zeta}^{(N)}\biggr)<2\pi^{2}D.

Proof.
We fix
N
and drop the
(N)
superscripts.

Clearly,
\theta\mapsto\mathbb{E}(|e^{i(W-\theta)}-1|^{2})
is continuous for every random variable
W
. So
\mathrm{CVar\,}(W)=\inf_{q\in\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{E}(|e^{i(W-q)}-1|^{2})
, an infimum over a countable set, whence

\mathrm{CVar\,}(\xi F_{n}|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n})=\inf_{q\in\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi F(\underline{L}_{n})-iq}-1|^{2}|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}=\xi_{n}).

The expectation can be expressed explicitly using integrals with respect to the bridge distributions, and this expression shows that

\eta\mapsto\mathrm{CVar\,}(\xi F_{n}|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}=\eta)

is measurable on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)}
.

Fix
N
and
\underline{\zeta}=\underline{\zeta}^{(N)}
. We say that
(\eta,q)\in S_{n}^{(N)}\times\mathbb{R}
have “property
P_{n}(\eta,q)
”, if the following condition holds:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})-iq}-1|^{2}|\{Z_{n}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}=\eta)

(
P_{n}(\xi,q)
)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{CVar\,}(\xi F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})|\{Z_{n}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}=\eta)+\frac{D}{n^{2}}

By the previous paragraph,
\{\eta:P_{n}(\eta,q)\text{ holds}\}
is measurable, and for every
\eta
there exists
q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(-\pi,\pi)
such that
P_{n}(\eta,q)
holds. Let

\theta_{n}(\eta)=\theta_{n}^{(N)}(\eta):=\inf\left\{q:{q\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(-\pi,\pi)}\text{ s.t. }P_{n}(\eta,q)\text{ holds}\right\}.

Again, this is a measurable function, and since for fixed
\eta
,
P_{n}(\eta,q)
is a closed property of
q
,

\theta_{n}^{(N)}(\eta)
itself satisfies property
P_{n}(\eta,\theta_{n}^{(N)}(\xi))
. So

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\left[\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})-i\theta_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n})}-1|^{2}|\{Z_{n}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{X}\left[\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathrm{CVar\,}\biggl(\xi F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})\bigg|\{Z_{n}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}\biggr)\right]+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}D

\displaystyle<2\pi^{2}D,\text{ by choice of $\underline{\zeta}$.}

Step 4 (the reduction).

Let
\underline{\zeta}=\underline{\zeta}^{(N)}
,
\theta_{n}=\theta_{n}^{(N)}
,
f_{n}=f_{n}^{(N)}
,
F_{n}=F_{n}^{(N)}
,
X_{n}=X_{n}^{(N)}
,
Z_{n}=Z_{n}^{(N)}
. Define

\displaystyle c_{n}^{(N)}:=f_{n}(\zeta_{n-2},\zeta_{n-1})

\displaystyle a_{n}^{(N)}(x):=\frac{1}{\xi}\biggl[\theta_{n}(X_{n})+\mathbb{E}\bigl(\langle\xi F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})-\theta_{n}(X_{n})\rangle\big|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}=x\bigr)\biggr]\ \ (x\in\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}:=\frac{1}{\xi}\biggl<\xi\bigl(\mathsf{f}-\nabla\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{c}\bigr)\biggr>

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}:=\mathsf{f}-\nabla\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{c}-\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}.

Then
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{c},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}},\mathsf{g}
are uniformly bounded, and
G_{alg}(\mathsf{g})\subset\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.
By choice of
\underline{\zeta}^{(N)}
,
|\mathsf{c}|\leq\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
, and by the definition of
\theta^{(N)}
and
\langle\cdot\rangle
,
|\mathsf{a}|\leq 2\pi/|\xi|
and
|\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}|\leq\pi/|\xi|
. It follows that
|\mathsf{g}|\leq 2\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|+3\pi/|\xi|.

Next,

\mathsf{g}\equiv\frac{1}{\xi}\biggl(\xi(\mathsf{f}-\nabla\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{c})-\langle\xi(\mathsf{f}-\nabla\mathsf{a}-\mathsf{c})\rangle\biggr).

The term in the brackets belongs to
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
by the definition of
\langle\cdot\rangle
, so
G_{alg}(\mathsf{g})\subset\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
, and the proof of step 4 is complete.

Notice that
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}=\nabla\mathsf{a}+\mathsf{c}+\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
. Gradients and constant functionals are center tight. So to complete the proof of the reduction lemma, it suffices to show:

Step 5:

\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
is center-tight.

Proof.
We fix
N
and drop the
(N)
superscripts.

We begin with a few identities.
Suppose
\{Z^{(N)}_{i}\}=\{\zeta^{(N)}_{i}\}
, and consider the hexagon

P_{n}:=\left(Z_{n-2}\ {\begin{array}[]{l}Z_{n-1}\\
Y_{n-1}\end{array}}\ {\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{n}\\
X_{n}\end{array}}\ X_{n+1}\right)=\left(\zeta_{n-2}\ {\begin{array}[]{l}\zeta_{n-1}\\
Y_{n-1}\end{array}}\ {\begin{array}[]{l}Y_{n}\\
X_{n}\end{array}}\ X_{n+1}\right),

then

-\Gamma(P_{n})=-f_{n-2}(Z_{n-2},Z_{n-1})-F_{n+1}(\underline{L}_{n-1})+F_{n}(\underline{L}_{n})+f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1}),

whence

\displaystyle\xi{\widetilde{f}}_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})=\biggl\langle\xi\bigl(-\Gamma(P_{n})+a_{n}(X_{n})-F(\underline{L}_{n})+F(\underline{L}_{n+1})-a_{n+1}(X_{n+1})\bigr)\biggr\rangle

\displaystyle=\biggl\langle-H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})+\xi\bigl(a_{n}(X_{n})-F(\underline{L}_{n})\bigr)+\xi\bigl(F(\underline{L}_{n+1})-a_{n+1}(X_{n+1})\bigr)\biggr\rangle.

Define a new functional
\mathsf{W}
of the ladder process
\{\underline{L}_{n}\}
with entries

W(\underline{L}_{n}):=\langle\xi F(\underline{L}_{n})-\theta_{n}(X_{n})\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left(\langle\xi F(\underline{L}_{n})-\theta_{n}(X_{n})\rangle\big|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta}^{(N)},X_{n}\right).

Notice that
W(\underline{L}_{n})=\xi(F(\underline{L}_{n})-a_{n}(X_{n}))\mod 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore

\xi{\widetilde{f}}_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})=\biggl\langle W(\underline{L}_{n+1})-W(\underline{L}_{n})-H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\biggr\rangle.

(10.5)

Claim.
Given
\delta>0
, let
T_{\delta}={11\pi^{2}D}/{\delta}.
Then
there exists
a measurable set
\Omega_{X}
of
\{X_{i}\}
such that
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega_{X})>1-\delta
and such that for all
\underline{\xi}\in\Omega_{X}
,

(1)

\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|W(\underline{L}_{n})|>\frac{\pi}{3}\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)<T_{\delta},

(2)

\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})|>\frac{\pi}{3}\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)<T_{\delta},

(3)

\mathbb{E}\biggl(\bigl|\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\bigr|\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)<T_{\delta}.

Proof of the claim.

\underline{L}_{n}
is conditionally independent of
\{X_{i}\}_{i\neq n}
given
\{Z_{i}\},X_{n}
. So

\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|W(\underline{L}_{n})|\geq\frac{\pi}{4}\biggl|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|W(\underline{L}_{n})|\geq\frac{\pi}{4}\biggl|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}=\xi_{n}\biggr).

Since
\mathbb{E}(W(\underline{L}_{n})|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n})=0
, we can use the Chebyshev inequality to bound the sum of probabilities from above by

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16}{\pi^{2}}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\rm Var}\bigl(\langle\xi F(\underline{L}_{n})-\theta_{n}(X_{n})\rangle|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 4\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\mathbb{E}\bigl(|e^{i\xi F(\underline{L}_{n})-i\theta_{n}(X_{n})}-1|^{2}\big|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},X_{n}\bigr),\text{ see \eqref{eix-1-inequality}}.

Integrating over
\{X_{i}\}
we have by the choice of
\theta_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n})
(step 3) that

\mathbb{E}_{X}\left[\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|W(\underline{L}_{n})|\geq\frac{\pi}{4}\biggl|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)\right]\leq 8\pi^{2}D.

By Markov’s inequality, the set

\Omega_{X}^{1}(T):=\left\{\underline{\xi}:\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|W(\underline{L}_{n})|>\frac{\pi}{3}\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)\leq T\right\}

has probability
{\mathbb{P}}[\Omega_{X}^{1}(T)]\geq 1-8\pi^{2}D/T
.

Similarly, by Markov’s inequality

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|H_{n}|\geq\frac{\pi}{4}\biggl|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)\leq\frac{16}{\pi^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl(H_{n}^{2}\biggl|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr).

By the choice of
\underline{\zeta}
,

\mathbb{E}_{X}\left[\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|H_{n}|\geq\frac{\pi}{4}\biggl|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}\biggr)\right]\leq 16D
.
So
the set

\Omega_{X}^{2}(T):=\left\{\underline{\xi}:\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})|>\frac{\pi}{3}\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)\leq T\right\}

has probability
{\mathbb{P}}[\Omega^{2}_{X}(T)]\geq 1-16D/T>1-2\pi^{2}D/T
.

Finally, since conditional expectations contract
L^{2}
-norms,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{X}\biggl[\mathbb{E}\biggl(\bigl|\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\bigr|\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\biggr)^{2}\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta}\biggr]\leq\pi^{2}D.

So

\Omega_{X}^{3}(T):=\left\{\underline{\xi}:\mathbb{E}\biggl(\bigl|\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})\bigr|\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)\leq T\right\}

has probability

{\mathbb{P}}[\Omega_{X}^{3}(T)]>1-\pi^{2}D/T^{2}.

We see that if
T>1
, then
{\mathbb{P}}[\Omega_{X}^{1}(T)\cap\Omega_{X}^{2}(T)\cap\Omega_{X}^{3}(T)]>1-\frac{11\pi^{2}D}{T}
. The claim follows.
\square

We can now complete the proof of the step 5 (and the reduction lemma) and show that
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
is center-tight.

Fix
\delta>0
and
\Omega_{X}
,
T_{\delta}
as in the claim.
Fix
N
and define the random set

A_{N}(\{\underline{L}_{n}^{(N)}\}):=\{3\leq n\leq k_{N}:|W(\underline{L}_{n})|\geq\frac{\pi}{3}\text{ or }|H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n},\underline{L}_{n+1})|\geq\frac{\pi}{3}\}.

For all
\underline{\xi}\in\Omega_{X}
, we have the following bound (Lemma
3.53
):

{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(|A_{N}|>4T_{\delta}\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)<\frac{1}{2}.

Similarly, for all
\underline{\xi}\in\Omega_{X}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\biggl(\bigl|\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}\bigr|>4T_{\delta}\bigg|\{Z_{i}\}=\underline{\zeta},\{X_{i}\}=\underline{\xi}\biggr)\leq\frac{1}{4}.

Since the probabilities of these events add up to less than one, the intersection of their complements is non-empty. So for every
\underline{\xi}\in\Omega_{X}
we can find
\{Y_{i}^{(N)}(\underline{\xi})\}_{i=2}^{k_{N}-1}
such that
\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast}:=\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast}(\underline{\xi})=(\zeta_{n-2}^{(N)},Y_{n-1}^{(N)}(\underline{\xi}),\xi_{n})
has the following two properties:

\left|\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{\ast})\right|\leq 4T_{\delta},\text{ and }

M:=\#\left\{3\leq n\leq k_{N}:|W(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast})|\geq\frac{\pi}{3}\text{ or }|H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{\ast})|\geq\frac{\pi}{3}\right\}\leq 4T_{\delta}.

Let
n_{1}<\cdots<n_{M}
be an enumeration of the indices
n
where
|W(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast})|\geq\frac{\pi}{3}
or

|H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{\ast})|\geq\frac{\pi}{3}
.
By (
10.5
), if
n_{i}<n<n_{i+1}-1
,

\xi{\widetilde{f}}_{n}(\xi_{n},\xi_{n+1})=W(\underline{L}_{n+1}^{\ast})-W(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast})-H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{\ast}),

because
\langle x+y+z\rangle=x+y+z
whenever
|x|,|y|,|z|<\frac{\pi}{3}
. So

-\sum_{n=n_{i}}^{n_{i+1}-1}\xi{\widetilde{f}}_{n}(\xi_{n},\xi_{n+1})=\sum_{n=n_{i}+1}^{n_{i+1}-1}H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{\ast})\pm 6\pi

where we have used the bounds
|\mathsf{W}|\leq 2\pi
and
|H_{n_{i}}|\leq\pi
.
Summing over
i
we find that for every
\underline{\xi}\in\Omega_{X}
,

\left|\xi\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\widetilde{f}}_{n}(\xi_{n},\xi_{n+1})\right|\leq\left|\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}H_{n}(\underline{L}_{n}^{\ast},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{\ast})\right|+10M\pi\leq 4T_{\delta}+40T_{\delta}\pi<42\pi T_{\delta}.

Setting
C_{\delta}:=42\pi T_{\delta}/\xi
, we find that
{\mathbb{P}}(\bigl|\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}{\widetilde{f}}_{n}^{(N)}\bigr|\geq C_{\delta})<\delta
for all
N
, whence the (center-)tightness of
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
.
\square

In chapter
5
we will need the following variant of the reduction lemma for integer valued
\mathsf{f}
.

Lemma 3.55
(Integer Reduction Lemma)

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a uniformly elliptic Markov chain, and
\mathsf{f}
an integer valued additive functional on
\mathsf{X}
s.t.
|f|\leq K
a.s. For every
N
,

f_{n}(x,y)=g_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)+a_{n}^{(N)}(x)-a_{n+1}^{(N)}(y)+c_{n}^{(N)}\ \ \ (n=1,\ldots,N)

where

(1)

c^{(N)}_{n}
are integers such that
|c^{(N)}_{n}|\leq K,

(2)

a_{n}^{(N)}
are measurable integer valued functions on
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
s.t.
|a^{(N)}_{n}|\leq 2K,

(3)

g_{n}^{(N)}
are measurable, integer valued, and
\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{N}\mathbb{E}[g_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{N+1})^{2}]\leq 10^{3}K^{4}\sum_{n=3}^{N}u_{n}^{2}
, with
u_{n}
the structure constants of
\mathsf{f}
.

Proof

Let
\left(Z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-1}&Y_{n}\\
Y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)
be a random hexagon.
By the definition of the structure constants,

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{n=3}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left(\Gamma\left(Z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-1}&Y_{n}\\
Y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)^{2}\bigg|Z_{n-2},Z_{n-1}\right)\right]=\sum_{n=3}^{N}u_{n}^{2}.

Therefore, for every
N
there exists
z_{n}=z_{n}(N)\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}

(n=1,\ldots,N-2)
such that

\sum_{n=3}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\Gamma\left(Z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-1}&Y_{n}\\
Y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)^{2}\bigg|Z_{n-2}=z_{n-2},Z_{n-1}=z_{n-1}\right)\right]\leq\sum_{n=3}^{N}u_{n}^{2}.

We emphasize that
z_{n}
depends on
N
.

Let
c^{(N)}_{n}:=f_{n-2}(z_{n-2},z_{n-1})
, and let
a_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n})
be the (smallest) most likely value of

f_{n-2}(z_{n-2},Y)+f_{n-1}(Y,x_{n}),

where
Y
has the bridge distribution of
X_{n-1}
conditioned on
X_{n-2}=z_{n-2}
and
X_{n}=x_{n}
.
The most likely value exists, and has probability bigger than

\delta_{K}:=\frac{1}{5K},

because
f_{n-2}(z_{n-2},Y)+f_{n-1}(Y,x_{n})\in[-2K,2K]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.

Set
g_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n},x_{n+1}):=f_{n}(x_{n},x_{n+1})+a_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n})-a_{n+1}^{(N)}(x_{n+1})-c^{(N)}_{n}.

Equivalently,
g_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n},x_{n+1})=-\Gamma\left(z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}z_{n-1}&y_{n}\\
y_{n-1}&x_{n}\end{array}\ x_{n+1}\right)
for the
y_{k}
which maximize the likelihood of the value
f_{k-1}(z_{k-1},Y)+f_{k}(Y,x_{k+1})
when
Y
has the bridge distribution of
X_{k}
given
X_{k-1}=z_{k-1},X_{k+1}=x_{k+1}
.

Our task is to estimate
\sum_{n=3}^{N}\mathbb{E}[g_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{n+1})^{2}]
. Define for this purpose
the functions
h_{n}^{(N)}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
,

h_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n},x_{n+1}):=\mathbb{E}\left(\Gamma\left(Z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-1}&Y_{n}\\
Y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)^{2}\bigg|\begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-2}=z_{n-2}&Z_{n-1}=z_{n-1}\\
X_{n}=x_{n}&X_{n+1}=x_{n+1}\end{array}\right)^{1/2},

Our plan is to show the following:

(a)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{N}\mathbb{E}(h_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{n+1})^{2})\leq\sum_{n=3}^{N}u_{n}^{2}

(b)

If
h_{n}^{(N)}(x_{n},x_{n+1})<\delta_{K}
, then
g^{(N)}_{n}(x_{n},x_{n+1})=0
.

(c)

\mathbb{E}(g^{(N)}_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})^{2})\leq(6K)^{2}{\mathbb{P}}[h_{n}^{(K)}\geq\delta_{k}]\leq 36K^{2}\delta_{K}^{-2}\mathbb{E}[h_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{n+1})^{2}]
.

Part (a) is because of the choice of
z_{n}
. To see part (b), note that since
\mathsf{f}
is integer valued, either the balance of a hexagon is zero, or it has absolute value
\geq 1
. Therefore, if
h_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{n+1})<\delta_{K}
, then necessarily

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Gamma\left(Z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-1}&Y_{n}\\
Y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)\neq 0\bigg|\begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-2}=z_{n-2}&Z_{n-1}=z_{n-1}\\
X_{n}=x_{n}&X_{n+1}=x_{n+1}\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Gamma\left(Z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-1}&Y_{n}\\
Y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)^{2}\bigg|\begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-2}=z_{n-2}&Z_{n-1}=z_{n-1}\\
X_{n}=x_{n}&X_{n+1}=x_{n+1}\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=h_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{n+1})^{2}<\delta_{K}^{2},

whence

{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Gamma\left(Z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-1}&Y_{n}\\
Y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)=0\bigg|\begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-2}=z_{n-2}&Z_{n-1}=z_{n-1}\\
X_{n}=x_{n}&X_{n+1}=x_{n+1}\end{array}\right]>1-\delta_{K}^{2}.

At the same time, by the structure of the distribution of random hexagons,

\Omega_{n}:=\left\{\left(Z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-1}&Y_{n}\\
Y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right):\begin{array}[]{l}f_{n-1}(Z_{n-1},Y_{n})+f_{n}(Y_{n},X_{n+1})=a_{n+1}^{(N)}(X_{n+1})\\
f_{n-2}(Z_{n-2},Y_{n-1})+f_{n-1}(Y_{n-1},X_{n})=a_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n})\end{array}\right\}

satisfies

{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Omega_{n}\bigg|\begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{n-2}=z_{n-2}&Z_{n-1}=z_{n-1}\\
X_{n}=x_{n}&X_{n+1}=x_{n+1}\end{array}\right]>\delta_{K}^{2},

If the sum of the probabilities of two events is bigger than one, then they must intersect. It follows that
there exist
y_{n-1},y_{n}
such that

\circ

a_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n})=f_{n-2}(z_{n-2},y_{n-1})+f_{n-1}(y_{n-1},X_{n})
;

\circ

a_{n+1}^{(N)}(X_{n+1})=f_{n-1}(z_{n-1},y_{n})+f_{n}(y_{n},X_{n+1})
;

\circ

\Gamma\left(z_{n-2}\ \begin{array}[]{ll}z_{n-1}&y_{n}\\
y_{n-1}&X_{n}\end{array}\ X_{n+1}\right)=0
.

By the definition of
g^{(N)}_{n}
, this implies that
g^{(N)}_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})=0
, which proves part (b).

Part (c) follows from part (b), Chebyshev’s inequality, and the estimate
\|g^{(N)}_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq 6K
(as is true for the balance of every hexagon).

\square

Combining Lemmas
3.52
and
3.55

we obtain the following result

Corollary 3.56

(Joint Reduction)

There is a constant
L=L({\varepsilon},K)
such that
under the conditions of the Reduction Lemma we can arrange, in addition to the other conclusions
of Lemma
3.52
, that
\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}\|g_{n}^{(N)}\|^{2}_{2}\leq LU_{N}.

Proof

Apply Lemma
3.52
and then apply Lemma
3.55
to the resulting integer
valued additive functional

\frac{\xi\mathsf{g}}{2\pi}
.
Notice that the reduction in this corollary depends on
N
even if
\mathsf{f}
is an additive functional of a Markov chain.

\square

Corollary
3.56
says the following. Suppose we have an additive functional

\mathsf{f}

such that both

U_{N}
is small and
D_{N}(\xi)
is small for some
\xi
(but
D_{N}(\xi)
can be much smaller
than
U_{N}
). Then we can adjust
\mathsf{f}
such that at time
N
, the resulting functional will have a small
norm as prescribed by
U_{N}
and small distance to
\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
as prescribed by

D_{N}
at the same time.

10.2
The possible values of the co-range

We prove Theorem
9.1
in its version for Markov arrays:
The co-range of an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
is equal to
\mathbb{R}
when
\mathsf{f}
is center tight, and to
\{0\}
or
t\mathbb{Z}

(t>0)
otherwise.

Recall that the co-range is defined by

H:=H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{\xi\in\mathbb{R}:\sup_{N}D_{N}(\xi)<\infty\},\text{ where }D_{N}(\xi)=\sum\limits_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}.

Step 1.

H
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

H=-H
, because
d_{n}^{(N)}(-\xi)=d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)
.

H\owns 0
, because
d_{n}^{(N)}(0)=0
.
H
is closed under addition, because if

\xi,\eta\in H
, then by Lemma
1.15
,

\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi+\eta)^{2}\leq 8\left[\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}+\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\eta)^{2}\right]<\infty.

Step 2.

If
\mathsf{f}
is center-tight, then
H=\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.
Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is center-tight.
By Corollary
2.31
and the center-tightness of
\mathsf{f}
,
\sup\limits_{N}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{k_{N}}(u_{k}^{(N)})^{2}<\infty
. By Lemma
1.15
(c),
\sup\limits_{N}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.

Step 3.

If
f
is not center-tight, then
\exists t_{0}
s.t.

H\cap(-t_{0},t_{0})=\{0\}.

(10.6)

Proof.
Let
K:=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
, then
|\Gamma(P)|\leq 6K
for a.e. hexagon
P
.

Fix
\tau_{0}>0
such that

|e^{it}-1|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}t^{2}\text{ for all }|t|<\tau_{0},

and let
t_{0}:=\tau_{0}(6K)^{-1}
.
Then
then for all
|\xi|<t_{0}
,

|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}-1|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\xi^{2}\Gamma(P)^{2}\text{ for all hexagons }P.

Taking the expectation over
P\in\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)
, we obtain that

d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\xi^{2}(u_{n}^{(N)})^{2}\text{ for all }|\xi|<t_{0},1\leq n\leq k_{N},N\geq 1.

(10.7)

Now assume by way of contradiction that there is

0\neq\xi\in H\cap(-t_{0},t_{0})
, then

\sup\limits_{N}\sum\limits_{n=3}^{k_{N}}(u_{n}^{(N)})^{2}\leq\frac{2}{\xi^{2}}\sup\limits_{N}\sum\limits_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty
.
By Corollary
2.31
,
f
is center-tight, in contradiction to our assumption.

Step 4.

If
f
is not center-tight, then
H=\{0\}
, or
H=t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\geq\frac{\pi}{6\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|}
.

Proof.
By steps 2 and 3,
H
is a proper closed subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
. So it must be equal to
\{0\}
or
t\mathbb{Z}
where
t>0
. To see that
t\geq\frac{\pi}{6\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|}
, assume by contradiction that
t=(\frac{\pi}{6\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|})\rho
with
0<\rho<1
, and let
\kappa:=\min\{|e^{iu}-1|^{2}/|u|^{2}:|u|\leq\pi\rho\}>0
. Then
|t\Gamma(P)|\leq 6t\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|=\pi\rho
for every position
n
hexagon
P
, whence

\displaystyle d_{n}^{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}(|e^{it\Gamma}-1|^{2})\geq\kappa\mathbb{E}(\Gamma^{2})=\kappa u_{n}^{2}.

This is impossible:
t\in H
so
\sum d_{n}^{2}(t)<\infty
, whereas
\mathsf{f}
is not center-tight so
\sum u_{n}^{2}=\infty
.

\Box

10.3
Calculation of the essential range

We prove Theorem
9.2
in its version for Markov arrays:
For every a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional
\mathsf{f}
on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
,

G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\begin{cases}\{0\}&H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}\\
\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}&H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\xi\mathbb{Z}\\
\mathbb{R}&H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{0\}.\end{cases}

(10.8)

Lemma 3.57

Suppose
\mathsf{f},\mathsf{g}
are two a.s. uniformly bounded additive functionals on the same uniformly elliptic Markov array.
If
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}
is center-tight, then
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{g}
have the same co-range.

Proof

By Corollary
2.31
, if
\mathsf{h}=\mathsf{g}-\mathsf{f}
is center-tight, then

\displaystyle\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}u_{n}^{(N)}(\mathsf{h})^{2}<\infty.

By Lemma
1.15
(b),(c),

\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{g})^{2}\leq 8\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi,\mathsf{f})^{2}+8\xi^{2}\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}u_{n}^{(N)}(\mathsf{h})^{2}.

So the co-range of
\mathsf{f}
is a subset of the co-range of
\mathsf{g}
.
By symmetry they are equal.
\square

Proof of Theorem
9.2
:
As we saw in the previous section, the possibilities for the co-range are

\mathbb{R}
,
t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
, and
\{0\}
.

Case 1:

The co-range equals
\mathbb{R}
.
As we saw above, this can only happen if
f
is center-tight, in which case the essential range is
\{0\}
because we may subtract
\mathsf{f}
from itself.

Case 2.:

The co-range equals
\xi\mathbb{Z}
with
\xi\neq 0
.
We show that
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
.

By assumption,
\xi
is in the co-range:
\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty
. By the Reduction Lemma,
\mathsf{f}
differs by a center-tight functional from a functional with algebraic range
\subseteq\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
. So
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})\subseteq\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
.

Assume by way of contradiction that
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})\subsetneq\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
, then there exists a center-tight
\mathsf{h}
such that the algebraic range of
\mathsf{g}:=\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{h}
is a subset of
\frac{2\pi\ell}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
for some integer
\ell>1
. The structure constants of
\mathsf{g}
must satisfy
d_{n}^{(N)}(\frac{\xi}{\ell},\mathsf{g})\equiv 0
, whence
\frac{\xi}{\ell}\in
co-range of
\mathsf{g}
. By Lemma
3.57
,
\frac{\xi}{\ell}\in
co-range of
\mathsf{f}
, whence
\frac{\xi}{\ell}\in\xi\mathbb{Z}
. But this contradicts
\ell>1
.

Case 3.:

The co-range equals
\{0\}
.
We claim that the essential range is
\mathbb{R}
. Otherwise, there exists a center-tight
\mathsf{h}
such that the algebraic range of
\mathsf{g}:=\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{h}
equals
t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
or
\{0\}
. But this is impossible:

(a)

If the algebraic range of
\mathsf{g}
is
t\mathbb{Z}
, then
d_{n}^{(N)}(\frac{2\pi}{t},\mathsf{g})=0
for all
3\leq n\leq k_{N}
,
N\geq 1
, so the co-range of
\mathsf{g}
contains
2\pi/t
. By Lemma
3.57
, the co-range of
\mathsf{f}
contains
2\pi/t
, in contradiction to the assumption that it is
\{0\}
.

(b)

If the algebraic range of
\mathsf{g}
is
\{0\}
, then
\mathsf{f}\equiv\mathsf{h}
, and
\mathsf{f}
is center-tight. But by Theorem
9.1
, the co-range of a center-tight functional is
\mathbb{R}
, whereas the co-range of our functional is
\{0\}
.
\Box

10.4
Existence of irreducible reductions

We prove Theorem
9.3
, in its version for Markov arrays:

For every a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
, there exists an irreducible functional
\mathsf{g}
such that
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}
is center-tight and

G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}).

Proof.
The essential range is a closed subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
, so
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},f)=\{0\},t\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}
.

(a)

If
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{0\}
, then
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathsf{f}
is center-tight. So take
\mathsf{g}\equiv 0
.

(b)

If
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},f)=t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
, then by Theorem
9.2
the co-range of
f
is
\xi\mathbb{Z}
with
\xi:=2\pi/t
. So
\sup\limits_{N}\sum\limits_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}({\xi},f)^{2}<\infty
.
By the reduction lemma, there exists an additive functional
\mathsf{g}
such that
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{g}
is center-tight, and
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})\subseteq t\mathbb{Z}
. By Lemma
3.57

G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})
, whence

G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})\subseteq G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})\subseteq t\mathbb{Z}=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, and
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

(c)

If
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},f)=\mathbb{R}
, take
\mathsf{g}:=\mathsf{f}
.
\Box

10.5
Proofs of results on hereditary arrays

Proof of Theorem
9.6
:
Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
.

The first part of the theorem asks for the equivalence of the following conditions:

(1)

\mathsf{f}
is hereditary

(2)

for all
\xi
,
\liminf\limits_{N\to\infty}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{k_{N}}d_{k}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty\Rightarrow\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{k_{N}}d_{k}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}<\infty

(3)

for all
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
,
D_{N}(\xi)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty

(4)

H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})=H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
for every sub-array
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{X}
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2):
Assume that
\mathsf{f}
is hereditary and
L_{\inf}(\xi):=\liminf D_{N}(\xi)<\infty
. We’ll show that
L_{\sup}(\xi):=\limsup D_{N}(\xi)<\infty
.
This is obvious for
\xi=0
, so suppose
\xi\neq 0
.

Choose
N_{\ell},M_{\ell}\uparrow\infty
such that

D_{N_{\ell}}(\xi)\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{}L_{\inf}(\xi)
,
D_{M_{\ell}}(\xi)\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{}L_{\sup}(\xi)
. Let

\mathsf{X}^{\prime}:=\{X^{(N_{\ell})}_{k}\}\text{ and }\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime}:=\{X^{(M_{\ell})}_{k}\}.

Since
L_{\inf}(\xi)<\infty
,
H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})
contains
\xi
, whence by (
10.8
),
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},f|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})\subseteq\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
. By the hereditary property,
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime}})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})\subseteq\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}
. This implies by (
10.8
) that
H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime\prime}})\owns\xi
, whence

L_{\sup}(\xi)<\infty
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3):
We assume that
L_{\inf}(\xi)<\infty\Rightarrow L_{\sup}(\xi)<\infty
and show that
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
for all
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.
If
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, then
\sup\limits_{N}D_{N}(\xi)=\infty
, so
L_{\sup}(\xi)=\infty
. By assumption, this forces
L_{\inf}(\xi)=\infty
, whence
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(4):

We assume that
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
for all
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, and show that
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})
for all sub-arrays
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}=\{X^{(N_{\ell})}_{n}\}
.
If
\xi\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, then
\sup\limits_{N}D_{N}(\xi)<\infty
, whence
\sup\limits_{\ell}D_{N_{\ell}}(\xi)<\infty
and
\xi\in H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})
. If
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, then
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
, whence
D_{N_{\ell}}(\xi)\to\infty
and
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})
.

(4)
\Rightarrow
(1):
We assume that
H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},f|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})=H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
for all sub-arrays
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
, and show that
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},f|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
for all sub-arrays. The inclusion
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},f|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})\subseteq G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is obvious, so we focus on
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},f|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})\supseteq G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

If
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})=\mathbb{R}
then there is nothing to prove.

Suppose
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})\neq\mathbb{R}
, then
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})=t\mathbb{Z}
for some
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\xi:=2\pi/t
when
t\neq 0
or any real number otherwise. By (
10.8
),

H(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})\owns\xi.

By assumption (4), this implies that
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})\owns\xi
, whence by (
10.8
),
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})\subseteq\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},f|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})
, and the proof of (1) is complete.

This finishes the proof that properties (1)–(4) are equivalent.

The second part of the theorem asks to show that
\mathsf{f}
is
stably
hereditary iff
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty

uniformly
on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary, then
\mathsf{f}
is hereditary, whence
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
for all
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
. To show that the convergence is uniform on compacts, we check that

\forall\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f}),\forall M>0,\exists N_{\xi},\delta_{\xi}>0\left(\begin{array}[]{l}N>N_{\xi}\\
|\xi^{\prime}-\xi|<\delta_{\xi}\end{array}\!\!\!\!\Rightarrow D_{N}(\xi^{\prime})>M\right).

(10.9)

Suppose this were false for some
\xi
and
M
, then

\exists\xi_{N}\to\xi\text{ such that }D_{N}(\xi_{N}){\leq}M.

But this implies that
\{(1+\epsilon_{N})f^{(N)}_{k}\}
is not hereditary for
\epsilon_{N}:=\frac{\xi_{N}}{\xi}-1
, in contradiction to our assumptions.

Conversely, if
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, and
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
, then
\{g^{(N)}_{k}\}=\{(1+\epsilon_{N})f^{(N)}_{k}\})
is hereditary, because for all
\xi\not\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
,

D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{g})\equiv D_{N}((1+\epsilon_{N})\xi,\mathsf{f})\to\infty
, and as we saw above (2)
\Rightarrow
(1).

\square

Proof of Theorem
9.7
:

The first part of the theorem assumes that
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z}
or
\{0\}
and that
\mathsf{f}
is hereditary, and asks to show that
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary.

We begin with several reductions.
It is sufficient to consider the case
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{Z}
: If
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
we work with
t^{-1}\mathsf{f}
, and if
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{0\}
then
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
and
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
(vacuously), so
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary by Theorem
9.6
.

Next we claim that it is enough to treat the special case
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{Z}
. Otherwise we use Theorem
9.3
to write
\mathsf{f}=\mathsf{g}-\mathsf{h}
where
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
and
\mathsf{h}
is center-tight. By Lemma
3.57
,
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})=H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, and by Lemma
1.15
and Corollary
2.31
,

D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{f})\geq\frac{1}{8}D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{g})-\frac{1}{8}\xi^{2}\sup_{n}\sum_{k=3}^{k_{n}+1}u_{k}^{(n)}(\mathsf{h})^{2}=\frac{1}{8}D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{g})-O(1).

Thus, if
D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{g})\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{g})
, then
D_{N}(\xi,\mathsf{f})\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

By assumption,
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|\leq K
for some integer
K
. Then for every hexagon
P\in\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)
,

\Gamma(P)\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[-6K,6K]
.

Let
m^{(N)}_{n}
denote the probability measure on the space of hexagons
\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)
and define for every
\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}\cap[-6K,6K]
,

\mu_{N}(\{\gamma\}):=\sum\limits_{n=3}^{k_{N}}m^{(N)}_{n}\{P\in\mathrm{Hex}(N,n):\Gamma(P)=\gamma\}.

Using the identity
|e^{i\xi\gamma}-1|^{2}=4\sin^{2}\frac{\xi\gamma}{2}
, we see that

d_{N}^{2}(\xi)=4\sum_{\gamma=-6K}^{6K}\mu_{N}(\gamma)\sin^{2}\frac{\xi\gamma}{2}.

Since
\mathsf{f}
is hereditary,
D_{N}\to\infty
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
,
and the expression for
d_{N}^{2}(\xi)
shows that if
D_{N}\to\infty
at
\xi
, then
D_{N}\to\infty
uniformly on an open neighborhood of
\xi
.

It follows that
D_{N}\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.
By Theorem
9.6
,
f
must be stably hereditary. This is the first part of the theorem.

The second part of the theorem says that
if
\mathsf{f}
is integer valued and not center-tight, and if
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|\leq K
, then
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=k\mathbb{Z}

for some integer
0<k\leq 12K
.

To see this recall that
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})\subset G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})\subset\mathbb{Z}
, whence

G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=k\mathbb{Z}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\mathsf{f}
is not center-tight,
k\neq 0
. By (
10.8
),
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\frac{2\pi}{k}\mathbb{Z}
.

The inequality
|f|\leq K
implies that every hexagon
P
has balance
|\Gamma(P)|\leq 6K
. This implies that
k\leq 12K
: Otherwise
|\frac{2\pi\Gamma(P)}{k}|<0.95\pi
and (
10.2
) gives

|e^{(2\pi i/k)\Gamma(P)}-1|^{2}\geq\mathrm{const}\;\Gamma(P)^{2}.

But this implies that
d_{n}^{(N)}(\frac{2\pi}{k})\geq\mathrm{const}\;u_{n}^{(N)}
, whence

\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\frac{2\pi}{k})^{2}\geq\sup_{N}\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}(u_{n}^{(N)})^{2}=\infty\text{ by non-center-tightness}.

This contradicts
\frac{2\pi}{k}\in H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
. Thus
0<k\leq 12K
.

It follows from the first part of the theorem and from Theorem
9.6
, that if
\mathsf{f}
is integer valued and not center-tight, then the properties of being hereditary and of being stably hereditary are equivalent.
\square

11
Notes and references

In the stationary world, a center-tight cocycle is a coboundary (Schmidt
Schmidt-Cocycles
) and the problems discussed in this chapter reduce to the question how small can one make the range of a cocycle by subtracting from it a coboundary. The question appears naturally in the ergodic theory of group actions, because of its relation to the ergodic decomposition of skew-products
(
Aaronson-Book
, chapter 8)
,
Schmidt-Cocycles
,
Conze-Raugi-Ergodic-Decomp
, and to the structure of locally finite ergodic invariant measures for skew-products
ANSS
,
Sa-horocycle
,
Raugi
. In the general setup of ergodic theory, minimal reductions such as in Theorem
9.3
are not always possible
Lem
, although they do sometime exist
Sa-horocycle
,
Raugi
.

The relevance of (ir)reducibility to the local limit theorem appears in different form in the papers of
Guivarc’h & Hardy
GH
, Aaronson & Denker
Aaronson-Denker-LLT
, and Dolgopyat
D-Ind
. There “irreducibility” is expressed in terms of a condition which rules out non-trivial solutions for certain cohomological equations.

It is more difficult to uncover the irreducibility condition in the probabilistic literature on the LLT for sums of independent random variables. Rozanov’s paper
Rozanov
, for example, proves a LLT for independent
\mathbb{Z}
-valued random variables

X_{k}
assuming Lindeberg’s condition (which is automatic for bounded random variables),

\sum{\rm Var}(X_{k})=\infty
, and subject to the assumption that

\prod_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\max_{0\leq m<t}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m\ \mathrm{mod}\ t)\right)=0\text{ for all integers }t\geq 2.

(11.1)

Let
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{k}\}
and
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{k}\}
where
f_{k}(x)=x
. Clearly, (
11.1
) implies that
G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{Z}
.
We claim that (
11.1
) is equivalent to the irreducibility:
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{Z}
.

To see why, it is useful first to note that
(
11.1
) is equivalent to

\sum_{k}{\mathbb{P}}[X_{k}\neq m_{k}\;\mathrm{mod}\;t]=\infty

(11.2)

where
m_{k}
is the (smallest) most likely residue mod
t
for
X_{k}.

Irreducibility
\Rightarrow
Rozanov’s condition:

Define for
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
2\leq t\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\{x\}_{t\mathbb{Z}}:=t\{x/t\}
,
[x]_{t\mathbb{Z}}:=x-\{x\}_{t\mathbb{Z}}
, and set

\circ

y_{k}(x):=\text{the (smallest) integer in $m_{k}+t\mathbb{Z}$ closest to $x$}

\circ

z_{k}(x):=x-y_{k}(x)

\circ

g_{k}(x):=(y_{k}(x)-m_{k})+[x-y_{k}(x)]_{t\mathbb{Z}}
(
g_{k}
takes values in
t\mathbb{Z}
)

\circ

h_{k}(x):=\{x-y_{k}(x)\}_{t\mathbb{Z}}
(
h_{k}
takes values in
\mathbb{Z}
). Then

X_{k}=g_{k}(X_{k})+h_{k}(X_{k})+m_{k}.

The algebraic range of
g_{k}
is inside
t\mathbb{Z}
, and by the Borel-Cantelli Lemma,

\eqref{Rozanov2}\text{ fails}\Leftrightarrow X_{k}\neq m_{k}\mod t\mathbb{Z}\text{ finitely often a.s.}\Leftrightarrow h_{k}(X_{k})\neq 0\text{ finitely often a.s. }

If (
11.2
) fails, then
\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}h_{k}(X_{k})
converges a.s.
(since a.s. there are only finitely non-zero terms).
Hence
\mathsf{h}
is center-tight. Since
G_{alg}(\mathsf{g})\subset t\mathbb{Z}
, we have a contradiction to irreducibility.

Rozanov’s condition
\Rightarrow
irreducibility:

Fix
\theta\in[0,t)
and let
m
be the closest integer in
[0,t)\cap\mathbb{Z}
to
\theta
. Then
|m^{\prime}-\theta|\geq\frac{1}{2}
for
m^{\prime}\neq m
, whence

\mathbb{E}[{\rm dist}^{2}(X_{n},\theta+t\mathbb{Z})]\geq\frac{1}{4}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}\neq m\,\mathrm{mod}\,t)\geq\frac{1}{4}[1-\max_{0\leq m<t}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}=m\ \mathrm{mod}\ t)].

Passing to the infimum over
\theta
, we obtain that

\mathfrak{D}^{2}(X_{n},\tfrac{2\pi}{t})\geq\frac{1}{4}[1-\max_{0\leq m<t}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}=m\ \mathrm{mod}\ t)].

(See §
3
.) We now obtain from Proposition
1.19
that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}d_{n}^{2}(\tfrac{2\pi}{t})\geq const\sum_{n=3}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathfrak{D}^{2}(X_{n-1},\tfrac{2\pi}{t})+\mathfrak{D}^{2}(X_{n},\tfrac{2\pi}{t})\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq const\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\left(1-\max_{0\leq m<t}{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}=m\ \mathrm{mod}\ t)\right)=\infty,\text{ by \eqref{Rozanov2}}.

We find that the co-range does
not
contain
2\pi/t
for
t=2,3,4,\ldots
. We already know that the co-range does contain
2\pi
(because
X_{k}
are integer valued). The only closed sub-group of
\mathbb{R}
with these properties is
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. So the co-range is
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, and the essential range is
\mathbb{Z}=
the algebraic range

Other sufficient conditions for the LLT for sums of independent random variables such as those appearing in
Mineka-Silverman
,
Statulevicius-Sums-of-Independent
and
Mukhin-1991
can be analyzed in a similar way.
The reduction lemma was proved for sums of independent random variables in
D-Ind
. A version of Theorem
9.5
for sums of independent random variables appears in
Mukhin-1991
.

Chapter 4
The local limit theorem in the irreducible case

In this chapter we prove the local limit theorem for
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))

when
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(S_{N})}}
converges to a finite limit and
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible. In this regime, the asymptotic behavior of
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))
does not to depend on the details of
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{f}
(“universality”).

12
Main results

12.1
Local limit theorems for Markov chains

In the next two theorems, we assume that
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov
chain

\mathsf{X}
, and we let
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}
,
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}
,
S_{N}=f_{1}(X_{1},X_{2})+\cdots+f_{N}(X_{N},X_{N+1})
, and
V_{N}:=\mathrm{Var}(S_{N})
.
We make no assumptions on the initial distribution and allow
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}_{x}={\mathbb{P}}(\ \cdot\ |X_{1}=x)
.

Theorem 12.1

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible, with algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
. Then
V_{N}\to\infty
, and for every interval
(a,b)
and
z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
converges to a finite limit
z
,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]=[1+o(1)]\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}(b-a),\text{ as }N\to\infty.

(12.1)

Theorem 12.2

Suppose
t>0
and
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible with algebraic range
t\mathbb{Z}
. Then
V_{N}\to\infty
and there are constants
0\leq\gamma_{N}<t
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and for all
z_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
converges to a finite limit
z
,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}=kt]=[1+o(1)]\;\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}t}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}},\hskip 11.74988pt\text{as}\hskip 11.74988ptN\to\infty.

(12.2)

The constants
\gamma_{N}
are determined by the condition
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}]=1
for all
N
.

The conditions of the theorems can be checked from the data of
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{f}
using the structure constants
d_{n}(\xi)
from §
3
:

Lemma 4.58

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
. Then

(1)

\mathsf{f}
is non-lattice and irreducible iff
\sum d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\infty
for all
\xi\neq 0
.

(2)

\mathsf{f}
is lattice and irreducible with algebraic range
t\mathbb{Z}
,
t>0
, iff
\sum d_{n}^{2}(\xi)<\infty
for
\xi\in(2\pi/t)\mathbb{Z}
and
\sum d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\infty
for
\xi\not\in(2\pi/t)\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

\mathsf{f}
is lattice and irreducible with algebraic range
\{0\}
iff
f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
are a.s. constant for all
n
.

Proof

\mathsf{f}
is non-lattice and irreducible iff
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
. By Theorem
9.1
, this happens iff
\mathsf{f}
has co-range
\{0\}
, which proves part (1). Part (2) is proved in a similar way, and part (3) is a triviality.
\square

12.2
Local limit theorems for Markov arrays

In this section, we assume that
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, and we let
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}^{(N)}\}
,
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}^{(N)}\}
,
S_{N}=\sum_{i=1}^{k_{N}}f_{i}^{(N)}(X_{i}^{(N)},X_{i+1}^{(N)})
, and
V_{N}:=\mathrm{Var}(S_{N})
. We make no assumptions on the initial distribution, and allow
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}={\mathbb{P}}(\ \cdot\ |X_{1}^{(N)}=x_{1}^{(N)})
.

The LLT for
S_{N}
may fail due to the possibility that
\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}}
may have different essential range for different sub-arrays
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
. To deal with this we need to assume hereditary behavior, see §
9.3
.

Theorem
12.1
’.

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
