is stably hereditary, non-lattice and irreducible. Then
V_{N}\to\infty
, and for every interval
(a,b)
and
z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}z\in\mathbb{R}
,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]=[1+o(1)]\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}(b-a),\text{ as }N\to\infty.

(12.3)

Theorem
12.2
’.

Suppose
t>0
and
\mathsf{f}
is hereditary, irreducible, and with algebraic range
t\mathbb{Z}
. Then
V_{N}\to\infty
, and there are

0\leq\gamma_{N}<t
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
z_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}z\in\mathbb{R}
,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}=kt]=[1+o(1)]\;\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}t}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}},\hskip 11.74988pt\text{as}\hskip 11.74988ptN\to\infty.

(12.4)

The constants
\gamma_{N}
are determined by the condition
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}]=1
for all
N
.

Notice that whereas in the non-lattice case we had to assume that
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary, in the lattice case it is sufficient to assume that
\mathsf{f}
is hereditary. This is because in the lattice case the two assumptions are equivalent, see Theorem
9.7
.

Again, it is possible to check the assumptions of the theorems from the data of
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{f}
using the structure constants:

Lemma
4.58
’.

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov array
\mathsf{X}
with row lengths
k_{N}+1
. Let
d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)
be as §
3
, then

(1)

\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary, irreducible, and with algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
iff

\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty\text{ uniformly on compacts in $\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}$}.

(2)

Suppose
t\neq 0
, then

\mathsf{f}
is hereditary and irreducible with algebraic range
t\mathbb{Z}
if and only if

\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty\text{ for all }\xi\not\in\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}.
In this case
\mathsf{f}
is also stably hereditary.

Proof

As in the case of Markov chains,
\mathsf{f}
is non-lattice and irreducible iff its co-range equals
\{0\}
. By Theorem
9.6
,
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary iff
\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty
uniformly on compacts in
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, which proves part (1).

Part (2) is proved in a similar way, with the additional observation that thanks to Theorem
9.7
, in the irreducible lattice case, every hereditary additive functional is automatically stably hereditary.

\square

12.3
Mixing local limit theorems

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an additive functional on a Markov
\mathsf{X}
with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, and state spaces
(\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n},\mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}))
. Let
S_{N}
and
V_{N}
be as in the previous section.

Theorem 12.3
(Mixing LLT)

Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is a uniformly elliptic Markov array, and
\mathsf{f}
is an additive functional on
\mathsf{X}
which is stably hereditary, a.s. uniformly bounded, and irreducible. Let
\mathfrak{A}_{N}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}
be measurable events such that
{\mathbb{P}}[X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N}]
is bounded away from zero, and let
x_{N}\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}
. Then for every
\phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
continuous with compact support,

(1)

Non-lattice case:
Suppose
\mathsf{f}
has algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
. For every
z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}[\phi(S_{N}-z_{N})|X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N},X^{(N)}_{1}=x_{N}]=\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi(u)du.

(2)

Lattice case:
Suppose
\mathsf{f}
has algebraic range
t\mathbb{Z}
(
t>0
) and
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}]=1
for all
N
. For every
z_{N}\in\gamma_{N}+t\mathbb{Z}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}[\phi(S_{N}-z_{N})|X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N},X^{(N)}_{1}=x_{N}]=\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|t|}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{u\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi(tu).

To understand what this means, think of
\phi\approx 1_{(a,b)}
.

In the next chapter, we will use
mixing LLT for
irreducible
additive functionals to study the LLT for some
reducible
additive functionals, as follows. Suppose

\mathsf{f}=\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}+\nabla h
, where
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible and
\mathsf{h}
is uniformly bounded. Then

S_{N}(\mathsf{f})={S}_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})+h_{1}^{(N)}(X_{1}^{(N)})-h_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}(X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}).

To pass from the LLT for
S_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})
(which we know since
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
is irreducible) to the LLT for
S_{N}({\mathsf{f}})
(which we do not know because of the reducibility of
\mathsf{f}
), we need to understand
the
joint
distribution of
{S}_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})
,
h_{1}^{(N)}(X_{1}^{(N)})
and
h_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}(X_{k_{N}+1})
.
This is the task achieved by the mixing LLT.

13
Proofs

We will provide the proofs in the general context of Markov arrays.

Standing assumptions and notation for the remainder of the chapter:

\mathsf{X}=\{X^{(N)}_{n}\}
is a Markov array with row lengths
k_{N}+1
, state spaces
\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}
, and transition probabilities
\pi^{(N)}_{n,n+1}(x,dy)
, and
\mathsf{f}=\{f^{(N)}_{n}\}
is an additive functional on
\mathsf{X}
. As always,
d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)
are the structure constants of
\mathsf{f}
.

We assume that
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|<K<\infty
, and that
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic with ellipticity constant
\epsilon_{0}
.
By the uniform ellipticity assumption,

\pi_{n,n+1}^{(N)}(x,dy)=p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(dy)

with
0\leq p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)<\epsilon_{0}^{-1}
such that
\int p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)p_{n+1}^{(N)}(y,z)\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(dy)>\epsilon_{0}
.
There is no loss of generality in assuming that
\mu_{k}^{(N)}(E)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{k}^{(N)}\in E)
, see Proposition
1.12
and the discussion which follows it.

13.1
Characteristic functions

The classical approach to limit theorems in probability theory, due to P. Lévy, is to apply the Fourier transform, and analyze the
characteristic functions
of the random variables in the problem. In our case the relevant characteristic functions are:

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left(e^{i\xi S_{N}}\right)\equiv{\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{i\xi S_{N}}|X^{(N)}_{1}=x\right).

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(x,\xi|\mathfrak{A})

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left(e^{i\xi S_{N}}|X_{k_{N}+1}\in\mathfrak{A}\right)\equiv{\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{i\xi S_{N}}|X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{A},X^{(N)}_{1}=x\right).

Here
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}
,
\mathfrak{A}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}
,
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathbb{E}_{x}(\cdot)=\mathbb{E}(\ \cdot\ |X^{(N)}_{1}=x)
.

We write these functions in terms of
perturbation operators
as in
N
.
For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
1\leq n\leq k_{N}+1
, define
\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}^{(N)}:L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N)})\to L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{n}^{(N)})
by

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}^{(N)}v\right)(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}^{(N)}}p_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)e^{i\xi f_{n}^{(N)}(x,y)}v(y)d\mu_{n+1}^{(N)}(y)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathbb{E}\bigl(e^{i\xi f_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n}^{(N)},X_{n+1}^{(N)})}v(X_{n+1}^{(N)})|X_{n}^{(N)}=x\bigr).

Lemma 4.59
(Nagaev)

Let
1(\cdot)\equiv 1
, then the following identities hold:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{i\xi S_{N}}v(X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)})\bigg|X^{(N)}_{1}=x\right)=\left(\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{2,\xi}^{(N)}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k_{N},\xi}^{(N)}v\right)(x),

(13.1)

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(x,\xi)=\left(\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{2,\xi}^{(N)}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k_{N},\xi}^{(N)}1\right)(x),

(13.2)

\displaystyle\Phi_{N}(x,\xi|\mathfrak{A})=\frac{\left(\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{2,\xi}^{(N)}\dots\mathcal{L}_{N,\xi}^{(N)}1_{\mathfrak{A}}\right)(x)}{{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{A}]}.

(13.3)

Proof

\mathbb{E}(e^{i\xi S_{N}}v(X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)})\big|X^{(N)}_{1}=x)=

\int p_{1}^{(N)}(x,y)e^{i\xi f_{1}^{(N)}(x,y)}{\mathbb{E}}\bigl(e^{i\xi\sum_{n=2}^{N}f_{n}^{(N)}}v|X^{(N)}_{2}=y\bigr)d\mu_{2}^{(N)}(y).

Proceeding by induction, we obtain (
13.1
), and
(
13.1
) implies (
13.2
),(
13.3
).

\square

Let
\|\cdot\|
denote the operator norm on
\mathrm{Hom}(L^{\infty},L^{\infty})
.

Lemma 4.60

\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}^{(N)}
are bounded linear operators, and there is a positive constant
{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}
which only depends on
{\varepsilon}_{0}
such that for all
N\geq 1
and
5\leq n\leq k_{N}
,
\|\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}^{(N)}\|\leq 1
, and

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{n-4,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{n-3,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{n-2,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{n-1,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}^{(N)}\right\|\leq e^{-{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}}.

Proof

Throughout this proof we fix
N
and drop the superscripts
(N)
, and we use the notation
x_{i},z_{i}
etc. to denote points in
\mathfrak{S}_{i}=\mathfrak{S}_{i}^{(N)}
.

It is clear that
\|\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}^{(N)}\|\leq 1
. To estimate the norm of

\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{L}_{n-4,\xi}\mathcal{L}_{n-3,\xi}\mathcal{L}_{n-2,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{n-1,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}:L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{n+1})\to L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{n-4}),

we represent this operator as an integral operator, and analyze the kernel.
Let

\circ

\displaystyle p(x_{k},\ldots,x_{m}):=\prod_{i=k}^{m-1}p_{i}(x_{i},x_{i+1}),

\circ

\displaystyle f(x_{k},\ldots,x_{m}):=\sum_{i=k}^{m-1}f_{i}(x_{i},x_{i+1}),

\circ

L(x_{n-4},z_{n+1})=

\displaystyle=\hskip-17.07182pt\int\limits_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-3}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\hskip-17.07182ptp(x_{n-4},z_{n-3},\ldots,z_{n+1})e^{i\xi f(x_{n-4},z_{n-3},\ldots,z_{n+1})}\mu_{n-3}(dz_{n-3})\cdots\mu_{n}(dz_{n}).

Then

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}v)(x_{n-4})=\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}\biggl[L(x_{n-4},z_{n+1})v(z_{n+1})\biggr]\mu_{n+1}(dz_{n+1})
, whence

\|\mathcal{L}v\|_{\infty}\leq\|v\|_{\infty}\sup_{x_{n-4}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-4}}\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}|L(x_{n-4},z_{n+1})|\mu_{n+1}(dz_{n+1}).

To estimate this integral we change the order of integration:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}|L(x_{n-4},z_{n+1})|\mu_{n+1}(dz_{n+1})\leq\iint\limits_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}\Bigg[|K_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})|

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\left.\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-3}}p(x_{n-4},z_{n-3},z_{n-2})\mu_{n-3}(dz_{n-3})\right]\mu_{n-2}(dz_{n-2})\mu_{n+1}(dz_{n+1}),

(13.4)

where
K_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1}):=

\displaystyle\hskip-8.5359pt\iint\limits_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n}}\hskip-5.69046ptp(z_{n-2},z_{n-1},z_{n},z_{n+1})e^{i\xi f(z_{n-2},z_{n-1},z_{n},z_{n+1})}\mu_{n-1}(dz_{n-1})\mu_{n}(dz_{n}).

Claim:

Let
p(z_{n-2}\to z_{n+1}):={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n+1}=z_{n+1}|X_{n-2}=z_{n-2})
, then

\displaystyle|K_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n-1})|\leq p(z_{n-2}\to z_{n+1})-

(13.5)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}p(z_{n-2}\to z_{n+1})\mathbb{E}\biggl(|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}-1|^{2}\bigg|{\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-2}=Y_{n-2}=z_{n-2}\\
X_{n+1}=Z_{n+1}=z_{n+1}\end{array}}\biggr).

Proof of the claim.

Set

\widetilde{K}_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1}):=\frac{K_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})}{p(z_{n-2}\to z_{n+1})},

then

\widetilde{K}_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})=\mathbb{E}\biggl(e^{i\xi\sum_{k=n-2}^{n}f_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1})}\bigg|{\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-2}=z_{n-2}\\
X_{n+1}=z_{n+1}\end{array}}\biggr).

Writing
|\widetilde{K}_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})|^{2}=\widetilde{K}_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})\overline{\widetilde{K}_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})}
, we find that

\displaystyle|\widetilde{K}_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left(e^{i\xi\Gamma\bigl({\tiny X_{n-2}\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-1}\\
Y_{n-1}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n}\\
Y_{n}\end{array}X_{n+1}}\bigr)}\bigg|{\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-2}=Y_{n-2}=z_{n-2}\\
X_{n+1}=Z_{n+1}=z_{n+1}\end{array}}\right),

where
\{Y_{n}\}
is an independent copy of
\{X_{n}\}
, and
\Gamma
is as in (
3.1
).

The imaginary part is necessarily zero, so writing
P=\bigl({X_{n-2}\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-1}\\
Y_{n-1}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n}\\
Y_{n}\end{array}X_{n+1}}\bigr)
we have by the identity
1-\cos\alpha=\frac{1}{2}|e^{i\alpha}-1|^{2}
that

\displaystyle|\widetilde{K}_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n-1})|^{2}=1-\mathbb{E}\bigl(1-\cos(\xi\Gamma(P))|{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-2}=Y_{n-2}=z_{n-2}\\
X_{n+1}=Z_{n+1}=z_{n+1}\end{array}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv 1-\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\bigl(|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}-1|^{2}|{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-2}=Y_{n-2}=z_{n-2}\\
X_{n+1}=Z_{n+1}=z_{n+1}\end{array}}\bigr).

The claim follows, since
\sqrt{1-t}\leq 1-\frac{t}{2}
for all
0\leq t\leq 1
.

We now substitute (
13.5
) in (
13.4
). The result is a difference of two terms:

(a)

The first term is obtained by replacing
K_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})
in (
13.4
) by
p(z_{n-2}\to z_{n+1})
. It has the following
upper
bound:

\iint_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-3}}p(x_{n-4},z_{n-3},z_{n-2})p(z_{n-2}\to z_{n+1})=1.

(b)

The second term is obtained by replacing
K_{n}(z_{n-2},z_{n+1})
in (
13.4
) by

\frac{1}{4}p(z_{n-2}\to z_{n+1})\mathbb{E}\bigl(|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}-1|^{2}|{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}X_{n-2}=Y_{n-2}=z_{n-2}\\
X_{n+1}=Z_{n+1}=z_{n+1}\end{array}}\bigr).

The inner-most integral satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n-3}}p(x_{n-4},z_{n-3},z_{n-2})\mu_{n-3}(dz_{n-3})\geq\epsilon_{0}

because of uniform ellipticity. This leads to the following
lower
bound for the second term:

\frac{1}{4}\epsilon_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\bigl(|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}-1|^{2}\bigr)=\frac{1}{4}\epsilon_{0}^{2}d_{n}(\xi)^{2}.

In total we get:
\int|L(x_{n-4},z_{n+1})|\mu_{n+1}(dz_{n+1})\leq 1-\widetilde{\epsilon}d_{n}(\xi)^{2}
, where

\widetilde{\epsilon}:=\frac{1}{4}\epsilon_{0}^{2}.

Since
1-t\leq e^{-t}
, we are done.
\square

Recall that

D_{N}(\xi)=\sum_{n=3}^{k_{N}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}
.
Write

\displaystyle D_{N}=\sum_{j=0}^{4}D_{j,N}
where

D_{j,N}(\xi)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}3\leq n\leq k_{N}\\
n\equiv j\text{ mod }5\end{subarray}}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi)^{2}.

Applying Lemma
4.60
iteratively we conclude that there is a constant
C
independent of
N
s.t. for all
N
,

\left|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi)\right|\leq Ce^{-{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}\max\left(D_{0,N},\ldots,D_{4,N}\right)}\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{5}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}D_{N}(\xi)}.

(13.6)

If
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{A})\geq{\overline{\delta}}
then by (
13.3
),

|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi|\mathfrak{A})|\leq{\overline{\delta}}^{-1}\|\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}^{(N)}\mathcal{L}_{2,\xi}^{(N)}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k_{N},\xi}^{(N)}1_{\mathfrak{A}}\|

whence

\left|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi|\mathfrak{A})\right|\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{5}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}D_{N}(\xi)}.

(13.7)

The next result shows that if
u_{n}^{(N)}
is big,
then
d_{n}^{N}(\cdot)
cannot be small at two nearby points. Recall the standing assumption
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup\|f^{(N)}_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq K
,
and the definition of the structure constants
u_{n}^{(N)}
in (
3.2
).

Lemma 4.61

\exists{\widetilde{\delta}}={\widetilde{\delta}}(K)>0
s.t. if
|\delta|\leq{\widetilde{\delta}}
then for all
3\leq n\leq k_{N}
,

\displaystyle d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi+\delta)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{3}\delta^{2}\left(u_{n}^{(N)}\right)^{2}-2|\delta|u_{n}^{(N)}d_{n}^{(N)}(\xi).

(13.8)

Proof

Fix a hexagon
P={\left(x_{n-2}\begin{array}[]{l}x_{n-1}\\
y_{n-1}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}x_{n}\\
y_{n}\end{array}y_{n+1}\right)}\in\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)
, and let

\mathfrak{u}_{n}:=\Gamma(P)\ ,\ \mathfrak{d}_{n}(\xi):=|e^{i\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n}}-1|,

then
the identity
|e^{i\theta}-1|^{2}=2(1-\cos\theta)
implies

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{2}(\xi+\delta)=|e^{i(\xi+\delta)\mathfrak{u}_{n}}-1|^{2}=2[1-\cos((\xi+\delta)\mathfrak{u}_{n})]

\displaystyle=2[1-\cos(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n})\cos(\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n})+\sin(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n})\sin(\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n})]

\displaystyle=2[(1-\cos(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n}))\cos(\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n})+(1-\cos(\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n}))+\sin(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n})\sin(\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n})]

(13.9)

\displaystyle\geq 2\bigl[(1-\cos(\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n}))-|\sin(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n})\sin(\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n})|\bigr]\text{ provided }|{\widetilde{\delta}}|<\frac{\pi}{12K},

because in this case
|\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n}|<\frac{\pi}{2}
, so
\cos(\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n})\geq 0
. Make
{\widetilde{\delta}}
even smaller to guarantee

0\leq|t|\leq 6K{\widetilde{\delta}}\Rightarrow\tfrac{1}{3}t^{2}\leq 1-\cos t\leq t^{2}
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{n}^{2}(\xi+\delta)\geq 2\bigl(\tfrac{1}{3}\delta^{2}\mathfrak{u}_{n}^{2}-|\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n}|\sqrt{1-\cos^{2}(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n})}\bigr)

\displaystyle=2\biggl(\tfrac{1}{3}\delta^{2}\mathfrak{u}_{n}^{2}-|\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n}|\sqrt{(1-\cos(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n}))(1+\cos(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n}))}\biggr)

\displaystyle\geq 2\biggl(\tfrac{1}{3}\delta^{2}\mathfrak{u}_{n}^{2}-|\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n}|\sqrt{2(1-\cos(\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n}))}\biggr)=\tfrac{2}{3}\delta^{2}\mathfrak{u}_{n}^{2}-2|\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n}||e^{i\xi\mathfrak{u}_{n}}-1|

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{3}\delta^{2}\mathfrak{u}_{n}^{2}-2|\delta\mathfrak{u}_{n}|\mathfrak{d}_{n}(\xi).

Integrating on
P\in\mathrm{Hex}(N,n)
, and using Cauchy-Schwarz to estimate the second term
we obtain the lower bound for
d_{n}(\xi+\delta)^{2}
.
\square

Lemma
4.61
and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality together give

\displaystyle D_{N}(\xi+\delta)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{3}\delta^{2}U_{N}-2|\delta|\sqrt{U_{N}D_{N}(\xi)}

(13.10)

where
\displaystyle U_{n}:=\sum_{k=3}^{k_{N}}(u_{k}^{(N)})^{2}
.
If
V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N})\to\infty
, then as soon as
V_{N}>2C_{2}

where
C_{2}
is the constant from Theorem
5.1
, we have

\frac{U_{N}}{2C_{1}}\leq V_{N}\leq 2C_{1}U_{N}.

(13.11)

So there are
{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}_{1},{\widehat{c}}_{1}>0
s.t.

D_{N}(\xi+\delta)\geq{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}_{1}\delta^{2}V_{N}-{\widehat{c}}_{1}|\delta|\sqrt{V_{N}D_{N}(\xi)}.

By (
13.6
), there are
{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}},{\widehat{c}}>0
s.t. for all
N
so large that
V_{N}>2C_{2}
, for all
\xi
and
|\delta|<{\widetilde{\delta}}

\left|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi+\delta)\right|\leq C\exp\left(-{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}V_{N}\delta^{2}+{\widehat{c}}|\delta|\sqrt{V_{N}D_{N}(\xi)}\right).

(13.12)

We rephrase (
13.12
) as follows. Given a compact interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
, let

A_{N}(I):=-\log\sup_{(x,\xi)\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}\times I}\bigl|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi)\bigr|

(13.13)

and choose some pair
({\widetilde{x}}_{N},{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}\times I
such that

A_{N}(I)\leq-\log|\Phi_{N}({\widetilde{x}}_{N},{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})|\leq A_{N}(I)+\ln 2.

So
|\Phi({\widetilde{x}}_{N},{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})|\geq\frac{1}{2}e^{-A_{N}(I)}=\frac{1}{2}\sup|\Phi_{N}(\cdot,\cdot)|
on
\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}\times I
.

Corollary 4.62

For each
{\overline{\delta}}
there are
{\widetilde{C}},{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}},{\overline{c}}>0
s.t. for every compact interval
I
s.t.
|I|\leq{\widetilde{\delta}}
,
for all
N

for every
(x,\xi)\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}\times I
,
for
every
\mathfrak{A}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}
s.t.
\mu_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}(\mathfrak{A})\geq{\overline{\delta}}
,

\displaystyle\left|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi)\right|\leq{\widetilde{C}}\exp\left(-{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}V_{N}(\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})^{2}+{\overline{c}}|\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N}|\sqrt{V_{N}A_{N}(I)}\right);

\displaystyle\left|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi|\mathfrak{A})\right|\leq{\widetilde{C}}\exp\left(-{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}V_{N}(\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})^{2}+{\overline{c}}|\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N}|\sqrt{V_{N}A_{N}(I)}\right).

Proof

We only give the proof in the case
V_{N}
is large, so that (
13.12
) holds.
This is the case we need. We remark that the result also holds generally, because
the estimate we seek is trivial when
V_{N}
is small.

Applying (
13.12
) with
{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N}

instead of
\xi
and
\delta=\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N}

gives

|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi)|\leq C\exp\left(-{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}V_{N}(\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})^{2}+{\widehat{c}}|{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N}-\xi|\sqrt{V_{N}D_{N}({\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})}\right).

By (
13.6
),

e^{-A_{N}({\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})}\leq 2|\Phi_{N}({\widetilde{x}}_{N},{\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})|\leq 2Ce^{-\tfrac{1}{5}{\widetilde{\varepsilon}}D_{N}({\widetilde{\xi}}_{N})}
. We conclude that

D_{N}({\widetilde{\xi}})\leq C_{1}A_{N}(I)+C_{2}

for some global constants
C_{1},C_{2}
.
The estimate of
|\Phi_{N}(x,\xi)|
follows.
The second estimate is proved in the same way.
\square

13.2
The LLT in the irreducible non-lattice case

We give the proof for arrays (Theorem
12.1
’).
Theorem
12.1
on chains follows, because every additive functional on a Markov chain is stably hereditary (Example
3.49
).

We begin by proving that
V_{N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty.

Otherwise
\liminf V_{N}<\infty
, and one can find
N_{\ell}\uparrow\infty
such that
{\rm Var}(S_{N_{\ell}})=O(1)
. Let
\mathsf{X}^{\prime}
denote the sub-array with rows
{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}}^{(\ell)}=\mathsf{X}^{(N_{\ell})}
. By Theorem
5.2
,
\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}}
is center-tight, whence

G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})=\{0\}.

At the same time,
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}
, because
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible and non-lattice. So
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}|_{\mathsf{X}^{\prime}})\neq G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, in contradiction to the assumption that
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary.

Next we fix
z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, and show that for every non-empty interval
(a,b)
, for every choice of
x_{1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{1}

(N\geq 1)
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\sim\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}(b-a),\text{ as $N\to\infty$.}

(13.14)

A well-known approximation argument
S
,
(
Br
, chapter 10)

reduces (
13.14
) to showing that for all
\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
whose Fourier transform

\widehat{\phi}(\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\xi u}\phi(u)du
has compact support,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}\bigl[\phi\bigl(S_{N}-z_{N}\bigr)\bigr]=\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi(u)du.

(13.15)

Fix
\phi\in L^{1}
such that
{\rm supp}({\widehat{\phi}})\subseteq[-L,L].

By the Fourier inversion formula,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}(\phi(S_{N}-z_{N}))=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-L}^{L}{\widehat{\phi}}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi.

So (
13.15
) is equivalent to

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-L}^{L}{\widehat{\phi}}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi=\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\widehat{\phi}(0).

(13.16)

Below, we give a proof of (
13.16
).

We note for future reference that the proof
of (
13.16
) below works under the milder assumption that
\widehat{\phi}
is
bounded, continuous at zero and has compact support, e.g.
\widehat{\phi}=\frac{1}{2\pi}1_{[-\pi,\pi]}

(which is the Fourier transform of

\phi(u)=\frac{\sin(\pi u)}{\pi u}\not\in L^{1}
).

Divide
[-L,L]
into segments
I_{j}
of length
\leq{\widetilde{\delta}}
where
{\widetilde{\delta}}
is given by Lemma
4.61
, so that
I_{0}
is centered at
0
.
Let

J_{j,N}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{I_{j}}{\widehat{\phi}}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi.

Claim 1 (contribution of
J_{0,N}
):

\sqrt{V_{N}}J_{0,N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-z^{2}/2}\widehat{\phi}(0).

(13.17)

Proof of the claim.

Fix
R>0
. Since
J_{0,N}\owns 0
,
A_{N}(J_{0,N})=0
.
By Corollary
4.62
, given
{\varepsilon}>0
there is
R>0
such that

\left|\sqrt{V_{N}}\int_{\{\xi\in I_{0}:|\xi|>R/\sqrt{V_{N}}\}}{\widehat{\phi}}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi\right|{\leq{\varepsilon}}.

Next, a change of variables
\xi=s/\sqrt{V_{N}}
gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{V_{N}}\int_{[|\xi|\leq R/\sqrt{V_{N}}]}{\widehat{\phi}}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi=\int_{[|s|\leq R]}{\widehat{\phi}}\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\;\mathbb{E}_{x^{(N)}_{1}}(e^{is\frac{S_{N}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}})\;ds.

By Dobrushin’s CLT for inhomogeneous Markov arrays (Theorem
5.3
)

\frac{S_{N}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
converges in distribution w.r.t.
{\mathbb{P}}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}
to the normal distribution
with mean
-z
and variance
1
.
By Lévy’s continuity theorem, this implies that

\mathbb{E}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}(e^{is\frac{S_{N}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}e^{-isz-s^{2}/2}

uniformly on compacts, and so

\displaystyle\sqrt{V_{N}}\int_{|\xi|\leq R/\sqrt{V_{N}}}{\widehat{\phi}}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi=\widehat{\phi}(0)\int_{-R}^{R}e^{-isz}e^{-s^{2}/2}ds+o_{N\to\infty}(1).

Since this is true for all
R
, we can let
R\to\infty
sufficiently slow to obtain (
13.17
).

Claim 2 (contribution of the other
J_{j,N}
):

\sqrt{V_{N}}J_{j,N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0\text{ for }j\neq 0.

Proof of the claim.
Since
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible with algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
, the co-range of
\mathsf{f}
is
\{0\}
(Theorems
9.1
,
9.4
). Since
\mathsf{f}
is stably hereditary,

D_{N}(\xi)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty\text{ uniformly on compacts in }\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}.

By (
13.6
),
\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)\to 0
uniformly on compacts in
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.

We will use this to show that for any interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}

\sqrt{V_{N}}\int_{I}|\Phi(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)|d\xi\to 0.

(13.18)

By subdividing
I
into finitely many subintervals we see that it suffices to prove the claim for

I=I_{j}
for some
j.

Recall that
A_{N}(I_{j})=-\log\sup|\Phi_{N}(\cdot,\cdot)|
on
\mathfrak{S}_{1}^{(N)}\times I_{j}
, and
({\widetilde{x}}_{j,N},{\widetilde{\xi}}_{j,N})
are points where this supremum is achieved up to factor
2
.
Set
A_{j,N}:=A_{N}(I_{j})
, then

A_{j,N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
for each
j\neq 0.

Take large
R
and split
I_{j}
into two regions

I_{j,N}^{\prime}:=\left\{\xi\in I_{j}:|\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{j,N}|\leq R\sqrt{\frac{A_{j,N}}{V_{N}}}\right\},\hskip 11.74988ptI_{j,N}^{\prime\prime}:=I_{j}\setminus I_{j,N}^{\prime}.

Split the integral
\int_{I_{j}}|\Phi(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)|d\xi
into two integrals

J_{j,N}^{\prime}
,
J_{j,N}^{\prime\prime}
accordingly.

\circ

On
I_{j,N}^{\prime}
,

|\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)|\leq e^{-A_{j,N}}

and
|I_{j,N}^{\prime}|\leq 2R\sqrt{\frac{A_{j,N}}{V_{N}}}
, so

\sqrt{V_{N}}|J_{j,N}^{\prime}|\leq 2R\sqrt{A_{j,N}}e^{-A_{j,N}}.

\circ

On
I_{j,N}^{\prime\prime}
, by Corollary
4.62
,

\displaystyle|\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)|\leq{\widetilde{C}}\exp\biggl(-{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}V_{N}|\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{j,N}|R\sqrt{\frac{A_{j,N}}{V_{N}}}+{\overline{c}}|\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{j,N}|\sqrt{V_{N}A_{j,N}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq{\widetilde{C}}\exp\biggl(-\frac{{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}}{2}{|\xi-{\widetilde{\xi}}_{j,N}|\sqrt{A_{j,N}V_{N}}}\biggr),{\text{ provided }R{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}>{\overline{c}}+\frac{{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}}{2}}.

Hence

\displaystyle\sqrt{V_{N}}J^{\prime\prime}_{j,N}\leq\sqrt{V_{N}}{\widetilde{C}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-\frac{{\widehat{{\varepsilon}}}}{2}|s|\sqrt{A_{j,N}V_{N}}}ds=O(A_{j,N}^{-\frac{1}{2}}).

Combining these estimates, we obtain

\sqrt{V_{N}}\|\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(I_{j})}\leq 2R\sqrt{A_{j,N}}\;e^{-A_{j,N}}+\frac{C}{\sqrt{A_{j,N}}}.

(13.19)

Since
A_{j,N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
(
13.18
) follows.

Since

\displaystyle|J_{j,N}|\leq\frac{\|{\widehat{\phi}}\|_{\infty}\;\|\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\cdot)\|_{L_{1}(I_{j})}}{2\pi}
,
claim 2 follows from (
13.18
).

Remark 4.63

Note that in the proof of
(
13.18
) the irreducibility assumption is only used at the last sentence, namely, to conclude that

A_{N,j}\to 0
as
N\to\infty.
In particular, (
13.19
) holds for arbitrary arrays, irreducible or not.

Claims 1 and 2 imply (
13.16
), and (
13.16
) implies (
13.14
) by

(
Br
, chapter 10)
.
This proves the LLT theorem for initial distributions concentrated at single points (i.e.
{\mathbb{P}}={\mathbb{P}}_{x^{(N)}_{1}}
). To deduce the theorem for arbitrary initial distribution
\mu^{(N)}_{1}(dx_{1}^{(N)})
, it is sufficient to prove the following claim and then integrate:

Claim 3:
(
13.14
) holds uniformly with respect to the choice of
\{x^{(N)}_{n}\}
.

Proof of the claim.
Assume by contradiction that this is false, then there exists
{\varepsilon}>0
and
N_{k}\to\infty
with
y^{(N_{k})}_{1}
such that

{\mathbb{P}}_{y_{1}^{(N_{k})}}[S_{N_{k}}-z_{N_{k}}\in(a,b)]\big/\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}(b-a)}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N_{k}}}}\not\in[e^{-{\varepsilon}},e^{{\varepsilon}}].
But this contradicts (
13.14
)
for any sequence
\{x_{1}^{(N)}\}
such that
x^{(N_{k})}_{1}=y^{(N_{k})}_{1}
.

\square

13.3
The LLT for the irreducible lattice case

We give the proof in the context of arrays (Theorem
12.2
’):
\mathsf{X}
is a uniformly elliptic array, and
\mathsf{f}
is an additive functional on
\mathsf{X}
which is a.s. uniformly bounded, hereditary, irreducible, and with algebraic range
t\mathbb{Z}
with
t>0
. Without loss of generality,
t=1
, otherwise work with
t^{-1}\mathsf{f}
.

By Lemma
3.51
and the assumption that
G_{alg}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{Z}
, there are constants
c^{(N)}_{n}
such that

f^{(N)}_{n}(X^{(N)}_{n},X^{(N)}_{n+1})-c^{(N)}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ a.s. }

We may assume without loss of generality that
c^{(N)}_{n}=0
, otherwise we work with
\mathsf{f}-\mathsf{c}
. So

S_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ a.s. for every $N\geq 1$.}

We will show that for every sequence of numbers
z_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, and for every
x_{1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{n}
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}(S_{N}=z_{N})=[1+o(1)]\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}},\text{ as $N\to\infty$}.

(13.20)

As in the irreducible case, once we prove (
13.20
) for all choices of
\{x_{1}^{(N)}\}
, it automatically follows that (
13.20
) holds uniformly in
\{x_{1}^{(N)}\}
. Integrating over
(\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{1},\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{1}),\mu^{(N)}_{1})
gives (
12.4
) with
k=0
. For general
k
, take
z_{N}^{\prime}:=z_{N}+k
.

The assumptions on
\mathsf{f}
imply that

{\rm Var}(S_{N})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty
. The proof is a routine modification of the argument we used in the non-lattice case, so we omit it.

Observe that
\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{im\xi}d\xi
is equal to zero when
m\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
, and equal to one when
m=0
. In particular, since
S_{N}-z_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}
almost surely, for every
x_{1}^{(N)}\in\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{1}

{\mathbb{P}}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}(S_{N}-z_{N}=0)=\mathbb{E}_{x_{1}^{(N)}}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{i\xi(S_{N}-z_{N})}d\xi\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\Phi(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi.

Thus to prove (
13.20
) it is sufficient to show that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\Phi_{N}(x_{1}^{(N)},\xi)e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-z^{2}/2}.

(13.21)

Notice that (
13.21
) is (
13.16
) in the case
\phi(u)=\frac{\sin(\pi u)}{\pi u}
,
\widehat{\phi}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi}1_{[-\pi,\pi]}(\xi)
, and can be proved in almost exactly the same way.

Here is a sketch of the proof. One divides
[-\pi,\pi]
into segments
I_{j}
of length less than the
\widetilde{\delta}
of Lemma
4.61
.

The contribution of the interval which contains zero is asymptotic to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}e^{-z^{2}/2}
. This is shown as in claim 1 of the preceding proof.

The remaining intervals are bounded away from
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
. Their contribution is
o(1/\sqrt{V_{N}})
. This can be seen as in claim 2 of the preceding proof, using the facts that since
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible with algebraic range
\mathbb{Z}
,
H(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=2\pi\mathbb{Z}
(Theorems
9.1
,
9.4
), and since
f
is hereditary and
G_{alg}(f)=\mathbb{Z}
,
f
is stably hereditary, whence

D_{N}(\xi)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0

uniformly on compacts in
\mathbb{R}\setminus 2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

\square

13.4
The mixing LLT

The proof is very similar to the proof of the local limit theorem, except that we use
\Phi(x,\xi|\mathfrak{A})
instead of
\Phi(x,\xi)
.

We outline the proof in the non-lattice case, and leave the lattice case to the reader.
Suppose
\mathsf{X}
is a uniformly elliptic Markov array, and that
\mathsf{f}
is a.s. uniformly bounded, stably hereditary, irreducible and with algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}_{N}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k_{N}+1}^{(N)}
be measurable sets s.t.
{\mathbb{P}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N})>\delta>0
, and let
x_{N}\in\mathfrak{S}^{(N)}_{1}
be points. Suppose
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
.
As before,
V_{N}\to\infty
, and a standard approximation argument (
Br
, chapter 10) says that it is enough to show that for every
\phi\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
s.t.
{\rm supp}(\widehat{\phi})\subset[-L,L]
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\cdot\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-L}^{L}{\widehat{\phi}}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{N},\xi|\mathfrak{A}_{N})e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi=\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\widehat{\phi}(0).

Divide
[-L,L]
as before into intervals
I_{j}
of length
\leq\widetilde{\delta}
where
\widetilde{\delta}
is given by Lemma
4.61
and
I_{0}
is centered at zero, and let

J_{j,N}:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{I_{j}}\widehat{\phi}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{N},\xi|\mathfrak{A}_{N})e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi.

Claim 1:

\sqrt{V_{N}}J_{0,N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}(2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z^{2}/2}\widehat{\phi}(0)
.

Proof of the claim
: Fix
R>0
.
As before,
applying Corollary
4.62
with
A_{N}=0
we conclude that for each
{\varepsilon}>0

there is
R>0
such that

\left|\sqrt{V_{N}}\int_{\{\xi\in I_{0}:|\xi|>R/\sqrt{V_{N}}\}}\widehat{\phi}(\xi)\Phi_{N}(x_{N},\xi|\mathfrak{A}_{N})e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi\right|{\leq{\varepsilon}.}

Next the change of variables
\xi=s/\sqrt{V_{N}}
gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{V_{N}}\int_{\{\xi\in I_{0}:|\xi|\leq R/\sqrt{V_{N}}\}}\widehat{\phi}(\xi)\Phi(x_{N},\xi|\mathfrak{A}_{N})e^{-i\xi z_{N}}d\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{-R}^{R}\widehat{\phi}\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\mathbb{E}_{x_{N}}\left(e^{is(\frac{S_{N}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})}\bigg|X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N}\right)d\xi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{{\mathbb{P}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N})}\int_{-R}^{R}\widehat{\phi}\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\mathbb{E}_{x_{N}}\left(e^{is(\frac{S_{N}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})}1_{\mathfrak{A}_{N}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})\right)d\xi.

(13.22)

We analyze the expectation in the integrand.
Take
1\leq r_{N}\leq k_{N}
such that
r_{N}\to\infty
and
r_{N}/\sqrt{V_{N}}\to 0
, and let

S_{N}^{\ast}:=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{N}-r_{N}}f_{j}^{(N)}(X^{(N)}_{j},X^{(N)}_{j+1})\equiv S_{N}-\sum_{j=k_{N}-r_{N}+1}^{k_{N}}f_{j}^{(N)}(X_{j}^{(N)},X^{(N)}_{j+1}).

Since
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|<\infty
,
|S_{N}-S_{N}^{\ast}|=o(\sqrt{V_{N}})
, and so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x_{N}}\left(e^{is(\frac{S_{N}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})}1_{\mathfrak{A}_{N}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})\right)=\mathbb{E}_{x_{N}}\left(e^{is(\frac{S_{N}^{\ast}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})}1_{\mathfrak{A}_{N}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})\right)+o(1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x_{N}}\left(e^{is(\frac{S_{N}^{\ast}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})}\mathbb{E}\biggl(1_{\mathfrak{A}_{N}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})|X^{(N)}_{1},\ldots,X^{(N)}_{k_{n}-r_{N}}\biggr)\right)+o(1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x_{N}}\left(e^{is(\frac{S_{N}^{\ast}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})}\mathbb{E}\biggl(1_{\mathfrak{A}_{N}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})|X^{(N)}_{k_{n}-r_{N}}\biggr)\right)+o(1)\text{ by the Markov property}

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}\mathbb{E}_{x_{N}}\left(e^{is(\frac{S_{N}^{\ast}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})}\left[{\mathbb{P}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N})+O(\theta^{r_{N}})\right]\right)+o(1),\text{ where $0<\theta<1$}

and
\overset{!}{=}
uses the exponential mixing estimate (
2.3
).
Since
{\mathbb{P}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N})
is bounded below, and
\frac{S_{N}^{\ast}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
converges in distribution to the standard normal distribution by Dobrushin’s theorem, we may conclude that

\mathbb{E}_{x_{N}}\left(e^{is(\frac{S_{N}-z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}})}1_{\mathfrak{A}_{N}}(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1})\right)=\frac{1+o(1)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-z^{2}/2-izs}{\mathbb{P}}\left(X^{(N)}_{k_{N}+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N}\right).

Substituting this in (
13.22
) gives the claim.

Claim 2:

\sqrt{V_{N}}J_{j,N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0
for
j\neq 0
.

The claim is proved as in the previous proof, but with (
13.7
) replacing (
13.6
).
Together, claims 1 and 2 imply the theorem.
\square

14
Notes and references

For a brief account of the history of the local limit theorem, see the end of the preface.

Many of the techniques we used in this chapter have a long history. The reduction of the LLT to the asymptotic analysis of the integrals (
13.16
) and (
13.21
) for
\phi\in L^{1}
with Fourier transforms with compact support was already used by Stone
S
for proving local limit theorems for sums of iid random variables. As mentioned at the end of the synopsis, the method of characteristic function operators is due to Nagaev
N
, who used it to prove central and local limit theorems for homogeneous Markov chains, and this method was used extensively in dynamical systems. Hafouta & Kifer
Hafouta-Kifer-Book
, Hafouta
Hafouta-Skew-Products
;
Hafouta-Sequential
, and Dragičević, Froyland, & González-Tokman
Dragicevic-Froyland-Gonzalez-Tokman
, used this technique to prove the local limit theorem in a non-homogeneous setup.

The terminology “mixing LLT” is due to Rényi
Renyi-Mixing
, who initiated the study of the stability of limit theorems under conditioning and changes of measure.
The relevance of Mixing LLT to the study of reducible case is noted by
Guivarc’h & Hardy
GH
. Mixing LLT have numerous other applications including mixing
of special flows
GH
;
DN19
, homogenization
DN-Mech
and skew products
(see in particular, Theorem
15.2

in Chapter
5
).
Mixing LLT for additive functionals of (stationary) Gibbs-Markov processes were proved
by Aaronson & Denker
Aaronson-Denker-LLT
.

Chapter 5
The local limit theorem in the reducible case

In this chapter we prove the local limit theorem for
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))

when
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{{\rm Var}(S_{N})}}
converges to a finite limit and
\mathsf{f}
is reducible. In the reducible case, the asymptotic behavior of
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))
depends on the details of
f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
. The dependence is strong for small intervals, and weak for large intervals.

15
Main results

15.1
Heuristics and warm up examples

An additive functional is called
reducible
if

\mathsf{f}=\mathsf{g}+\mathsf{c}

where
\mathsf{c}
is center-tight, and the algebraic range of
\mathsf{g}
is strictly smaller than the algebraic range of
\mathsf{f}
. By the results of Chapter
3
,
if
{\rm Var}(S_{N}(\mathsf{f}))\to\infty
,
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic, and
\mathsf{f}
is a.s. bounded, then
we can choose
\mathsf{g}
to be irreducible.
In this case

S_{N}(\mathsf{f})=S_{N}(\mathsf{g})+S_{N}(\mathsf{c}).

where
{\rm Var}(S_{N}(\mathsf{g}))\sim{\rm Var}(S_{N}(\mathsf{f}))\to\infty
,
{\rm Var}(S_{N}(\mathsf{c}))=O(1)
, and
S_{N}(\mathsf{g})
satisfies the lattice local limit theorem.

The contribution of
S_{n}(\mathsf{c})
cannot be neglected.

In this chapter we give the corrections to the LLT needed to take
S_{n}(\mathsf{c})
into account.

Before stating our results in general, we discuss two simple examples which demonstrate some of the possible effects of
S_{N}(\mathsf{c})
.

Example 5.64

(Simple random walk with continuous first step and drift):

Suppose
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
are independent real-valued random variables, where
X_{1}
is distributed like a random variable
\mathfrak{F}
, and
X_{i}

(i\geq 2)
are equal to
0,1
with equal probabilities.

\mathfrak{F}
could be arbitrary, but we assume for simplicity that
0\leq\mathfrak{F}<1
a.s.,
\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{F}]=\frac{1}{2}
, the distribution of
\mathfrak{F}
has a density, and
\mathfrak{F}

is
not
uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
. Let
\mu_{\mathfrak{F}}
denote the probability measure associated with the distribution of
\mathfrak{F}
.

S_{N}:=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{N}
is exactly
S_{N}(\mathsf{f})
, where
f_{n}(x,y):=x
. Since the distribution of
\mathfrak{F}
has a density,
\mathsf{f}
has algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
.

The following decomposition shows that
\mathsf{f}
is reducible, with essential range
\mathbb{Z}
:
Let
\delta_{ij}
be Kronecker’s delta, then
\mathsf{f}=\mathsf{g}+\mathsf{c}
where

g_{n}(x,y):=(1-\delta_{1,n})x,\ c_{n}(x,y):=\delta_{1,n}x,

\mathsf{g}
is irreducible with essential range
\mathbb{Z}
, and
\mathsf{c}
is center tight.

We have

S_{N}=(\underset{S_{N}(\mathsf{g})}{\underbrace{X_{2}+\cdots+X_{N}}})+\underset{S_{N}(\mathsf{c})}{\underbrace{X_{1}}}.

Clearly,
S_{N}(\mathsf{g})
,
S_{N}(\mathsf{c})
are independent;
S_{N}(\mathsf{c})\sim\mathfrak{F}
; and
S_{N}(\mathsf{g})
has the binomial distribution
B(\frac{1}{2},N-1)
.
So
S_{N}
has distribution

\mu_{\mathfrak{F}}\ast B(\frac{1}{2},N-1).

This distribution has a density, which we denote by
p_{N}(x)dx
. The following holds as
N\to\infty
:

(A) Non-uniform scaling limit for
p_{N}(x)dx
:

m_{N}:=p_{N}(x)dx
is a positive functional on
C_{c}(\mathbb{R})=\{\text{continuous functions with compact support}\}
. Fix
z_{N}:=\mathbb{E}(S_{N})=N/2
and let
V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N})\sim N/4
. Then for every
\phi\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
N
even,

\displaystyle\int\phi(x-z_{N})p_{N}(x)dx=\mathbb{E}[\phi(S_{N}-z_{N})]=\mathbb{E}[\phi(S_{N}(\mathsf{g})+S_{N}(\mathsf{c})-z_{N})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}[\phi(\mathfrak{F}+m-z_{N})]{\mathbb{P}}[S_{n}(\mathsf{g})=m]=\sum_{m=0}^{N-1}{N-1\choose m}\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}\mathbb{E}[\phi(\mathfrak{F}+m-z_{N})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}\sum_{m=0}^{N-1}{N-1\choose m}\psi(m-\tfrac{N}{2}),\text{ where }\psi(m):=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\mathfrak{F}+m)]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{N-1}}\sum_{m=-N/2}^{N/2-1}{N-1\choose m+N/2}\psi(m)\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\psi(m)\text{ by Stirling's formula}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}[\phi(\mathfrak{F}+m)],\text{ as $N\to\infty$. This also holds for $N$ odd.}

Thus the distribution of
S_{N}-z_{N}
tends to zero in the vague topology of Radon measure on
\mathbb{R}
“at a rate of
1/\sqrt{2\pi N}
,” and if we inflate it by
\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}
then it converges in the vague topology to
\mu_{\mathfrak{F}}\ast
(counting measure on
\mathbb{Z}
).

By the assumptions on
\mathfrak{F}
, the scaling limit

\mu_{\mathfrak{F}}\ast
(counting measure on
\mathbb{Z}
) is not a Haar measure on a closed subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
. This is different from the irreducible case, when the scaling limit is the Haar measure on
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
.

(B) Non-standard limit for
\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
:

Fix
a,b\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}
s.t.
|a-b|>1
.
Repeating the previous calculation with
\phi_{i}\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})

such that
\phi_{1}\leq 1_{(a,b)}\leq\phi_{2}
and

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}[\phi_{i}(m+\mathfrak{F})]\approx\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}[1_{(a,b)}(m+\mathfrak{F})]

gives
for
z_{N}=\mathbb{E}(S_{N})
that

\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}[1_{(a,b)}(m+\mathfrak{F})].

(15.1)

This is different than the limit in the irreducible non-lattice LLT (Theorem
12.1
):

{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}|a-b|;

(15.2)

or the limit in the irreducible lattice LLT with range
\mathbb{Z}
(Theorem
12.2
):

{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}1_{(a,b)}(m).

(15.3)

(C) Robustness for large intervals:
Although different, the limits in (
15.1
),(
15.3
) and (
15.2
) are nearly the same as
|a-b|\to\infty
.

The ratio between the limits in (
15.3
),(
15.2
) tends to one as
|a-b|\to\infty
. The ratio between the limits in (
15.1
),(
15.3
) tends to one too, because
{\rm supp}(\mathfrak{F})\subset[0,1]
, so

\displaystyle|a-b|-2\leq\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}1_{(a,b)}(m+\mathfrak{F})\leq|a-b|+2
a.s., whence

\left|\frac{\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}[1_{(a,b)}(m+\mathfrak{F})]}{\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}1_{(a,b)}(m)}-1\right|\leq\frac{2}{|a-b|}\xrightarrow[|a-b|\to\infty]{}0.

Example
5.64
is very special in that
S_{n}(\mathsf{g}),S_{N}(\mathsf{c})
are independent. Nevertheless, we will see below that (A), (B), (C) are general phenomena, which also happen when
S_{N}(\mathsf{g})
,
S_{N}(\mathsf{h})
are strongly correlated.
The following simple example demonstrates another pathology that is quite general:

Example 5.65
(Gradient perturbation of the lazy random walk)

:

Suppose
X_{n},Y_{n}
are independent random variables such that
X_{n}=-1,0,+1
with equal probabilities, and
Y_{n}
are uniformly distributed in
[0,1]
.
Let
\mathsf{X}=\{(X_{n},Y_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
.

\circ

The additive functional

g_{n}((x_{n},y_{n});(x_{n+1},y_{n+1}))=x_{n}
generates the lazy random walk on
\mathbb{Z}
,

S_{N}(\mathsf{g})=X_{1}+\cdots+X_{N}.

It is irreducible, and satisfies the lattice LLT with range
\mathbb{Z}
.

\circ

The additive functional
c_{n}((x_{n},y_{n}),(x_{n+1},y_{n+1}))=y_{n}-y_{n+1}
is center-tight, and

S_{N}(\mathsf{c})=Y_{N+1}-Y_{1}.

\circ

The sum
\mathsf{f}=\mathsf{g}+\mathsf{c}
is reducible, with algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
(because of
\mathsf{c}
) and essential range
\mathbb{Z}
(because of
\mathsf{g}
). It generates the process

S_{N}(\mathsf{f})=S_{N}(\mathsf{g})+Y_{N+1}-Y_{1}.

S_{N}(\mathsf{f})
lies in a
random coset

b_{N}+\mathbb{Z}
, where
b_{N}=Y_{N+1}-Y_{1}
. Since the distribution of
b_{N}
is continuous,
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}=k]=0
for all
z_{N},k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the standard lattice LLT fails. To deal with this, we must “shift”
S_{N}-z_{N}
back to
\mathbb{Z}
. This leads to the following (correct) statement: For all
z_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}-b_{N}=k]\sim\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}

Notice the shift by a
random
bounded quantity
b_{N}
.

15.2
The LLT in the reducible case

Theorem 15.1

Let
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}
be a uniformly elliptic Markov chain, and let
\mathsf{f}
be a reducible a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional with essential range
\delta(\mathsf{f})\mathbb{Z}
, where
\delta(\mathsf{f})\neq 0
. Then there are random variables
b_{N}=b_{N}(X_{1},X_{N+1})
and
\mathfrak{F}=\mathfrak{F}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots)
with the following properties:

(1)

For every
z_{N}\in\delta(\mathsf{f})\mathbb{Z}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, for every
\phi\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
,

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\phi(S_{N}-z_{N}-b_{N})\right]=\frac{\delta(\mathsf{f})e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{x}[\phi(m\delta(\mathsf{f})+\mathfrak{F})].

(2)

For every
\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{N+1}
measurable such that
{\mathbb{P}}[X_{N+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}]
is bounded below, and for every
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
,

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\sqrt{V_{N}}\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\phi(S_{N}-z_{N}-b_{N})\big|X_{N+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}\right]=\frac{\delta(\mathsf{f})e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{x}[\phi(m\delta(\mathsf{f})+\mathfrak{F})].

(3)

\|b_{N}\|_{\infty}\leq 9\delta(\mathsf{f})
, and
\mathfrak{F}\in[0,\delta(\mathsf{f}))
.

The statement may seem at first sight different from the previous LLT we discussed, so we’d like to spend some time on clarifying what it is saying.

\circ

\mathbb{E}_{x}\left[\phi(S_{N}-z_{N}-b_{N})\right],

when viewed as a positive functional on
C_{c}(\mathbb{R})
, represents the measure on
\mathbb{R}
,

m_{x,N}(E)={\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}-b_{N}(X_{1},X_{N+1})\in E].

This is the distribution of
S_{N}
, conditioned on
X_{1}=x
, after a shift by
z_{N}+b_{N}(X_{1},X_{N+1})
. The deterministic shift by
z_{N}
cancels the drift of
S_{N}
(notice that
z_{N}\approx\mathbb{E}(S_{N})\approx\mathbb{E}_{x}(S_{N})
).
The random shift
b_{N}
is needed to force
S_{N}
to stay inside
\delta(\mathsf{f})\mathbb{Z}
, see Example
5.65
.

\circ

The linear functional

\mathcal{A}_{x}(\phi):=\delta(\mathsf{f})\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}_{x}[\phi(m\delta(\mathsf{f})+\mathfrak{F})]

(15.4)

defines the element of
C_{c}(\mathbb{R})^{\ast}
which represents the measure

\mu_{x,\mathfrak{F}}\ast m_{\delta(\mathsf{f})}
, where
\mu_{x,\mathfrak{F}}(E)={\mathbb{P}}_{x}(\mathfrak{F}\in E)
and

m_{\delta(\mathsf{f})}:=\delta(\mathsf{f})\times\text{counting measure on $\delta(\mathsf{f})\mathbb{Z}.$}

So part (1) of Theorem
15.1
says that
m_{x,N}\to 0
in
C_{c}(\mathbb{R})^{\ast}
at rate
1/\sqrt{V_{N}}
, and gives the scaling limit

\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}m_{N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{w^{\ast}}\mu_{x,\mathfrak{F}}\ast m_{\delta(\mathsf{f})}
when
z=0
.
See Example
5.64
.

\circ

As in Example
5.64
,
part (1) implies the following: For all
a<b
s.t.
\mathfrak{F}
has no atoms in

\{a,b\}+\delta(\mathsf{f})\mathbb{Z}
, and for all

z_{N}\in\delta(\mathsf{f})\mathbb{Z}
s.t.
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}-b_{N}\in(a,b)]=[1+o(1)]\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\cdot\mathcal{A}_{x}(1_{(a,b)}),\text{ and }

\mathcal{A}_{x}(1_{(a,b)})\sim\begin{cases}|a-b|&\text{ as }|a-b|\to\infty\\
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[\mathfrak{F}\in(a,b)]&\text{ for }(a,b)\subset[0,\delta(\mathsf{f})].\end{cases}

Viewed from this perspective,
A_{x}(1_{(a,b)})
is a “correction” to the term
|a-b|
in classical LLT (
12.1
), which is needed for intervals with length of order
\delta(\mathsf{f})
.

These observations should be sufficient to understand the content of part (1). Part (2) is a “mixing” version of part (1), in the sense of §
12.3
. Such results are particularly useful in the reducible setup for the following reason. The random shift
b_{N}(X_{1},X_{N+1})
is sometimes a nuisance, and it is tempting to turn it into a deterministic quantity by conditioning on
X_{1},X_{N+1}
. We would have liked to say that part (1) survives such conditioning, but we cannot. The best we can say in general is that part (1) remains valid
under conditioning of the form
X_{1}=x_{1},X_{N+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}

provided
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{N+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N+1})
is bounded below.
This the content of part (2). For an example how to use such a statement,
see §
16.3
.

In the following sections, we explore some of the consequences of Theorem
15.1
.

15.3
Irreducibility as a necessary condition for the mixing LLT

Theorem
15.1
exposes the pathologies that could happen in the reducible case. But is irreducibility a necessary condition for the non-lattice LLT? No!

Example 5.66

Take example
5.64
with fixed
x
and
\mathfrak{F}
uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
, given
X_{1}=x
.
In this case,
\delta(\mathsf{f})=1
,

\mu_{x,\mathfrak{F}}\ast m_{\delta(\mathsf{f})}=
Lebesgue’s measure,
A_{x}(1_{(a,b)})=|a-b|
, and

\displaystyle\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z\Rightarrow{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\sim\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}|a-b|,

even though
\mathsf{f}
is reducible, with essential range
\mathbb{Z}
. Of course, such behavior is immediately destroyed if we modify
X_{1}
.

In this section we show that irreducibility
is
a necessary condition for the mixing LLT, provided we impose the mixing LLT not just for
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, but also for all
(\mathsf{X}^{\prime},\mathsf{f}^{\prime})
obtained from
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
by changing finitely many terms.

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an additive functional on a Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
. Denote the state spaces of
\mathsf{X}
by
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, and write
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
,
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
. A sequence of events
\mathfrak{A}_{k}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{k}
is called
regular
if
\mathfrak{A}_{k}
are measurable, and
{\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}\in\mathfrak{A}_{n})
is bounded away from zero.

\circ

We say that
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
satisfies the
mixing non-lattice local limit theorem

if

V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N})\to\infty
, and for every regular sequence of events
\mathfrak{A}_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
, for all
z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, and for each non-empty interval
(a,b)
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}\biggl(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)\big|X_{N+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}\biggr)=[1+o(1)]\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}|a-b|\text{ as $N\to\infty$}.

\circ

Fix
t>0
. We say that
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
satisfies the
mixing uniform distribution mod
t
property
,

if for every regular sequence of events
\mathfrak{A}_{n}\subset\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
,
and a non-empty interval
(a,b)
with length less than one,

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}\bigl(S_{N}\in(a,b)+t\mathbb{Z}|X_{N+1}\in\mathfrak{A}_{N+1}\bigr)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\frac{|a-b|}{t}.

Theorem 15.2

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain. Given
m
, let

(\mathsf{X}_{m},\mathsf{f}_{m}):=(\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq m},\{f_{n}\}_{n\geq m})
.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathsf{f}
is irreducible with algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
;

(2)

(\mathsf{X}_{m},\mathsf{f}_{m})
satisfy the mixing non-lattice local limit theorem for all
m
;

(3)

(\mathsf{X}_{m},\mathsf{f}_{m})
satisfy the mixing uniform distribution mod
t
for all
m
and
t>0
.

15.4
Universal bounds for
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]

So far we have considered the problem of finding
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
up to asymptotic equivalence. We now consider the problem of finding
{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
up to bounded multiplicative error, assuming only that
V_{N}\to\infty
.

We already saw that the predictions of the LLT for
large
intervals
(a,b)
are nearly the same both in the reducible and irreducible, lattice and non-lattice cases. Therefore we expect
universal
lower and upper bounds, for all sufficiently large intervals without further assumptions on irreducibility or on the arithmetic structure of the range. The question is how large is “sufficiently large.”

We certainly cannot expect universal lower and upper bounds for intervals smaller than the
graininess constant
of
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
:

\delta(\mathsf{f}):=\begin{cases}t&G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=t\mathbb{Z},\;\;t>0\\
0&G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\mathbb{R}\\
\infty&G_{ess}(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})=\{0\},\end{cases}

(15.5)

because intervals with length less than
\delta(\mathsf{f})
may fall in the gaps of the support of
S_{N}-z_{N}
.
Theorem
15.1
can be used to see that universal bounds do apply as soon as
|a-b|>\delta(\mathsf{f})
:

Theorem 15.3

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
. Then for every interval
(a,b)
of length
L>\delta(\mathsf{f})
, for all
\epsilon>0
,

x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
and
z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\to z
, for all for all
N
large enough,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|a-b|}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\left(1+\frac{21\delta(\mathsf{f})}{L}+\epsilon\right),

(15.6)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}|a-b|}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\left(1-\frac{\delta(\mathsf{f})}{L}-\epsilon\right).

(15.7)

In addition, if
0<\delta(\mathsf{f})<\infty
and
k\delta(f)\lvertneqq L\lvertneqq(k+1)\delta(f)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\left(\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\right)k\delta(f)\lesssim{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b))\lesssim\left(\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\right)(k+1)\delta(f).

Here
A_{N}\lesssim B_{N}
means that
\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}(A_{N}/B_{N})\leq 1
.

We note that both upper and lower bound become asymptotic to the Gaussian density as
L\to\infty.

Notice also that the theorem makes no assumptions on the irreducibility of
\mathsf{f}
.

Theorem
15.3
is an easy corollary of Theorem
15.1
, see §
16.4
,
but this is an overkill. At the end of the chapter we will supply a proof of universal bounds for intervals of length
L>2\delta(\mathsf{f})
,
which does not require the full force of Theorem
15.1
, and which also applies
to arbitrary initial distributions and to arrays.

16
Proofs

16.1
Characteristic functions

Setup:
Throughout this section we assume that
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}
is a uniformly elliptic Markov
chain
with state spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, marginals
\mu_{n}(E)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{n}\in E)
, and transition probabilities
\pi_{n,n+1}(x,dy)=p_{n}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}(dy)
which satisfy the uniform ellipticity condition with ellipticity constant
\epsilon_{0}
.

For every bounded measurable function
\varphi:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
, we let

\mathbb{E}(\varphi):=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X_{n},X_{n+1})]\ ,\ \sigma(\varphi):=\sqrt{{\rm Var}(\varphi(X_{n},X_{n+1}))}.

Next we assume that
K>0
,
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
\mathsf{f}=\{f^{(N)}_{n}:1\leq n\leq N<\infty\}
is an
array
of measurable functions
f_{n}^{(N)}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}\to\mathbb{R}
which satisfy the following assumptions for all
N
:

(I)

\mathbb{E}(f^{(N)}_{n})=0
and
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|<K
.

(II)

Let
\displaystyle S_{N}:=\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{n}^{(N)}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
and
V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N})
, then there exists
{\widehat{C}}>0
s.t.

V_{N}\to\infty\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}(f^{(N)}_{n})\leq{\widehat{C}}.

(16.1)

(III)

\mathsf{f}=\mathbb{F}+\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}
, where

(a)

\mathbb{F}=\{\mathbb{F}^{(N)}_{n}\}
are measurable functions such that

\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathbb{F}|\leq K\ ,\ G_{alg}(\mathsf{X},\mathbb{F})\subset\mathbb{Z}.

(b)

\mathsf{h}=\{h^{(N)}_{n}\}
are measurable functions such that

\mathbb{E}(h^{(N)}_{n})=0,\hskip 11.74988pt\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{h}|<K,\hskip 11.74988pt\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}(h^{(N)}_{n})\leq\epsilon.

(c)

\mathsf{c}=\{c^{(N)}_{n}\}
are constants.
Necessarily
|c^{(N)}_{n}|\leq 3K
and
c^{(N)}_{n}=-\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{F}^{(N)}_{n})
. Let
c^{(N)}:=\sum_{n=1}^{N}c^{(N)}_{n}
.

We are
not
assuming that
\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{F}^{(N)}_{n})=0
:
\mathbb{F}^{(N)}_{n}
are integer valued, and we do not wish to destroy this by subtracting the mean.

Lemma 5.67

Under the above assumptions,  for every
{\overline{K}}>0
,
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, there are
{\overline{C}},{\overline{N}}>0
s.t. for every
N>{\overline{N}}
,
|s|\leq{\overline{K}}
,
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
, and
v_{N+1}:\mathfrak{S}_{N+1}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\|v_{N+1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left(e^{i\bigl(2\pi m+\frac{s}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\bigr)S_{N}}v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})\right)=e^{2\pi imc^{(N)}}e^{-s^{2}/2}\;{\mathbb{E}}(v_{N+1}(X_{N+1}))+\eta_{N}(x)

where
\mathbb{E}(|\eta|)\leq{\overline{C}}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}(h^{(N)}_{n})\right]^{1/2}\leq{\overline{C}}\sqrt{\epsilon}
.

Proof

In this proof we fix the value of
N
, and drop the superscripts
N
for the ease of notation (for example
c^{(N)}=c
).

We develop a perturbation theory of transfer operators similar to
Ba
.
Recall the operators
\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}:L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{n+1})\to L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{n})

given by

(\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}u)(x)=\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}p_{n}(x,y)e^{i\xi f_{n}(x,y)}u(y)\mu_{n+1}(dy).

Let
\xi=\xi(m,s):=2\pi m+\dfrac{s}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
.
Since
\mathbb{F}_{n}
is integer valued,

e^{i\xi f_{n}}=\exp[{2\pi im\mathbb{F}_{n}+\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\mathbb{F}_{n}+i\xi c_{n}+i\xi h_{n})}]={e^{2\pi imc_{n}}}e^{i\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}(\mathbb{F}_{n}{+c_{n}})+\xi h_{n}\right)}.

We now split

e^{-{2\pi imc_{n}}}\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}+\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}+\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}

where

\displaystyle\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}u\right)(x)=\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}p_{n}(x,y)e^{\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}(\mathbb{F}_{n}(x,y){+c_{n}})}u(y)\mu_{n+1}(dy),

\displaystyle\left(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}u\right)(x)=i\xi\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}p_{n}(x,y)h_{n}(x,y)u(y)\mu_{n+1}(dy),\text{ and }

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}u\right)(x)=\!\!\!\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{n+1}}p_{n}(x,y)\left[e^{i\xi h_{n}+\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}(\mathbb{F}_{n}(x,y){+c_{n}})}\!-e^{\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}(\mathbb{F}_{n}(x,y){+c_{n}})}\!-i\xi h_{n}(x,y)\right]u(y)\mu_{n+1}(dy).

We claim that there exists
C_{1}({\overline{K}},m)>1
such that for
|s|\leq{\overline{K}},

n\geq 1

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}\right\|:=\left\|\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\leq 1,

(16.2)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\leq C_{1}({\overline{K}},m),

(16.3)

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}\right\|:=\left\|\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{\infty}}\leq 1,

(16.4)

\displaystyle\left\|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}\leq C_{1}({\overline{K}},m)\sigma(h_{n}),

(16.5)

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}\leq C_{1}({\overline{K}},m)\left[\sigma^{2}(h_{n})+\frac{\sigma(h_{n})\sigma(f_{n})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right].

(16.6)

To see this, we represent these operators as integral operators, and estimate their kernels.
For example,
\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}
is an integral operator whose kernel has absolute value

|i\xi p_{n}(x,y)h_{n}(x,y)|\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}|\xi||h_{n}(x,y)|
. So

\|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{4\pi^{2}m^{2}+{\overline{K}}^{2}}\|h_{n}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\sqrt{4\pi^{2}m^{2}+{\overline{K}}^{2}}\|h_{n}\|_{L^{2}},

and (
16.5
) follows from the identity
\|h_{n}\|_{L^{2}}\equiv\sigma(h_{n})
.
Similarly,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}
has kernel with absolute value

\hskip 11.74988ptp_{n}(x,y)\bigl|e^{i\xi h_{n}+is\frac{\mathbb{F}_{n}(x,y)+c_{n}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}}-e^{is\frac{\mathbb{F}_{n}(x,y)+c_{n}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}}-i\xi h_{n}(x,y)\bigr|\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\bigl|e^{is\frac{\mathbb{F}_{n}(x,y)+c_{n}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}}\bigl(e^{i\xi h_{n}}-1\bigr)-i\xi h_{n}\bigr|

=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\bigl|e^{is\frac{\mathbb{F}_{n}(x,y)+c_{n}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}}\bigl(i\xi h_{n}+O(\xi^{2}h_{n}^{2})\bigr)-i\xi h_{n}\bigr|=\epsilon_{0}^{-1}\bigl|e^{is\frac{\mathbb{F}_{n}(x,y)+c_{n}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}}-1\bigr||\xi h_{n}|+O\left(h_{n}^{2}\right)

=O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\left|h_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{n}+c_{n})\right|\right)+O\left(h_{n}^{2}\right)

where the implicit constants in
O(\cdot)
are uniform on compact sets of
\xi.

It follows that uniformly on compact sets of
\xi
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}=O(V_{N}^{-1/2})\mathbb{E}(|h_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{n}+c_{n})|)+O(\|h_{n}\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=O(V_{N}^{-1/2})\|h_{n}\|_{2}\|\mathbb{F}_{n}+c_{n}\|_{2}+O(\|h_{n}\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=O(V_{N}^{-1/2})\|h_{n}\|_{2}(\|f_{n}-h_{n}\|_{2})+O(\|h_{n}\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=O(V_{N}^{-1/2})\|h_{n}\|_{2}(\|f_{n}\|_{2}+\|h_{n}\|_{2})+O(\|h_{n}\|_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{\|h_{n}\|_{2}\|f_{n}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}+\|h_{n}\|_{2}^{2}\right)=O\left(\frac{\sigma(h_{n})\sigma(f_{n})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}+\sigma^{2}(h_{n})\right),

as claimed in (
16.6
).

Recall Nagaev’s identity (
13.1
):

\mathbb{E}_{x}[e^{i\xi S_{N}}v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})]=(\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\mathcal{L}_{2,\xi}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{N,\xi}v_{N+1})(x)
.
The decomposition
e^{-2\pi imc_{n}}\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}=\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}+\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}+\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{n,\xi}
implies that

{\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left(e^{i\xi S_{N}}v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})\right)=e^{2\pi imc}\left({\overline{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)+{\widehat{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)+{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)\right)

(16.7)

where
c=c^{(N)}=c_{1}+\dots+c_{N}
, and

\displaystyle{\overline{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{1,\xi}\dots\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N,\xi}v_{N+1}\right)(x),

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}e^{-2\pi im(c_{1}+\cdots+c_{k-1})}\left(\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}\dots\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N,\xi}v_{N+1}\right)(x),

\displaystyle{\widehat{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}e^{-2\pi im(c_{1}+\cdots+c_{k-1})}\left(\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}\dots\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N,\xi}v_{N+1}\right)(x).

We will analyze each of these summands.

Claim 1:
For every
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\left|{\overline{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)-e^{-s^{2}/2}\mathbb{E}_{x}(v_{N+1}(X_{N+1}))\right|\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0
uniformly in
s
on
\{s\in\mathbb{R}:|s|\leq{\overline{K}}\}
,
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
,

v_{N+1}\in\{v\in L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{N+1}):\|v\|\leq 1\}
.

Proof:

{\overline{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)={\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left(\exp\left(is\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{F}_{k}{+c}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})\right)
, where
\mathbb{E}(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{F}_{n})=-c
.
Fix
1\leq r\leq N
. Using the decomposition
\mathsf{f}=\mathbb{F}+\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}
, we find that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\biggl(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{F}_{k}+c\biggr)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\sum_{k=1}^{N-r}f_{k}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\biggl(O(r)-\sum_{k=1}^{N}h_{k}\biggr).

By assumption III(b), the
L^{2}
norm of the second summand is
O(1/\sqrt{V_{N}})
. Therefore the
second term converges to
0
in probability as
N\to\infty
, and

{\overline{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)={\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left(e^{\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}S_{N-r}}v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})\right)+o(1),

(16.8)

where we have abused notation and wrote
S_{N-r}=f_{1}^{(N)}+\cdots+f_{N-r}^{(N)}
.

The rate of convergence to
0
depends on
r
and
m
, but is uniform when
|s|\leq{\overline{K}}
and
\|v_{N+1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
.
At the same time, by exponential mixing (see (
2.3
)), there is
0<\theta<1
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left(e^{\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}S_{N-r}}v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})\right)={\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left[e^{\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}S_{N-r}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x}\bigl(v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})\big|X_{1},\ldots,X_{N-r}\bigr)\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left[e^{\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}S_{N-r}}{\mathbb{E}}_{x}\bigl(v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})\big|X_{N-r}\bigr)\right]\text{(Markov property)}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{x}\left(e^{\frac{is}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}S_{N-r}}[\mathbb{E}_{x}(v_{N+1}(X_{N+1})+O(\theta^{r})]\right)\hskip 11.74988pt\text{(exponential mixing)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x}(e^{isS_{N-r}/\sqrt{V_{N}}})\mathbb{E}_{x}(v_{N+1}(X_{N+1}))+O(\theta^{r})

(16.9)

where the
O(\theta^{r})
is uniform in
\|v_{N+1}\|_{\infty}
.

A similar mixing argument shows that

\mathbb{E}_{x}(S_{N-r})=\mathbb{E}(S_{N-r}|X_{1}=x)=\mathbb{E}(S_{N})+O(1)=O(1)

uniformly in
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
. By Dobrushin’s CLT,

\mathbb{E}_{x}(e^{isS_{N-r}/\sqrt{V_{N}}})=[1+o(1)]\mathbb{E}_{x}(e^{is\frac{S_{N}-\mathbb{E}_{x}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}})=[1+o(1)]e^{-s^{2}/2}\text{ as $N\to\infty$}.

The claim follows from this,
(
16.8
), and (
16.9
).

Claim 2.

There exists
C_{2}({\overline{K}},m)
s.t. for all
|s|\leq{\overline{K}}
and
\|v_{N+1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,

\bigl\|\widetilde{\Phi}_{N}(x,\xi)\bigr\|_{L^{1}}\leq C_{2}({\overline{K}},m)\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}.

Proof:

\|{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)\|_{1}\leq\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1,\xi}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}\left\|\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{2,\xi}\right\|\cdots\left\|\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N,\xi}\right\|

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{N}\left(\left\|\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\right\|\cdots\left\|\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\right\|\left\|\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}\right\|\cdots\left\|\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N,\xi}\right\|\right)

Suppose
|s|\leq{\overline{K}}
, then (
16.3
), (
16.4
)
and (
16.6
) tell us that

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1,\xi}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}\left\|\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{2,\xi}\right\|\cdots\left\|\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N,\xi}\right\|\leq C_{1}({\overline{K}},m)\left[\sigma(h_{1})^{2}+\frac{\sigma(h_{1})\sigma(f_{1})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right],

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\right\|\leq\left\|\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\right\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\left\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\right\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}\leq C_{1}({\overline{K}},m)^{2}\left[\sigma(h_{k})^{2}+\frac{\sigma(h_{k})\sigma(f_{k})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right].

Therefore
\|{\widetilde{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)\|_{1}\leq C_{1}({\overline{K}},m)^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\left[\sigma(h_{k})^{2}+\frac{\sigma(h_{k})\sigma(f_{k})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right]
. By Cauchy-Schwarz,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\sigma(h_{k})^{2}+\frac{\sigma(h_{k})\sigma(f_{k})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\sigma(h_{k})^{2}+\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\sigma^{2}(h_{k})\cdot\frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\sigma^{2}(f_{k})}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\sqrt{{\widehat{C}}\epsilon},\text{ by assumptions II and III(b). The claim follows.}

Claim 3.

There exists
C_{3}({\overline{K}},m)
s.t. for all
|s|\leq{\overline{K}}
, and
\|v_{N+1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,

\|{\widehat{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)\|_{1}\leq C_{3}({\overline{K}},m)\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}
.

Proof.

Fix
N
,
v_{N+1}\in L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{N+1})
such that
\|v_{N+1}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, and define
\zeta_{k}\in L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{k})
,
\eta_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
s.t.

\phi_{k}(\cdot):=(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\dots\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N,\xi})v_{N+1}=\zeta_{k}(\cdot)+\eta_{k}

where
\eta_{k}:={\mathbb{E}}\bigl[(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\cdots\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{N,\xi})v_{N+1}(X_{k})\bigr]
, and
{\mathbb{E}}[\zeta_{k}(X_{k})]=0
. Then

\bigl\|{\widehat{\Phi}}_{N}(x,\xi)\bigr\|_{1}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{N}\|\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(\zeta_{k+1}+\eta_{k+1}1)\|_{1}.

(16.10)

By (
16.4
),
|\eta_{k}|\leq 1
. We will now work towards a control of
\zeta_{k}
:

Sub-claim.

We can decompose
\zeta_{k}=\zeta_{k}^{\prime}+\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}
so that for all
|s|\leq{\overline{K}}
,
there exist
{\widehat{C}}_{0},{\widehat{K}}_{0}>0
and
0<{\widehat{\theta}}_{0}<1
s.t. for all
k=1,\ldots,N-2

\|\zeta_{k}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq{\widehat{\theta}}^{2}_{0}\|\zeta_{k+2}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+{\widehat{K}}_{0}\|\zeta_{k+2}^{\prime\prime}\|_{1},

(16.11)

\|\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq{\widehat{C}}_{0}\left(\frac{\sigma(f_{k})+\sigma(f_{k+1})+\sigma(h_{k})+\sigma(h_{k+1})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right).

(16.12)

Proof.

In what follows,
\mathcal{L}_{k}=\mathcal{L}_{k,0}
.
Write

\eta_{k}+\zeta_{k}=\phi_{k}=\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}\right)\phi_{k+2}=\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}\right)\left(\eta_{k+2}+\zeta_{k+2}\right)

=\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\eta_{k+2}+\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\zeta_{k+2}+\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}-\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\phi_{k+2}.

Observe that
\mathcal{L}_{k}1=1
, so
\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\eta_{k+2}=\eta_{k+2}
.
This leads to the decomposition

\zeta_{k}=\underset{\zeta_{k}^{\prime}}{\underbrace{\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\zeta_{k+2}}}+\underset{\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}}{\underbrace{\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}-\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\phi_{k+2}+\eta_{k+2}-\eta_{k}}}

We use this decomposition to define
\zeta_{k}^{\prime},\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}
. This gives the following recursion:

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\zeta^{\prime}_{k+2}+\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\zeta_{k+2}^{\prime\prime},

(16.13)

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}-\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\phi_{k+2}+\eta_{k+2}-\eta_{k}.

Notice that
\zeta_{k}^{\prime},\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}
both have zero means.
Indeed in our setup,
\mu_{j}(E)={\mathbb{P}}(X_{j}\in E)
and
(\mathcal{L}_{k}u)(x)=\mathbb{E}(u(X_{k+1})|X_{k}=x)
,
whence

\int\zeta_{k}^{\prime}d\mu_{k}=\mathbb{E}(\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(X_{k}))=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{E}(\zeta_{k+2}(X_{k+2})|X_{k+1})|X_{k})]=\mathbb{E}(\zeta_{k+2}(X_{k+2}))=0,

and
\mathbb{E}(\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime})=\mathbb{E}(\zeta_{k})-\mathbb{E}(\zeta_{k}^{\prime})=0-0=0
.

To prove the estimates on
\|\zeta^{\prime}_{k}\|_{\infty}
, we first make the following general observations.
If
\psi_{k+2}\in L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S}_{k+2})
, then
\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\right)(x)=\int{\widetilde{p}}(x,z)\psi_{k+2}(z)\mu_{k+2}(dz)
, where

{\widetilde{p}}(x,z)=\int_{\mathfrak{S}_{k+1}}p_{k}(x,y)p_{k+1}(y,z)\mu_{k+1}(dy).

By uniform ellipticity,

{\widetilde{p}}\geq{\varepsilon}_{0}
so we can decompose
{\widetilde{p}}_{k}={\varepsilon}_{0}+(1-{\varepsilon}_{0}){\widetilde{q}}_{k}

where
{\widetilde{q}}_{k}
is a probability density.
Hence if
\psi_{k+2}
has zero mean then

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\right)(x)={\varepsilon}_{0}\int\psi_{k+2}d\mu_{k+2}+(1-{\varepsilon}_{0})\int{\widetilde{q}}_{k}(x,y)\psi_{k+2}(y)\mu_{k+2}(dy)

\displaystyle=(1-{\varepsilon}_{0})\int{\widetilde{q}}_{k}(x,y)\psi_{k+2}(y)\mu_{k+2}(dy).

Thus

\left\|\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\psi_{k+2}\right\|_{\infty}\leq(1-{\varepsilon}_{0})\|\psi_{k+2}\|_{\infty}.

We apply this to
\zeta_{k+2}^{\prime}=\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\zeta^{\prime}_{k+2}+\left(\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\zeta_{k+2}^{\prime\prime}
:

\displaystyle\|\zeta_{k}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\epsilon_{0})\|\zeta_{k}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\zeta_{k+2}^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq(1-\epsilon_{0})\|\zeta_{k}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+\|\mathcal{L}_{k}\|_{L^{1}\to L^{\infty}}\|\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\|_{L^{1}\to L^{1}}\|\zeta_{k+2}^{\prime\prime}\|_{1}\leq(1-\epsilon_{0})\|\zeta_{k}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}+\epsilon_{0}^{-2}\|\zeta_{k+2}^{\prime\prime}\|_{1}.

The last step is because
0\leq p_{n}(x,y)\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}
.
This proves (
16.11
).

Next we analyze
\|\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}
. Since
\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}
has zero mean and
\eta_{k+2}-\eta_{k}
is constant, we can write
\zeta_{k}^{\prime\prime}=\widehat{\zeta}_{k}^{\prime\prime}-\mathbb{E}(\widehat{\zeta}_{k}^{\prime\prime})
with
\widehat{\zeta}_{k}^{\prime\prime}:=\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}-\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)\phi_{k+2}
.
Observe that the kernel of

\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1,\xi}-\mathcal{L}_{k}\mathcal{L}_{k+1}\right)
is bounded by

\textrm{const}\frac{|s|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\int\bigl(\mathbb{F}_{k}(x,z)+\mathbb{F}_{k+1}(z,y)+c_{k}+c_{k+1}\bigr)\mu_{k+1}(dz).

By assumptions II and III,
the
L^{1}
-norm of the kernel is bounded by

\displaystyle O\left(\frac{|s|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\biggl(\|f_{k}-h_{k}\|_{1}+\|f_{k+1}-h_{k+1}\|_{1}\biggr)=O\left(\frac{|s|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\biggl(\|f_{k}\|_{1}+\|h_{k}\|_{1}+\|f_{k+1}\|_{1}+\|h_{k+1}\|_{1}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\frac{|s|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\biggl(\|f_{k}\|_{2}+\|h_{k}\|_{2}+\|f_{k+1}\|_{2}+\|h_{k+1}\|_{2}\biggr).

This implies that
\|\widehat{\zeta}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}=O\left(\frac{|s|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\bigl(\sigma(f_{k})+\sigma(f_{k+1})+\sigma(h_{k})+\sigma(h_{k+1})\bigr),
whence
\|{\zeta}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq 2\|\widehat{\zeta}_{k}^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}=O\left(\frac{|s|}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right)\bigl(\sigma(f_{k})+\sigma(f_{k+1})+\sigma(h_{k})+\sigma(h_{k+1})\bigr).

(
16.12
) and the sub-claim are proved.

We return to the proof of Claim 3.
Iterating the estimate in the sub-claim, we conclude that for some constant
{\overline{C}}

\|\zeta_{k}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq{\overline{C}}\left[{\widehat{\theta}}^{2\lfloor\frac{N-k}{2}\rfloor}_{0}+\sum_{r=1}^{\lfloor\frac{N-k}{2}\rfloor-1}\frac{{\widehat{\theta}}^{2r}_{0}\left(\sigma(f_{k+2r})+\sigma(f_{k+2r+1})+\sigma(h_{k+2r})+\sigma(h_{k+2r+1})\right)}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\right]

\leq{\overline{C}}\widehat{\theta}_{0}^{-1}\left[{\widehat{\theta}}^{N-k}_{0}+\sum_{r=1}^{N-k}\frac{\widehat{\theta}^{r}_{0}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}\biggl(\sigma(f_{k+r})+\sigma(h_{k+r})\biggr)\right].

Since
\mathcal{L}_{j,\xi}
are contractions and

\|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}\|_{L^{\infty}\to L^{1}}\leq C_{1}({\overline{K}},m)\sigma(h_{k})
, this implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\bigl\|\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(\zeta_{k+1}^{\prime})\bigr\|_{L^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq{\overline{C}}C_{1}({\overline{K}},m)\left[\sum_{r}{\widehat{\theta}}^{r}_{0}\sum_{k}\sigma(h_{k})\frac{\sigma(f_{k+r})+\sigma(h_{k+r})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}+\sum_{k}\sigma(h_{k}){\widehat{\theta}}^{N-k}_{0}\right].

As in the proof of Claim 2, it follows from the Cauchy Schwartz inequality, (
16.1
), and assumption III(b) that
the sum over
k
is
O(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
.
Hence

\sum_{k}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(\zeta_{k+1}^{\prime})\right\|_{L^{1}}=O(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}).

(16.14)

Next we claim that

\sum_{k}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(\zeta_{k+1}^{\prime\prime})\right\|_{L^{1}}=O(\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}).

(16.15)

The proof is similar to the proof of (
16.14
), except that now we use (
16.13
) to see that as in the proofs of (
16.5
),(
16.6
) and (
16.12
),

\|\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(\zeta_{k+1}^{\prime\prime})\|_{L^{1}}\leq C_{4}({\overline{K}},m)\sigma(h_{k})\frac{\sigma(f_{k+1})+\sigma(f_{k+2})+\sigma(h_{k+1})+\sigma(h_{k+2})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}

for some constant
C_{4}({\overline{K}},m)
.

(
16.14
) and (
16.15
) give us an
O(\sqrt{\epsilon})
bound for contribution of
\zeta_{k+1}
to (
16.10
).
It remains to estimate the contribution of
\eta_{k+1}
to (
16.10
).

Split

\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}=e^{2\pi imc_{n}}\mathcal{L}_{n}+\mathcal{L}_{n,\xi}^{\prime}
. As before,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(1)=e^{2\pi im(c_{1}+\cdots+c_{k-1})}\mathcal{L}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{k-1}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(1)

(16.16)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}e^{2\pi im(c_{j+1}+\cdots+c_{k-1})}\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{j-1,\xi}\mathcal{L}_{j,\xi}^{\prime}\mathcal{L}_{j+1}\cdots\mathcal{L}_{k-1}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(1).

Since
{\mathbb{E}}(h_{k})=0
,
{\mathbb{E}}[(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}1)(X_{k})]=0.

By exponential mixing (
2.3
),
the first term on the RHS of (
16.16
) has

L^{\infty}
(whence
L^{1}
) norm no larger than

C_{mix}\theta^{k-1}\|\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}1\|_{\infty}\leq{\widehat{C}}_{3}\sigma(h_{k})\theta^{k}

for some constant
{\widehat{C}}_{3}={\widehat{C}}_{3}({\overline{K}},m)
and
0<\theta<1
.
Similarly each summand in the second term on the RHS of (
16.16
) has
L^{1}
norm less than

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}_{j,\xi}^{\prime}\|{\widehat{C}}_{3}\sigma(h_{k})\theta^{k-j}\leq{\widehat{C}}_{4}\sigma(h_{k})\theta^{k-j}\left\|\frac{s}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}(\mathbb{F}_{j}+c_{j})+\xi h_{j}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq{\widehat{C}}_{4}\sigma(h_{k})\theta^{k-j}\left(\frac{\sigma(f_{j})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}+\sigma(h_{j})\right),

for
{\widehat{C}}_{4}={\widehat{C}}_{4}({\overline{K}},m).

So the second term on the RHS of (
16.16
) has norm less than

{\widehat{C}}_{5}\sigma(h_{k})\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta^{k-j}\biggl(\frac{\sigma(f_{j})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}+\sigma(h_{j})\biggr)

(16.17)

for some constant
{\widehat{C}}_{5}
.

It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\left\|\mathcal{L}_{1,\xi}\dots\mathcal{L}_{k-1,\xi}\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{k,\xi}(1)\right\|_{1}
is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{N}\left({\widehat{C}}_{3}\sigma(h_{k})\theta^{k}+{\widehat{C}}_{5}\sigma(h_{k})\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta^{k-j}\biggl(\frac{\sigma(f_{j})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}+\sigma(h_{j})\biggr)\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\widehat{C}}_{3}\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma^{2}(h_{k})}\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{N}\theta^{2k}}+{\widehat{C}}_{5}\sum_{r=0}^{N-1}\theta^{r}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\biggl(\frac{\sigma(f_{j})}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}+\sigma(h_{j})\biggr)\sigma(h_{j+r})
