), we obtain the following:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{x}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]\sim\left[\frac{e^{-V_{N}\mathcal{I}_{N}(\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}})}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}}}\int_{a}^{b}e^{-\xi_{N}t}dt\right]\,\times

\displaystyle\times\underset{\widehat{\rho}_{N}\bigl(\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\bigr)}{\underbrace{\left[e^{V_{N}\mathcal{I}_{N}(\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}})-V_{N}H_{N}(\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}})}\sqrt{H_{N}^{\prime\prime}(\tfrac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}})}\right]}}\times\underset{\overline{\rho}_{N}\bigl(x,\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\bigr)}{\underbrace{\left[h_{1}^{\xi_{N}}(x)\mu_{N+1}\left(\tfrac{1}{h_{N+1}^{\xi_{N}}}\right)\right]}}

Let
\eta_{N}:=\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}
, then
\xi_{N}=\xi_{N}(\eta_{N})
where

\xi_{N}:[{\widehat{a}}_{N},{\widehat{b}}_{N}]\to[-(R+1),(R+1)]
is defined implicitly by
P_{N}^{\prime}(\xi_{N}(\eta))=\eta V_{N}+\mathbb{E}(S_{N})
. Lemma
6.87
shows that
\xi_{N}(\cdot)
is well-defined.

Notice that there exists a constant

L=L(R)
such that
|\eta_{N}|\leq L(R)
. Indeed,
\eta_{N}\in[{\widehat{a}}_{N}^{R},{\widehat{b}}_{N}^{R}]
and
|{\widehat{a}}_{N}^{R}|,|{\widehat{b}}_{N}^{R}|\leq|\mathfs F^{\prime}(\pm R)-\mathfs F^{\prime}(0)|\leq R\sup\limits_{[-R,R]}\mathfs F_{N}^{\prime\prime}
, which is uniformly bounded by Theorem
19.1
(3).

The functions
\widehat{\rho}_{N}:{[-L,L]}\to\mathbb{R}
are defined by

\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\eta):=e^{V_{N}\mathcal{I}_{N}\left(\eta+\frac{\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\right)-V_{N}H_{N}\left(\eta+\frac{\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\right)}\sqrt{{H_{N}^{\prime\prime}}(\eta)}.

Lemma
6.93
and Theorem
19.2
say that there exists
C
such that

C^{-1}\leq\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\eta)\leq C\text{ for all $N$ and $|\eta|\leq L$}.

They also say that for every
\epsilon>0
there are
\delta,N_{\epsilon}>0
s.t.

e^{-\epsilon}\leq\widehat{\rho}_{N}(\eta)\leq e^{\epsilon}\text{ for all $N>N_{\epsilon}$ and $|\eta|\leq\delta$}.

In particular, if
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\to 0
, then
\widehat{\rho}_{N}\bigl(\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\bigr)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}1
.

The functions
\overline{\rho}_{N}:\mathfrak{S}_{1}\times(-c,c)\to\mathbb{R}
are defined by

\overline{\rho}_{N}(x,\eta):=h_{1}(x,\xi(\eta))\mu_{N+1}\left(\frac{1}{h_{N+1}(x,\xi(\eta))}\right).

By Lemma
6.82
, there exists a constant
C
such that

C^{-1}\leq\overline{\rho}_{N}(x,\eta)\leq C\text{ for all $N$ and $|\eta|\leq L$}.

By Lemma
6.84
and the obvious identity
h_{n}(\cdot,0)\equiv 1
,
\|h_{n}^{\xi}-1\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow[\xi\to 0]{}0
uniformly in
n
.
Since
|\xi(\eta)|\leq C|\eta|
, for every
\epsilon>0
there are
\delta,N_{\epsilon}>0
such that

e^{-\epsilon}\leq\overline{\rho}_{N}(x,\eta)\leq e^{\epsilon}\text{ for all $x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}$, $N>N_{\epsilon}$, and $|\eta|\leq\delta$}.

Setting
\rho_{N}:=\widehat{\rho}_{N}\cdot\overline{\rho}_{N}
we complete the proof of theorem in the non-lattice case.
The modifications needed for the lattice case are routine, and are left to the reader.
\square

20.8
Rough bounds in the reducible case.

Proof of Theorem
19.6
:

We proceed as in the proof of Theorem
19.5
in §
20.7
, but using
the rough bounds of
§
16.5

instead of
the precise LLT to estimate the probabilities for the change of measure.

Let
\hslash=100K+1
where
K=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup(\mathsf{f})
.
Then
using Theorem
16.1
and the assumption that
z_{N}\in[\mathfs F_{N}^{\prime}({\varepsilon}),b_{N}^{R}]
we get that there exist a constant
{\overline{c}}={\overline{c}}(R)
and
\xi_{N}:=\xi_{N}\left(\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}}\right)\in[{\varepsilon},R+1]
such that
for all
N
large enough,

{\overline{c}}e^{{-\xi_{N}\hslash}}{\hslash}\leq\frac{\sqrt{V_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in[0,\hslash])}{e^{-V_{N}\mathcal{I}_{N}\left(\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}}\right)}}.

(20.36)

Note that Theorem
16.1
is applicable since
\hslash>2\delta(\mathsf{f})

due to Corollary
3.45
).

Since
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\geq z_{n})\geq{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in[0,\hslash])
the lower bound follows.

Likewise applying Lemma
5.74
we conclude
that there is a constant

C^{*}=C^{*}(R)
s.t
for all
N
large enough we have, uniformly in
j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,

\frac{\sqrt{V_{N}}{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}-z_{N}\in[\hslash j,\hslash(j+1)])}{e^{-V_{N}\mathcal{I}_{N}\left(\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}}\right)}}\leq C^{*}e^{{-\xi_{N}\hslash j}}.

Summing over
j
we obtain the lower bound.

\square

21
Large deviations threshold

The results of this chapter are all stated for
z_{N}
s.t. for some
R>0
and all sufficiently large
N,

\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}\in[{\widehat{a}}_{N}^{R},{\widehat{b}}_{N}^{R}]
. In this section we will discuss how restrictive is this assumption.

We say that a sequence
\{z_{N}\}
is
R
-admissible

if there is a constant
N_{0}
s.t. for
N\geq N_{0}

\exists\xi_{N}\in[-R,R]
such that

\displaystyle P_{N}^{\prime}(\xi_{N})=z_{N}.

A sequence
\{z_{N}\}
is
admissible
if it is
R
-admissible for some
R
.

A number

z
is called
reachable
(respectively
R
-reachable
) if the
sequence
\{zV_{N}\}
is admissible (respectively
R
-admissible).

We denote the set of
R
–reachable points by
\mathcal{C}_{R}
and the set of reachable points by
\mathcal{C}.

Since
P_{N}^{\prime}
is monotone increasing,

\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{C})=(\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{+})

for some

\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}=\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}(\mathsf{X}).

Example 6.94
(Sums of iid’s)

Let
\displaystyle S_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}
where
X_{n}
are iid random variables having law
X
with expectation zero and variance one.
Recall from
Example
6.76

that in this case
\mathcal{F}_{N}
does not depend on
N

10
10

10

Note that in this case we also have
P_{N}(\xi)/N=\mathcal{F}_{N}(\xi)
,
since
\mathcal{L}_{N,\xi}(e^{\xi x_{n+1}})={\mathbb{E}}(e^{\xi X})\cdot e^{\xi x_{n}}
, whence

p_{n}(\xi)=\ln{\mathbb{E}}(e^{\xi X})
.

so by property (ii) of
Example
6.76
we obtain

\mathfrak{c}_{-}=\mathrm{ess\,}\inf(X),\hskip 11.74988pt\mathfrak{c}_{+}=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup(X).

(21.1)

Then
S_{N}/N\in[\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{+}]
almost surely for all
N
, and therefore
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-zN\in(a,b)]
is zero when
z\not\in[\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{+}]
. Henceforth we refer to such
z
as “irrelevant.”

Not all relevant
z
are reachable:
z
is reachable only when
z\in(\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{+})
. Our results do not apply for
z=\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}
. Indeed different asymptotic behavior may hold for
z_{N}
s.t.

\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}}\to\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}
, see Example
6.75
.
Still, the large deviation LLT for
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-zN\in(a,b)]
holds for most “relevant” values of
z
.
Our next example shows that this is not always the case:

Example 6.95

Let
X_{n}=(Y_{n},Z_{n})
where
\{Y_{n}\}
,
\{Z_{n}\}
are two independent sequences of iid
random variables having uniform distribution on
[0,1].
Fix a sequence
\{p_{n}\}
and let

f_{n}(Y_{n},Z_{n})=\begin{cases}Z_{n}&\text{if }Y_{n}>p_{n}\\
2&\text{if }Y_{n}\leq p_{n}.\end{cases}

We now discuss two possible choices of
\{p_{n}\}.

(a)
Let
\mathsf{f}^{\prime}
be defined as above with
p_{n}\equiv\frac{1}{2}.

Then
f_{n}^{\prime}
are iid so by discussion of the Example
6.94
the results of the present
chapter apply to

{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}^{\prime}\in zN+(a,b))
provided that

z\in(0,2)
while the possible range of
\frac{S_{N}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime})}{N}
is
[0,2].

(b)
Let
\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime}
be defined as above with
p_{n}
tending to
0
as
n\to\infty.

Since
{\rm Var}(Z_{n})=\frac{1}{12}
it follows that
\displaystyle V_{N}=(1+o(1))\frac{N}{12}.

We shall show below that in case (b)

\mathfrak{c}_{-}=0,\hskip 11.74988pt\mathfrak{c}_{+}=12.

(21.2)

In other words the results of the present chapter apply to

{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime})\in zN+(a,b))
provided that

z\in(0,1)
. On the other hand, the possible range of
\frac{S_{N}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime})}{N}
is
[0,2]

since for each fixed
N
the distributions of
S_{N}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime})
and
S_{N}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime})
are absolutely continuous with respect
to each other. We will see that the reason our results
do not apply for
z>1
is that in that case
{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}(\mathsf{f}^{\prime\prime})\geq zN)
decays super exponentially.

In this section we discuss methods for computing
\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}
(in particular, proving (
21.2
))
and provide sufficient conditions for good behavior, when
(\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{+})
covers
“most” relevant
z.

Lemma 6.96

\forall R>0

\exists{\varepsilon}={\varepsilon}(R)>0
s.t. if
\{z_{N}\}
is
R
-admissible, and

|{\overline{z}}_{N}-z_{N}|\leq{\varepsilon}V_{N},
then
\{{\overline{z}}_{N}\}
is
(R+1)
-admissible.

Proof

By the uniform strict convexity of
\frac{P_{N}}{V_{N}}
on
[-(R+1),(R+1)]
,
there exists
{\varepsilon}>0
such that

\displaystyle P_{N}^{\prime}(R+1)\geq z_{N}+{\varepsilon}V_{N}
and

\displaystyle P_{N}^{\prime}(-(R+1))\leq z_{N}-{\varepsilon}V_{N}.

\square

Corollary 6.97

(a)
\mathcal{C}
is open, and (b)
if
{\mathbb{E}}(S_{n})\equiv 0
, then
\mathcal{C}
is a non-empty neighborhood of zero.

Proof

Part (a) follows from Lemma
6.96
. Part (b) follows from (
20.23
).

\square

Without the assumption
\mathbb{E}(S_{N})=0
,
\mathcal{C}
may be empty.
Even though Theorem
19.2
provides many admissible sequences,
the associated
\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}}
need not converge:

Example 6.98

An example with
\mathcal{C}=\varnothing
and with admissible sequences
\{z_{N}\}
such that
z_{N}/V_{N}
does not converge.

Let
N_{k}=10^{k}.
Consider
X_{n}=a_{n}+U_{n}
where
U_{n}
are iid having uniform distribution on
[0,1]

and

a_{n}=\begin{cases}10&\text{if }N_{2k}\leq n<N_{2k+1},\\
-10&\text{if }N_{2k+1}\leq n<N_{2k+2}.\end{cases}

With probability one

\displaystyle S_{N_{2k+1}}>N_{2k+1}
,

\displaystyle S_{N_{2k}}<-N_{2k}.

The first inequality gives
\mathcal{C}\cap(-\infty,0]=\varnothing,

the second one gives
\mathcal{C}\cap[0,+\infty)=\varnothing.

Hence
\mathcal{C}=\varnothing.

In this example, if
\{z_{N}\}
is an admissible sequence then

\frac{z_{N_{2k+1}}}{N_{2k+1}}\geq 1
and
\frac{z_{N_{2k}}}{N_{2k}}\leq-1.

Since
V_{N}=\frac{N}{12}
the ratio
\frac{z_{N}}{V_{N}}
does not converge.

Theorem 21.1

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a
uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
, with essential range
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}
.
The following are equivalent:

(a)

\{z_{N}\}
is admissible.

(b)

\exists{\varepsilon}>0,\eta>0
s.t.

\forall\{{\overline{z}}_{N}\}
with
|{\overline{z}}_{N}-z_{N}|\leq{\varepsilon}V_{N}

and
\forall a_{N},b_{N}
s.t.

|a_{N}|,|b_{N}|\leq 10
and
b_{N}-a_{N}>1
we have

{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\in{\overline{z}}_{N}+(a_{N},b_{N}))\geq\eta^{V_{N}}.

(c)

\exists{\varepsilon}>0,\eta>0
s.t.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\geq z_{N}+{\varepsilon}V_{N})\geq\eta^{V_{N}}
and

{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\leq z_{N}-{\varepsilon}V_{N})\geq\eta^{V_{N}}.

Example 6.99

The case
{\varepsilon}=0
.

Let
\displaystyle S_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}X_{n}
where
X_{n}
are iid supported on
[\alpha,\beta]
and such that

X
has an atom on the right edge:
{\mathbb{P}}(X=\beta)=\gamma>0.
Then

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq\beta N]={\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}=\beta N]=\gamma^{N}

while

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq\beta N+1]=0.
Thus
\{\beta N\}
is not admissible. This example shows
that taking
{\varepsilon}=0
in part (c) of Theorem
21.1

gives a condition which is
not
equivalent to
the conditions (a)–(c) of the theorem.

Proof

\mathbf{(a)\Rightarrow(b):}

If
\{z_{N}\}
is admissible then by Lemma
6.96

\exists{\varepsilon}>0
such that if
|{\overline{z}}_{N}-z_{N}|\leq{\varepsilon}V_{N}
then
\{{\overline{z}}_{N}\}
is admissible. Now
(b) follows from formula (
20.36
)
in the proof of Theorem
19.6
.

\mathbf{(b)\Rightarrow(c):}

The bound

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq z_{N}+{\varepsilon}V_{N}]\geq\eta^{V_{N}}
follows from part (b) with
{\overline{z}}_{N}=z_{N}+{\varepsilon}V_{N},

a_{N}=0,

b_{n}=1.1.
The lower bound is similar.

\mathbf{(c)\Rightarrow(a):}
Our assumptions on the essential range imply that
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is not center-tight, and therefore
V_{N}\to\infty
. By Lemma
6.86
(5)
P_{N}(R)-V_{N}\mathfs F_{N}(R)
is eventually bounded, and therefore for some
c(R)>0
and all
N>N(R)
,

e^{P_{N}(R)}\geq c(R){\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{RS_{N}}\right)\geq c(R){\mathbb{E}}\left(e^{RS_{N}}1_{[S_{N}\geq z_{N}+\epsilon V_{N}]}\right)\geq c(R)\eta^{V_{N}}e^{R(z_{N}+{\varepsilon}V_{N})}.

This implies that for all
N
large enough

P_{N}(R)\geq R(z_{N}+({\varepsilon}/2))V_{N}
.

Since
P_{N}(0)=0
the Mean Value Theorem tells us that

\exists\xi_{N}^{+}\in[0,R]
such that

\displaystyle P_{N}^{\prime}(\xi_{N}^{+})\geq z_{N}+\frac{{\varepsilon}V_{N}}{2}.

Likewise we can find
\xi_{N}^{-}\in[-R,0]
such that

\displaystyle P_{N}^{\prime}(\xi_{N}^{-})\leq z_{N}-\frac{{\varepsilon}V_{N}}{2}.
By the Intermediate Value Theorem

\exists\xi_{N}\in[\xi_{N}^{-},\xi_{N}^{+}]
s.t.
P_{N}^{\prime}(\xi_{N})=z_{N}.

\square

Corollary 6.100

Under the assumptions of the previous theorem,
if
{\mathbb{E}}(S_{N})\equiv 0
then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{+}=\sup\{z:\mathfrak{I}(z)<\infty\},
where

\mathfrak{I}(z)=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{|\log{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\in zV_{N}+[-1,1])|}{\log V_{N}}.

Proof

By Theorem
21.1
(b), if
z\in(\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{+})
, then
\mathfrak{I}(z)<\infty
. So
\mathfrak{c}_{+}\leq\sup\{z:\mathfrak{I}(z)<\infty\}
.

To see the other inequality, note that
\mathfrak{c}_{+}>0
(by Corollary
6.97
), and
\mathfrak{I}(0)<\infty
(by (
20.36
)). We will show that

\frac{1}{2}\sup\{z:\mathfrak{I}(z)<\infty\}<{\overline{z}}<\sup\{z:\mathfrak{I}(z)<\infty\}\Rightarrow\text{${\overline{z}}$ is admissible,}

(21.3)

and deduce that
\mathfrak{c}_{+}\geq\sup\{z:\mathfrak{I}(z)<\infty\}
.

Fix
{\overline{z}}
as in (
21.3
), then
\exists{\varepsilon}>0
s.t.
\mathfrak{I}({\overline{z}}+2\epsilon)<\infty
and
{\overline{z}}-\epsilon>0
. Necessarily
\exists\eta>0
s.t. for all
N
large enough

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq({\overline{z}}+\epsilon)V_{N}]\geq{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\in({\overline{z}}+2{\varepsilon})V_{N}+[-1,1]]\geq\eta^{N}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\leq({\overline{z}}-\epsilon)V_{N}]\geq{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\leq 0]=\frac{1}{2}+o(1)\geq\eta^{N}

By Theorem
21.1
(c),
z
is admissible.

\square

We say that
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
and
(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})

are related by the change of measure

if

f_{n}\equiv{\widetilde{f}}_{n}
and
\pi_{n}(x,dy)
is equivalent to
\widetilde{\pi}_{N}(x,dy)
with

{\overline{{\varepsilon}}}\leq\frac{\widetilde{\pi}_{n}(x,dy)}{\pi_{n}(x,dy)}\leq{\overline{{\varepsilon}}}^{-1}.

Lemma 6.101

Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an a.s. uniformly bounded additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
.
If
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
and
(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})

are related by the change of measure
and
V_{N}\geq cN
for some
c>0
,
then

\{z_{N}\}
is
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
-admissible
iff
\{z_{N}\}
is
(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})
-admissible.

Proof

Since
\mathsf{X}
is uniformly elliptic,
\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}
is uniformly elliptic. The exponential mixing bounds for uniformly elliptic chains imply that
\widetilde{V}_{N}:={\rm Var}[S_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})]
and
V_{N}:={\rm Var}[S_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})]
are both
O(N)
. Without loss of generality,
cN\leq V_{N}\leq c^{-1}C_{N}
.

Under the assumptions of the Lemma, the structure constants of
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
are equal to the structure constants of
(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})
up to bounded multiplicative error.
By Theorem
2.30
,
\widetilde{V}_{N}:={\rm Var}[S_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})]\asymp V_{N}
as
N\to\infty
. So
\exists\widetilde{c}>0
s.t.
\widetilde{c}N\leq\widetilde{V}_{N}\leq\widetilde{c}^{-1}N
.

Let
\{z_{N}\}
be
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
-admissible. Then there are
{\varepsilon}>0,\eta>0
such that

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq z_{N}+{\varepsilon}V_{N}]\geq\eta^{N},\hskip 11.74988pt{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\leq z_{N}-{\varepsilon}{V}_{N}]\geq\eta^{N}.

It follows that

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})\geq z_{N}+\widetilde{{\varepsilon}}\widetilde{V}_{N}]\geq\widetilde{\eta}^{N},\hskip 11.74988pt\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})\leq z_{N}-\widetilde{{\varepsilon}}\widetilde{V}_{N}]\geq\widetilde{\eta}^{N}

where
\widetilde{\eta}=\eta{\overline{{\varepsilon}}}
and
\widetilde{{\varepsilon}}:=\widetilde{c}c{\varepsilon}
.
Hence
\{z_{N}\}
is
\widetilde{\mathsf{X}}
-admissible.

\square

Lemma 6.102

Let
\mathsf{f}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
be two a.s. uniformly bounded additive functionals
on the same uniformly elliptic Markov chain. Suppose
V_{N}:={\rm Var}[S_{N}(\mathsf{f})]\to\infty
and

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\|S_{N}(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})-S_{N}(\mathsf{f})\|_{\infty}}{V_{N}}=0.

(21.4)

Then
\{z_{N}\}
is
\mathsf{f}
-admissible
iff
\{z_{N}\}
is
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
-admissible.

Proof

We write
S_{N}=S_{N}(\mathsf{f})
, and
\widetilde{S}_{N}=S_{N}(\mathsf{f})
. By the assumptions of the lemma,
\widetilde{V}_{N}:={\rm Var}(\widetilde{S}_{N})\sim V_{N}
as
N\to\infty
.

Let
\{z_{N}\}
be
\mathsf{f}
-admissible. By Theorem
21.1
(b),
there are
{\varepsilon}>0,\eta>0
such that

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq z_{N}+{\varepsilon}V_{N}]\geq\eta^{N},\hskip 11.74988pt{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\leq z_{N}-{\varepsilon}V_{N}]\geq\eta^{N}.

It now follows from (
21.4
) that for large
N

\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\left[\widetilde{S}_{N}\geq z_{N}+\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\widetilde{V}_{N}\right]\geq\eta^{N},\hskip 11.74988pt\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}\left[\widetilde{S}_{N}\leq z_{N}-\frac{{\varepsilon}}{2}\widetilde{V}_{N}\right]\geq\eta^{N}.

Hence
\{z_{N}\}
is
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
-admissible.

\square

We end this section by proving (
21.2
).

Proof of (
21.2
).
To show that
\mathfrak{c}_{+}\leq 12
assume by contradiction that
\mathrm{int}(\mathfs C)
contained some
z>12
.
Then Theorem
19.6
would imply that

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq zV_{N}]\geq\eta^{V_{N}}\text{ for some }\eta>0.

(21.5)

Note that

\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi f_{n}(Y_{n},Z_{n})})=\log\left(p_{n}e^{2\xi}+(1-p_{n})\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi U[0,1]})\right)

=\log\left(p_{n}e^{2\xi}+(1-p_{n})\frac{e^{\xi}-1}{\xi}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\log\frac{e^{\xi}-1}{\xi}

because
p_{n}\to 0.
So

\mathfs F_{N}(\xi)=\frac{1}{V_{N}}\log\prod_{n=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\left(e^{\xi f_{n}(Y_{n},Z_{n})}\right)\sim\frac{12}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\log\mathbb{E}\left(e^{\xi f_{n}(Y_{n},Z_{n})}\right)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}12\log\left(\frac{e^{\xi}-1}{\xi}\right).

The last expression is strictly smaller than
12\xi
if
\xi>0.

Therefore for any
\xi>0
we have for sufficiently large
N,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(e^{\xi S_{N}}\right)\leq e^{12V_{N}\xi}.
By Markov’s inequality,

{\mathbb{P}}\left[S_{N}\geq zV_{N}\right]\leq e^{(12-z)V_{N}\xi}\text{ for all $\xi>0$ and $N$ sufficiently large}.

But this is incompatible with (
21.5
), since
z>12.

Therefore
\mathfrak{c}_{+}\leq 12
.

Next we show that
(0,12)\in
Int
(\mathcal{C}).
By Theorem
21.1
it suffices to show that
for every
\mathfrak{z}:=\frac{z}{12}\in(0,1)
,

\exists{\varepsilon},\eta>0
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[A_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}(N)]{\geq}\eta^{N}
where

A_{\varepsilon}^{+}(N)={\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq(\mathfrak{z}+{\varepsilon}){N}],\hskip 11.74988ptA_{\varepsilon}^{-}(N)={\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\leq(\mathfrak{z}-{\varepsilon})N].

Take
{\varepsilon}>0
so small that
\overline{\mathfrak{z}}:=z+{\varepsilon}<1.

Since
S_{N}\geq Z_{N}
we have

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq\overline{\mathfrak{z}}N]\geq{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{n=1}^{N}Z_{n}\geq\overline{\mathfrak{z}}N\right].

The RHS is greater than some
{\overline{\eta}}^{N}
in view of
Theorem
19.6
and equation (
21.1
)
from Example
6.94
. It follows that
\mathfrak{c}^{+}=12.

The proof of the fact that
\mathfrak{c}^{-}=0
is similar but easier.

\Box

22
Notes and references

The reader should note the difference between the LLT for large deviations and the large deviations principle (LDP): LLT for large deviations give the asymptotics of
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
or
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}>z_{N}]
; The LDP gives the asymptotics of the
logarithm
of
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}>z_{N}]
, see Dembo & Zeitouni
Dembo-Zeitouni
and Varadhan
Varadhan-LD-Book
.

The interest in precise asymptotics for
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}>z_{N}]
in the regime of large deviations goes back to the first paper on large deviations, by Cramér
Cramer-LDP
. That paper gave an asymptotic series expansion for
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})>x]
for
S_{N}=
sums of iid’s. The first sharp asymptotics for
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)]
appear to be the work of
Richter
Richter
,
(
Ibragimov-Linnik
, chapter 7)
and Blackwell & Hodges
Blackwell-Hodges
.

These results were refined by many authors, with important contributions by Petrov
Petrov-LD
, Linnik
Linnik
, Moskvin
Moskvin
, Bahadur & Ranga Rao
Bahadur-Ranga-Rao
, Statulavicius
Statulevicius-1966
and Saulis
Saulis
.
We refer the reader to the books of Ibragimov & Linnik
Ibragimov-Linnik
, Petrov
Petrov-Book
, and of Saulis & Statulevicius
Saulis-Statulevicius-Book
for accounts of these and other results, and also to the survey of Nagaev
Nagaev-LD
for a discussion of the case of sums of independent random variables which are not necessarily identically distributed.

Plachky and Steinebach
Plachky-Steinebach
and Chaganty & Sethuraman
Chaganty-Sethuraman-1985
;
Chaganty-Sethuraman-1993
proved LLT for large deviations for arbitrary sequences of random variables
T_{n}
(e.g. sums of dependent random variables), subject only to assumptions on the asymptotic behavior of the normalized log-moment generating functions of
T_{n}
and their Legendre-Fenchel transforms (their rate functions). Our LLT for large deviations are in the spirit of these results.

Corollary
6.78
is an example of a limit theorem conditioned on a large deviation. For other examples of such results, in the context of statistical physics, see
Derrida-Sadhu
.

We comment on some of the technical devices in the proofs. The “change of measure” trick discussed in section
20.1
goes back to Cramér
Cramer-LDP
and is a standard idea in large deviations.
In the classical homogeneous setup, a single parameter
\xi_{N}=\xi
works for all times
N
, but in our inhomogeneous setup, we need to allow the parameter
\xi_{N}
to depend
N
. For other instances of changes of measure which involve a time dependent parameter, see Dembo & Zeitouni
Dembo-Zeithouni-LD-parameter-dependent
and references therein.

Birkhoff’s Theorem on the contraction of Hilbert’s projective metric is proved in
Bi
. Results similar to Lemma
6.81
on the existence of the generalized eigenfunction
h^{\xi}_{n}
were proved by many authors in many different contexts, see for example
K
,
FS
,
BG
,
Rugh
,
Du
,
Hafouta-Kifer-Book
,
Hafouta-Sequential
. The analytic dependence of the generalized eigenvalue and eigenvector on the parameter
\xi
was considered in a different context (the top Lyapunov exponent) by Ruelle
Ruelle-Analyticity
and Peres
Peres
. Our proof of Lemma
6.84
follows closely a proof in
Du
. For an account of the theory of real-analyticity for vector valued functions, see
Die
and
Wh
.

Chapter 7
Miscellaneous examples and special cases

In this chapter we consider several special cases where our general results take stronger form. These include homogeneous Markov chains, asymptotically homogeneous additive functionals.
We also explain how continuity assumptions can be used to strengthen the results of the previous
chapters.

23
Homogenous Markov chains

A Markov chain
\mathsf{X}=\{X_{n}\}
is called
homogeneous
if its state spaces and transition probabilities do not depend on
n

\mathfrak{S}_{n}=\mathfrak{S},\hskip 11.74988pt\mu_{n}=\mu,\hskip 11.74988pt\pi_{n}(x,dy)=\pi(x,dy)\hskip 11.74988pt\text{ for all }n,

and
X_{n}
is stationary.

An additive functional on a homogeneous Markov chain is called
homogeneous
if
\mathsf{f}=\{f_{n}\}
and

f_{n}(x,y)=f(x,y)\text{ for all }n.

The LLT for homogeneous countable state Markov chains is due to Nagaev. The following version, which allows continuous spaces, follows from results in
HenHer
.

Theorem 23.1

Let
\mathsf{f}
denote an a.s. uniformly bounded homogeneous additive functional on a uniformly elliptic homogeneous Markov chain
\mathsf{X}
.

(1)

Asymptotic Variance:
The limit
\displaystyle\sigma^{2}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Var}(S_{N})

exists, and

\sigma^{2}=0
iff we can represent
f(X_{1},X_{2})=a(X_{2})-a(X_{1})+\kappa\text{ a.s.}

where
a:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is a bounded measurable
function and
\kappa
is a constant, equal to
\mathbb{E}(f(X_{1},X_{2}))
.

(2)

CLT:
If
\sigma^{2}>0
, then

\frac{S_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{N}}
converges in probability as
N\to\infty

to the Gaussian distribution with mean zero and variance
\sigma^{2}
.

(3)

LLT:
If
\sigma^{2}>0
then exactly one of the following options holds:

(a)

Non-Lattice LLT: If

\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{\sqrt{N}}\to z
, then for every interval
[a,b]
,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}-z_{N}\in[a,b]]=[1+o(1)]\frac{e^{-z^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}N}}(b-a),\text{ as }N\to\infty;

(b)

Periodicity: There exist
\kappa\in\mathbb{R},t>0
and a bounded measurable function
a:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

f(X_{1},X_{2})+a(X_{1})-a(X_{2})+\kappa\in t\mathbb{Z}
a.s.

Proof.

Let
V_{N}:={\rm Var}(S_{N})
and
f_{k}:=f(X_{k},X_{k+1})
, and assume without loss of generality that
\mathbb{E}[f(X_{1},X_{2})]=0
.

Proof of part (1):

By stationarity,
\mathbb{E}(f_{n})=0
for all
n
, and so

V_{N}={\mathbb{E}}\left(f_{n}^{2}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\mathbb{E}}(f_{n}^{2})+2\sum_{1\leq m<n=N}{\mathbb{E}}(f_{n}f_{m}).

By stationarity,
\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(f_{n}f_{m})={\mathbb{E}}(f_{0}f_{n-m})
and

\frac{1}{N}V_{N}={\mathbb{E}}(f_{0}^{2})+2\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}{\mathbb{E}}(f_{0}f_{k})\left(1-\frac{k}{N}\right).

|{\mathbb{E}}(f_{0}f_{m})|
decays exponentially (Prop.
1.11
), so
\sum|\mathbb{E}(f_{0}f_{k})|<\infty
, whence

\sigma^{2}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}{\rm Var}(S_{N})={\mathbb{E}}(f_{0}^{2})+2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}(f_{0}f_{k}).

(23.1)

(This identity for
\sigma^{2}
is called the
Green-Kubo formula
.)

Let
u_{n}
denote the structure constants of
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
. The homogeneity assumptions implies that
u_{n}
is independent of
n
, say
u_{n}=u
for all
n
. It follows that
U_{N}\equiv u_{3}^{2}+\cdots+u_{N}^{2}=(N-2)u^{2}
. Now we have two cases:

(I)

u>0
: In this case by Theorem
2.30
,
V_{N}\asymp U_{N}\asymp N
, whence
\sigma^{2}>0
.

(II)

u=0
: In this case,
{\rm Var}(S_{N})=O(1)
by Theorem
2.30
, whence
\sigma^{2}=0
and
\mathsf{f}
is center-tight. By the Gradient Lemma, (Lemma
2.32
),

f(X_{1},X_{2})=a_{2}(X_{2})-a_{1}(X_{1})+\kappa
for some
a_{1},a_{2}:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
bounded and measurable and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
. In the homogeneous case, we may take
a_{1}\equiv a_{2}
, see (
6.7
) in the proof of the Gradient Lemma. So
f(X_{1},X_{2})=a(X_{2})-a(X_{1})+\kappa
a.s.

Proof of part (2):
This follows from part (1) and Dobrushin’s CLT.

Proof of part (3):
By homogeneity, the structure constants
d_{n}(\xi)
are independent of
n
, and they are all equal to
d(\xi):=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma}-1|^{2})^{1/2}
, where
\Gamma
is the balance of a random hexagon at position
3
. So
D_{N}(\xi)=\sum_{k=3}^{N}d^{2}_{k}(\xi)=(N-3)d^{2}(\xi)
.

If
d(\xi)\neq 0
for all
\xi\neq 0
, then
D_{N}(\xi)\to\infty
for all
\xi\neq 0
,
f
is irreducible by Theorem
9.2
.
and the LLT follows
from Theorem
12.1
.

If
d(\xi)=0
for some
\xi\neq 0
, then
D_{N}(\xi)=0
for all
N
,
\xi
is in the co-range of
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
, and our reduction lemma says that there exist
c_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and uniformly bounded measurable
a_{n}:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
and
h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
such that
\sum h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
converges a.s., and

f(X_{n},X_{n+1})+a_{n}(X_{n})-a_{n+1}(X_{n+1})+h_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+\kappa_{n}\in\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}\text{ a.s.}

Let
A_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1},\ldots):=a_{n}(X_{n})+\sum_{k\geq n}h_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1})
, then for all
n

f_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+A_{n}(X_{n},X_{n+1},\ldots)-A_{n+1}(X_{n+1},X_{n+2},\ldots)+\kappa_{n}\in\frac{2\pi}{\xi}\mathbb{Z}\text{ a.s. .}

(23.2)

We need to replace
A_{i}(X_{i},X_{i+1},\ldots)
by
a(X_{i})
. This is the purpose of the following proposition, whose proof will complete the proof of the theorem:

Proposition 7.103

Let
\mathsf{X}
be a uniformly elliptic homogeneous Markov chain with state space
(\mathfrak{S},\mathcal{B},\mu)
, and let
f:\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function such that
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f(X_{1},X_{2})|<\infty
. If there exist measurable functions
A_{n}:\mathfrak{S}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\kappa_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfying (
23.2
),
then there exist
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
and a measurable
a:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

f(X_{n},X_{n+1})+a(X_{n})-a(X_{n+1})+\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ a.s. for all $n$.}

Proof

Throughout this proof, let
\Omega:=\mathfrak{S}^{\mathbb{N}}
, equipped with the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfs F
generated by the cylinder sets

[A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}]:=\{x\in\mathfrak{S}^{\mathbb{N}}:x_{i}\in A_{i}\ (i=1,\ldots,n)\}\ \ (A_{i}\in\mathcal{B})

and the unique probability measure
m
on
(\Omega,\mathfs F)
s.t.

m[A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}]={\mathbb{P}}[X_{1}\in A_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\in A_{n}]

Let
\sigma:\Omega\to\Omega
denote the left-shift map,
\sigma[(x_{n})_{n\geq 1}]=(x_{n+1})_{n\geq 1}
. The stationarity of
\mathsf{X}
translates to the shift invariance of
m
:
m\circ\sigma^{-1}=m
.

Step 1 (
Zero-One Law
):
Let
\sigma^{-n}\mathfs F:=\{\sigma^{-n}(A):A\in\mathfs F\}
, then for every
A\in\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\sigma^{-n}\mathfs F
, either
m(A)=0
or
m(A)=1
.

Proof.
Fix a cylinder
A:=[A_{1},\ldots,A_{\ell}]
.

By uniform ellipticity, for every cylinder
B=[B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}]
,

m(A\cap\sigma^{-({\ell+1})}B)=m([A_{1},\ldots,A_{\ell},\ast,B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}])\geq\epsilon_{0}m(A)m(B).

Applying this to cylinders

[\underbrace{\Omega,\ldots,\Omega}_{k\text{ times}},B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}]

we find that

m(A\cap\sigma^{-(\ell+k)}[B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}])\geq\epsilon_{0}m(A)m(B)\text{ for all }k\geq 1.

By the monotone class theorem,

m(A\cap\sigma^{-(\ell+k)}E)\geq\epsilon_{0}m(A)m(E)\text{ for {every} }\mathfs F\text{--measurable }E\text{ and }k\geq 1.

(23.3)

Suppose
E\in\bigcap_{k\geq 1}\sigma^{-n}\mathfs F
, and let
A
be an arbitrary cylinder of length

\ell
.
By the assumption on
E
,
E=\sigma^{-n}E_{n}
with
E_{n}\in\mathfs F
and
n>\ell
. So

m(A\cap E)=m(E\cap\sigma^{-n}E_{n})\geq\epsilon_{0}m(A)m(E_{n})=\epsilon_{0}m(A)m(E).

We see that
\frac{m(A\cap E)}{m(A)}\geq\epsilon_{0}m(E)
for all cylinders
A
, whence

\mathbb{E}(1_{E}|X_{1},\ldots,X_{\ell})\geq\epsilon_{0}m(E)\text{ for all $\ell$}.

By the martingale convergence theorem,
1_{E}\geq\epsilon_{0}m(E)
a.e., whence
m(E)=0
or
1
.

Step 2:

Identify
f
with a function
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
s.t.
f[(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}]=f(x_{1},x_{2})
. Then there exist
A:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
measurable and
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
s.t.

f+A-A\circ\sigma+\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ almost surely. }

Proof.
The assumptions of the proposition say that there exist
A_{n}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
measurable and
\kappa_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
s.t.

f\circ\sigma^{n}+A_{n}\circ\sigma^{n}-A_{n+1}\circ\sigma^{n+1}+\kappa_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\text{ $m$-a.e. for every $n$.}

Let
w_{n}:=e^{2\pi iA_{n}}
, then

e^{2\pi if\circ\sigma^{n}}\frac{w_{n}\circ\sigma^{n}}{w_{n+1}\circ\sigma^{n+1}}=1

m
-a.s. Since
m\circ\sigma^{-1}=m
we have

e^{2\pi if}\frac{w_{n}}{w_{n+1}\circ\sigma}=1\text{ a.s. for all $n$}
. This gives the chain of identities

\displaystyle w_{n}=e^{-2\pi if}w_{n+1}\circ\sigma=e^{-2\pi i(f+f\circ\sigma)}w_{n+2}\circ\sigma^{2}=\cdots=e^{-2\pi i\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}f\circ\sigma^{k}}w_{n+k}\circ\sigma^{k}.

It follows that
w_{n}/w_{n+1}=(w_{n+k}/w_{n+k+1})\circ\sigma^{k}
for all
k
. Hence

w_{n}/w_{n+1}
is
\sigma^{-k}\mathfs F
–measurable for all
k
. By the zero-one law,
w_{n}/w_{n+1}
is constant almost surely.
In particular, there exists a constant
c
such that
A_{2}-A_{1}\in c+\mathbb{Z}

m
–a.e., and the step follows with
A:=A_{1}
and
\kappa:=\kappa_{1}+c
.

Step 3:

There exists
a:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
constant on cylinders of length one such that
f+a-a\circ\sigma+\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}

m
-a.e.

Proof.
Let
L:L^{1}(\Omega)\to L^{1}(\Omega)
denote the
transfer operator
of
\sigma:\Omega\to\Omega
, which describes the action of
\sigma
on mass densities on
\Omega
:

\sigma_{\ast}[\varphi d\mu]=L\varphi d\mu.

Formally,
L\varphi:=\frac{dm_{\varphi}\circ\sigma^{-1}}{dm},\text{ where }m_{\varphi}:=\varphi dm
. We will need the following (standard) facts:

(a)

If
\varphi
depends only on the first
m
-coordinates, then
L\varphi
depends only on the first
(m-1)\vee 1
–coordinates. Specifically,
(L\varphi)[(y_{i})_{i\geq 1}]=\Phi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m-1})
where

\Phi(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m-1}):=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})|X_{i}=y_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq m-1)];

(b)

L\varphi
is characterized by the condition
\int\psi L\varphi dm=\int\psi\circ\sigma\varphi dm

\forall\psi\in L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S})
;

(c)

L(\varphi\psi\circ\sigma)=\psi L\varphi

\forall\varphi\in L^{1},\psi\in L^{\infty}
;

(d)

L1=1
;

(e)

\forall\varphi\in L^{\infty}
,
L^{n}\varphi\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\int\varphi dm
in
L^{1}
.

Part (b) is standard. Parts (c) and (d) follow from (b) and the
\sigma
-invariance of
m
.
Part (a) follows from (b), and the identity

\displaystyle\int\psi L\varphi dm=\int\psi dm_{\varphi}\circ\sigma^{-1}=\int\psi\circ\sigma\varphi dm=\mathbb{E}[\psi(X_{2},X_{3},\ldots)\varphi(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(X_{2},X_{3},\ldots)\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X_{1},\ldots,X_{m})|X_{2},X_{3},\ldots]\bigr)\overset{!}{=}\mathbb{E}\bigl(\psi(X_{2},X_{3},\ldots)\mathbb{E}[\varphi|X_{2},\ldots,X_{m}]\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int\psi\Phi dm

where
\overset{!}{=}
is because of the Markov property.
To see part (e) note that it is enough to consider
\varphi\in L^{\infty}
such that
\int\varphi dm=0
(otherwise work with
\varphi-\int\varphi dm
). For such functions,

\displaystyle\|L^{n}\varphi\|_{1}=\int\mathrm{sgn}(L^{n}\varphi)L^{n}\varphi\,dm=\int\mathrm{sgn}(L^{n}\varphi)\circ\sigma^{n}\cdot\varphi\,dm

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{sgn}(L^{n}\varphi)\circ\sigma^{n}\mathbb{E}(\varphi|\sigma^{-n}\mathfs F)\,dm\leq\int|\mathbb{E}(\varphi|\sigma^{-n}\mathfs F)|dm

The integrand is uniformly bounded (by
\|\varphi\|_{\infty}
), and it converges pointwise to
\mathbb{E}(\varphi|\bigcap\sigma^{-n}\mathfs F)=\mathbb{E}(\varphi|\{\varnothing,\Omega\})=\mathbb{E}(\varphi)=0
.

Let
w:=e^{2\pi iA}
where
A:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is as in step 2, and assume w.l.o.g. that
\kappa=0
(else absorb it into
f
). Set
S_{n}=f+f\circ\sigma+\cdots+f\circ\sigma^{n-1}
, then
e^{-2\pi if}=w/w\circ\sigma
, whence
e^{-2\pi iS_{n}}=w/w\circ\sigma^{n}
. By (c), for all
\varphi\in L^{1}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle wL^{n}(e^{-2\pi iS_{n}}\varphi)

\displaystyle=L^{n}(e^{-2\pi iS_{n}}w\circ\sigma^{n}\varphi)=L^{n}(w\varphi)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{L^{1}}\int w\varphi dm.

Since
|w|=1
a.e.,
\exists m\geq 2
and
\exists\varphi=\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})
bounded measurable so that
\int w\varphi dm\neq 0
. For this
\varphi
, we have

w^{-1}=\text{$L^{1}$-}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{L^{n}(e^{-2\pi iS_{n}}\varphi)}{\int w\varphi dm}.

We claim that the right-hand-side depends only on the first coordinate. This is because
e^{-2\pi if}\varphi
is function of the first
m
coordinates, whence by (a),

L(e^{-2\pi if}\varphi)
is a function of the first
(m-1)\vee 1
coordinates. Applying this argument again we find that
L^{2}(e^{-2\pi iS_{2}}\varphi)=L[e^{-2\pi if}L(e^{-2\pi if}\varphi)]
is a function of the first
(m-2)\vee 1
coordinates. Continuing by induction, we find that
L^{n}(e^{-2\pi iS_{n}}\varphi)
is a function of
(m-n)\vee 1
-coordinates, and eventually of the first coordinate only.

So
w^{-1}
is an
L^{1}
-limit of a functions of the first coordinate. Therefore we can write
w[(x_{i})_{i\geq 1}]=\exp[2\pi ia(x_{1})]
a.e., where
a:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is measurable. By construction
e^{2\pi if}w/w\circ\sigma=1
, so
f(X_{1},X_{2})+a(X_{1})-a(X_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}
almost surely. By stationarity,

f(X_{n},X_{n+1})+a(X_{n})-a(X_{n+1})\in\mathbb{Z}
almost surely for all
n
.

\square

We now determine the domain of the rate functions for large deviations. We note that the results of
Chapter
6
concern

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq zV_{N}]={\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq z\sigma^{2}(1+o(1))N]
, while in large deviation literature
it is common to use the normalization
{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq zN].
To simplify the comparison with
other results we will assume till the end of this section that
\sigma^{2}=1
which can always be achieved
by scaling
f.

Let
\mathcal{S}_{N}=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup S_{N}
. Using the stationarity of
\{X_{n}\}
and the homogeneity of
\mathsf{f}
it is not difficult to see that
\mathcal{S}_{n+m}\leq\mathcal{S}_{n}+\mathcal{S}_{m}
, and therefore the limit

\mathfrak{s}_{+}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{ess\,}\sup S_{N}}{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}}{N}

exists. Repeating the same argument for
(-\mathsf{f})
gives that

\mathfrak{s}_{-}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{ess\,}\inf S_{N}}{N}

exists as well.

Recall the notation for large deviation thresholds
\mathfrak{c}_{-},\mathfrak{c}_{+}
introduced in
§
21
.

Theorem 23.2

Let

\mathsf{f}
be an a.s. uniformly bounded homogenous additive functional on a uniformly elliptic homogeneous Markov chain, and assume
\mathsf{f}
has zero mean and asymptotic variance
\sigma^{2}=1.

Then

\mathfrak{c}_{+}=\mathfrak{s}_{+}
and
\mathfrak{c}_{-}=\mathfrak{s}_{-}
.

Proof

We prove the first identity, the second one is similar.

First, for any
{\varepsilon}>0
,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}\geq(\mathfrak{s}_{+}+{\varepsilon})N]=0
for sufficiently large
N
, whence by Theorem
21.1
,
\mathfrak{c}_{+}\leq\mathfrak{s}_{+}.

Let
K:=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
.
For every
{\varepsilon}>0
, for all sufficiently large
M
,

\delta_{M}:={\mathbb{P}}[S_{M}\geq(\mathfrak{s}_{+}-{\varepsilon})M]>0.

Let
\sigma(X_{i},\ldots,X_{j})
denote the
\sigma
-field generated by
X_{i},\ldots,X_{j}
. By uniform ellipticity, if
E\in\sigma(X_{1},\ldots,X_{M+1})
and
F\in\sigma(X_{M+3},\ldots,X_{2M+3})
, then

{\mathbb{P}}[E\cap F]\geq\epsilon_{0}{\mathbb{P}}(E){\mathbb{P}}(F)
(see (
23.3
)).
Consequently,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}[S_{2(M+2)}\geq 2(\mathfrak{s}_{+}-{\varepsilon})M-2K]

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{M}f_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1})\geq M(\mathfrak{s}_{+}-\epsilon)\ ,\sum_{k=M+3}^{2M+2}f_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1})\geq M(\mathfrak{s}_{+}-\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon_{0}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{M}f_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1})\geq M(\mathfrak{s}_{+}-\epsilon)\right]{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{k=M+3}^{2M+2}f_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1})\geq M(\mathfrak{s}_{+}-\epsilon)\right].

Thus by stationarity,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{2(M+2)}\geq 2(\mathfrak{s}_{+}-{\varepsilon})M-2K]\geq\epsilon_{0}\delta_{M}^{2}.

Applying this argument repeatedly, we find that for each
\ell
,

{\mathbb{P}}[S_{(M+2)\ell}\geq((\mathfrak{s}_{+}-{\varepsilon})M-2K)\ell]\geq\left({\varepsilon}_{0}\delta_{M}^{2}\right)^{\ell}.

Now Corollary
6.100
tells us that for all sufficiently large
M
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{c}_{+}\geq\frac{(\mathfrak{s}_{+}-{\varepsilon})M-2K}{M+2}.

Letting
M\to\infty
we obtain
\mathfrak{c}_{+}\geq\mathfrak{s}_{+}-{\varepsilon}.
Since
{\varepsilon}
is arbitrary,

\mathfrak{c}_{+}\geq\mathfrak{s}_{+}
.

\square

24
Perturbations of homogeneous chains

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})

be a bounded homogenous additive functional on a uniformly elliptic Markov chain with stationary measure
\mu
and transition probability
\pi(x,dy)=p(x,y)\mu(dy)
.
We consider
non-homogeneous
perturbations
(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})
of the form

{\widetilde{f}}_{n}(x,y)=f(x,y)+g_{n}(x,y)\ ,{\widetilde{\pi}}_{n}(x,dy)={\widetilde{p}}_{n}(x,y)\mu(dy).

We assume that the strength of the perturbation decays at infinity.
Namely for each
{\varepsilon}>0
there is
n_{0}
such that for
n\geq n_{0}

\|g_{n}\|_{\infty}\leq{\varepsilon}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt1-{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{{\widetilde{p}}_{n}(x,y)}{p(x,y)}\leq 1+{\varepsilon}.

Theorem 24.1

If the additive functional
\mathsf{g}
is center tight on
\mathsf{X}
, then
G_{ess}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=G_{ess}({\mathsf{X}},\mathsf{f}).
If
\mathsf{g}
is not center tight then

G_{ess}(\widetilde{\mathsf{X}},\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=\mathbb{R}.

Proof

We note that it suffices to prove the result in the case
\widetilde{p}_{n}\equiv p.
Indeed by our assumptions,

\frac{1}{2}\leq\frac{{\widetilde{p}}_{n}(x,y)}{p(x,y)}\leq 2

(24.1)

if
n
is sufficiently large. Since discarding
a finite number of terms does not change the essential range (since any functional vanishing for
large
n
is center tight) we may assume that (
24.1
) holds for all
n.

Now Example
3.50
shows that the essential range of the
functionals defined via
p
and via
\widetilde{p}_{n}
are the same. Thus we assume henceforth
that
\widetilde{p}_{n}\equiv p
for all
n.

If
\mathsf{g}
is center tight then the essential ranges of
\mathsf{f}
and
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}
are the same,
so we shall assume that
\mathsf{g}
is not center tight, and prove that
D_{N}(\xi,\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})\to\infty
for every
\xi\neq 0
.
Let
\mathfrak{d}:=d_{n}(\xi,\mathsf{f})
(the RHS does not depend on
n
by stationarity).

Suppose first that
\mathfrak{d}\neq 0.
By Lemma
1.15
(2) we have

\mathfrak{d}^{2}=d_{n}^{2}(\xi,\mathsf{f})\leq 8\left[d_{n}(\xi,\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})^{2}+d_{n}(\xi,{\mathsf{g}})^{2}\right].

Next, the assumption
\|g_{n}\|_{\infty}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0
implies that
d_{n}^{2}(\xi,{\mathsf{g}})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0
.
Accordingly
\displaystyle d_{n}(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}},\xi)^{2}\geq\frac{\mathfrak{d}^{2}}{10}
for all
n
large enough, so that

D_{N}(\xi,\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})\to\infty
as needed.

Next assume
\mathfrak{d}=0.
In this case for any hexagon
P_{n}
we have

e^{i\xi\Gamma(\mathsf{f},P_{n})}=1
, where
\Gamma(\mathsf{f},\cdot)
denotes the balance for the additive functional
\mathsf{f}
.
Hence

e^{i\xi\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}},\cdot)}=e^{i\xi\Gamma({\mathsf{g}},\cdot)}
,
and so

d_{n}(\xi,\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=d_{n}(\xi,{\mathsf{g}}).

Let
\gamma_{N}:=\max_{n\geq N}\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|g_{n}|
, and
fix
\tau_{0}>0
such that

|e^{it}-1|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}t^{2}\text{ for all }|t|<\tau_{0}.

If
n\geq N
and

0<|\eta|\leq\tau_{0}(6\gamma_{N})^{-1}
, then
(
10.7
) tells us that

d_{n}^{2}(\eta,\mathsf{g})\geq\frac{\eta^{2}}{2}u_{n}^{2}(\mathsf{g})\text{ for all $n>N+3$}.

By assumption,
\mathsf{g}
is not center-tight, so
\sum u_{n}^{2}(\mathsf{g})=\infty
. It follows that

D_{N}(\eta,g)\to\infty
for all
0<|\eta|\leq\tau_{0}(6\gamma_{N})^{-1}
.

By assumption,
\gamma_{N}\to 0
, so
D_{N}(\eta,g)\to\infty
for all
\eta\neq 0
.
It follows that the co-range of
\mathsf{g}
equals
\{0\}
, and the essential range of
\mathsf{g}
equals
\mathbb{R}
.
\square

Next we discuss the large deviation thresholds for
\widetilde{\mathsf{f}}.

Theorem 24.2

(a) If
\mathsf{f}
is not a coboundary then

\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}(\mathsf{f})=\mathfrak{s}_{\pm}(\mathsf{f}).

(b) If
\mathsf{f}
is a homogeneous
gradient,
{\mathbb{E}}(g_{n})=0
for all
n
, and
\mathsf{g}
is not center tight,
then
\mathfrak{c}_{+}(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=+\infty,\hskip 11.74988pt\mathfrak{c}_{-}(\widetilde{\mathsf{f}})=-\infty.

Proof

The proof of part (a) is very similar to the proof of Lemma
6.102
so we omit it.

In the proof of part (b) we may assume that
\mathsf{f}=0
since adding a homogeneous gradient does not change the
large deviation threshold. In particular in the rest of the proof we will abbreviate

S_{N}=S_{N}(\mathsf{g}),

S_{n_{1},n_{2}}=S_{n_{1},n_{2}}(\mathsf{g})=\sum_{k=n_{1}}^{n_{2}-1}g_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1}),

V_{N}={\rm Var}(S_{N}(\mathsf{g})).

Since
\mathsf{g}
is not center tight,
V_{N}\to\infty.

Assume without loss of generality that
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|g|\leq\frac{1}{2}
,
then
{\rm Var}[g_{k}(X_{k},X_{k+1})]\leq 1
for all
k
.
Divide the interval
[0,N]
into blocks

\biggl[n_{1},n_{2}\biggr]\cup\{n_{2}+1\}\cup\biggl[n_{3},n_{4}\biggr]\cup\cdots\cup\biggl[n_{k},n_{k+1}\biggr]\cup\{n_{k+1}+1\}\cup\biggl[n_{k+2},N\biggr]

where
n_{i}
is increasing,

1\leq\mathrm{Var}(S_{n_{j},n_{j+1}})\leq 2

for
j\leq k+1
, and
{\rm Var}(S_{n_{k+2},N})\leq 1
.

Since
\|g_{n}\|_{\infty}\to 0
,
\min\{n_{j+1}-n_{j}:\ell\leq j\leq k\}\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{}\infty
.
Also,
the analysis of §
20.4
shows that

\frac{1}{V_{N}}\sum_{j}\mathrm{Var}(S_{n_{j},n_{j+1}})\to 1.

In particular, the number of blocks
\beta_{N}
, is between
V_{N}/2
and
3V_{N}/2.

Let
\displaystyle M_{j}=\max_{n_{j}\leq l\leq n_{j+1}}\|g_{l}\|_{\infty}.
Note that
M_{j}\to 0.

Therefore applying Dobrushin CLT to the array
\displaystyle\{g_{l}/M_{j}\}_{n_{j}\leq l\leq n_{j+1}}

we conclude that
S_{n_{j},n_{j+1}}/\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(S_{n_{j},n_{j+1}})}
is asymptotically normal.
In particular, for each
z>0
there exists
\eta=\eta(z)>0
such that for
j
large enough and all
x_{j}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n_{j}}

{\mathbb{P}}_{x_{j}}(S_{n_{j},n_{j+1}}\geq 3z)\geq\eta.

(24.2)

A uniform ellipticity argument similar to the one we used in the proof of Theorem
23.2
gives

{\mathbb{P}}_{x_{j}}(S_{N}\geq\beta_{N}z)\geq c\eta^{\beta_{N}},

where
c
incorporates the contribution of blocks (with small
j
) where (
24.2
) fails.

Now Corollary
6.100
implies that
\mathfrak{c}^{+}\geq z.
Since
z
is arbitrary,
\mathfrak{c}^{+}=+\infty.

A similar argument
shows that
\mathfrak{c}_{-}(\mathsf{g})=-\infty.

\square

25
Small additive functionals.

The perturbations of
\mathsf{f}\equiv 0
were analyzed in the previous section, however, since this case is
of independent interest it makes sense to summarize the results obtained for this particular case.

Theorem 25.1

Let
\mathsf{g}
be a uniformly bounded additive functional of uniformly elliptic Markov chain.
Suppose that
{\mathbb{E}}(g_{n})=0
and that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|g_{n}\|_{\infty}=0.
Then

either
\mathsf{g}
is center tight in which case
\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}g_{n}
converges almost surely

or
\mathsf{g}
is not center tight in which case
S_{N}(\mathsf{g})

satisfies non lattice LLT (
12.1
) and
\mathfrak{c}_{\pm}(\mathsf{g})=\pm\infty.

Proof

The non-center tight case was analyzed in §
24
. In the center tight case
the results of Chapter
2
tell us that
g
can be decomposed as

g_{n}(x,y)=c_{n}+a_{n+1}(y)-a_{n}(x)+h_{n}(x,y)\hskip 11.74988pt\text{where}\hskip 11.74988pt\sum_{n}{\rm Var}(h_{n})<\infty.

Changing
a_{n}
if necessary we may assume that
{\mathbb{E}}(a_{n})=0
in which case

{\mathbb{E}}(g_{n})=0={\mathbb{E}}(h_{n}+c_{n}).

Therefore the additive functional

\widetilde{\mathsf{h}}=\mathsf{h}+\mathsf{c}
has zero mean and finite variance. Hence
by Theorem
5.4

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(h_{n}+c_{n})
converges almost surely.
In summary
S_{N}(\mathsf{g})-a_{N}+a_{1}
converges almost surely, and hence

S_{N}(\mathsf{g})-a_{N}
converges almost surely. On the other hand equation
(
6.7
) shows that
\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}a_{N}=0

completing the proof.

\square

The following result which a direct consequence of Theorem
25.1
shows that
for small additive functionals a vague limit of the local distribution of
S_{N}
always exists.

Corollary 7.104

Let
\mathsf{g}
satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
25.1
. Then either
and
S_{N}
converges a.s. to some random variable

\mathcal{S}
in which case for each continuous compactly supported function
\phi

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\mathbb{E}}(\phi(S_{N}))={\mathbb{E}}(\phi(\mathcal{S}))

or
S_{N}
satisfies a non-lattice LLT. That is, for each continuous compactly supported function
\phi

for each sequence
z_{N}
such that the limit
\displaystyle z=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{z_{N}}{\sqrt{V_{N}}}
exists
we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}V_{N}{\mathbb{E}}(\phi(S_{N}))={\mathbb{E}}(\phi(\mathcal{S}))=\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi(s)ds.

26
Equicontinuous additive functionals

In this section we examine the consequences of
topological
assumptions on
\mathsf{f}
and
\mathsf{X}
.
Specifically we will say that
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is
equicontinuous
if

(T)

(\mathfrak{S}_{n},\mathcal{B}_{n},\mu_{n})
are complete separable metric spaces,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
are the Borel
\sigma
-algebras, and
\mu_{n}
are Borel probability measures;

(S)

for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
x_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
n\geq 1
,
\mu_{n}[B(x_{n},\epsilon)]>\delta
. Here
B(x,{{\varepsilon}}):=\{y\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}:{\rm dist}(x,y)<{{\varepsilon}}\}
.

(U)

for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
and
x_{n},y_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,

{\rm dist}(x_{n},y_{n})<\delta\Rightarrow|f_{n}(x_{n})-f_{n}(y_{n})|<\epsilon
.

26.1
Range.

Theorem 26.1

Suppose
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
is equicontinuous and a.s. uniformly bounded. Assume in addition the following:

(a)

One-step ellipticity condition:

\exists\epsilon_{0}
s.t. for every
n
,
\pi_{n}(x,dy)=p_{n}(x,y)\mu_{n+1}(dy)
where
\epsilon_{0}\leq p_{n}(x,y)\leq\epsilon_{0}^{-1}
.

(b)

\mathfrak{S}_{n}
are all connected.

Then
\mathsf{f}
is either irreducible with algebraic range
\mathbb{R}
, or it is center tight.

Proof

Choose
c_{1}>0
such that

\displaystyle|e^{i\theta}-1|^{2}=4\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\geq c_{1}\theta^{2}
for all
|\theta|\leq 0.1
.
We fix
\xi\neq 0
, and consider the following two cases:

(I)

\exists N_{0}
such that
|\xi\Gamma(P)|<0.1
for every position
n
hexagon
P
, for each
n\geq N_{0}
.

(II)

\exists n_{k}\uparrow\infty
and
\exists
position
n_{k}
hexagons
P_{n_{k}}
such that
|\xi\Gamma(P_{n_{k}})|\geq 0.1
.

In case (I), for all
n\geq N_{0}
,
d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma}-1|^{2})\geq c_{1}\mathbb{E}(\Gamma^{2})\equiv c_{1}u_{n}^{2}
.
So either
\sum u_{n}^{2}=\infty
and then
\sum d_{n}^{2}(\xi)=\infty
for all
\xi\neq 0
, and
\mathsf{f}
is irreducible with essential range
\mathbb{R}
; or
\sum u_{n}^{2}<\infty
and then
\mathsf{f}
is center-tight by Corollary
2.31
.

In case (II), for every
k
there is a position
n_{k}
hexagon

P_{n_{k}}
with
|\xi\Gamma(P_{n_{k}})|\geq 0.1.
There is also a position
n_{k}
hexagon
P_{n_{k}}^{\prime}
with balance zero (such hexagons always exist because we can take

y_{n_{k}-1}=x_{n_{k}-1},

y_{n_{k}}=x_{n_{k}}
). We would like to apply the intermediate value to deduce the existence of a position
n_{k}
hexagon
\overline{P}_{n_{k}}
such that
0.05<\xi\Gamma(\overline{P}_{n_{k}})<0.1
. To do this we note that:

\circ

Because of the one-step ellipticity condition, the space of position
n_{k}
hexagons is homeomorphic to
\mathfrak{S}_{n_{k}-2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n_{k}-1}^{2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n_{k}}^{2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{n_{k}}
.

\circ

The product of connected topological spaces is connected.

\circ

Real-valued continuous functions on connected topological spaces satisfy the intermediate value theorem.

\circ

The balance of hexagon depends continuously on the hexagon.

So
\overline{P}_{n_{k}}
exists. Necessarily,
|e^{i\xi\Gamma(\overline{P}_{n_{k}})}-1|\geq c_{1}\xi^{2}\Gamma^{2}(\overline{P}_{n_{k}})=:c_{2}
.

Write
\displaystyle{\overline{P}}_{n_{k}}
in coordinates:
\displaystyle{\overline{P}}_{n_{k}}:=\left({\overline{x}}_{n_{k}-2};\begin{array}[]{c}{\overline{x}}_{n_{k}-1}\\
{\overline{y}}_{n_{k}-1}\end{array};\begin{array}[]{c}{\overline{x}}_{n_{k}}\\
{\overline{y}}_{n_{k}}\end{array};{\overline{y}}_{n_{k}+1}\right)
.
By the equicontinuity of
\mathsf{f}
,
\exists\epsilon>0
such that
|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}-1|>\frac{1}{2}c_{2}
for every hexagon
P
whose coordinates are in the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of the coordinates of
{\overline{P}}_{n_{k}}
.
By the equicontinuity of
\mu_{n}
and the one-step ellipticity condition, this collection of hexagons
P
have hexagon measure
\geq\delta
for some
\delta>0
independent of
k
. So

d_{n_{k}}^{2}(\xi)\geq\frac{1}{2}c_{2}\delta.

Summing over all
k
, we find that
\sum d_{n_{k}}^{2}(\xi)=\infty
. Since
\xi\neq 0
was arbitrary,
(\mathsf{X},\mathsf{f})
has essential range
\mathbb{R}
.

\square

26.2
Large deviation threshold.

Lemma 7.105

Suppose that
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
are metric spaces,
f_{n}
are equicontinuous, and
for each
{\varepsilon}>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that
if
p_{n}(x,y)>0
then

\pi_{n}(x,B(y,{\varepsilon}))>\delta.

(26.1)

Suppose that
V_{N}>cN
and that

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\inf_{x_{1},\dots,x_{N+1}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}(x_{j},x_{j+1})}{V_{N}}<z<\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\sup_{x_{1},\dots,x_{N+1}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}(x_{j},x_{j+1})}{V_{N}}.

Then
z\in\mathcal{C}.

Note that assumption (
26.1
) is satisfied whenever
\mathsf{X}
satisfies (S) and the one step ellipticity condition.
We also remark that Example
6.95
shows that
equicontinuity assumption on
\mathsf{f}
is essential.

Proof

Fix
N.
Consider first the case where
z>\frac{{\mathbb{E}}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}.

By assumption there is an
{\varepsilon}
such that for all sufficiently large
n
there is a sequence
{\overline{x}}_{1},\dots,{\overline{x}}_{N+1}

such that

\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}({\overline{x}}_{j},{\overline{x}}_{j+1})\geq(z+{\varepsilon})V_{N}.

By ellipticity, for each
x\in\mathfrak{S}_{1}
there a sequence
{\widetilde{x}}_{1},{\widetilde{x}}_{2}\dots{\widetilde{x}}_{N+1}
such that

{\widetilde{x}}_{1}=x
and

\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}({\widetilde{x}}_{j},{\widetilde{x}}_{j+1})\geq(z+{\varepsilon})V_{N}-4K,\text{ where }K:=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|.

(In fact one can take
{\widetilde{x}}_{j}={\overline{x}}_{j}
for
j\geq 3
.) By uniform continuity of
f_{j}
and the fact that

V_{N}
grows linearly, there is

r
such that if
X_{j}\in B(\widetilde{x}_{j},r)
for
j\leq N+1
then

\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}(X_{j},X_{j+1})\geq(z+{\varepsilon}/2)V_{N}-4K.

By (
26.1
) there is
\delta>0
such that

{\mathbb{P}}_{x}(X_{j}\in B({\widetilde{x}}_{j},r))\geq\delta^{N}.

Hence

{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\geq(z+{\varepsilon}/3)V_{N})\geq\delta^{N}.

Next, by the CLT and the assumption that
z>\frac{\mathbb{E}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}
, if
{\varepsilon}
is small enough and
N
is large enough, then
11
11

11

Alternatively, combining Theorem
19.2
(1)) and Theorem
19.6

(applied to
-S_{N}
)
we get that
\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(S_{N}\leq-\frac{{\mathbb{E}}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}-{\varepsilon}\right)\geq\delta^{N}
provided that

{\varepsilon}
is small enough and
\delta
is close to
1.

{\mathbb{P}}(S_{N}\leq(z-{\varepsilon})V_{N})\geq\delta^{N}
.
Now Theorem
21.1
shows that
z\in\mathcal{C}.

The case
z<\frac{{\mathbb{E}}(S_{N})}{V_{N}}
is analyzed similarly now using the estimate

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\inf_{x_{1},\dots,x_{N+1}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}(x_{j},x_{j+1})}{V_{n}}\leq z-{\varepsilon}.

\square

Corollary 7.106

Under the assumptions of Lemma
7.105
if

\mathfrak{z}^{-}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\inf_{x_{1},\dots,x_{N+1}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}(x_{j},x_{j+1})}{V_{N}}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{exists then}\hskip 11.74988pt\mathfrak{z}^{-}=\mathfrak{c}^{-},

\mathfrak{z}^{+}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\sup_{x_{1},\dots,x_{N+1}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}(x_{j},x_{j+1})}{V_{N}}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{exists then}\hskip 11.74988pt\mathfrak{z}^{+}=\mathfrak{c}^{+}.

Proof

We will prove the second statement, the first one is similar.

\mathfrak{z}^{+}\leq\mathfrak{c}^{+}
by Lemma
7.105
. On the other hand if

z>\mathfrak{z}^{+}
then for large
N
,

{\mathbb{P}}\left(S_{N}>V_{N}z\right)=0.
Hence
\mathfrak{c}^{+}\leq\mathfrak{z}^{+}.

\square

We now restate the result of the last
corollary in a slightly different way under an extra assumption.
Namely, we suppose that

\mathfrak{S}_{n}
are compact &
\forall x_{n},x_{n+1}:p_{n}(x_{n},x_{n+1})>0

(26.2)

Definition 7.107

Let
\mathcal{M}_{N}
denote the space of sequences
\displaystyle\mathbf{x}\in\prod_{n}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that if

\mathbf{y}_{n}=\mathbf{x}_{n}
for
n\geq N+1
then

\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n+1})\geq\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n+1}).

Denote
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\bigcap_{N=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}_{N}.

The elements of
\mathcal{M}
will be called
minimizers
.

The properties of
\mathcal{M}_{N}
are summarized below.

Lemma 7.108

Suppose that (
26.2
) holds and that
f_{n}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\to[-K,K]
are continuous. Then

(a)

\mathcal{M}_{N}
are closed sets.

(b)

If
N\geq M
then
\mathcal{M}_{N}\subset\mathcal{M}_{M}
.

(c)

\mathcal{M}
is non empty.

(d)

If
\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n+1})=\inf_{\mathbf{y}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n+1})

then
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}.

(e)

If
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}
then

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n+1})\leq\inf_{\mathbf{y}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n+1})+2K.

Proof

(a) If
\mathcal{M}_{N}\ni\mathbf{x}^{j}\xrightarrow[j\to\infty]{}\mathbf{x}\not\in\mathcal{M}_{N}
, then there would exist
\overline{\mathbf{x}}
such that

\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{n}=\mathbf{x}_{n}
for
n\geq N+1
, and

\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{n},\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{n+1})<\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n+1}).

Let
\mathbf{y}^{j}
be the sequence such that
\mathbf{y}^{j}_{n}=\mathbf{x}^{j}_{n}
for
n\geq N+1
,

\mathbf{y}^{j}_{n}=\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{n}
for
n\leq N
. By the continuity of
f
,

\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{y}_{n}^{j},\mathbf{y}_{n+1}^{j})<\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{x}_{n}^{j},\mathbf{x}_{n+1}^{j})

for large
j
contradicting,
\mathbf{x}^{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}.

Next let
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}
and
\mathbf{x}_{n}=\mathbf{y}_{n}
for
n\geq M
with
N>M.

Then

\sum_{n=1}^{M}\left[f_{n}(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n+1})-f_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n+1})\right]=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\left[f_{n}(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n+1})-f_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n+1})\right]\geq 0.

This proves (b).

Combining (a) and (b) we see that
\mathcal{M}_{n}
are nested compact sets, hence their intersection is
non-empty.

(d) is clear.

Next, let
\mathbf{x}
be the argmin of
\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\mathbf{z}_{n},\mathbf{z}_{n+1})

and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{M}_{N}.
Let
\mathbf{z}
be such that

\mathbf{z}_{n}=\mathbf{x}_{n},
for
1\leq n\leq N-1
and
\mathbf{z}_{n}=\mathbf{y}_{n}
for
n\geq N.

Then

\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{n}(\mathbf{y}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{n+1})\leq\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{n}(\mathbf{z}_{n},\mathbf{z}_{n+1})\leq\sum_{n=1}^{N}f_{n}(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n+1})+2K

proving (e).

\square

If, in addition to the assumptions of Lemma
7.108
we also suppose that

\mathsf{f}
satisfies (U), then part (e) of the lemma implies that for each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{M}
(which is non-empty by part (c))

\mathfrak{c}_{-}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(\mathbf{x}_{n},\mathbf{x}_{n+1}).

27
Notes and references

Theorem
23.1
is well-known, see
N
;
HenHer
;
RE
;
GH
;
PP90
.
We note that in the homogeneous
setting the assumptions on
f
can be significantly weakened. In particular, the assumption that

f
is bounded can be replaced by the assumption that the distribution of
f
is in the domain
of attraction of the Gaussian distribution
N
, one can allow
f
to depend on infinitely
many
X_{n}
assuming that the dependence of
f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots)
on
(x_{n},x_{n+1},\ldots)
decays exponentially in
n

GH
, and the ellipticity
assumption can be replaced by the assumption that the generator has a spectral gap

N
;
HenHer
. In particular, the LLT holds under the Doeblin condition saying that
\exists{\varepsilon}_{0}>0

and a measure
\zeta
on
\mathfrak{S}
such that

\pi(x,dy)={\varepsilon}_{0}\zeta+(1-{\varepsilon}_{0})\widetilde{\pi}(x,dy)

where
\widetilde{\pi}
is an arbitrary transition probability (cf. equation (
2.2
) in the proof of
Lemma
1.10
).
There are also versions of this theorem for
f
in the domain of attraction of a stable law, see
Aaronson-Denker-LLT
.

The aforementioned weaker conditions however are not sufficient
to get LLT in the large deviation regime, in fact large deviation probabilities could be polynomially
small for unbounded functions, see
Wentzell
.

There is a vast literature on the sufficient conditions for the Central Limit Theorem for homogenous chains,
see
DL
;
Gordin
;
GL
;
HenHer
;
KV
;
KLO
;
MW
and references wherein, however, the local limit
theorem is much less understood, see notes to Chapter
4
.

The characterization of coboundaries in terms of vanishing of the asymptotic variance
\sigma^{2}

is due to Leonov
Leonov
. A large number of papers discuss the regularity of the gradients
in case an additive functional is a gradient, see

BHN
;
dLMM
;
KK96
;
Liv71
;
Liv72
;
PP06
;
PP90
;
Wil

and the references wherein. Our approach is closest to
D-Prev
;
KK96
;
PP90
.
We note that the condition
u(f)=0
which is sufficient for
f
being a coboundary, is
simpler than the equivalent condition
\sigma^{2}=0.
For example for finite chains,
to compute
\sigma^{2}
one needs to compute infinitely many correlations

{\mathbb{E}}(f_{0}f_{n})
while checking that
u=0
involves checking balance of finitely many
hexagons.

Inhomogeneous Markov processes arising from perturbations of homogeneous Markov chains as in section
24
arise naturally in some stochastic optimization algorithms such as the Metropolis algorithm. For large deviations and other limit theorems for such examples, see
Dietz-Sethuraman-Electronic
;
Dietz-Sethuraman-LDP
and references therein.

Minimizers play important role is statistical mechanics where they are called
ground states
.
See e.g.
Sinai-SM
;
RAS
. In the case the phase spaces
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
are non-compact and/or
the observable
f(x,y)
is unbounded, the minimizers have an interesting geometry, see e.g.

ConIt
. For finite states we have the following remarkable result
Br03
:
for each
d
there is a constant

p(d)
such that for any homogeneous Markov chain with
d
states for any additive functional
we have

\mathfrak{s}_{+}=\max_{q\leq p}\frac{1}{q}\max_{x_{1},\dots x_{q}}\left[f(x_{1},x_{2})+\dots+f(x_{q-1},x_{q})+f(x_{q},x_{1})\right].

This result is false for more general homogenous chains, consider for example the case

\mathfrak{S}=\mathbb{N}
and
f(x,y)=1
if
y=x+1
and
f(x,y)=0
otherwise.

Corollary
7.104
was proven in
D-Ind
for inhomogeneous sums of independent
random variables (in the independent case one does not need the assumption that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|g_{n}\|_{\infty}=0
since the gradient obstruction does not appear in the
independent case).

Chapter 8
LLT for Markov chains in random environment

We prove quenched local limits theorems for Markov chains in random environment with stationary ergodic noise processes.

28
Markov chains in random environment

Informally, Markov chains in random environment (MCRE) are Markov chains whose transition probabilities depend on a noisy parameter
\omega
which varies in time.
12
12

12

MCRE should not be confused with “random walks in random environment,” see §
31
.
It is customary to model the time evolution of
\omega
by orbits of a dynamical system called the “noise process.” Here are the formal definitions:

Noise process:
This is an ergodic
measure preserving invertible Borel transformation
T
on a standard
measure space
(\Omega,\mathfs F,{m})
. “
Invertible
” means that there exists
\Omega_{1}\subset\Omega
of full measure such that
T:\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{1}
is injective and surjective, and
T^{-1},T:\Omega_{1}\to\Omega_{1}
are measurable.
13
13

13

Invertibility is convenient, but not necessary. Non-invertible ergodic noise processes can always be replaced by their ergodic and invertible natural extensions. See
(
Cornfeld-Fomin-Sinai-Book
, Ch. 10)
“
Measure preserving
” means that
for every
E\in\mathfs F
,
m(T^{-1}E)=m(E)
. “
Ergodic
”
means that for every
E\in\mathfs F
,

T^{-1}E=E\Rightarrow m(E)=0
or
m(E^{c})=0
.

If
m(\Omega)<\infty
then we will speak of a
finite noise process
, and we will always normalize
m
so that
m(\Omega)=1
. If
m(\Omega)=\infty
, then we will speak of an
infinite noise process
. The infinite noise processes we consider here will all be defined on
\sigma
-finite non-atomic measure spaces. Such processes arise naturally in the study of noise driven by a null recurrent Markov chain, see Example
8.113
below.

Markov chains in Random Environment (MCRE):
A MCRE with noise process
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
is given by the following data:

\circ

State space:
A separable complete metric space
\mathfrak{S}
, with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathfs B
.

\circ

Random transition kernel:
A measurable family of Borel probability measures
\pi(\omega,x,dy)
on
(\mathfrak{S},\mathfs B)
, indexed by
(\omega,x)\in\Omega\times\mathfrak{S}
. Measurability means that
(\omega,x)\mapsto\int\varphi(y)\pi(x,\omega,dy)
is measurable for every bounded Borel
\varphi:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
.

\circ

Initial probability distribution:
A measurable family of Borel probability measures
\mu_{\omega}
on
(\mathfrak{S},\mathfs B)
indexed by
\omega\in\Omega
,
Measurability means that for all bounded Borel
\varphi:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\omega\mapsto\int\varphi(x)\mu_{\omega}(dx)
is measurable.

This data gives for each
\omega
an inhomogeneous Markov chain
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}=\{X^{\omega}_{n}\}
with state space
\mathfrak{S}
, initial distribution
\mu_{\omega}
, and
transition kernels

\pi_{n}^{\omega}(x,dy)=\pi(T^{n}\omega,x,dy).

Here a some examples. Suppose
(\mathfrak{S},\mathfs B,\mu_{0})
is a standard probability space,
S
is a finite or countable set, and
\{\pi_{i}(x,dy)\}_{i\in S}
are transition probabilities on
\mathfrak{S}
.

Example 8.109
(Bernoulli noise)

Consider the noise process
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
where

\circ

\Omega=S^{\mathbb{Z}}=\{(\cdots,\omega_{-1},\omega_{0},\omega_{1},\cdots):\omega_{i}\in S\}
;

\circ

\mathfs F
is generated by the
cylinders

{{}_{k}}[a_{k},\ldots,a_{n}]:=\{\omega\in\Omega:\omega_{i}=a_{i},k\leq i\leq n\}

\circ

\{p_{i}\}_{i\in S}
are non-negative numbers s.t.
\sum p_{i}=1
, and
m
is the unique measure s.t.
m({{}_{k}}[a_{k},\ldots,a_{n}])=p_{a_{k}}\cdots p_{a_{n}}
for all cylinders.

\circ

T:\Omega\to\Omega
is the
left shift map
,
T[(\omega_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}]=(\omega_{i+1})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}

It’s well-known that
(\Omega,\mathfs F,\mu,T)
is an ergodic probability preserving map.

Define

\pi(\omega,x,dy):=\pi_{\omega_{0}}(x,dy)
.
Notice that
\pi(T^{n}\omega,x,dy)=\pi_{\omega_{n}}(x,dy)
, and
\omega_{n}
are iid’s taking the values
i\in S
with probabilities
p_{i}
. Since
\omega_{n}
are iid,
\{\mathsf{X}^{\omega}:\omega\in\Omega\}
represent a random Markov chain whose transition probabilities vary randomly and independently in time.

Example 8.110
(Positive recurrent Markov noise)

Suppose
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a stationary ergodic Markov chain with state space
S
and a stationary probability vector
(p_{s})_{s\in S}
. In particular,
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is positive recurrent.
Let:

\circ

\Omega:=\{(\omega_{i})\in S^{\mathbb{Z}}:{\mathbb{P}}[Y_{1}=\omega_{i},Y_{2}=\omega_{i+1}]\neq 0\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
;

\circ

\mathfs F
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the cylinders (see above);

\circ

m
is the unique (probability) measure such that
m({{}_{k}[}a_{k},\ldots,a_{n}])={\mathbb{P}}[Y_{k}=a_{k},\ldots,Y_{n}=a_{n}]
for all cylinders;

\circ

T
is the left shift map (see above).

Define as before,
\pi(\omega,x,dy):=\pi_{\omega_{0}}(x,dy)
. The resulting MCRE represents a Markov chain whose transition probabilities at time
n=1,2,3,\ldots
are
\pi_{Y_{n-1}}(x,dy)
.

Example 8.111
(General stationary ergodic noise processes)

The previous construction works verbatim with any stationary ergodic stochastic process
\{Y_{n}\}
taking values in
S
.
The assumption that
S
is countable can be replaced by requiring only that
S
be complete, separable, metric space, see e.g.
Doob
.

Example 8.112
(Quasi-periodic noise)

Let
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
be the circle rotation:
\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{1}:=\{\omega\in\mathbb{C}:|\omega|=1\}
;
\mathfs F
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra;
m
is the normalized Lebesgue measure; and
T:\Omega\to\Omega
is the rotation by an angle
\alpha
:
T(\omega)=e^{i\alpha}\omega
.

T
is probability preserving, and it is well-known that
T
is ergodic iff
\alpha/2\pi
is irrational.

Choose a partition of the unit circle
\Omega
into disjoint arcs
\{I_{i}\}_{i\in S}
and define
\varphi:\Omega\to S
by
\varphi(\omega)=i
for
\omega\in I_{i}
. For example, if
S=\{1,2\}
we can take
I_{1},I_{2}
to be two equal halves of the circle. Next define

\pi(\omega,x,dy)=\pi_{\varphi(\omega)}(x,dy)

Now
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
are inhomogeneous Markov chains whose transition probabilities vary quasi-periodically: They are given by
\pi_{\varphi(e^{in\alpha}\omega)}(x,dy)
.

More generally, one can take a
d
parameter measurable family of transition probabilities
\pi_{{\boldsymbol{\omega}}}(x,y)
,
where
\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\dots\omega_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, fix some “initial phase”
({\overline{\omega}}_{1},\dots,{\overline{\omega}}_{d})
, and consider the chain with
transition probabilities

\pi_{n}(x,y)=\pi_{({\overline{\omega}}_{1}+n\alpha_{1},\dots,{\overline{\omega}}_{d}+n\alpha_{d})\mod\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(x,y).

Example 8.113
(Null recurrent Markov noise)

This is an example with infinite noise process.
Suppose
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
is an ergodic null recurrent Markov chain with countable state space
S
, and stationary positive vector
(p_{i})_{i\in S}
. Here
p_{i}>0
and (by null recurrence)
\sum p_{i}=\infty
. For example,
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
could be the simple random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d=1,2
, with the stationary measure which assigns the same mass to each site of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Let

\circ

\Omega=\{(\omega_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\in S^{\mathbb{Z}}:{\mathbb{P}}[Y_{1}=\omega_{i},Y_{2}=\omega_{i+1}]\neq 0\text{ for all }i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
;

\circ

\mathfs F
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the cylinders;

\circ

m
is the unique (infinite) Borel measure which satisfies for each cylinder

m({{}_{k}[}a_{k},\ldots,a_{n}])=p_{a_{k}}{\mathbb{P}}[Y_{i}=a_{i}\ (k\leq i\leq n)|Y_{k}=a_{k}]

\circ

T:\Omega\to\Omega
is the left shift map
T[(\omega_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}]=\omega_{i+1}
.

Then it is well-known that
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
is an
infinite
ergodic measure preserving invertible map, see
Aaronson-Book
.

Just as in Example
8.110
,
one can easily construct many MCRE with transition probabilities
\pi_{Y_{n}}(x,dy)
which vary randomly in time according to
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. For each particular realization of
\omega=(Y_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
,
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
is an ordinary inhomogeneous Markov chain (on a probability space). But as we shall see below, some features of
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
such as the growth of variance, are different than in the finite noise process case.

Example 8.114
(Transient Markov noise: a non-example)

The previous construction fails for transient Markov chains such as the random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
for
d\geq 3
, because in the transient case,
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
is not ergodic,
Aaronson-Book
.

We could try to work with the ergodic components of
m
, but this does not yield a new mathematical object, because of the following general fact
Aaronson-Book
: Every ergodic component of an
invertible
totally dissipative infinite measure preserving map is concentrated on a single orbit
\{T^{n}(\omega)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
. MCRE with such noise processes have just one possible realization of noise up to time shift. Their theory is the same as the theory of general inhomogeneous Markov chains, and does not merit separate treatment.

Suppose
\mathsf{X}^{\Omega}
is a MCRE with noise space
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
. A
Random additive functional
is a measurable function
f:\Omega\times\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
. This induces the additive functional
\mathsf{f}^{\omega}
on
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}

f_{n}^{\omega}(x,y)=f(T^{n}\omega,x,y).

For each
\omega\in\Omega
we define

\displaystyle S_{N}^{\omega}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n=1}^{N}f^{\omega}_{n}(X^{\omega}_{n},X^{\omega}_{n+1})\equiv\sum_{n=1}^{N}f(T^{n}\omega,X^{\omega}_{n},X^{\omega}_{n+1}),

\displaystyle V_{N}^{\omega}

\displaystyle:={\rm Var}(S_{N}^{\omega})\ \ \text{w.r.t. the distribution of $\mathsf{X}^{\omega}$}.

Throughout this chapter, we make the following
standing assumptions
:

(B)

Uniform boundedness:

|f|\leq K
where
K<\infty
is a constant;

(E)

Uniform ellipticity:
There is a constant
0<\epsilon_{0}<1
and a Borel function
p:\Omega\times\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}\to[0,\infty)
such that

(a)

\pi(\omega,x,dy)=p(\omega,x,y)\mu_{\omega}(dy)
;

(b)

0\leq p\leq 1/\epsilon_{0}
;

(c)

\int_{\mathfrak{S}}p(\omega,x,y)p(T\omega,y,z)\mu_{T\omega}(dy)>\epsilon_{0}
for all
\omega,x,z
.

(S)

Stationarity:
For every
\varphi:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
bounded and Borel, for every
\omega\in\Omega
,

\int\varphi(y)\mu_{T\omega}(dy)=\int_{\mathfrak{S}}\left(\int_{\mathfrak{S}}\varphi(y)\pi(\omega,x,dy)\right)\mu_{\omega}(dx).

(B) and (E) imply that
\mathsf{f}^{\omega}
is a uniformly bounded additive functional and that
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
is uniformly elliptic for every
\omega
. (S) is equivalent to saying that
if
X_{0}
is distributed according to
\mu_{\omega}
then

X_{n}
is distributed according to
\mu_{T^{n}\omega}
for all
n>0
.
Subject to (E), (S) can always be assumed without loss of generality, because of
Proposition
1.12
and the discussion which follows it.

Some of our results will require the following
continuity hypothesis
:

(C)

The Borel structure of
\Omega
and
\mathfrak{S}
is generated by a topologies so that
\Omega
and
\mathfrak{S}
are complete and separable metric spaces, and

(C1)

T:\Omega\to\Omega
is a homeomorphism and
{\rm supp}(m)=\Omega
.

(C2)

(\omega,x,y)\mapsto p(\omega,x,y)
is continuous, and
\omega\mapsto\int_{\mathfrak{S}}\varphi d\mu_{\omega}
is continuous for every bounded continuous
\varphi:\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
.

(C3)

(\omega,x,y)\mapsto f(\omega,x,y)
are continuous.

(C) is not part of our standing assumptions, and we will state it explicitly whenever it is used.

29
Main results

Let
{\mathbb{P}}
denote the measure on
\Omega\times\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}
which represents the joint distribution of
(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega},X_{2}^{\omega})
:

{\mathbb{P}}(d\omega,dx,dy):=\int_{\mathfrak{S}}\int_{\mathfrak{S}}\int_{\Omega}m(d\omega)\mu_{\omega}(dx)\pi(\omega,x,dy).

(29.1)

(1)

f(\omega,x,y)
is called
relatively cohomologous to a constant

if there are bounded measurable functions
a:\Omega\times\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
and
c:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
such that

f(\omega,x,y)=a(\omega,x)-a(T\omega,y)+c(\omega)\text{ ${\mathbb{P}}$-a.e.}

(2)

Fix
t\neq 0
, then
f(\omega,x,y)
is
relatively cohomologous to a coset of
t\mathbb{Z}
if there are measurable functions
a:\Omega\times\mathfrak{S}\to S^{1}
and
\lambda:\Omega\to S^{1}
s.t.

e^{(2\pi i/t)f(\omega,x,y)}=\lambda(\omega)\frac{a(\omega,x)}{a(T\omega,y)}\text{ ${\mathbb{P}}$-a.e.}

Theorem 29.1

Assume
\mathsf{f}
is an additive functional on a MCRE with finite noise process.
Under the standing assumptions (B), (E), (S):

(1)

If
f
is relatively cohomologous to a constant,
then
|V^{\omega}_{N}|\leq C
for all
N
, for a.e.
\omega
, where
C=C(\epsilon_{0},K)
is a constant.

(2)

If
f
is not relatively cohomologous to a constant, then there is a constant

\sigma^{2}>0
such that for a.e.
\omega
,
V^{\omega}_{N}\sim N\sigma^{2}
as
N\to\infty
.

Theorem 29.2

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an additive functional on a MCRE with finite noise process.
Assume the standing assumptions (B), (E),(S) and that

(a)

Either
|\mathfrak{S}|\leq\aleph_{0}
, or
|\mathfrak{S}|>\aleph_{0}
and the continuity hypothesis (C) holds.

(b)

f
is not relatively cohomologous to a coset of
t\mathbb{Z}
for any
t\neq 0
.

Then there exists
\sigma^{2}>0
such that for a.e.
\omega
, for every open interval
(a,b)
, and for every
z_{N},z\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}^{\omega}(S^{\omega}_{N})}{\sqrt{N}}\to z
,

{\mathbb{P}}\bigl[S^{\omega}_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)\bigr]\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left(\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2\sigma^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\right)|a-b|\text{ as }N\to\infty.

Theorem 29.3

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an additive functional on a MCRE with finite noise process.
Assume the standing assumptions (B),(E),(S), and that all the values of
f
are integers. If
f
is not relatively cohomologous to a coset of
t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 1
,
then there exists
\sigma^{2}>0
such that for a.e.
\omega
, and for every
z_{N},z\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}^{\omega}(S^{\omega}_{N})}{\sqrt{N}}\to z
,

{\mathbb{P}}\bigl[S^{\omega}_{N}=z_{N}\bigr]\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left(\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2\sigma^{2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^{2}}}\right)\text{ as }N\to\infty.

Theorem 29.4

Let
\mathsf{f}
be an additive functional on a MCRE with finite noise process
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
.
Assume (B),(E),(S). If
f
is not relatively cohomologous to a constant, then

(1)

There exists a continuously differentiable and strictly convex function
\mathfs F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
,

\mathfs F(\xi)=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi S_{N}^{\omega}})\text{ for all }\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

(2)

\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{E}(S^{\omega}_{N})\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathfs F^{\prime}(0)
for a.e.
\omega
.

(3)

Let
\mathfs F^{\prime}(\pm\infty):=\lim\limits_{\xi\to\pm\infty}\mathfs F^{\prime}(\xi)
, and let
\mathfs I_{N}(\eta,\omega)
,
\mathfs I(\eta)
denote the Legendre transforms of
\mathfs F_{N}(\xi):=\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi S_{N}^{\omega}})
,
\mathfs F(\xi)
. Then for a.e.
\omega
, for every
\eta\in(\mathfs F^{\prime}(-\infty),\mathfs F^{\prime}(\infty))
,

\mathfs I_{N}(\eta,\omega)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathfs I(\eta).

(4)

\mathfs I(\eta)
is strictly convex, has compact level sets, is equal to zero at
\eta={\mathfs F^{\prime}}(0)
, and is strictly positive elsewhere.

(5)

With probability one

\mathfrak{c}_{-}=\mathcal{F}(-\infty)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{ess\,}\inf S_{N}^{\omega}}{N},\hskip 11.74988pt\mathfrak{c}_{+}=\mathcal{F}(+\infty)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathrm{ess\,}\sup S_{N}^{\omega}}{N}.

Corollary 8.115

Under the conditions of the previous theorem, for a.e.
\omega
,
S_{N}^{\omega}/N
satisfies the large deviations principle with the rate function
\mathfs I(\eta)
:

(1)

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}^{\omega}/N\in K]\leq-\inf_{z\in K}\mathfs I(z)
for all closed sets
K\subset\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

\limsup\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\log{\mathbb{P}}[S_{N}^{\omega}/N\in G]\geq-\inf_{z\in K}\mathfs I(z)
for all open sets
G\subset\mathbb{R}
.

Proof

This is a consequence of the Gärtner-Ellis Theorem.

\square

So far we have only considered MCRE with finite noise spaces. We will now discuss the case of infinite noise spaces
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
. The main new phenomena in this case are:

Example 8.116

For MCRE with an infinite noise process:

(a)

It is possible that
V_{N}^{\omega}\to\infty

m
-a.e., but that
V_{N}^{\omega}=o(N)
a.e.

(b)

It is possible that
\not\exists a_{N}
s.t.
V_{N}^{\omega}\sim a_{N}
for
m
-a.e.
\omega
.

Proof

Let
X_{n}
be iid bounded real random variables with variance one and distribution

\mu
. Let
f_{n}(x)=x
. Let
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
be an infinite noise process, and fix
E\in\mathfs F
of finite positive measure. Let

\pi(\omega,x,dy):=\mu(dy)\ ,\ f(\omega,x,y):=1_{E}(\omega)x

Then
\displaystyle S_{N}^{\omega}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}1_{E}(T^{n}\omega)X_{n}
, and
V_{N}^{\omega}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}1_{E}(T^{n}\omega)
.

We now appeal to the following general results from infinite ergodic theory. Let
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
be an ergodic, invertible, measure preserving map on a non-atomic
\sigma
-finite measure space, and let

L^{1}_{+}:=\{A\in L^{1}(\Omega,\mathfs F,m):A\geq 0,\int Adm>0\}
. If
m(\Omega)=\infty
, then

(1)

\sum_{n=1}^{N}A\circ T^{n}=\infty
almost everywhere for all
A\in L^{1}_{+}
;

(2)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}A\circ T^{n}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0
almost everywhere for all
A\in L^{1}
;

(3)

Let
a_{N}
