be a sequence of positive real numbers, then at least one of the following possibilities happens:

(a)

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{a_{N}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}A\circ T^{n}=0
a.e. for all
A\in L^{1}_{+}
;

(b)

\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{a_{N}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}A\circ T^{n}=\infty
a.e. for all
A\in L^{1}_{+}
.

So
\not\exists a_{N}\uparrow\infty
s.t.
\sum_{n=1}^{N}A(T^{n}\omega)\sim a_{N}
for a.e.
\omega
, even for a single
A\in L^{1}_{+}
.

These results can all be found in
Aaronson-Book
:
(1) is a consequence of the Halmos Recurrence Theorem; (2) follows from the Ratio Ergodic Theorem; and (3) is a theorem of J. Aaronson.
Specializing to the case
A=1_{E}
we find that
V_{N}^{\omega}\to\infty
a.e.;
V_{N}^{\omega}=o(N)
a.e. as
N\to\infty
; and
\not\exists a_{N}
so that
V_{N}^{\omega}\sim a_{N}
for a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
.

Here are our general results on MCRE with infinite noise spaces.

Theorem 29.5

Suppose
\mathsf{f}^{\omega}
is a random additive functional on a MCRE with infinite noise space on a non-atomic
\sigma
-finite measure space.
Under the standing assumptions (B), (E), (S):

(1)

If
f
is relatively cohomologous to a constant,
then
|V^{\omega}_{N}|\leq C
for all
N
, for a.e.
\omega
, where
C=C(\epsilon_{0},K)
is a constant.

(2)

If
f
is not relatively cohomologous to a constant then
V^{\omega}_{N}\to\infty
a.s.

Theorem 29.6

Suppose
\mathsf{f}^{\omega}
is a random additive functional on a MCRE with infinite noise space on a non-atomic
\sigma
-finite measure space.
Assume the standing assumptions (B), (E),(S) and that

(a)

Either
|\mathfrak{S}|\leq\aleph_{0}
, or
|\mathfrak{S}|>\aleph_{0}
and the continuity hypothesis (C) holds.

(b)

f
is not relatively cohomologous to a coset of
t\mathbb{Z}
for any
t\neq 0
.

Then for a.e.
\omega
, for every open interval
(a,b)
, and for every
z_{N},z\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}^{\omega}(S^{\omega}_{N})}{V_{N}^{\omega}}\to z
,

\displaystyle{{\mathbb{P}}\bigl[S^{\omega}_{N}-z_{N}\in(a,b)\bigr]\sim\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}^{\omega}}}|a-b|\text{ as }N\to\infty.}

Theorem 29.7

Suppose
\mathsf{f}^{\omega}
is a random additive functional on a MCRE
with infinite noise space on a non-atomic
\sigma
-finite measure space.
Assume the standing assumptions (B),(E),(S), and that all the values of
f
are integers. If
f
is not relatively cohomologous to a coset of
t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 1
,
then for every
z_{N},z\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\frac{z_{N}-\mathbb{E}^{\omega}(S^{\omega}_{N})}{\sqrt{V_{N}^{\omega}}}\to z
,
for a.e.
\omega
,

{\mathbb{P}}\bigl[S^{\omega}_{N}=z_{N}\bigr]\sim\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi V_{N}^{\omega}}}
as
N\to\infty
.

30
Proofs

Throughout this section
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
is a Markov chain in random environment with stationary ergodic, possibly infinite, noise process
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
, and
\mathsf{f}^{\omega}
is a random additive functional on
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
. We assume throughout (B),(E),(S).

30.1
The essential range is a.s. constant

The purpose of this section is to prove the following result:

Proposition 8.117

There exist closed subgroups
H,G_{ess}\leq\mathbb{R}
s.t. for
m
–a.e.
\omega
,
the co-range of
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
equals
H
, the essential range of
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
equals
G_{ess}
, and

G_{ess}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{R}&H=\{0\},\\
\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}&H=t\mathbb{Z},\;\;t\neq 0,\\
\{0\}&H=\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

We call
H
and
G_{ess}
the
a.s. co-range
and
a.s. essential range
.

We begin with a calculation of the structure constants of
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
.
Fix an element
\omega
in the noise space, and let
\mathrm{Hex}(\omega)
denote the probability space of position 3 hexagons for
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
. Let
m_{\omega}
denote the hexagon measure, as defined in
§
3.1
. Recall the definition of the balance
\Gamma(P)
of a hexagon
P
, and define

\begin{array}[]{l}u(\omega):=\mathbb{E}(|\Gamma(P)|^{2})^{1/2}\\
d(\xi,\omega):=\mathbb{E}(|e^{i\xi\Gamma(P)}-1|^{2})^{1/2}\end{array}\text{ (expectation on $P\in\mathrm{Hex}(\omega)$ w.r.t. $m_{\omega}$)}.

Since the space of position
n+3
hexagons for
\mathrm{X}^{\omega}
is
\mathrm{Hex}(T^{n}\omega)
, together with the hexagon measure
m_{T^{n}\omega}
,
it follows that the structure constants of
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
are

d_{n+3}(\xi,f^{\omega})=d(T^{n}\omega,\xi)\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988ptu_{n+3}(f^{\omega})=u(T^{n}\omega)\ \ (n\geq 0).

(30.1)

Lemma 8.118

u(\cdot),d(\cdot,\cdot)
are Borel measurable, and for every
\omega
,
d(\cdot,\omega)
is continuous. Under the continuity hypothesis (C),
u(\cdot),d(\cdot,\cdot)
are continuous.

Proof

To check this, express the hexagon measure explicitly as a measure on
\mathfrak{S}^{6}
in terms of the transition kernel
\pi(\omega,x,y)
, using the formulas for the bridge distributions of §
2.3
, and write
\Gamma(P)
explicitly a function on
\mathfrak{S}^{6}
in terms of
f(\omega,x,y)
. We omit the details, which are routine.
\square

Proof of Proposition
8.117
.

Let
H_{\omega}:=H(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})

be the essential range of
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
.
By Theorem
9.1
,
H_{\omega}
is either
\mathbb{R}
or
t\mathbb{Z}
for some
t\geq 0
.
By (
30.1
)

D_{N}(\xi,\omega):=\sum\limits_{n=3}^{N}d_{n}(\xi,\mathsf{f}^{\omega})^{2}\equiv\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N-3}d(T^{n}\omega,\xi)^{2}.

Step 1:

U(a,b):=\{\omega\in\Omega:D_{N}(\cdot,\omega)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty\text{ uniformly on }(a,b)\}

is measurable and
T
-invariant for all
a<b
.

Proof.

T
-invariance is because
d^{2}\leq 4
whence
|D_{N}(\xi,T\omega)-D_{N}(\xi,\omega)|\leq 8
. Measurability is because of the identity

U(a,b)=\left\{\omega\in\Omega:\begin{array}[]{l}\forall M\in\mathbb{Q}\;\exists N\in\mathbb{N}\text{ s.t. }\\
\text{ for all }\xi\in(a,b)\cap\mathbb{Q},\ D_{N}(\omega,\xi)>M\end{array}\right\}.

The inclusion
\subseteq
is obvious. The inclusion
\supseteq
is because if
\omega\not\in U(a,b)
then for some
M\in\mathbb{Q}
, for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
there exists some
\eta\in(a,b)
s.t.
D_{N}(\omega,\eta)<M
, whence by the continuity of
\eta\mapsto D_{N}(\omega,\eta)
there is some
\xi\in(a,b)\cap\mathbb{Q}
such that
D_{N}(\omega,\xi)<M
. So
\omega\not\in U(a,b)\Rightarrow\omega\not\in\text{RHS}
.

Step 2:

The sets
\Omega_{1}:=\{\omega\in\Omega:H_{\omega}=\{0\}\}
,
\Omega_{2}:=\{\omega\in\Omega:H_{\omega}=\mathbb{R}\}
, and
\Omega_{3}:=\{\omega\in\Omega:H_{\omega}=t\mathbb{Z}\text{ for some }t\neq 0\}
are measurable and
T
-invariant. Therefore by ergodicity, for each
i
, either
m(\Omega_{i})=0
or
m(\Omega_{i}^{c})=0
.

Proof.
Recall that for Markov chains,
D_{N}\to\infty
uniformly on compact subsets of the complement of the co-range (Theorem
9.5
). So

\Omega_{1}=\bigcap_{n=1}^{\infty}U(\tfrac{1}{n},n)\ ,\ \Omega_{2}=\bigcap_{0<a<b\text{ rational}}U(a,b)^{c}\ ,\ \Omega_{3}=\Omega_{1}^{c}\cap\Omega_{2}^{c}.

By step 1,
\Omega_{i}
are
T
-invariant and measurable. Since
T
is ergodic, these sets are either of measure zero or of full measure.

By Theorem
9.2
, if
\Omega_{1}
has full measure, then the essential range is a.s.
\mathbb{R}
. Similarly, if
\Omega_{2}
has full measure, then the essential range is
\{0\}
almost surely. It remains to consider the case when
\Omega_{3}
has full measure.

Step 3:

If
\Omega_{3}
has full measure, then there exist
t\neq 0
such that
\Omega_{3}(t):=\{\omega\in\Omega:H_{\omega}=t\mathbb{Z}\}
has full measure, and then the essential range is
(2\pi/t)\mathbb{Z}
almost surely.

Proof.
For every
\omega\in\Omega_{3}
there exists
t(\omega)>0
such that
H_{\omega}=t(\omega)\mathbb{Z}
. We can characterize
t(\omega)
as follows:

t(\omega)=\sup\left\{t\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\infty):\begin{array}[]{l}D_{N}(\omega,\cdot)\to\infty\text{ uniformly }\\
\text{on compact subsets of }(0,t)\end{array}\right\}.

It is clear from this expression that
t(T\omega)=t(\omega)
, and that for every
A>0
,

[t(\omega)\geq A]=\bigcap_{0<a<b<A\text{ rational}}U(a,b).

So
t(\cdot)
is a measurable
T
-invariant function, whence by ergodicity constant. Let
t
denote this constant, then
H_{\omega}=t\mathbb{Z}
for a.e.
\omega
. By Theorem
9.2
,
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})=(2\pi/t)\mathbb{Z}
almost surely.
\square

30.2
Variance growth

In this section we prove Theorems
29.1
and
29.5
on the behavior of
V_{N}^{\omega}
as
N\to\infty
.

Lemma 8.119

Suppose
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
is an invertible, ergodic, measure preserving map of a probability space or of a non-atomic infinite measure space. Let
A:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a non-negative measurable function. Either
A=0
a.e.,
or
\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 0}A\circ T^{n}=\infty
a.e.

Proof

If
m(\Omega)<\infty
, then the Lemma follows from the Birkhoff ergodic theorem. In the more general case
m(\Omega)\leq\infty
, the lemma follows from the well-known fact that invertible ergodic measure preserving maps on non-atomic measure spaces are conservative. We supply the details, for completeness.

If
A
is not equal to 0 a.e., then there is
{\varepsilon}>0
s.t.

E:=\{\omega\in\Omega:A(\omega)\geq{\varepsilon}\}
has positive measure.
We claim that

\sum_{n\geq 0}1_{E}(T^{n}\omega)=\infty

(30.2)

almost everywhere on
E
.
Since
A\geq{\varepsilon}1_{E}
(
30.2
) implies that
\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 0}A(T^{n}\omega)=\infty

almost everywhere on
E,
and, by ergodicity, almost everywhere on
\Omega,

proving the lemma.

It remains to prove (
30.2
).
Suppose by way of contradiction that it is not true that
\displaystyle\sum_{n\geq 0}1_{E}(T^{n}\omega)=\infty

almost everywhere on
E
. Then there exists
N
s.t.

W:=\{\omega\in E:\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}1_{E}(T^{n}\omega)=N\}

has positive measure. The invertibility and measurability of
T
implies that
T^{n}(W)
is measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, and that
\{T^{n}(W)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are pairwise disjoint.

Since
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m)
is non-atomic, we can break
W=W_{1}\cup W_{2}

where
W_{i}
are measurable, disjoint, and with positive measure. By invertibility,
\widehat{W}_{i}:=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}T^{n}W_{i}
are disjoint
T
-invariant sets with positive measure. But this contradicts ergodicity.
\square

Part 1:
V_{N}^{\omega}
is bounded, or tends to infinite almost surely.

Recall that
K
is a bound for
\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|f|
, and
\epsilon_{0}
is a uniform ellipticity constant for
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
. By Theorem
2.30
and (
30.1
) there are positive constants
C_{i}=C_{i}(\epsilon_{0},K)

(i=1,2)
such that for all
N
,

C_{1}^{-1}\sum_{n=3}^{N}u(T^{n}\omega)^{2}-C_{2}\leq V^{\omega}_{N}\leq C_{1}\sum_{n=3}^{N}u(T^{n}\omega)^{2}+C_{2}.

If
u(\omega)=0

m
-a.e., then for a.e.
\omega
,
V_{N}^{\omega}\leq C_{2}
for all
N
. Otherwise, by Lemma
8.119
,

\sum_{n=3}^{N}u(T^{n}\omega)^{2}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\infty
, whence
V^{\omega}_{N}\to\infty
almost everywhere.

Part 2: Linear growth of variance when
V_{N}^{\omega}\to\infty
a.e. and
m(\Omega)=1
.
Suppose
m(\Omega)=1
and
V_{N}^{\omega}\to\infty
almost surely. We claim that

\exists\sigma^{2}>0\text{ s.t. }V^{\omega}_{N}\sim N\sigma^{2}\text{ a.s.}

(30.3)

Let
\sigma_{0}^{2}:=\int_{\Omega}u^{2}dm
. This is a finite number, because
\|u\|_{\infty}\leq 6K
and
m(\Omega)=1
. This is a positive number, because as we saw in part 1, if
u=0
a.e., then
V_{N}^{\omega}=O(1)
a.e. contrary to our assumptions. By the pointwise ergodic theorem,
\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{N}u(T^{n}\omega)^{2}=[1+o(1)]\sigma_{0}^{2}N
. Hence

V^{\omega}_{N}\geq[1+o(1)]C_{1}(\epsilon_{0},K)^{-1}N\sigma^{2}_{0}\to\infty.

Let

F_{n}:=f(T^{n-1}\omega,X^{\omega}_{n},X^{\omega}_{n+1})
and let
\mathbb{E}^{\omega}
,
{\rm Var}^{\omega}
,
{\rm Cov}^{\omega}
denote the expectation, variance and covariance with respect to
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
, then

\displaystyle V^{\omega}_{N}=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\rm Var}^{\omega}(F_{n})+2\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{m=n+1}^{N}{\rm Cov}^{\omega}(F_{n},F_{m})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}{\rm Var}^{\omega}(F_{n})+2\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N-n}{\rm Cov}^{\omega}(F_{n},F_{n+k}).

By assumption (S)
\{\mu_{\omega}\}
are stationary, so

\{X^{T^{n}\omega}_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
has the same distribution as
\{X^{\omega}_{1}\}_{i\geq n}
. Therefore
{\rm Var}^{\omega}(F_{n})={\rm Var}^{T^{n-1}\omega}(F_{1})
and
{\rm Cov}^{\omega}(F_{n},F_{n+k})={\rm Cov}^{T^{n-1}\omega}(F_{1},F_{1+k})
. Thus

V_{N}^{\omega}=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\psi_{0}(T^{n}\omega)+2\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=1}^{N-n}\psi_{k}(T^{n}\omega),

where
\psi_{0}(\omega):={\rm Var}^{\omega}[f(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1},X^{\omega}_{2})]
and

\psi_{k}(\omega)={\rm Cov}^{\omega}[f(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1},X^{\omega}_{2}),f(T^{k}\omega,X^{\omega}_{k+1},X^{\omega}_{k+2})].

By the ergodic theorem

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}\psi_{0}(T^{n}\omega)=\int\psi_{0}dm.
To find the limit of the normalized double sum we first recall that by the uniform mixing of
\{X^{\omega}_{n}\}
(a consequence of the ellipticity assumption),
\|\psi_{k}\|_{\infty}\leq C_{mix}\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\theta^{k}
with
C_{mix},0<\theta<1
which only depend on
\epsilon_{0}
(Proposition
1.11
). Therefore for every
M
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=1}^{N-n}\psi_{k}(T^{n}\omega)={\Big[}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{k=1}^{M-1}\psi_{k}(T^{n}\omega){\Big]}+O(\theta^{M}),

whence by the ergodic theorem

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{N-n}\psi_{k}(T^{n}\omega)=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{\infty}\int\psi_{k}dm
, with the last sum converging exponentially fast. In summary,

\frac{1}{N}V^{\omega}_{N}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\sigma^{2}:=\int\left[\psi_{0}+2\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\psi_{k}\right]dm.

Since as we saw above
\liminf\frac{1}{N}V^{\omega}_{N}\geq C_{1}\sigma_{0}^{2}
and
\sigma_{0}^{2}>0
, it must be the case that
\sigma^{2}>0
, and (
30.3
) is proved.

We now relate the following two properties:

(a)

f
is relatively cohomologous to a constant;

(b)

V_{N}^{\omega}
is bounded
m
-a.e.

Part 3: (a)
\Rightarrow
(b):

Suppose
f
is relatively cohomologous to a constant. By Fubini’s theorem, for
m
-a.e.
\omega
, for every
n
,

f^{\omega}_{n}(X^{\omega}_{n},X^{\omega}_{n+1})=a(T^{n}\omega,X^{\omega}_{n})-a(T^{n+1}\omega,X^{\omega}_{n+1})+c(T^{n}\omega)\text{ a.s.}

with respect to the distribution of
\{X^{\omega}_{n}\}
.
14
14

14

Here we use the assumption that
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m)
is
\sigma
-finite. Fubini’s theorem may be false otherwise.

Summing over
n
, we obtain that for a.e.
\omega
, for every
N
,

|S_{N}^{\omega}-\sum_{n=1}^{N}c(T^{n}\omega)|=|a(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1})-a(T^{N}\omega,X^{\omega}_{N+1})|\leq 2\mathrm{ess\,sup\,}a(\cdot,\cdot).

In particular, for every
\omega
,
V^{N}_{\omega}
is bounded. By the first part of the proof, for a.e.
\omega
, for all
N
,
|V^{\omega}_{N}|\leq C_{2}(\epsilon_{0},K).

Part 4: (b)
\Rightarrow
(a):

Next suppose that
f
is not relatively cohomologous to a constant.
Recall that
\sigma_{0}^{2}=\int u^{2}dm
.

We claim that
\sigma^{2}_{0}>0
, and deduce from the first part of the proof that

V_{N}^{\omega}\to+\infty
a.e.

Assume by way of contradiction that
\sigma^{2}_{0}=0
, then
u(\omega)=0
a.e., whence for a.e.
\omega
, for every
n
, almost every position
n
hexagon of
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
has balance zero.
Applying the gradient lemma to
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
, we find bounded functions
g^{\omega}_{n}
and constants
c^{\omega}_{n}
such that

f^{\omega}_{n}(X^{\omega}_{n},X^{\omega}_{n+1})=g^{\omega}_{n}(X^{\omega}_{n})-g^{\omega}_{n+1}(X^{\omega}_{n+1})+c^{\omega}_{n}\text{ a.s. }

The issue is to show that
g^{\omega}_{n},c^{\omega}_{n}
can be given the form
g_{n}^{\omega}(x)=a(T^{n}\omega,x)
and
c^{\omega}_{n}=c(T^{n}\omega)
where
a(\cdot,\cdot),c(\cdot)
are measurable.

This is indeed the case, because the proof of the gradient lemma shows that we can take

\displaystyle c^{\omega}_{n}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\omega}[f^{\omega}_{n-2}(X^{\omega}_{n-2},X^{\omega}_{n-1})]

\displaystyle g_{n}^{\omega}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl(f^{\omega}_{n-2}(X^{\omega}_{n-2},X^{\omega}_{n-1})+f^{\omega}_{n-1}(X^{\omega}_{n-1},X^{\omega}_{n})\bigg|X^{\omega}_{n}=z\biggr).

So
c^{\omega}_{n}=c(T^{n}\omega)
and
g^{\omega}_{n}(z)=a(T^{n}\omega,z)
for

\displaystyle c(\omega):=\int_{\mathfrak{S}^{2}}\mu_{T^{-2}\omega}(x)\mu_{T^{-1}\omega}(dy)p(T^{n-2}\omega,x,y)f(T^{-2}\omega,x,y).

\displaystyle a(\omega,z):=\int_{\mathfrak{S}^{3}}\mu_{T^{-2}\omega}(dx)\mu_{T^{-1}\omega}(dy)p(T^{n-2}\omega,x,y)p(T^{-1}\omega,y,z)\times

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt{{f(T^{-2}\omega,x,y)+f(T^{-1}\omega,y,z)}\over{\int_{\mathfrak{S}^{2}}\mu_{T^{-2}\omega}(dx)\mu_{T^{-1}\omega}(dy)[p(T^{n-2}\omega,x,y)p(T^{-1}\omega,y,z)}]}.

These are measurable functions, and our standing assumptions imply that they are bounded.

We see that
f
is relatively cohomologous to a constant in contradiction to our assumption.
So
\sigma^{2}_{0}>0
, whence by the first part of the proof
V_{N}^{\omega}
tends to infinity.
\Box

30.3
The local limit theorem

In this section, we prove Theorems
29.2
,

29.3
,
29.6
and
29.7
on the local limit theorem for Markov chains in random environment.
We need the following lemmas:

Lemma 8.120

Suppose
\Omega
is a Borel space,
\mathfrak{S}
is a separable metric space, and
\psi:\Omega\times\mathfrak{S}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Borel function such that for every
\omega\in\Omega
,
\psi(\omega,\cdot)
is continuous on
\mathfrak{S}
and positive somewhere. Then there exists a Borel measurable
x:\Omega\to\mathfrak{S}
such that
\psi(\omega,x(\omega))>0
.

Proof

Fix a countable dense set
\{x_{i}\}\subset\mathfrak{S}
. Our assumptions on
\psi
imply that for every
\omega
there exists an
i
such that
\psi(\omega,x_{i})>0
. So

i(\omega):=\min\{i\in\mathbb{N}:\psi(\omega,x_{i})>0\}

is well-defined and Borel measurable. Take
x(\omega):=x_{i(\omega)}
.
\square

Lemma 8.121

If
W_{1},W_{2}
are two independent random variables such that for some
a,t\in\mathbb{R}
,
W_{1}+W_{2}\in a+t\mathbb{Z}
with full probability, then
a=a_{1}+a_{2}
where
W_{1}\in a_{1}+t\mathbb{Z}
,
W_{2}\in a_{2}+t\mathbb{Z}
with full probability.

Proof

Without loss of generality
a=0
,
t=2\pi
. Then

|\mathbb{E}(e^{iW_{1}})|\cdot|\mathbb{E}(e^{iW_{2}})|=|\mathbb{E}(e^{i(W_{1}+W_{2})})|=1,

whence
|\mathbb{E}(e^{iW_{k}})|=1\ \ (k=1,2).

Choose
a_{k}
such that
\mathbb{E}(e^{i(W_{k}-a_{k})})=1
, then
\mathbb{E}(\cos(W_{k}-a_{k}))=1
, whence
W_{k}-a_{k}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
almost surely.
Necessarily
a_{1}+a_{2}\in 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, and there is no problem in adjusting
a_{1}
to get that the sum zero.
\square

Proof of Theorems
29.2
and
29.6
on the non-lattice case.

Theorems
29.2
and
29.6
provide the LLT for Markov chains in random environment with finite and infinite noise process, under the assumption that

f
is not relatively cohomologous to a coset of
t\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
.

In this case,
f
is also not relatively cohomologous to a constant, and by Theorems
29.1
and
29.5
,

V^{\omega}_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
. Moreover, if the noise process
(\Omega,\mathfs F,m,T)
satisfies
m(\Omega)=1
, then
\exists\sigma^{2}>0
s.t.
V_{N}^{\omega}\sim N\sigma^{2}
.

To prove the theorems it is sufficient to show that for a.e.
\omega
,
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})=\mathbb{R}
, as this will imply the LLT by the general results of Chapter
4
.

Assume by way of contradiction that
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})\neq\mathbb{R}

on a
set of
\omega
’s of positive measure. By Proposition
8.117
,
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})=G_{ess}
a.e. where
G_{ess}=\{0\}
or
\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}
with
t\neq 0
. The first possibility cannot happen, because it implies that
\mathsf{f}^{\omega}
is center-tight, whence
V^{\omega}_{N}=O(1)
, whereas
V^{\omega}_{N}\to\infty
. So there exists
t\neq 0
such that
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})=(2\pi/t)\mathbb{Z}
a.s., and

H_{\omega}:=H(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})=t\mathbb{Z}
a.e.

By the reduction lemma, for every
\omega
s.t.
H_{\omega}=t\mathbb{Z}
there are measurable functions
g^{\omega}_{n}(x)
,
h_{n}^{\omega}(x,y)
with
\sum{\rm Var}[h_{n}^{\omega}]<\infty
,
and constants
c^{\omega}_{n}
such that

\exp\bigl[it(f^{\omega}_{n}(x,y)-g^{\omega}_{n}(x)+g^{\omega}_{n+1}(y)+h_{n}^{\omega}(x,y)-c^{\omega}_{n})\bigr]=1

a.s. with respect to the distribution of
(X^{\omega}_{n},X^{\omega}_{n+1})
.
So
e^{it(f(\omega,x,y)+h_{n}^{\omega}(x,y))}=\lambda^{\omega}_{n}\frac{a^{\omega}_{n}(x)}{a^{\omega}_{n+1}(y)}
, where

\lambda^{\omega}_{n}=e^{itc^{\omega}_{n}}
,
a^{\omega}_{n}(x)=e^{itg^{\omega}_{n}(x)}
.

But now we run into a problem: Our proof of the reduction lemma does not provide
g_{n}^{\omega}
and
c^{\omega}_{n}
of the form

c^{\omega}_{n}=c(T^{n}\omega)\ ,\ a^{\omega}_{n}=a(T^{n}\omega,x)

with
c(\cdot),a(\cdot,\cdot)
measurable, and we need to
show that
h_{n}^{\omega}=0
.

To this end we use the following additional structure: For a.e.
\omega
,
H_{\omega}=t\mathbb{Z}
so

\sum d(T^{n}\omega,t)^{2}<\infty

\mu
-almost everywhere. By the ergodic theorem, this can only happen if

d(\omega,t)=0
almost everywhere. Hence

\Gamma\left(Z^{\omega}_{1},\begin{array}[]{l}Z^{\omega}_{2}\\
Y^{\omega}_{2}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{l}Y^{\omega}_{3}\\
X^{\omega}_{3}\end{array},X^{\omega}_{4}\right)\in\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}\text{ a.e. in $\mathrm{Hex}(\omega)$ for $m$-a.e. $\omega$.}

(30.4)

Recall the ladder process
\underline{L}_{n}^{\omega}=(Z^{\omega}_{n-2},Y_{n-2}^{\omega},X^{\omega}_{n})
associated with
\{X^{\omega}_{n}\}
. Let
{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}
denote its distribution, and define as in the proof of the reduction lemma,

\displaystyle H^{\omega}(\underline{L}_{n}^{\omega},\underline{L}_{n+1}^{\omega}):=\Gamma\left(Z^{\omega}_{n-2},\begin{array}[]{l}Z^{\omega}_{n-1}\\
Y^{\omega}_{n-1}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{l}Y^{\omega}_{n}\\
X^{\omega}_{n}\end{array},X^{\omega}_{n+1}\right)

\displaystyle\Gamma\left(Z^{\omega}_{1},\begin{array}[]{l}Z^{\omega}_{2}\\
Y^{\omega}_{2}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{l}Z^{\omega}_{3}\\
X^{\omega}_{3}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{l}Y^{\omega}_{4}\\
X^{\omega}_{4}\end{array},X^{\omega}_{5}\right)\overset{!}{:=}H^{\omega}(\underline{L}_{3}^{\omega},\underline{L}_{4}^{\omega})+H^{\omega}(\underline{L}_{4}^{\omega},\underline{L}_{5}^{\omega})

The last definition requires justification because the RHS seems at first sight to depend on
Y^{\omega}_{3}
. In fact it does not. To see this observe that the last expression is the balance of the octagon obtained by stacking

\left(Z^{\omega}_{2},\begin{array}[]{l}Z^{\omega}_{3}\\
Y^{\omega}_{3}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{l}Y^{\omega}_{4}\\
X^{\omega}_{4}\end{array},X^{\omega}_{5}\right)
on top of
\left(Z^{\omega}_{1},\begin{array}[]{l}Z^{\omega}_{2}\\
Y^{\omega}_{2}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{l}Y^{\omega}_{3}\\
X^{\omega}_{3}\end{array},X^{\omega}_{4}\right)
and removing the common edge
(Z^{\omega}_{2},Y^{\omega}_{3},X^{\omega}_{4})
which “cancels out.”

Claim 1.

Let
{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}
denote the distribution of
\{\underline{L}^{\omega}_{n}\}
, then there exist measurable functions
\zeta_{1}(\omega),\zeta_{2}(\omega)\in\mathfrak{S}
such that for a.e.
\omega

\Gamma\left(\zeta_{1}(\omega),\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{2}(\omega)\\
Y^{\omega}_{2}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{1}(\omega)\\
X^{\omega}_{3}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}Y^{\omega}_{4}\\
X^{\omega}_{4}\end{array},X^{\omega}_{5}\right)\in\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}\hskip 14.22636pt;\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}\left(\ \ \cdot\bigg|\ \ {\begin{array}[]{l}Z^{\omega}_{3}=\zeta_{1}(\omega)\\
Z^{\omega}_{2}=\zeta_{2}(\omega)\\
Z^{\omega}_{1}=\zeta_{1}(\omega)\\
\end{array}}\right)\text{--a.e.}

Proof.
By (
30.4
),
\Gamma\in\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}
with full
{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}
–probability, for a.e.
\omega
. The point it to obtain this a.s. with respect to the conditional measures.

Suppose first that
\mathfrak{S}
is countable, then for fixed
\omega
, the
{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}
-distribution of
(\underline{L}^{\omega}_{3},\underline{L}^{\omega}_{2},\underline{L}^{\omega}_{3})
is purely atomic, and

\Gamma\in\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}
for every octagon with positive
{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}
–probability. So the claim holds for any pair
(\zeta_{1}(\omega),\zeta_{2}(\omega))\in\mathfrak{S}
such that

{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}\bigl[(Z^{\omega}_{1},Z^{\omega}_{2},Z^{\omega}_{3})=(\zeta_{1}(\omega),\zeta_{2}(\omega),\zeta_{1}(\omega))\bigr]>0.

Such pairs exist by the ellipticity assumption. Since
\mathfrak{S}
is countable there is no problem to choose such
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
measurably.

Now suppose
\mathfrak{S}
is uncountable but with the continuity property (C). By Fubini’s theorem and (
30.4
), for a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
, for a.e.
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3})
with respect to the distribution
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3})\sim(Z_{1}^{\omega},Z_{2}^{\omega},Z_{3}^{\omega})
,

\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}}\left(\bigl|e^{(2\pi i/t)\Gamma\left({\tiny Z_{1}^{\omega},\begin{array}[]{c}Z^{\omega}_{2}\\
Y^{\omega}_{2}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}Z^{\omega}_{3}\\
X^{\omega}_{3}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}Y^{\omega}_{4}\\
X^{\omega}_{4}\end{array},X^{\omega}_{5}}\right)}-1\bigr|^{2}\bigg|{\tiny\begin{array}[]{l}Z^{\omega}_{1}=\zeta_{1}\\
Z^{\omega}_{2}=\zeta_{2}\\
Z^{\omega}_{3}=\zeta_{3}\\
\end{array}}\right)=0.

(30.5)

By the Markov property, this conditional expectation has canonical interpretation for
every

(\omega,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3})
in the set

A=\{(\omega,a,b,c):p(\omega,a,b)p(T\omega,b,c)>0\}.

By assumption (C2),
A
is open. By assumption (C1), every open subset of
A
has positive measure with respect to the measure
\int{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}m(d\omega)
. By assumption (C2), the left-hand-side of (
30.5
) depends continuously on

(\omega,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3})
.
Therefore (
30.5
) is true for
all

(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3})\in A
.

Thus to prove the claim it remains to construct measurable functions
\zeta_{1}(\omega),\zeta_{2}(\omega)
such that
(\omega,\zeta_{1}(\omega),\zeta_{2}(\omega),\zeta_{1}(\omega))\in A
for all
\omega
.

By the ellipticity condition
\int_{\mathfrak{S}}p(\omega,a,\zeta)p(T\omega,\zeta,a)\mu_{T\omega}(d\zeta)>\epsilon_{0}
, so for every
\omega
there are
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
s.t.

\psi(\omega,(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})):=p(\omega,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})p(T\omega,\zeta_{2},\zeta_{1})>0.

By Lemma
8.120
it is possible to choose measurable
\zeta_{1}(\omega),\zeta_{2}(\omega)
like this. Claim 1 is proved.

Given
\omega\in\Omega
and
a,b\in\mathfrak{S}
, construct the bridge distribution
{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}_{ab}(E)={\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}(Y^{\omega}_{2}\in E|Z_{1}^{\omega}=a,X^{\omega}_{3}=b)
as in §
2.3
.

Claim 2.

For a.e.
\omega
, for a.e.
(\xi_{3},\xi_{4},\xi_{5})
sampled from the joint distribution of
(X^{\omega}_{3},X^{\omega}_{4},X^{\omega}_{5})
, the random variables

\displaystyle W^{\omega}_{3}:=f(\omega,\zeta_{1}(\omega),Y_{2})+f(T\omega,Y_{2},\xi_{3}),\ \ \ Y_{2}\sim{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}_{\zeta_{1},\xi_{3}}

\displaystyle W^{T^{2}\omega}_{5}:=f(T^{2}\omega,\zeta_{1}(\omega),Y_{4})+f(T^{3}\omega,Y_{4},\xi_{5}),\ \ \ Y_{4}\sim{\mathbb{P}}^{T^{2}\omega}_{\zeta_{1},\xi_{5}}

are purely atomic, and belong to some coset of
\frac{2\pi}{t}{\mathbb{Z}}
with full probability. (These cosets could be different.)

Proof.
By choice of
\zeta_{i}(\omega)
and Fubini’s theorem, for a.e.
(\xi_{3},\xi_{4},\xi_{5})\sim(X^{\omega}_{3},X^{\omega}_{4},X^{\omega}_{5})
,

\Gamma\left(\zeta_{1}(\omega),\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{2}(\omega)\\
Y^{\omega}_{2}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{1}(\omega)\\
\xi_{3}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}Y^{\omega}_{4}\\
\xi_{4}\end{array},\xi_{5}\right)\in\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}\left(\cdot\bigg|{\tiny\begin{array}[]{ll}Z^{\omega}_{3}=\zeta_{1}&X^{\omega}_{3}=\xi_{3}\\
Z^{\omega}_{2}=\zeta_{2}&X^{\omega}_{4}=\xi_{4}\\
Z^{\omega}_{1}=\zeta_{1}&X^{\omega}_{5}=\xi_{5}\\
\end{array}}\right)\text{--a.e.}

Notice that
\Gamma\left(\zeta_{1}(\omega),\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{2}(\omega)\\
Y^{\omega}_{2}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{1}(\omega)\\
\xi_{3}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}Y^{\omega}_{4}\\
\xi_{4}\end{array},\xi_{5}\right)
is equal to the
independent
difference of
W^{\omega}_{3}
and
W^{T^{2}\omega}_{5}
, plus a constant which only depends on
\omega
.
The claim now follows from Lemma
8.121
.

Claim 3.

Given
\omega
and
(\xi_{3},\xi_{4},\xi_{5})
as in claim 2, let

\displaystyle g(\omega,\xi_{3})

\displaystyle:=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\text{the smallest positive atom of $W^{\omega}_{3}$ if $\exists$ positive atoms, }\\
\text{otherwise, the largest non-positive atom of $W^{\omega}_{3}$}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle c(\omega)

\displaystyle:=-f(\omega,\zeta_{1}(\omega),\zeta_{2}(\omega))-f(T\omega,\zeta_{2}(\omega),\zeta_{1}(\omega)).

These functions are well-defined, measurable, and

[f(T^{2}\omega,\xi_{3},\xi_{4})+f(T^{3}\omega,\xi_{4},\xi_{5})]+g(\omega,\xi_{3})-g(T^{2}\omega,\xi_{5})+c(\omega)\in\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}

(30.6)

for
\mu
-a.e.
\omega
, for a.e.
(\xi_{3},\xi_{4},\xi_{5})\sim(X^{\omega}_{3},X^{\omega}_{4},X^{\omega}_{5})
.

Proof.
The function
g(\omega,\xi_{3})
is well-defined for a.e.
\omega
because of claim 2. To see that it is measurable, we note that
(\omega,\xi_{3})\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}(W^{\omega}_{3}\in(a,b))
are measurable, and

\displaystyle[g(\omega,\xi_{3})>a]=\{(\omega,\xi_{3}):{\mathbb{P}}(0<W^{\omega}_{3}\leq a)=0\ ,{\mathbb{P}}(W^{\omega}_{3}>a)\neq 0\}

\displaystyle(a>0)

\displaystyle[g(\omega,\xi_{3})>a]=\{(\omega,\xi_{3}):{\mathbb{P}}(W^{\omega}_{3}>a)\neq 0\}

\displaystyle(a\leq 0)

are measurable.
The measurability of
c(\omega)
is clear.

Equation (
30.6
) holds because the left-hand-side of (
30.6
) is, up to a sign, an atom of the random variable

\Gamma\left(\zeta_{1}(\omega),\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{2}(\omega)\\
Y^{\omega}_{2}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta_{1}(\omega)\\
\xi_{3}\end{array},\begin{array}[]{c}Y^{\omega}_{4}\\
\xi_{4}\end{array},\xi_{5}\right),\ \ \ (\underline{L}^{\omega}_{3},\underline{L}^{\omega}_{4})\sim{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}\left(\cdot\bigg|{\begin{array}[]{ll}Z^{\omega}_{3}=\zeta_{1}(\omega)&X^{\omega}_{3}=\xi_{3}\\
Z^{\omega}_{2}=\zeta_{2}(\omega)&X^{\omega}_{4}=\xi_{4}\\
Z^{\omega}_{1}=\zeta_{1}(\omega)&X^{\omega}_{5}=\xi_{5}\\
\end{array}}\right)

and we chose
(\zeta_{1}(\omega),\zeta_{2}(\omega))
so that this random variable takes values in
\frac{2\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}
a.s.

Claim 3 gives us measurable functions
a(\omega,x):=\exp(-itg(T^{-2}\omega,x))
and

\lambda(\omega):=\exp(-itc(T^{-2}\omega))
such that

e^{it[f(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1},X^{\omega}_{2})+f(T\omega,X^{\omega}_{2},X^{\omega}_{3})]}=\lambda(\omega)\frac{a(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1})}{a(T^{2}\omega,X^{\omega}_{3})}.

Multiplying both sides of the equation by
e^{it[f^{\omega}-f^{\omega}\circ T]}
gives

e^{2itf(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1},X^{\omega}_{2})}=\lambda(\omega)\frac{b(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega},X_{2}^{\omega})}{b(T\omega,X^{\omega}_{2},X^{\omega}_{3})},

where
b(\omega,x,y):=a(\omega,x)a(T\omega,y)e^{itf(\omega,x,y)}
.

This not quite a relative cohomology to a coset of
(\pi/t)\mathbb{Z}
, because
b(\omega,x,y)
seems to depend not just on
x
but also on
y
. In fact there is a bounded measurable function
\beta(\omega,x)
such that

b(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1},X^{\omega}_{2})=\beta(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega})\ \ \mathbb{P}-\text{ almost everywhere},

where
\mathbb{P}
is given by (
29.1
).
This can be seen as follows. Rearrange terms to see that

b(T\omega,X_{2}^{\omega},X^{\omega}_{3})=\lambda(\omega)e^{-2itf(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1},X^{\omega}_{2})}b(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega},X_{2}^{\omega}).

By the Markov property of
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
, for fixed
\omega
, the left-hand-side and the right-hand-side of this equation are conditionally independent given
X^{\omega}_{2}
. Two independent random variables which are equal, must be constant. So for
m
-a.e.
\omega
,

b(T\omega,X_{2}^{\omega},X_{3}^{\omega})=\mathbb{E}^{\omega}(b(T\omega,X_{2}^{\omega},X_{3}^{\omega})|X_{2}^{\omega}).

Setting

\beta(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega}):=\mathbb{E}^{\omega}(b(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega},X_{2}^{\omega})|X_{1}^{\omega})

and using stationarity to shift indices where needed, we find that

b(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega},X_{2}^{\omega})=\beta(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega})\;\;\mathbb{P}-a.e.,\hskip 11.74988ptb(T\omega,X_{2}^{\omega},X_{3}^{\omega})=\beta(T\omega,X_{2}^{\omega})\;\;\mathbb{P}-a.e.

Hence

e^{2itf(\omega,X^{\omega}_{1},X^{\omega}_{2})}=\lambda(\omega)\frac{\beta(\omega,X_{1}^{\omega})}{\beta(T\omega,X^{\omega}_{2})}\ \ \mathbb{P}{\text{-a.e.}}.

So
f
is relatively cohomologous to a coset of
\frac{\pi}{t}\mathbb{Z}
.

We obtained a contradiction to our assumptions. This contradiction shows that
G_{ess}(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})=\mathbb{R}
for a.e.
\omega
. The local limit theorem now follows from
Theorem
12.1
, applied to
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
, since Theorem
29.1
gives the a.s. asymptotic
V_{N}^{\omega}\sim N\sigma^{2}
for some
\sigma^{2}>0
independent of
\omega
.

\Box

Proofs of Theorem
29.3
and
29.7
on the lattice case.

Theorems
29.3
and
29.7
provide the LLT for Markov chains in random environment with finite and infinite noise processes for integer valued additive functionals, under the assumption that
f
is not relatively cohomologous to a coset of
t\mathbb{Z}

with
t\neq 1
.

The proof is similar to the proof in the non-lattice case, except that now to check irreducibility we need to show that
H_{\omega}=\mathbb{Z}
almost surely. Since
f
is integer valued,
1\in H_{\omega}
, so if this is not the case then necessarily
H_{\omega}=t\mathbb{Z}
for
t=\frac{1}{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Now repeat the proof of Theorems
29.2
and
29.6
verbatim.
\Box

30.4
Log-moment generating functions and rate functions

We prove Theorem
29.4
on the a.s. convergence of the log-moment generating functions of
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
and their Legendre transforms.
Suppose
\mathsf{f}
is an essentially bounded additive functional on a MCRE with a finite noise space
(\Omega,\mathfs B,m,T)
. Without loss of generality,
m(\Omega)=1
.

Part (1): Convergence of log-moment generating functions:

We are asked to show that for a.e.
\omega
,
\mathfs G_{N}^{\omega}(\xi):=\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi S_{N}^{\omega}})
converge pointwise on
\mathbb{R}
. To do this we recall three facts from chapter
6
:

Fact 1:

Given
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, for every
\omega\in\Omega
there are unique numbers
\overline{p}_{n}(\xi,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}
and unique non-negative functions
\overline{h}_{n}(\cdot,\xi,\omega)\in L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{S},\mathfs B(\mathfrak{S}),\mu_{T^{n-1}\omega})
such that
\int_{\mathfrak{S}}\overline{h}_{n}(x,\xi,\omega)\mu_{T^{n-1}\omega}(dx)=1
for all
n\geq 1
, and

\int_{\mathfrak{S}}e^{\xi f(T^{n}\omega,x,y)}\frac{\overline{h}_{n+1}(y,\xi,\omega)}{e^{\overline{p}_{n}(\xi,\omega)}\overline{h}_{n}(x,\xi,\omega)}\pi(T^{n}\omega,x,dy)=1.

(30.7)

Furthermore,
\overline{p}_{n}(\xi,\omega)=\overline{p}(\xi,T^{n}\omega)
for all
n
, where and
\overline{p}(\xi,\omega)
is measurable.

Proof.
The existence and uniqueness of
\overline{h}_{n},\overline{p}_{n}
follows from Lemma
6.81
, applied to
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
with
a_{n}=0
. Writing (
30.7
) first for
(n,\omega)
and then for
(n-1,T\omega)
, and then invoking uniqueness, we find that
\overline{p}_{n}(\xi,\omega)=\overline{p}_{n-1}(\xi,T\omega)
. So

\overline{p}_{n}(\xi,\omega)=\overline{p}_{n-1}(\xi,T\omega)=\cdots=\overline{p}_{1}(\xi,T^{n-1}\omega)=\overline{p}(\xi,T^{n}\omega),

where
\overline{p}(\xi,\omega):=\overline{p}_{1}(\xi,T^{-1}\omega)
. The proof of Lemma
6.81
represents
h_{n}(x,\xi,\omega)
as a limit of expressions which are measurable in
(x,\xi,\omega)
, so
(x,\xi,\omega)\mapsto\overline{h}_{n}(x,\xi,\omega)
is measurable. By (
30.7
),
(\omega,\xi)\mapsto\overline{p}(\xi,\omega)
is measurable.

Fact 2:

Let
K:=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
and let
\epsilon_{0}
denote a uniform ellipticity bound for
\mathsf{X}^{\omega}
. For every
R>0
there exists a constant
C(\epsilon_{0},K,R)
such that
|\overline{p}(\xi,\omega)|\leq C(\epsilon_{0},K,R)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
and
|\xi|\leq R
.

Proof.
See the proof of Lemma
6.82
.

Fact 3:

Let
\displaystyle\overline{P}_{N}(\xi,\omega):=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\overline{p}(\xi,T^{k}\omega)
, then for a.e.
\omega\in\Omega
,

\mathfs G_{N}^{\omega}(\xi)=\left(\frac{V_{n}^{\omega}}{N}\right)\left[\frac{\overline{P}_{N}(\xi,\omega)}{V_{N}^{\omega}}+O\left(\frac{1}{V^{\omega}_{N}}\right)\right]\text{ uniformly on compact subsets of $\xi\in\mathbb{R}$.}

Proof.
It is convenient to work with
\mathfs F_{N}^{\omega}(\xi):=\frac{1}{V_{N}^{\omega}}\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi S_{N}^{\omega}})\equiv(N/V_{N}^{\omega})\mathfs G_{N}^{\omega}(\xi)
. Let
P_{N}(\xi,\omega):=\overline{P}_{N}(\xi,\omega)+\bigl(\mathbb{E}(S_{N}^{\omega})-\tfrac{d}{d\xi}\big|_{\xi=0}\overline{P}_{N}^{\prime}(0,\omega)\bigr)\xi
.
For each
\omega\in\Omega
such that
V_{N}^{\omega}\to\infty
,

(1)

\tfrac{d}{d\xi}\big|_{\xi=0}\overline{P}_{N}^{\prime}(0,\omega)
exists, by Lemma
6.84
.

(2)

|P_{N}(\xi,\omega)-\overline{P}_{N}(\xi,\omega)|=O(1)
uniformly on compact subsets of
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, by Lemmas
6.85
and
6.86
.

(3)

|\mathfs F_{N}^{\omega}(\xi)-P_{N}(\xi)/V_{N}^{\omega}|=O(1/V_{N}^{\omega})
uniformly on compact subsets of
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, by Lemma
6.86
. Fact 3 follows.

We can now prove the a.s. convergence of
\mathfs F_{N}^{\omega}(\xi)
.
By the assumptions of the theorem,
\mathsf{f}
is not relatively cohomologous to a constant.
Therefore, by Theorem
29.1
, there exists
\sigma^{2}>0
such that
V_{N}^{\omega}\sim\sigma^{2}N
as
N\to\infty
for a.e.
\omega
.

Fix a countable dense set
\{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots\}\subset\mathbb{R}
. For each
i
,
\omega\mapsto\overline{p}(\xi_{i},\omega)
is bounded and measurable. So for a.e.
\omega
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathfs G_{N}^{\omega}(\xi_{i})=\sigma^{2}\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{V_{N}^{\omega}}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\overline{p}(\xi_{i},T^{k}\omega)=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\overline{p}(\xi_{i},T^{k}\omega)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\overline{p}(\xi_{i},\omega)m(d\omega),\text{ by the Birkhoff ergodic theorem}.

This shows that for all
i
there exists
\mathfs G(\xi_{i})\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathfs G_{N}^{\omega}(\xi_{i})=\mathfs G(\xi_{i})
for a.e.
\omega
. Let
\Omega^{\prime}
denote the set of full measure of
\omega
where this holds for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Fix
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
, then the functions
\xi\mapsto\mathfs F_{N}^{\omega}(\xi)
are equicontinuous on compacts, because if
K:=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup|\mathsf{f}|
, then

|(\mathfs F_{N}^{\omega})^{\prime}(\xi)|\leq\left|\frac{|\xi|\mathbb{E}(|S_{N}^{\omega}|e^{\xi S_{N}^{\omega}})}{V_{N}^{\omega}\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi S_{N}^{\omega}})}\right|=\frac{|\xi|KN}{V_{N}^{\omega}}=O(|\xi|).

Therefore for a.e.
\omega
, the functions
\xi\mapsto\mathfs G_{N}^{\omega}(\xi)
are equicontinuous on compacts.

Recall that if a sequence of functions
\varphi_{n}(\xi)
which is equicontinuous on compacts converges on a dense subset of
\mathbb{R}
, then
\varphi_{n}(\xi)
converges for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, the limit is continuous. So there is a continuous function
\mathfs F^{\omega}(\xi)
such that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathfs G_{N}^{\omega}(\xi)=\mathfs F^{\omega}(\xi)\text{ for all $\xi\in\mathbb{R}$, $\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}$}.

In fact
\mathfs F^{\omega}(\xi)
does not depend on
\omega
, because by virtue of continuity,

\mathfs F^{\omega}(\xi)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathfs F^{\omega}(\xi_{i_{k}})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathfs G(\xi_{i_{k}}),\text{ whenever }\xi_{i_{k}}\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\xi_{i}.

We are therefore free to write
\mathfs F^{\omega}(\xi)=\mathfs F(\xi)
.

It remains to show that
\mathfs F(\xi)
is differentiable and strictly convex on
\mathbb{R}
. Fix
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
. Applying Theorem
19.1
to
(\mathsf{X}^{\omega},\mathsf{f}^{\omega})
we find that for every
R>0
there is a
C=C(R)
such that
C^{-1}\leq(\mathfs F^{\omega}_{N})^{\prime\prime}\leq C
on
[-R,R]
. This implies that
\mathfs F
is differentiable and strictly convex on
(-R,R)
because of the following general lemma:

Lemma 8.122

Suppose
\varphi_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are twice differentiable convex functions such that
C^{-1}\leq\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{n}\leq C
with
C>0
, on
(-R,R)
. If
\varphi_{n}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\varphi
pointwise on
(-R,R)
, then
\varphi
is continuously differentiable and strictly convex on
(-R,R)
.

Proof

A pointwise limit of convex functions is convex, and convex functions have one sided derivatives. Let
\varphi^{\prime}_{\pm}(\xi)
denote the one-sided derivatives of at
\xi
.

Differentiability:

For all
|\xi|<R
,

\displaystyle|\varphi^{\prime}_{+}(\xi)-\varphi^{\prime}_{-}(\xi)|=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\left|\frac{\varphi(\xi+h)-\varphi(\xi)}{h}-\frac{\varphi(\xi-h)-\varphi(\xi)}{h}\right|

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\left|\frac{\varphi_{n}(\xi+h)-\varphi_{n}(\xi)}{h}-\frac{\varphi_{n}(\xi-h)-\varphi_{n}(\xi)}{h}\right|

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\lim_{n\to\infty}|\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{n})-\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\eta_{n})|\text{ for some }\xi_{n},\eta_{n}\in(\xi-h,\xi+h)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\lim_{n\to\infty}2Ch=0,\text{ because }|\varphi_{n}^{\prime\prime}|\leq C\text{ on a neighborhood of $\xi$.}

We find that
\varphi^{\prime}_{+}(\xi)=\varphi^{\prime}_{-}(\xi)
, whence
\varphi
is differentiable at
\xi
.

Strict convexity:
Suppose
-R<\xi<\eta<R
, then

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}(\eta)-\varphi^{\prime}(\xi)=\varphi^{\prime}_{+}(\eta)-\varphi^{\prime}_{-}(\xi)=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\frac{\varphi(\eta+h)-\varphi(\eta)}{h}-\frac{\varphi(\xi-h)-\varphi(\xi)}{h}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\varphi_{n}(\eta+h)-\varphi_{n}(\eta)}{h}-\frac{\varphi_{n}(\xi-h)-\varphi_{n}(\xi)}{h}

\displaystyle=\lim_{h\to 0^{+}}\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\eta_{n})-\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{n})\text{ for some }\xi_{n}\in[\xi-h,\xi],\ \eta_{n}\in[\eta,\eta+h]

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{n\to\infty}C^{-1}|\eta_{n}-\xi_{n}|\geq C^{-1}(\eta-\xi),\text{ because }\varphi_{n}^{\prime\prime}>C^{-1}\text{ on }(-R,R).

It follows that
\varphi^{\prime}
is strictly increasing on
(-R,R)
.

The derivative is continuous:
The same calculation as before shows that if
-R<\xi<\eta<R
, then

|\varphi^{\prime}(\eta)-\varphi^{\prime}(\xi)|\leq C|\xi-\eta|
, whence
\varphi^{\prime}
is (Lipschitz) continuous on
(-R,R)
.

\square

Part (2): Convergence of
\mathbb{E}(S_{N}^{\omega})/N
:
We need the following standard fact.

Lemma 8.123

Suppose
\varphi_{n}(\xi),\varphi(x)
are finite, convex, and differentiable on
(-R,R)
. If
\varphi_{n}(\xi)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\varphi(\xi)
on
(-R,R)
, then
\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\varphi^{\prime}(\xi)
on
(-R,R)
.

Proof

Fix
\xi\in(-R,R)
. By convexity, for every
h>0
sufficiently small,

\frac{\varphi_{n}(\xi)-\varphi_{n}(\xi-h)}{h}\leq\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi)\leq\frac{\varphi_{n}(\xi+h)-\varphi_{n}(\xi)}{h}.

(30.8)

To see this note that the LHS is at most
(\varphi_{n})^{\prime}_{-}(\xi)
, the RHS is at least
(\varphi_{n})^{\prime}_{+}(\xi)
, and both one-sided derivatives equal
\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi)
.

Passing to the limit
n\to\infty
in (
30.8
), we find that

\limsup\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi),\liminf\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi)\in\left[\frac{\varphi(\xi)-\varphi(\xi-h)}{h},\frac{\varphi(\xi+h)-\varphi(\xi)}{h}\right].

We now invoke the differentiability of
\varphi
, pass to the limit
h\to 0^{+}
, and discover that

\limsup\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi)
and
\liminf\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi)
are both equal to
\varphi^{\prime}(\xi)
.
\square

For a.e.
\omega
,
(V_{N}^{\omega}/N)\mathfs F_{N}^{\omega}(\xi)\equiv\mathfs G_{N}(\xi,\omega)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathfs F(\xi)
. So by the lemma

(V_{N}^{\omega}/N)\left.\frac{d}{d\xi}\right|_{\xi=0}\mathfs F_{N}^{\omega}(\xi,\omega)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathfs F^{\prime}(0).

A calculation shows that the derivative equals
\mathbb{E}(S_{N}^{\omega})/V_{N}^{\omega}
. So
\mathbb{E}(S_{N}^{\omega})/N\to\mathfs F^{\prime}(0)
.

Part (3): Convergence of Legendre transforms.
Again, the proof is based on a general property of convex functions.

Lemma 8.124

Suppose
\varphi_{n}(\xi),\varphi(\xi)
are finite, strictly convex, continuously differentiable functions on
\mathbb{R}
, s.t.
\varphi_{n}(\xi)\to\varphi(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\varphi^{\prime}(\pm\infty):=\lim\limits_{\xi\to\pm\infty}\varphi^{\prime}(\xi)
. Let
\varphi_{n}^{\ast},\varphi^{\ast}
denote the Legendre transforms of
\varphi_{n},\varphi
. For all
\eta\in(\varphi^{\prime}(-\infty),\varphi^{\prime}(+\infty))
,
\varphi_{n}^{\ast}(\eta)
is well-defined for all
n
sufficiently large, and
\varphi_{n}^{\ast}(\eta)\to\varphi^{\ast}(\eta)
.

Proof

Fix
\eta\in(\varphi^{\prime}(-\infty),\varphi^{\prime}(+\infty))
. By assumption,
\varphi^{\prime}
is continuous and strictly increasing. Therefore, there exists
\xi
such that
\varphi^{\prime}(\eta)=\xi
.

Fix
\epsilon>0
and
\xi_{1}<\xi<\xi_{2}
such that
|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|<\epsilon
. Then

\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{1})<\eta<\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{2}).

By Lemma
8.123
,
\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{i})\to\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{i})
, and therefore there exists
N
such that for all
n>N
,

\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{1})-1<\varphi^{\prime}_{n}(\xi_{1})<\eta<\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{2})<\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{2})+1.

Since

\eta\in(\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{1}),\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{2}))

and
\varphi_{n}^{\prime}
is continuous and strictly increasing, there exists a unique
\xi_{n}\in(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
so that

\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{n})=\eta.

So
\varphi_{n}^{\ast}(\eta)
is well-defined, and

\varphi_{n}^{\ast}(\eta)=\xi_{n}\eta-\varphi_{n}(\xi_{n}).

Similarly,
\varphi^{\ast}(\eta)=\xi\eta-\varphi(\xi)
.

We now estimate the distance between
\varphi_{n}^{\ast}(\eta)
and
\varphi^{\ast}(\eta)
. Recall first that for all
n>N
,
\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{1})-1<\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{1})<\varphi_{n}^{\prime}(\xi_{2})<\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{2})+1
. Let

M:=\max\{|\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{1})-1|,|\varphi^{\prime}(\xi_{2})+1|\},

then
|\varphi_{n}^{\prime}|\leq M
on
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
for all
n>N
. Consequently,

\displaystyle|\varphi_{n}^{\ast}(\eta)-\varphi^{\ast}(\eta)|\leq|\xi_{n}-\xi|\cdot|\eta|+|\varphi_{n}(\xi_{n})-\varphi(\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|\cdot|\eta|+|\varphi_{n}(\xi_{n})-\varphi_{n}(\xi)|+|\varphi_{n}(\xi)-\varphi(\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq{\varepsilon}|\eta|+M|\xi_{n}-\xi|+|\varphi_{n}(\xi)-\varphi(\xi)|\leq\epsilon(M+|\eta|)+o(1),\text{ as $n\to\infty$},

because
\varphi_{n}(\xi)\to\varphi(\xi)
,
\xi,\xi_{n}\in(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
, and
|\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}|\leq\epsilon
. Since
\epsilon
is arbitrary, we have that
\varphi_{n}^{\ast}(\eta)\to\varphi^{\ast}(\eta)
.
\square

Part (4): Properties of
\mathfs I(\eta)
.
Fix
\omega
such that
\varphi_{N}(\xi):=\frac{1}{N}\log\mathbb{E}(e^{\xi S_{N}^{\omega}})
converges pointwise to
\mathfs F
. By
Lemma
8.124
,
\varphi_{N}^{\ast}
converges pointwise to
\mathfs I
. Since
\varphi_{N}^{\prime\prime}
is uniformly bounded away from zero and infinity on compacts (see the first part of the proof),
(\varphi_{N}^{\ast})^{\prime\prime}
is uniformly bounded away from zero and infinity on compacts. Hence
by Lemma
8.122

\mathfs I=\lim\varphi_{N}^{\ast}\text{ is strictly convex and continuously differentiable}.

By Lemma
8.123
,

(\varphi_{N}^{\ast})^{\prime}(\eta)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathfs I^{\prime}(\eta)
for all
\eta
in the interior of the range of
\varphi^{\prime}
, and
\varphi_{N}(\xi)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}\mathfs F^{\prime}(\xi)
for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
. The convergence is uniform on compacts, because
(\varphi_{N}^{\ast})^{\prime\prime},\varphi_{N}^{\prime\prime}
are bounded on compacts.

It is easy to verify that
\varphi_{N}
is twice differentiable. Therefore by Lemma
6.91
,
\varphi_{N}^{\ast}
is twice differentiable and

(\varphi_{N}^{\ast})^{\prime}(\varphi_{N}^{\prime}(\xi))=\xi\text{ for all }\xi.

Passing to the limit as
N\to\infty
we obtain the important identity

\mathfs I^{\prime}(\mathfs F^{\prime}(\xi))=\xi\text{ for all }\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

One consequence of the identity
\mathfs I^{\prime}(\mathfs F^{\prime}(\xi))=\xi
is that
\mathfs I^{\prime}(\mathfs F^{\prime}(0))=0
, so
\eta=\mathfs F^{\prime}(0)
is a critical point of
\mathfs I(\cdot)
. By strict convexity,
\mathfs I
attains its global minimum at
\mathfs F^{\prime}(0)
. The value there is zero:

\mathfs I(\mathfs F^{\prime}(0))=0\cdot\mathfs F^{\prime}(0)-\mathfs F(0)=0.

We conclude that
\mathfs I(\eta)=0
when
\eta=\mathfs F^{\prime}(0)
, and
\mathfs I(\eta)>0
for
\eta\neq\mathfs F^{\prime}(0)
.

Another consequence of the identity
\mathfs I^{\prime}(\mathfs F^{\prime}(\xi))=\xi
(and the fact that
\mathfs F^{\prime}
is increasing) is that
\mathfs I^{\prime}(\xi)\xrightarrow[\xi\to\pm\infty]{}\pm\infty
, and therefore

\mathfs I

has compact level sets.
\Box

Part (5): Large deviation threshold.
We prove the identity for
\mathfs F^{\prime}(\infty)
, the identity for
\mathfs F^{\prime}(-\infty)
follows by replacing

\mathsf{f}\to-\mathsf{f}.

Step I.

\mathfrak{c}_{+}\geq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(+\infty).

Proof.
Given
\eta\in(\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(-\infty),\mathcal{F}(+\infty))
choose

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(-\infty)<\eta^{-}<\eta<\eta^{+}<\mathcal{F}(+\infty).

Take
\xi^{\pm}
be s.t.

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(\xi^{\pm})=\eta^{\pm}.
By Lemma
8.123
,
\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{N}^{\prime}(\xi^{\pm})=\eta^{\pm}.

Hence for large
N

\mathcal{F}_{N}^{\prime}(\xi^{-})\leq\eta\leq\mathcal{F}_{N}(\xi^{+})
and so
\eta
is reachable.

Step II.

\mathfrak{c}_{+}\leq\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(+\infty).

Proof.
Take
\eta>\mathfrak{c}_{+}.
If
\eta\in\mathcal{C}_{R}
for some
R
we would have that for some
R
, for all
N
large enough

\mathcal{F}_{N}^{\prime}(R)\geq\eta
(see Lemma
6.86
(5)). However by
Lemma
8.123
,
\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{F}_{N}(R)=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(R)<\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(+\infty)<\eta

contradicting our assumption that
\eta
is reachable.

Step III.
Denote
\mathcal{S}_{N}(\omega)=\mathrm{ess\,}\sup S_{N}^{\omega}.
Then the limit

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}^{+}:=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}(\omega)}{N}
exists and is independent
of
\omega
with probability one.

Proof.
By our ellipticity assumption

\mathcal{S}_{N+M}(\omega)\leq\mathcal{S}_{N}(\omega)+S_{M}(T^{N}\omega)-4K.

Thus the sequence
\mathcal{T}_{N}(\omega)=\mathcal{S}_{N}(\omega)-4K
is subadditive.
Since
\mathcal{S}_{N}(\omega)\geq-KN
the Subadditive Ergodic Theorem implies that
the limit

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{S}_{N}(\omega)}{N}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{T}_{N}(\omega)}{N}
exists and is independent
of
\omega
with probability one.

Step IV.

\mathfrak{c}_{+}\leq\mathfrak{s}^{+}
because for each
{\varepsilon}>0
we have that with probability one
for large
N
,
{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}(S_{N}\geq(\mathfrak{s}^{+}+{\varepsilon})N)=0.

Step V.

\mathfrak{c}_{+}\geq\mathfrak{s}^{+}

Proof.
Fix
{\varepsilon}>0.
By Step III for each sufficiently large
N_{0}

there exists
\gamma_{{\varepsilon},N_{0}}>0
and a set
\Omega_{{\varepsilon},N_{0}}
s.t.

m(\Omega_{{\varepsilon},N_{0}})\geq 1-{\varepsilon}
and for all
\omega\in\Omega_{\epsilon,N_{0}}
,
for
\mu_{\omega}
-a.e.
x\in\mathfrak{S}
,

{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}(S_{N_{0}}\geq(\mathfrak{s}^{+}-{\varepsilon})N_{0}|X_{1}=x)\geq\gamma_{{\varepsilon},N_{0}}.

(30.9)

Given
M
let
j_{1}(\omega)<j_{2}(\omega)<\cdots<j_{n_{M}(\omega)}(\omega)
be all the times
1\leq j<M
when
T^{jN_{0}}(\omega)\in\Omega_{\epsilon,N_{0}}
, then

{\mathbb{P}}^{\omega}\left(\mathcal{S}_{N_{0}M}\geq n_{M}(\mathfrak{s}^{+}-{\varepsilon})N_{0}-(M-n_{M})N_{0}K\right)\geq\gamma_{{\varepsilon},N_{0}}^{N_{0}M}.

(To see this, estimate conditional probabilities of this event given
X_{j_{1}}^{\omega},\ldots,X_{j_{n_{M}}}^{\omega}
using (
30.9
),
and take expectation over
X_{j_{1}}^{\omega},\ldots,X_{j_{n_{M}}}^{\omega}
.)

By the Ergodic Theorem, for a.e.
\omega
there is a limit

\beta(\omega)=\lim_{M\to\infty}\frac{n_{M}(\omega)}{M}\hskip 11.74988pt\text{and}\hskip 11.74988pt\int\beta(\omega)dm=m(\Omega_{\epsilon,N_{0}}).

So for large
M
, and on a set
{\overline{\Omega}}_{\varepsilon}
of positive measure,
n_{M}/M>1-2\epsilon
whence

n_{M}(\mathfrak{s}^{+}-{\varepsilon})N_{0}-(M-n_{M})N_{0}K\geq\left[(1-2{\varepsilon})(\mathfrak{s}^{+}-{\varepsilon})-2{\varepsilon}K\right]N_{0}M.

Now Theorem
21.1
(c) shows that on
{\overline{\Omega}}_{\varepsilon}
,

\mathfrak{c}^{+}(\omega)\geq(1-2{\varepsilon})(\mathfrak{s}^{+}-{\varepsilon})-2{\varepsilon}K.

By steps I and II above,
\mathfrak{c}^{+}
actually does not depend on
\omega

(in fact, using Theorem
21.1
, it is easy to verify directly
that
\mathfrak{c}^{+}
is
T
-invariant, and therefore by ergodicity, constant).
we get
that

\mathfrak{c}^{+}(\omega)\geq(1-2{\varepsilon})(\mathfrak{s}^{+}-{\varepsilon})-2{\varepsilon}K

almost surely. Since
{\varepsilon}
is arbitrary the result follows.

\Box

31
Notes and references

Markov chains in random environment (MCRE) should not be confused with “random walks in random environment” (RWRE). In the RWRE model, the transition kernel at time
n
depends on the
position
of random walk at time
n
, i.e.
\pi_{n}(x,dy)=\pi(S_{n},x,dy)
. In a MCRE, the transition kernel at time
n
depends on the
noise
at time
n
, i.e.
\pi_{n}(x,dy)=\pi(T^{n}\omega,x,dy)
. For a recent treatment of the LLT for RWRE, see
Castell
and references therein.

Markov chains in random environment were introduced by Cogburn
Cogburn-1980
. The setup is a particular case of a “random dynamical system.”
For a fixed realization of noise, a Markov chain in random environment reduces to an inhomogeneous Markov chain, and a random dynamical system reduces to a “sequential” (aka “time-dependent” or “non-autonomous”) dynamical system.
Various authors considered probabilistic limit theorems in these contexts.
Limit theorems for Markov chains in random environment are given in Cogburn
Cogburn
, Seppäläinen
Seppalainen
, Kifer
Kifer-RPF
,
Kifer-CLT
and Hafouta & Kifer
(
Hafouta-Kifer-Book
, chapters 6,7)
,
Hafouta-Kifer-Nonconventional
. Results for random dynamical systems can be found in Kifer
Kifer-CLT
,
Conze, Le Borgne & Roger
Conze-Le-Borgne
, Denker & Gordin
Denker-Gordin
, Aimino, Nicol & Vaienti
Aimino-Nicol-Vaienti
, Nicol, Török & Vaienti
Nicol-Torok-Vaienti
, and Dragičević, Froyland & González-Tokman
Dragicevic-Froyland-Gonzalez-Tokman
. For limit theorems for sequential dynamical systems, see Bakhtin
Ba
,
Conze & Raugi
Conze-Raugi-Sequential
, Haydn, Nicol & Török
Haydn-Nicol-Torok
, Korepanov, Kosloff & Melbourne
Korepanov-Kosloff-Melbourne
, and Hafouta
Hafouta-Skew-Products
;
Hafouta-Sequential
.

If we set the noise process to be the identity on the one point space, then the LLT in this chapter reduce to LLT for homogeneous stationary Markov chains, as in Theorem
23.1
. For more general LLT for homogeneous Markov chains, see Nagaev
N
, Guivarc’h & Hardy
GH
.

The results of this chapter are all essentially known in the case
T
preserves
a finite measure.
Theorem
29.1
was proved in the more general setup of random dynamical systems by Kifer
Kifer-CLT
,
Kifer-1986-book
. Theorems
29.2
and
29.3
are close to the (earlier) results of Dragičević, Froyland & González-Tokman
Dragicevic-Froyland-Gonzalez-Tokman
, and Hafouta & Kifer
(
Hafouta-Kifer-Book
, chapter 7, Theorem 7.1.5)
. The main difference is in the irreducibility assumptions. Our condition of non-relative cohomology to a coset is replaced in
Hafouta-Kifer-Book
by what these authors call the “lattice” and “non-lattice” cases (this is not the same as our terminology). In the paper
Dragicevic-Froyland-Gonzalez-Tokman
, the non-cohomology condition is replaced by a condition on the decay of the norms of certain perturbed characteristic function operators, and a connection to a non-cohomology condition is made under additional assumptions.

The results for infinite noise processes seem to be new.
The reason we can also treat this case, is that the LLT we provide in this work do not require any assumptions on the rate of growth of
V_{N}
, and they also work when it grows sub-linearly.
